# Changelog -- August 2026

Split out of `docs/CHANGELOG.md` verbatim on 2026-09-07; that file is
now the index. Newest first, as before.

## The first live town visit, and the three bugs it found (2026-08-31)

The town layer was built, offline-tested at 2072 tests, and had never sent a
click. This is what happened when it did.

### The navigation strip is not on the Hamlet

`building_navigation.layout.darkest` indexes all ten buildings and
`townlayout.building_button` derives each one correctly, so the opener clicked
the derived point. Nothing opened. **DD draws that strip INSIDE a building
panel** -- it is how you hop between buildings once one is open; from the town
screen you click the building in the scene. `town_screen_layout` carries
`button_navigation_offscreen_pos -40 230` beside `button_navigation_pos
70 230`, which was the warning sign and was read as "it slides in", not "it is
somewhere else entirely".

**The save-acknowledgement design is the only reason this was harmless**, and
it is worth quoting because it is exactly what it was built for:

```
[town] open the stage_coach      CLICK 181,291
[night] OK          open the stage coach
[night] UNCONFIRMED numrecruits.a      (the wallet did not move)
[night] UNCONFIRMED 3 crest -> 1 deed  (the wallet did not move)
[night] UNCONFIRMED numrecruits.b      (the wallet did not move)
[night] UNCONFIRMED hire plague_doctor (the roster did not grow)
[night] UNCONFIRMED hire houndmaster   (the roster did not grow)
```

Six clicks and two drags went into an open Hamlet. **Nothing was claimed as
done**, the wallet on disk was still `bust 10, portrait 10, deed 10, crest 20`
afterwards, and the loop embarked and carried on -- because a town visit is
never fatal to the night. Screen-based acknowledgement would have reported
five successes.

**The x is derivable, the y is not.** Hue-detecting the Hamlet's red banners
off a live frame -- the method that measured `TOWN_CLOSE_PANEL` -- puts the
stage coach at design x **313.9** against `stage_coach_layout.pos` of **315**,
and the statue at **939** against **940**. The y fits no linear model: the
scene is a perspective projection with a per-building z, and `alert_offset` is
in units of its own (fitting the stage coach and the graveyard predicts the
statue 23 px out and the nomad wagon 146 px out). So `BUILDING_SCENE_POINTS`
is measured, holds one building, and `plan_open_building` **refuses** for the
other nine rather than falling back to the derivable-but-wrong strip.

### The guard that should have caught it had its polarity backwards

`_panel_is_open` was written before this run for exactly this risk and let the
clicks through. It read `recognise(img) not in TOWN_SCREENS`, so an
unrecognised frame -- a mid-transition, or an interior with no banked
reference -- counted as "the panel opened". **A one-sided test has to say NO
when it cannot see.**

It now returns True only for a screen in `BUILDING_SCREENS`, which is empty
until an interior is banked. On refusal it banks the frame (that frame is how
the first interior gets its reference) and **still clicks the close**, because
"did not open" and "opened but unrecognised" are the same observation, and a
panel left over the Hamlet swallows the embark walk and reports as an
unrecognised screen with no clue why.

### `dungeon_intro` is a LOADING GATE, and it cost a working campaign

The boot reached the dungeon's title plate, pressed SPACE, and the screen did
not change. It pressed twice more over 12 s, hit `MAX_REPEAT`, and reported
`PAUSED - 'dungeon_intro' did not respond to 3 clicks`. The night recorded
`boot-failed` and **deleted the slot**. A minute later the party was standing
in the Ruins: the boot had worked.

SPACE dismisses that plate only once the dungeon has finished loading, and the
plate stays up meanwhile. `PATIENT_SCREENS` already existed for precisely this
shape -- its comment on the loading screen says "its Continue crest does
nothing until the raid has finished loading... the screen after it is the
dungeon, which has no reference, so `app.log` is what ends the wait". That is
`dungeon_intro` word for word, and it was not in the list.

**A repeat counter measures "is this screen responding", which is the wrong
question for a gate waiting on a load** -- and `app.log`, the correct signal,
cannot report a raid that has not started loading yet. Two tests pin the rule
and the fact that `MAX_STEPS` cannot undercut a patient screen's own
allowance.

### And the front end is CANVAS space, so the derivation stops there

`fe_flow_positions` positions the save list in a tall scrolling canvas
(`ruins_center 0 -1080`, `insanity_center 0 -2360`, `saves_scroll 0 -1520`) --
camera stops, not screen points. Its numbers do not reproduce the measured
`SAVE_ROW_*` constants (144 against a measured pitch of 136.5). So
`bridge/townlayout.py`'s method is for the TOWN; the front end stays measured.
`captures/save_list_scrollbar.png` banks the state that blocks the loop -- two
campaigns, the first free slot on row 3, the scrollbar and both arrows visible
-- and is what a scroll capability should be measured from.

## The test suite deleted the evidence the loop runs on (2026-08-31)

Found while starting a live session: `tools/overnight.py` refused to begin
because `bridge/reset.claim_slot` could not prove it owned `profile_1` -- a
campaign it had itself created that same morning.

**The refusal was correct. The evidence had been destroyed by pytest.**

`claim_slot(slot, save_root=..., journal=CLAIM_JOURNAL)` took the journal as a
default argument. `tests/bridge/test_menus.py` overrides `save_root` with a
`tmp_path` -- which is the whole point of those tests -- but most of its calls
do not override `journal`. So **every suite run wrote about twenty claims
about pytest temp directories into the production
`archive/reset_claims.json`**, and `JOURNAL_KEEP` (50) evicted the real ones
within two or three runs. Measured: after four suite runs in one session the
production journal held twenty entries and **every single one was a
`pytest-of-Francisco` path**.

The consequence is worse than a lost file. The journal exists so an iteration
that crashes between creating a campaign and deleting it can ADOPT its own
leftover on the next run; without it the strict refusal stands, the slot is
permanently unusable, and the only ways forward are to delete a save by hand
or to hand-write a journal entry -- both of which defeat the invariant that
`delete_owned` can only ever be handed something `claim_slot` produced.

**`journal_for(save_root)` is the fix, and it is structural.** The production
root resolves to `CLAIM_JOURNAL`; any other root gets a journal inside itself.
A claim about another world cannot reach this world's journal even from a
caller that forgets the argument, which is the property a convention could not
give. Verified by capturing the journal's bytes, running
`tests/bridge/test_menus.py`, and comparing: identical.

**It goes INSIDE the given root, not beside it.** `root.parent` was the first
attempt and it is wrong for the same class of reason: for pytest that is the
shared `tmp_path` base, so two tests -- or two xdist workers -- would share one
journal and the isolation would be lost again one level up. It still outlives
what it describes, because a claim is about
`<root>/<id>/262060/remote/profile_N`, several levels down.

Three tests pin it, including one that asserts the production journal is
byte-identical after a temp-root claim.

**The operational rule that follows:** a leftover the tool cannot prove it owns
is not a reason to delete anything. Point the runner at a free slot --
`tools/overnight.py --slot N`, added the same day, because the slot had been
hardcoded in four places. When no free slot is reachable either (DD shows two
save rows and refuses below the fold), that is a real gap in the front-end
layer, not a licence to bypass the invariant.

## Three answers from Fran's review, and two of them were bugs (2026-08-31)

He watched several tutorial runs and asked three combat questions. One was
already right, one was a genuine defect in the observation, and one was a
deadlock that had been stopping runs dead.

### AoE does NOT double-hit a size-2 enemy -- no bug, and it is now pinned

His question, quoting the wiki: *"an attack will affect a unit once at most,
regardless of how many ranks are hit. For example, a size 2 enemy will not take
double damage from an attack that hits both of its ranks."*

`CombatEngine._resolve_action_targets` iterates **combatants, not ranks**:

```python
return [c for c in pool if reachable(c)
        and not positions.isdisjoint(c.occupied_positions)]
```

One `CombatantState` cannot appear twice in a comprehension over `pool`, and
the legality path does `sorted(set(...))` then appends a **single** Action for
an AoE. Effects apply inside `for target in targets:` over that unique list.

What existed already was `test_an_aoe_covering_a_large_monsters_back_rank_hits_it`
-- the half that proves a large monster is REACHED by an AoE covering one of its
ranks. The other half, that covering BOTH does not charge it twice, was NOT
pinned, and they are separate failure modes. It is now
(`test_an_aoe_damages_a_size_2_enemy_ONCE_not_twice`).

**One genuine asymmetry surfaced and is deliberately not "fixed".** The
monster -> hero AoE path matches `h.position in positions`, not
`occupied_positions`. That is correct today only because every hero is size 1 --
a size-2 hero would be missed by an AoE covering just its back rank, which is
the same bug that was fixed on the enemy side. No shipped hero is size 2, so
the premise is pinned by a test that names the site to change if a mod or DLC
ever ships one, rather than shipping speculative code.

### `alive_frac` divided by 4 -- a real observation bug for any party that is not four

`env/gym_env.py` emitted `sum(alive) / RANKS` with `RANKS` hardcoded at 4. So a
**perfectly healthy two-hero party reported 0.5** -- indistinguishable from a
four-hero party that had just lost half of itself, which is the single thing the
feature exists to say. Nothing pinned either denominator.

`ExpeditionSequenceEnv` computes a SECOND alive fraction in its context block,
correctly, against `len(self._original_hero_ids)`. So a sequence observation
carried **both numbers and they disagreed** for any party that was not four.

The situation is reachable, not theoretical: an expedition continues with
survivors after a death, and `bridge/seed.py` builds an engine from whatever the
live save holds -- including the **two-hero tutorial party**, which is what every
overnight iteration plays first. `env/expedition_env.py` also skips 1-hero
parties outright (`if len(hero_ids) < 2: continue`), so the case is barely
trained.

Fixed to divide by `len(self.engine.heroes)`, which IS the party that started
this combat: the list is appended to only at `reset()` and never removed from, so
a dead hero still occupies a slot in it. The two fractions are now deliberately
different quantities that are both 1.0 for an intact party of any size --
"how much of the party that walked into this fight is still up" against "how much
of the expedition's roster is left".

**The enemy side keeps `/RANKS`, and that is not an inconsistency.** A board
holds up to four enemy ranks whatever arrived, so "how full is the enemy line" is
the question there. `tests/env/test_small_party_obs.py` pins both, so they can never
be made consistent by mistake.

**This is an observation-CONTENT change.** By this project's own rule, every
checkpoint trained before it is off-distribution for non-4 parties. The effect is
confined to a case the pool barely trains; do not re-quote a pre-fix small-party
number.

### The tutorial retreat stall was a DEADLOCK, not a misclick

Fran: *"The combat model tries to retreat on the tutorial and gets stuck
sometimes, on the tutorial, there's no retreat icon on the hud."*

Traced end to end. Retreat is legal on essentially every turn -- the only gate is
`if not (self.party_surprised and self.round == 0)` -- and **the bridge had no
concept of "the game offers no control for this action"**:

1. the advisor ranks `__retreat__` first;
2. `run.py::advised_button` returns `-1` (correctly: retreat is its own button,
   not one of the four skill icons);
3. `plan_battle` refuses -- "retreat has no calibrated button";
4. `ActRunner` increments `refused` and **returns without adding the turn key to
   `self.acted`**, which only happens on a successful send or a human decline.

So the dedupe guard never trips, nobody plays the turn, the save never changes,
the same update republishes, and the identical refusal repeats every poll
forever. **It never misclicked -- it stopped.**

Two halves to the fix, and the second is what makes the first safe:

- **The advisor's whole ranked shortlist reaches the actuator**
  (`Advisor.last_actions`, `Update.alternatives`). `plan_battle` now falls
  through to the next-best legal move when the top pick has no control on
  screen. This is general: it also covers a provision (used from the inventory)
  and the Abomination being offered a beast-form skill that is not on his bar.
- **The TIMING refusals never fall through.** Provisional advice, pending enemy
  turns and a pending repositioner are all statements that the turn is not due
  yet, and **every candidate for that turn shares them** -- so walking the
  shortlist would only find a different way to act too early. `plan_battle`
  checks those once, before any candidate, and refuses outright.
  `tests/bridge/test_act.py::test_a_TIMING_refusal_never_falls_through` pins it,
  because a green-by-accident here turns the fallback into a way to play
  provisional advice, which is the one thing layer 3 must not do.

**A terminal refusal now marks the turn handled**, so it is reported once rather
than every poll. That is safe for the transient refusals too, and it is a
property of the key rather than a hope: `turn_key` is
`(round, guid, turns_taken, enemies_first)` and all three timing refusals are
gated on `enemies_first`, so the moment those enemy turns resolve the key is a
different one and the turn is planned again from scratch.

## The bridge runs itself: reset, drag, the town, and three bugs it found (2026-08-31)

Every defect this bridge has ever had was found by a live run and read out of
its own log. That loop was rate-limited by a human sitting between iterations
to delete the save and start the next campaign. `tools/overnight.py` is that
human, and within three iterations it had found two bugs nobody was looking
for.

### What it does

Launch the game, boot a campaign, play the Old Road, walk back to the Hamlet,
provision, embark on a generated dungeon, play that, and repeat -- then delete
the slot and start over. Per-iteration logs, a rolling `summary.json`, and a
stall watchdog. It refuses to start beside a training run or a pytest suite,
both of which have crashed this machine.

### The safety argument is a TYPE, not a check

`delete_owned` accepts only an `OwnedSlot`, and the only constructor is
`claim_slot`, which **refuses a directory that already exists**. A save on
disk before the process started cannot be represented, so it cannot be passed
to the delete at all -- rather than being caught by a condition inside it that
a later edit could weaken. Proven by exhaustion over every occupied slot, the
same way the delete-X tripwire works, and both gates were confirmed to fail
their tests when weakened.

**The delete is by PATH, not by clicking the red X.** `BRIDGE.md` has refused
that X since the module was written, because the save list shows only two rows
and a real campaign sits on row 1. Naming the directory removes the whole
class of failure.

**A crash must not end the night, and the strict rule meant it did.** After
the first crashed iteration left `profile_1` behind, every later run refused
-- correctly, and fatally. Claims are written to a journal OUTSIDE the game's
save directory, and an existing slot may be ADOPTED when the journal says this
tool created it and never released it. The invariant holds rather than
weakens: a campaign a human made has no entry, a delete releases its claim,
and an entry expires so last week's claim cannot speak for what is in that
slot today.

### Fran's finding: the game must be RESTARTED between iterations

Deleting a save without restarting leaves DD's cached slot list stale, and it
reproduced exactly: the next campaign created in that slot skipped the
difficulty screen and came out with 0 gold and an estate named **"Click the
letter to begin..."** -- the save-select HINT TEXT, captured as the name. So
`delete_owned` accepts a CLOSED game as the stronger unloaded state, teardown
closes it, and a launch per iteration is the normal path. The relaunch bound
had to be re-scoped to consecutive FAILURES to get a window; counting launches
would have capped the night at six iterations.

### Completing the tutorial once changes what a new campaign does

DD then skips the Old Road and drops new campaigns straight into the Hamlet.
Observed as a campaign with 0 gold while `run.py` -- reading the raid template
the slot was created from -- cheerfully advised on `rooA` in a dungeon that was
not loaded. `-resetcampaigntutorials` restores it. Related: a boot that lands
in the Hamlet is still a boot, and `menus.py` used to call it a failure and
throw the working save away.

### THREE BUGS THE LOOP FOUND

**Only one of the game's eight corpse classes was recognised.**
`CORPSE_MONSTER_CLASS = "corpse_A"`, tested with `==`, against a game that
ships `corpse_A..D` and `corpse_large_A..D` -- one small and one large per
difficulty tier. Every other corpse fell through as an ordinary monster, was
absent from `store.enemies`, and `bridge/seed.py` refused the whole board:
`cannot seed: unknown monster id(s): ['corpse_large_A']`, after which the
advisor is silent for the rest of the fight. **At Veteran and Champion that is
EVERY corpse.** It hid because the Old Road is Apprentice with small monsters,
so the only corpse the tutorial can produce is the one id that worked. The
check is a prefix now, and the trailing underscore is load-bearing:
`corpsegrinder` is a real Warrens monster.

**Every self-buff in the game was unplayable.** `advised_slot` returned
`("", -1)` whenever the target id was negative -- how the engine encodes "no
target" -- so `plan_battle` refused with "advice names no target rank to
click" and the actuator never played Bulwark of Faith, Take Aim, Bolster or
Solemnity. The refusal was right about the DATA and wrong about the SCREEN: DD
casts a self-targeted skill by pressing the digit and clicking the caster. The
ORDER of the checks is load-bearing, since pass, retreat and provisions also
carry a negative id and are genuinely rankless.

**A pipe nobody drains deadlocks the child.** The play phase was launched with
`stdout=PIPE` and never read; at ~64 KB `run.py` blocked on its next write and
sat there, looking exactly like a stuck bridge. It writes its own `--log`, so
stdout is a duplicate and is now DEVNULL.

### The town chain

Six screens sit between one raid and the next, and DD draws **one action
control** at the bottom centre and relabels it per screen -- Next, Return to
Town, Embark, Provision, Embark. So `bridge/town.py` is a TABLE from a
recognised screen to what that button means there, not six calibrations.
Anything not in the table refuses.

Four things the live walk taught it:

- **A click can be swallowed by an animation.** The progression page reveals
  quirk rewards over a couple of seconds; the same click that "failed" worked
  a moment later, going quirks -> town in 3.6 s. The walk retries, bounded.
- **The activity log is a screen to CLOSE, not a destination.** Its X is
  isolated by hue -- a luminance threshold picks up the panel's roses instead
  -- and two red glyphs share that row, of which the left is the X.
- **Provisioning happens ON the provisioner.** The first version bought once
  up front, which on a walk starting in the Hamlet is always the wrong screen,
  so it silently bought nothing. It buys the eight food DD itself asks for,
  and the point is to PREVENT a dialog rather than to provision well.
- **Telling modals apart is not the job; knowing one is up is.** The food
  warning sits 0.159 from `modal` and 0.287 from `embark_underprepared` -- a
  0.128 margin, under MARGIN, so `recognise` correctly answers "I do not know"
  and the walk stalled for want of a label it did not need. `_modal_family`
  asks the weaker question, ignoring MARGIN, which is safe ONLY because every
  dialog's first option is the same fixed point.

### A reversal, recorded as one

`embark_underprepared` was removed from the screen corpus earlier the same day
on the argument that it bought nothing actionable -- both modals take the same
click. That was wrong: without it the walk cannot pass the game's own "without
a full contingent?" warning, because being unable to NAME a screen is a cost
even when the action would have been identical. It is back, the corpus's worst
separation is **0.305**, and THRESHOLD (0.35) now exceeds it. That is a
genuine weakening and the test says so rather than papering over it. What
still holds is the property that matters: a near-tie fails MARGIN and comes
back UNKNOWN, which every caller treats as a pause.

### Drag

The one input primitive the actuator never had, and everything town-side needs
it. **The motion between press and release IS the mechanic** -- SDL decides a
drag happened from the motion events in between, so a press, one jump and a
release is commonly read as a plain click on the source, which in a roster
panel means "select this hero" rather than "move this hero". The pointer is
walked across in twelve hops with a dwell at each end, positioned through the
verified `move` so a drop cannot land one slot over. A zero-length drag is
refused: sent, it is an ordinary click that succeeds and therefore reads as a
working drag in the log.

Verified live on the raid pack, and it exposed something worth knowing: **DD
does not write the save on a pack rearrangement.** The item moved on screen
from the top row to the second, exactly as asked, with the save's mtime
unchanged and its slot index still reporting the old cell. So the bridge's
usual "the save changed" acknowledgement cannot verify a drag -- the proof has
to be pixels.

## The first REAL dungeon, and three things only a generated map could expose (2026-08-30)

Everything the bridge knew about dungeons it had learned from the Old Road,
whose map is hand-authored and ships with the game. Embarking on a Ruins quest
from the Hamlet -- a generated, hub-and-spoke, 8-room map -- broke three
separate assumptions in the first ten minutes, and each had been invisible
because the tutorial happens to satisfy it.

### The graph was a phantom hub

`corA` was read as `roo` + `r` + `A`.

The Old Road ships `coAB`, whose name genuinely IS its endpoint pair, and
`Area.rooms` splits it correctly. A generated dungeon NUMBERS its corridors --
`corA`..`corH` -- and the same split invents a room `roor`, which the save does
not contain, and hangs all eight corridors off it.

`DungeonMap.path()` plans over that graph, so **every route on every real raid
was computed against a fabricated topology**. It showed up live as
`NOT clicking the map: roor has no coordinates in the save` -- the actuator
refusing, correctly, on a room that does not exist, after a route that had
already walked the party through it twice.

Connectivity comes from GEOMETRY now, with the name as fallback. `Tile.mappos`
is a contiguous unit grid and a corridor's two ends each sit exactly one unit
-- MANHATTAN, so a diagonal room across a corner does not count -- from the
room it opens into. On the captured map that gives eight corridors each
resolving to exactly two adjacent rooms, every room reachable from the
entrance, and no invented ids. The route went from 14 moves and 6 revisits to
**8 moves and 2 revisits** on the same dungeon.

The tutorial resolves identically to before, because `coAB`'s ends really do
sit one unit from rooA and rooB: geometry AGREES with the name wherever the
name was right. A corridor is only taken from geometry when BOTH ends resolve
and differ -- half a derived pair mixed with half a parsed one is a topology
neither source agrees with.

### The map panel is stateful, and reopening it is an ANIMATION

`mappanel` could fit a generated map once its divisors were derived, and the
bridge still could not use it: the fit kept refusing live with "2 cells drawn"
where the SAME SAVE, seconds earlier, had given 8. The knowledge bytes were
identical in both, so nothing about the dungeon had changed. The PANEL had --
it keeps whatever zoom and pan it was left in, and DD re-centres it on the
party.

Reopening resets it, and reopening is an animation rather than a state change,
which is the part a fixed delay gets wrong. Measured after one reopen:

| t | cells | t | cells |
|---|---|---|---|
| +0.01s | 2 | +2.06s | 8 |
| +0.52s | 5 | +2.57s | 5 |
| +1.03s | 8 | +3.08s | 2 |
| +1.55s | 8 | | |

The whole map is on screen for about a second and a half in the middle, so
`_reset_map_view` walks frames instead of sleeping.

**Which frame it keeps took both wrong answers first, and the pair of failures
is the useful part.** Keeping the WIDEST frame refused a click: the widest
fitted at 8.8 px/unit on 6 of 8 boxes and predicted the destination outside
every detected cell. Keeping the FIRST frame `locate` succeeded on then sent a
click that missed -- it resolved on frame 1, still zoomed in, where **5 cells
admit a fit that is self-consistent and wrong**. A small cell count supports a
plausible scale that a wider view immediately contradicts.

So the whole window is scanned and the MOST-SUPPORTED success wins. Cell count
stays the tie-break among failures, so the frame banked on a refusal is still
the most informative one.

**Only a FIT failure triggers a reset.** `locate` has three refusals and the
other two -- a room with no coordinates, and a room genuinely not drawn
because it is unscouted -- cannot be changed by reopening a panel. A refusal
that quietly presses keys first is not a refusal.

The wheel is NOT the lever, though `SendInputBackend.scroll` works and is kept.
Scrolling is relative to wherever the view already is and has no stop, so it
zoomed the panel further in and left it stuck showing two cells. That is how
the panel was found to be stateful in the first place.

### A corridor's heading could not be UNLEARNED

`Walk.heading` is `D` when the party entered at tile 0 and `A` when it entered
at the far end. True over every capture -- all of them `coXY` corridors. On
`corH`, whose tiles run (1,16)..(1,21) with the party entering at index 5, the
save says `A` while the screen shows the entrance on the LEFT, so forward is
`D`. **The tile order is not the render order in a generated dungeon**, which
is the same class of trap as the corridor NAME not being the endpoint pair.

`Heading` exists to correct exactly this by walking and flipping, and it could
not: `_forward_key` reasserted `heading.current` from the save on EVERY call,
so the flip was undone before the next step used it. The party walked `A` out
of the corridor, flipped, walked back in, was reset to `A`, and repeated --
for ten minutes.

`ActRunner.heading_wrong` records the corridors whose measurement has been
falsified, per AREA because `Heading` is a single global bit. A corridor is
only ever left at one of its two ends, so a step meant to go deeper that
instead ended the area is the one observation that can falsify it.

### What then ran

`rooH -> corH -> rooG` in a generated dungeon: the map click leaves the room,
the corridor is walked in the right direction, the curio is approached tile by
tile, the fight is fought, the loot is taken and the formation restored.
Nothing had got past the entrance of a real dungeon before.

### Two things it stopped on, recorded rather than guessed

- **A relight was advised, `T` was sent, and the bridge reported "the input
  did not land" three times.** `torchlight.advise` does gate on
  `torches_held > 0`, so it believed the party had one. By the time the state
  was read back the party held **no provisions at all** and the torch was at
  **0**. Either the pack emptied between the advice and the send, or
  `LiveState.provisions` is not reporting what the party is carrying. The
  distinction matters: the first is a race worth a re-read before sending, the
  second means every provision decision in the dungeon layer is being made on
  an empty dict. **Not diagnosed, and the two are not distinguishable from the
  log alone.**
- **The curio panel still is not found on screen.** `W` was pressed from the
  correct tile and `no panel on screen after W` followed. The banked frame
  shows the party at the left of the corridor with the sconce well to the
  right, so the approach is what needs measuring, not the panel detector.
  `tools/find_button.py` is the instrument for it now.

## The bridge played a campaign from the title screen, and one flag was why it could not (2026-08-30)

A Tailscale session made the desktop reachable from an agent session for the
first time, which turned every "Fran has to be at the keyboard" item into
something measurable. The first thing it measured was a bug that had made the
whole of layer 3 inoperable on this machine.

### Every click landed on the wrong pixel

`SendInputBackend._normalise` maps a screen pixel into the 0..65535 range
`SendInput` wants, normalised over the **virtual** desktop -- all monitors --
and its docstring says so, correctly, because getting that wrong "puts every
click on the wrong monitor".

Windows only *reads* the range that way when the event carries
`MOUSEEVENTF_VIRTUALDESK`. None of them did. Without it the range spans the
**primary** monitor, whatever the caller normalised against.

Measured, with `GetCursorPos` after each send, on a 3840x1080 desktop whose
primary is 1920x1080:

| asked | landed | dx | dy |
|---|---|---|---|
| (732, 744) | (366, 743) | **-366** | -1 |
| (400, 300) | (200, 299) | **-200** | -1 |
| (1200, 500) | (600, 499) | **-600** | -1 |

**Every x exactly halved, every y exact.** The y is right because the virtual
and primary *heights* happen to match at 1080, and that asymmetry is what
identified the cause -- a coordinate bug would not respect one axis.

Two things made it survive this long. It is **invisible on a single-monitor
machine**, where virtual and primary coincide and the two interpretations are
the same number. And it presents as the game's fault: the keyboard worked
throughout (`GetAsyncKeyState` confirmed every injection), so
`menus.py --act` clicked Campaign three times, saw nothing happen, and
reported the title screen unresponsive -- which reads exactly like the
documented "PostMessage clicks do not register in DD" finding rather than like
a bug on our side.

After the flag: (732,744) -> (732,743), and every probe within a pixel
including x=3000 on the second monitor. `tests/bridge/test_act.py` pins the flag on
both `click` and `move`, and both tests were confirmed to FAIL with it removed
-- which matters more than usual here, because on a one-monitor machine
nothing else would notice the regression.

### The mapping is not invertible, so `move` measures instead

`MOUSEEVENTF_VIRTUALDESK` made clicks land on this desktop. It did not make
the mapping right in general, and checking four points hid the residual: over
the whole virtual desktop every point landed **-1 or 0** off, never +1, which
is a floor rather than noise.

Sweeping the normalised value and reading the cursor back settles what Windows
actually does, and the answer is that it cannot be inverted in closed form
here. Breakpoints sit 17 units apart (65536/3840 = 17.07) but fall where
neither `floor`, `round`, nor a half-pixel offset puts them, and no single
linear inverse fits the low, middle and top of one axis at once. Some pixels
are **not addressable at all**: normalised 14654 gives y=240 and 14655 gives
y=242, repeatably, with `GetClipCursor` showing no confinement and both
monitors enumerating at 96 dpi.

So the position is read back and corrected by `error_px * 65536/span`, which
is exact to first order, **keeping the best result and stopping when the error
stops improving**. That last clause is the load-bearing one: an unreachable
pixel makes a naive corrector oscillate between its two neighbours and finish
further from the target than its first shot, which is what it did before the
guard. **28/40 exact, worst error 1 px, 40/40 within 1 px**, against 8/20
open-loop; every real layout point lands exactly.

The general-purpose half is `_norm_axis`, split out to be testable over
monitor layouts this machine has not got -- a second monitor left or above
(negative origin), portrait, 4K, three-across 8K, a one-pixel desktop. Ten are
pinned, asserting the range is never left, the mapping is monotonic and spans
the range, and a round trip stays within the pixel the hardware costs. A
single-monitor developer machine can falsify none of that, which is how the
original bug survived a probe written to settle exactly this question.

`python bridge/act.py --geometry` reports the desktop, origin, per-monitor DPI
and this process's DPI awareness, then measures the round trip. It warns on the
one dangerous combination -- a DPI-unaware process with a scaled monitor, where
Windows reports virtualised coordinates while input is placed in physical
ones -- and exits non-zero above a pixel of error so it can gate arming.

### The probe could not reach a verdict, and reported the opposite

`act.py --probe` asks a human three times whether the contextual help overlay
appeared. `_ask` returned `False` on `EOFError`, so with no TTY an autonomous
run answered its own questions "no" and printed `-> Neither reached the game.`
three lines under its own `reached input : yes`.

An unanswered question is now `None`, and there is a third outcome that is
neither: a send Windows confirms delivering, over a screen that then does not
change, is **INCONCLUSIVE**. The probe key is `H` -- contextual help -- which
is inert on the front end, so recording that as a failure blamed the backend
for the probe's own choice of screen.

`--auto` answers the question from the screen instead, with a control: DD's
menus animate, so an idle baseline is sampled first at the same spacing and a
verdict needs a change well clear of it. Same shape of argument as the mouse
probe parking the pointer on rank 1 and posting a move to rank 4.

`_force_foreground` takes focus via `AttachThreadInput` rather than asking a
human to click, because `SetForegroundWindow` from a background process is
refused -- it returns success and flashes the taskbar, which is why one round
reported SKIPPED (never focused) immediately after one that worked. Focus is
still **verified**, never assumed.

### What then ran, end to end

Title screen -> new campaign in `profile_1` -> the Old Road -> combat -> loot
-> objective -> Return to Hamlet -> the Hamlet, with no human input.

`bridge/run.py --act`, one raid: **76 actions, 15 battle turns, 8 MATCH and 0
DIFFER**, every send confirmed against the save. The map panel solved the room
exit at **24.0 px/unit, 6/6 boxes explained** -- a fourth zoom level, the
others recorded being 48.0, 15.3 and 10.7.

`bridge/town_state.py` then read the resulting town save: 5,920 gold, matching
the screen exactly, both heroes at resolve 1 carrying 21.0 and 14.0 stress out
of the raid.

### The raid does not end when the quest does

Completing the objective raises a banner offering **Return to Hamlet** or
**Continue Raid**, and waits. `run.py` printed `no room advice to act on` at
the one moment the room genuinely had nothing left to advise, and every live
run had stopped there.

`RoomAdvice.raid_end` decides it, and **the default is to leave**: everything
still on the map is optional by definition once the quest is won, so
continuing spends real risk on loot while returning banks a completed quest.
The objective being met is `RoutePlan.targets` being empty -- not the printed
note read back. A "continue" needs both something priced positive still
reachable and a party in shape to take it; the risk gate is stress and
affliction rather than HP, because `LiveHero` carries no `max_hp` and a bare
HP number cannot be made a fraction there.

**`_end_raid` requires the save AND the screen to agree** before it clicks.
Both buttons are irreversible in opposite directions, so an unrecognised
screen refuses rather than clicking where the button usually is. It is
confirmed by the banner clearing, not by a save write: the button is a UI
transition and demanding a save change would read the ordinary success as a
failure -- the `_curio_active` trap.

### Locating a button became a command

`PARTY_ORDER` and the pack grid are the two points in `layout.py` that were
measured rather than hand-clicked, and both were measured once by hand with
only the answer written down. `tools/find_button.py` alpha-matches a shipped
asset against a live frame over a scale sweep and reports the centre as a
fraction of the client rect.

**The check that it measures what it claims**: it recovers PARTY_ORDER's own
fraction, `(0.8629, 0.7712)` from a 2560x1440 capture and `(0.8629, 0.7715)`
from a live 1280x720 frame, against the hardcoded `(0.8629, 0.7715)` -- same
fraction, two resolutions, to within a pixel, having been told nothing.

With it, `QUEST_RETURN_TO_HAMLET` and `QUEST_CONTINUE_RAID` at 0.9778 and
0.9874. Two facts neither given to nor used by the matcher corroborate them:
the pair share a y exactly, and their x are symmetric about 0.5.

It is diagnostics-only and imports OpenCV, which nothing under `bridge/` may
depend on -- the output is a fraction to paste into `layout.py`, so the
runtime still needs nothing but numpy. Same split as Pillow.

`RAID_RESULTS_NEXT` could **not** be found this way: the base game draws the
label as text and the only `raid_results*.png` in the install belong to the
Arena DLC. Measured off a live frame by **hue** rather than brightness, since
the panel frame directly above the label is bright too and a luminance
threshold lands the click on it.

### A capture session no longer needs a code edit

`build_refs` named references through a hardcoded prefix map, so adding a
screen meant editing `screen.py`. That is why the front end went unmodelled
for months while the screens sat one click away from a running game: the cost
was never the pixels, it was the diff. Anything under
`captures/screens/<name>.png` is now a reference called `<name>`, and
`--capture NAME` banks the live frame.

**`--capture` refuses a colliding frame**, which is not caution: `recognise`
demands the runner-up be `MARGIN` clear, so banking a frame that lands inside
`MARGIN` of a different screen turns *that* screen into a permanent UNKNOWN.
It earned its place on its first live use, refusing to bank the title screen
under the name `intro_text` because the intro had ended a moment sooner than
expected.

Six screens added from the live boot: `quest_complete`, `raid_results`,
`raid_progression`, `raid_progression_quirks`, `town`, and `pause_menu` --
which is not on the path at all and is the most important of them, because ESC
opens it and it carries **Exit to Desktop**.

Worst separation over the corpus moves **0.484 -> 0.432**. The new closest
pair is the two progression states, which are genuinely one page before and
after the quirk rewards animate in; both are kept because the page has to be
recognised in either state and takes the same button. Still nearly 3x MARGIN.

**And the suite had been blind to every one of them.** `test_screen.py`
globbed `00_*.png` only, so six references entered `screen_refs.json` without
a single test recognising them -- exactly the silent failure that file exists
to catch. Both corpus tests now walk the named family too.

## Did it learn to PLAY or to SCORE? Four expert rulers say: some of both (2026-08-30)

Every piece of evidence for the 2026-08-29 recipe came from **one instrument**.
`--all-comps` scores `run_score`, which is built from `_run_quality`, which is
the quantity the reward shapes. A recipe tuned against it and validated against
it had never been checked by anything sharing no machinery with it.

Four rulers that never compute `run_score`, every arm re-measured here with
identical commands rather than quoting the documented figures:

| ruler | 3M forecastv2 | shipped 12M+decay s1 | decay s0 |
|---|---|---|---|
| tutorial SUCCESS | 96.0% | 100.0% | 94.0% |
| tutorial steps | 14.6 | **10.9** | 11.5 |
| style_probe within-cat LOW gap | +3.6 | -2.1 | -6.9 |
| style_probe within-cat MID gap | -3.8 | -0.3 | -1.1 |
| style_probe within-cat HIGH gap | **-10.3** | **-1.9** | **+0.8** |
| playbook 3.2 unjustified-heal rate | 44.3% | 44.9% | -- |
| playbook heals as % of hero turns | 13.1% | **9.4%** | -- |
| playbook doomed-enemy avoidance | -19.6pp | -19.0pp | -- |
| class crusader battle_heal | 46.4% | 46.0% | -- |
| class occultist wyrd_recon | 49.2% | 48.1% | -- |
| class arbalest bandage | 39.8% | **23.5%** | -- |

**The declared falsifier -- flat or worse on 3 of 4 -- was NOT met.** But the
honest headline is the magnitude, not the sign: **`run_score` moved +6.5 long
QUALITY while the expert rulers moved a point or two.** It learned to play
somewhat better and to score a lot better. Quote the recipe win as real and as
partly instrument-specific.

### RETIRED: "skill choice degrades to 10.3pp BELOW random as sustain rises"

Dead, on **both** seeds of the decay arm: the HIGH-sustain within-category gap
is **-1.9 and +0.8** against the documented -10.3. The 3M arm reproduces the old
numbers exactly (57.3/53.7, 54.8/58.6, 48.9/59.2), so this is a real change and
not a different measurement.

**What replaces it is not a clean win.** The gradient flattened partly by
getting WORSE at low sustain (+3.6 -> -2.1 / -6.9). The standing claim is now:
**within-category skill choice is about as good as chance at every sustain
level.** The sustain-dependent pathology is gone; the underlying mediocrity is
not, and it is still a ruler and still never a target.

### CORRECTION: the Crusader has never stopped healing on ~46% of turns

`CLAUDE.md` carried "the Crusader spends **51.9%** of its turns healing". That
figure does not belong to `forecastv2_20260825`, which reads **46.4%** under the
same command; the shipped checkpoint reads 46.0%. **Crusader healing has been
flat, not falling.** Do not quote 51.9% against any checkpoint measured here.

What did change is heal VOLUME rather than heal JUDGEMENT: 13.1% -> 9.4% of
hero turns, so unjustified heals fall from 5.8% to 4.2% of all turns while the
*fraction* of heals that are unjustified is unmoved (44.3% -> 44.9%). The policy
heals less often and is no more discriminating about when.

## The search under-filled the QUIRK slots, which is the trinket bug one axis over (2026-08-30)

`RANDOM_QUIRKS_PER_POLARITY` is **(1, 3)**, so the search gave each hero one to
three positive quirks. A hero can hold **five**, and `random_quirks`' own
docstring already argues that a fully-groomed sheet "is a state a player can
actually buy". So a legal, buyable resource was being left unused -- the same
shape as the trinket bug `TRINKETS_PER_HERO` fixed, where the first run emitted
seven trinkets across four heroes.

Measured by topping every hero of the **seven Cove comps** up to five live
quirks, holding ranks, skills, trinkets and camp constant, paired, on **two**
checkpoints:

| | mean delta | positive |
|---|---|---|
| shipped | **+1.94** | 6/7 |
| independent | **+2.59** | 7/7 |

**13 of 14 comparisons positive.** The gains land on the WEAK comps -- cove7
+8.1, cove6 +11.7 -- because the strong ones already sit against the pool's 97.5
ceiling and have nothing to win.

**The shared constant is NOT touched.** `RANDOM_QUIRKS_PER_POLARITY` also feeds
`--kit-random-p` in TRAINING, so moving it would silently shift the training
distribution and invalidate every archived measurement. The search got its own
`SEARCH_QUIRKS_PER_POLARITY = (5, 5)`; `--sparse-quirks` restores the old draw
exactly, and `tests/env/test_quirk_slots.py` pins both halves.

**Negative quirks keep the old range.** Filling five of those is not a state a
player buys on purpose, and the flag would otherwise make `--negative-quirks`
much harsher than it has ever been.

### 11% of the filled slots are inert HERE, and that is deliberately not fixed

With the slots filled, the obvious follow-up is to filter the draw to quirks
that do something. Measured on the Weald batch: **124 of 140 equipped slots are
LIVE, 16 are not** -- 11%, which is just the pool's own rate (82 live, 8 inert,
1 with no buffs at all). The draw is uniform and picks up inert quirks in
proportion.

**Filtering them out would be optimising for this simulator's blind spot.**
Every one of the inert ones carries `scouting_chance` and nothing else, and
scouting is a *declared out-of-scope mechanic* -- it is inert here and worth
real money in the actual game. The search already has this discipline for
trinkets: `trinket_is_dead` judges `in_rank`, `rangedonly` and `meleeonly`,
and explicitly does NOT judge `lightbelow`/`lightabove` or `monsterType`
because the run and the region decide those; and "an unknown buff id is never
called dead: that is the loader missing something, not an inert trinket".

So the honest accounting is that the quirk fix delivers about **89% of the +2.3
it was measured at**, and the missing 11% is a simulator limitation rather than
a search bug. **Do not add a liveness filter to the quirk draw** without first
modelling scouting.

### The region-quirk question this came out of, and the answer

The search kept equipping Cove Explorer / Tactician / Scrounger / Adventurer,
and Fran's rework replaced all of them and scored higher -- so the obvious
reading was that region quirks are a mistake. **The first test said so and the
first test was confounded**: the variant also filled every hero to four quirks,
so it moved the COUNT as well as the CONTENT.

Re-run with the count held fixed, replacing region quirks one-for-one with live
combat quirks: **+1.4, -1.0, +1.2, +2.3** -- three of four positive, mean +1.0,
nothing clearing 2 sigma and one arm negative. **Region quirks are close to
free, and `retarget_region_quirks` is left exactly as it is.**

Two of the four are nonetheless inert *here*: `cove_explorer` and
`cove_scrounger` carry `scouting_chance` and nothing else, and scouting is a
declared out-of-scope mechanic. `cove_tactician` (`combat_stat_multiply` x2) and
`cove_adventurer` (`stress_dmg_received_percent -20%`) are live. So the honest
statement is that the search wastes about half a slot, not a whole one -- and
in the REAL game the scouting ones buy something this simulator cannot score.

## Fran's hand-optimisation of four GA comps: three replicate, one does not (2026-08-30)

He rewrote all four proposals -- new trinkets on every hero, full five-quirk
sheets, formation swaps and skill changes -- and promoted them into
`curated_comps/` under his own names (pool 168 -> **172**). Scored on the same
scenario and seed as the originals, and **on two independently trained
checkpoints each**, because a single policy's opinion of a comp is not evidence:

| comp (was) | shipped s1 | independent s0 | mean delta |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Ember Trail: Twin Burn** (GA_warrens7) | 79.6 -> **90.1** | 78.9 -> **90.4** | **+11.0** |
| **Curious Coin: Twin Sand** (GA_warrens6) | 86.8 -> **94.7** | 85.3 -> **91.6** | **+7.1** |
| Waiting Blade: Twin Duel (GA_warrens5) | 87.0 -> 89.2 | 82.0 -> 85.9 | +3.1 |

**Six of six comparisons positive**, and the two largest clear twice their
combined standard error on both policies. `Curious Coin` lands **9th of 172** on
the shipped checkpoint at **1.9 mean end stress with zero deaths**; `Ember
Trail` -- the burn comp -- goes 100% success on both.

**The Sibyl comp is the counter-example, and it is why both arms were run:**

| | GA original | hand-optimised |
|---|---|---|
| the policy that SEARCHED it (s0) | 87.7 +-2.1 | **84.8 +-4.9** |
| an independent policy (s1) | 85.2 +-3.5 | **90.2 +-1.0** |

**-2.9 and +5.0 -- opposite signs.** On one checkpoint the rewrite is a
regression, on the other it is second of 185. Neither delta clears 1.5 sigma.
**The Sibyl hand-optimisation is not distinguishable from its original**, and
quoting either number alone would have produced a confident claim in whichever
direction the arm was chosen.

**The method note is the transferable part.** The Warrens numbers were measured
on one policy first and looked decisive (+7.9, +10.5). The Sibyl split is what
prompted re-running them on a second checkpoint -- where they held. A comp
delta from a single policy is a hypothesis; the fitness is *P(this policy
completes the run)*, so one policy liking a comp and the comp being good are
different statements. Two checkpoints cost three minutes each.

**A measurement trap worth recording:** `HeroLoadout.quirk_ids` is a **dict**
keyed `positive`/`negative`, so `len(quirk_ids)` is **always 2** and reads
exactly like a hero wearing two of its five quirks. Every quirk in all four
comps resolves (the 2026-08-27 punctuation net handles `Corvids Eye` ->
`corvids_eye`); the drop was in the checking script. Read
`quirk_ids["positive"]`.

## The Sibyl comp survived a second opinion, and `--model-b` proved it cannot bend the search (2026-08-30)

`GA_sibyl2_01` was searched with one policy, and the GA's fitness is *P(this
policy completes the run)* -- so a comp can score well by being **easy for one
policy** rather than by being good, and a single-model search cannot tell those
apart. `models/sibyl_12M_s1.zip` (12M, seed 1, mods on) is the independent
checkpoint that makes the distinction measurable.

| comp | searching policy (s0) | independent policy (s1) |
|---|---|---|
| `GA_sibyl2_01` | 87.9 +-2.1 (98.3rd pct) | **85.2 +-3.5 (96.7th pct)** |
| `GA_sibyl1_01` | 56.1 +-7.0 (51.4th) | 61.0 +-6.5 (58.6th) |

**-2.7 +-4.1 is inside the noise: the comp is good, not merely easy for its
finder.** Note the pool means differ between the two policies (52.0 vs 55.2), so
the percentile is the fairer read of the two.

**Both mods-on seeds independently name `--tier 5 --band long`** (worse-arm SD/SE
6.3 and 6.1), so that corner belongs to the mods-on family rather than to one
run -- while the mods-off shipped checkpoint names tier 3 long. The corner is a
property of the POLICY, and this is now the third demonstration in two days.

**A re-run with `--model-b` produced a BYTE-IDENTICAL comp** (`GA_sibyl3_01`,
deletable). That is the design working rather than a wasted run: `--model-b` is
*reported, never selected on*, so the second model provably cannot change what
the search emits -- ranking on both would make it a second fitness and move the
problem up a layer. Its internal cross-check (84.9 +-3.8) reproduces the
independent leaderboard figure (85.2 +-3.5) by a second route.

## The abandon exit is correctly priced, and the audit tool was undercounting deaths (2026-08-30)

The eval reports ABANDON at 2.6-3.0% of expeditions and warns that "a HIGH rate
on a strong checkpoint means the exit is too cheap". `tools/abandon_audit.py`
replied **"56% of abandoned runs were WINNABLE"**. Both were misleading, for two
separate reasons.

**1. `completed` is not SUCCESS.** SUCCESS is completed AND zero deaths, and
**two thirds of abandons happen after a hero is already dead** -- those runs had
forfeited SUCCESS before the exit was taken, so abandoning them costs nothing.
Splitting the counterfactual on deaths-at-exit, over 700 episodes:

| | n | forced on: completed | ...with ZERO deaths |
|---|---|---|---|
| abandoned with >=1 death | 12 | 6 | **0** |
| abandoned CLEAN | 6 | 4 | **1** |

**SUCCESS recoverable by never abandoning: 1 of 700 = 0.14pp**, an order of
magnitude below the long band's own +-1.38pp error bar. **The lead is closed,
not dropped.**

**2. The tool counted deaths against the CURRENT roster, which re-packs.** A
dead hero is dropped from the next combat's `engine.heroes`, so
`sum(1 for h in engine.heroes if not h.is_alive())` undercounts -- and it
reported runs that had already lost a hero as later "completing with zero
deaths", which is impossible. That self-contradiction is what exposed it.
`eval_expedition` uses `len(hero_ids) - len(living)`; the tool now does too.
Corrected, deaths at exit go **0.78 -> 1.12** and deaths from playing on go
**0.89 -> 2.38 per run** -- which strengthens the case that abandoning is
correct rather than weakening it.

The tool now prints the zero-death column beside `completed` and **scores its
verdict on SUCCESS**, because the old verdict printed "the policy is bailing out
of runs it could have finished" directly above a line showing 0.00pp
recoverable.

**Method note worth keeping:** the first run of this audit used 200 episodes and
6 abandons and read **83% winnable**; at 700 episodes and 18 abandons it read
56%, and on the SUCCESS-correct accounting 0.14pp. **A counterfactual on a rare
event needs the event count, not the episode count, in the denominator of your
confidence** -- 6 samples was never going to decide it.

## The PPO hyperparameters had never been tuned, and the learning rate was the one that mattered (2026-08-29)

Eleven thousand lines of this file contained exactly **one** entry about the
optimiser (the net widening from `[64,64]` to `[256,256]`). Every other PPO
value was the one it was first written with. Three of them had direct
diagnostic evidence against them, so each got one arm: 6M at `--batch-size 256`,
seed 0, measured against `models/cand6M_b256.zip` on `--all-comps --episodes 4`.

| arm | change | clip_fraction | approx_kl | long SUCCESS | long QUALITY |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| control | — | 0.205 | 0.0243 | 85.8 | 79.3 |
| A | `--net-arch 512,512` | **0.245** | **0.0343** | **81.7** | **76.3** |
| B | `--lr-schedule linear` | **0.030** | **0.0040** | **87.3** | **79.8** |
| C | `--n-epochs 5` | 0.132 | 0.0149 | 84.8 | 77.4 |

### A: the wider net, and why this project's own argument was wrong

The case for A was the strongest of the three on paper, and it came from
`train.py`'s own comment. `[256,256]` was chosen because the first layer was
"narrower than its own input" when the observation was ~330 floats — a ratio of
5.2x. The observation is **1636** now, so the ratio is **6.4x**, worse than the
one that prompted the widening, and nobody had revisited it across three
observation rewrites.

It came back **−4.1 SUCCESS and −3.0 QUALITY** on the long band, and the
training diagnostics say why: `clip_fraction` **rose** to 0.245 and `approx_kl`
to 0.034. **The wider net took larger steps, not better ones.** Capacity was
never the binding constraint; optimisation stability was.

That is recorded at the site that argued for widening, because the argument is
still sitting there and reads persuasively. The generalisation worth keeping:
**an input-to-width ratio is not evidence about capacity** when the optimiser is
already clipping a fifth of every minibatch.

### B: the learning rate, which had no schedule at all

`learning_rate=3e-4` constant, for the life of the project. Linear decay to zero
is the standard remedy for a high late-training clip fraction, and it did
exactly what it should to the diagnostics — `clip_fraction` **0.205 → 0.030**,
`approx_kl` **0.0243 → 0.0040** — and then to the play. Paired against a matched
constant-LR control on **both seeds**:

| long band | ctl s0 | decay s0 | ctl s1 | decay s1 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| SUCCESS | 85.8 | **87.3** | 84.0 | **88.5** |
| QUALITY | 79.3 | **79.8** | 77.7 | **80.0** |
| stress | 21.3 | **18.8** | 23.2 | **19.8** |
| deaths | 0.22 | **0.17** | 0.27 | **0.15** |

Medium-band SUCCESS is the most consistent gain, **+2.2 and +2.1**. Individual
bands are 1–2 sigma; the signal is that **eleven of twelve band-metric
comparisons move the same way** across two seeds. Short-band SUCCESS is the
single exception (−0.7 / +0.2, inside noise).

### C: fewer epochs, and why it is NOT the same lever as B

C also lowers `clip_fraction` (0.132, between A and B) and it still loses
**−1.0 SUCCESS / −1.9 QUALITY**. So the three arms do not order cleanly on that
number, and it would be wrong to write down "lower clip fraction is better".

What separates B from C is **how** the stability is bought. B keeps all ten
epochs of gradient work on each rollout and merely **sizes** the steps; C
**discards** half the optimisation on data already collected, paying in sample
efficiency for the same nominal stability. Stability from throwing updates away
is not stability from making them smaller.

### The budget knee was the optimiser, not the data

The most useful consequence, and it was not the point of the experiment.
`CLAUDE.md` had recorded a knee at 6M: measured at constant LR, 6M -> 12M bought
**+0.4 SUCCESS and +0.3 QUALITY** on the long band, so "do not pay for 12M" was
written down as settled. Re-measured under the schedule, at seed 1:

| long band | 6M + decay | **12M + decay** |
|---|---|---|
| SUCCESS | 88.5 | **90.1** |
| QUALITY | 80.0 | **81.9** |
| stress | 19.8 | **16.8** |
| per-cell SD | 18.5 | **17.9** |

Better on **all nine** band-metric comparisons, and short SUCCESS reaches 97.6%.
So the same doubling of data is worth +0.3 QUALITY with a badly-sized step and
**+1.9 with a well-sized one**. The schedule did not merely raise the endpoint;
it **restored the budget's ability to pay**.

**And the real knee is 24M**, probed immediately afterwards so the recipe would
not simply inherit the same mistake in the other direction. Seed 1, 12M → 24M:

| long band | 12M | 24M |
|---|---|---|
| SUCCESS | 90.1 | 91.1 |
| QUALITY | **81.9** | 81.1 |
| hp | **74.5%** | 72.4% |
| stress | 16.8 | 16.3 |

**SUCCESS +1.0, QUALITY −0.8, both inside one standard error and pointing
opposite ways** — it completes marginally more expeditions by coming home in
worse shape. A genuine plateau looks like this: the metrics stop agreeing. The
6M → 12M step did not (+1.6 / +1.9, both metrics, both seeds), which is what
made it a real gain rather than a noisy one. 12M stays shipped; 24M costs double
for nothing.

**The generalisation:** a plateau measured with an untuned optimiser is a
statement about the optimiser, not about the data. This project had two of them
on the books at once — "training longer buys noise" (which was really the eval's
resolution) and "6M is the knee" (which was really the learning rate) — and both
read as facts about the problem.

**Replicated on both seeds.** Seed 0 gives the same answer as seed 1:

| long band | 6M s0 | 12M s0 | 6M s1 | 12M s1 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| SUCCESS | 87.3 | **89.6** | 88.5 | **90.1** |
| QUALITY | 79.8 | **81.6** | 80.0 | **81.9** |
| stress | 18.8 | **16.8** | 19.8 | **16.8** |
| deaths | 0.17 | **0.11** | 0.15 | **0.14** |

**+1.8 and +1.9 QUALITY** — seventeen of eighteen band-metric comparisons
improve, and the two seeds agree to a tenth of a point on the headline. That is
as consistent as anything this project has measured.

### The standing claim this replaces

The schedule is worth more than doubling the data was: a **6M** decay run
already beat the 12M constant-LR checkpoint (long SUCCESS 88.5 against 86.2) at
half the cost, and the 12M decay run beats it by **+3.9 SUCCESS / +2.3 QUALITY**
at a matched budget. `models/hpB12M_lrdecay_s1.zip` is the new
`models/dd_combat_ppo_full_expedition.zip`; both predecessors are kept,
`archive/models/dd_combat_ppo_full_expedition_1636_6M_lrdecay_s1_20260829.zip`
and `..._1636_12M_constlr_pre20260829.zip`.

**Nothing about the architecture changed**, so the observation is still 1636 and
every tool, the bridge and `deterministic_actor`'s fast path work unchanged —
verified by loading it and playing 40 steps before shipping.

### The transferable habit

Two arms pushed on the same diagnostic and got opposite results, and reading
`clip_fraction` **before** either ran would have predicted both. It costs
nothing: SB3 prints it every update. This project spends its time on fidelity
and observability, which is where its history says the wins are — and the
largest single-flag improvement it has ever measured was sitting in the
optimiser log the whole time.

## A modded class loads its KIT now, and the Sibyl is the case that proves it (2026-08-27)

Fran: *"now i want to introduce the 1º modded class, sibyl ... this is a hard
class to introduce, as she has none other than 3 stances, and several attacks
depend on the stance as well"*.

The stances turned out to be the easy half. **Nothing in the engine needed
changing for her** — `.valid_modes` gating, `SkillLevelData.mode_effects` and
`.set_mode` have all been there since the Duelist, and the Abomination is the
vanilla instance of the same convention. What was missing was the data.

### 93.8% of her kit resolved to nothing, and that was silent

`HeroLoader` has discovered modded hero classes since 2026-08-24. That was half
a class. A hero file names effects, buffs, trinkets and camping skills that live
in the mod's OWN `effects/`, `shared/buffs/`, `trinkets/` and `raid/camping/`
subtrees, and every one of those loaders read `GAME_DIR` alone. Measured on the
Sibyl: **150 of 160 effect references unresolved (93.8%)**, the ten that
resolved being vanilla `Push`/`Destealth`.

Among the missing were `switch_mode_moon_self`, `switch_mode_eclipse_self` and
`switch_mode_sun_self` — the entire mechanism of the class. She started in
`moon`, could not leave it, and **five of her seven skills were permanently
illegal**. No error anywhere; the same inertness the Runaway's burn had and
Backdraft's empty effect list had.

`DataLoader.load_all` now merges the mod subtrees the way it already merged
DLC's, and `CampingLoader` and the localization scan take the same roots. After:
**0 of 160 unresolved.** Across all twelve subscribed Workshop classes, ten now
resolve 100%. The two that do not (`Navigator`, `wanderer`) fail inside
`EffectLoader` on malformed lines in their own files — `'100% queue true'`, a
missing dot — which is mod-data breakage, not a path problem.

### One rule that is NOT the DLC rule: a mod may add, never shadow

The DLC merge uses `dict.update()`, so a later file overrides an earlier id.
That is tolerable for first-party DLC, which does not redefine base ids. It is
not tolerable for Workshop content: a mod redefining `Stun 1` would silently
change what every vanilla comp does, with no error and no missing id — just
different numbers in a store half this project's measurements are anchored to.

`data/mod_roots.py::merge_new_only` is the counterpart of `update()` and adds
an id only when nothing holds it. Same guarantee `HeroLoader._load_mod_heroes`
already gave for class ids. `tests/data/test_mod_kit.py` checks it against the real
tables and as a unit.

### The store cache did not key on `DDSIM_MOD_DIRS`, and that was a trap

`store_cache.fingerprint` digests the game subtrees and `data/*.py`. It knew
nothing about the environment, so flipping the variable did not invalidate a
cached store — the documented advice was to pair it with
`DDSIM_NO_STORE_CACHE=1`, which costs a full rebuild in every one of a search's
twelve workers. The sweep now includes the mod roots and the stamp includes the
variable's value, so mods on and mods off are different cache entries and both
are fast. Verified three ways round: 20 heroes, 21 heroes, 20 heroes again.

### `UNRESTRICTED_HEROES` was identity, and the game states the fact itself

`env.expedition_env.UNRESTRICTED_HEROES` is `frozenset({"abomination",
"duelist"})` — the classes with no 4-of-7 to choose, because they carry all
seven always and a MODE decides which are usable. A hand-kept list of two ids,
which a modded class can never join, in a project whose observation was
deliberately rewritten to encode mechanics instead of identity.

The game already says it: `skill_selection: .can_select_combat_skills`, shipped
`false` on exactly the Abomination and the Duelist, `true` on the other
eighteen — and `false` on the Sibyl. `HeroDefinition.can_select_combat_skills`
parses it and `unrestricted_heroes(store)` derives the set.

**The derivation reproducing the shipped pair exactly is the check that it
reads the right key**, and it is the same style of argument as `viable_ranks`
agreeing with common knowledge about which classes stand where. The constant
stays and stays exported — a dozen tools import it and it is right for the
shipped roster — but anything with a store in hand should prefer the function.

It matters concretely: cut to a 4-of-7 the Sibyl can lose `align`, and a Sibyl
without `align` is stranded in one stance for the whole raid with three skills
she can never use.

### A comp could not name a modded class at all

`HERO_DISPLAY_NAME_TO_ID` is twenty hand-written rows and an unrecognised
`heroClass` is dropped **silently** — the comp trains as a smaller party.
`CompLoadoutLoader._hero_class_map(store)` keeps those rows first and
authoritative (they are the only thing that knows "Man At Arms" is
`man_at_arms`) and adds everything the store loaded underneath, under both its
id and the title form. Shipped rows always win, so no existing comp can resolve
differently; the fallback can only rescue a slot that was being dropped.

### What this cost the observation: nothing

**The width is still 1636 with a 21st class present, and the shipped checkpoint
loads and plays.** That is the 2026-08-23 mechanics-not-identity rewrite paying
off exactly as argued: the class one-hot is gone, so a new class is new payload
values in slots that already exist rather than a new index. A whole third-party
class costs zero observation width and breaks no checkpoint.

What it does NOT buy is competence. `--hold-out-class` measured **+46.9pp
excess gap** for a class the policy never trained on, so the shipped checkpoint
can *represent* the Sibyl and will still play her badly.

### She has no comps, so she gets composed coverage

`build_comp_pool` iterates `store.comp_loadouts`; a class in no comp file is
never sampled and never trained on. `composed_comps/` is the sanctioned answer
and exists for this exact reason — six recombinations written when the Duelist
had two comps and the Runaway one. `tools/compose_class_comps.py` is the
repeatable version, written for the general case because "the first modded
class" implies a second.

Three of four slots are copied **verbatim** from a curated comp; only one is
replaced, and the invented block is as close to not-a-choice as the data
allows: all seven skills (she cannot select), her own camp skills before the
shared ones, two class-locked unlimited trinkets, and **no quirks at all** —
an invented quirk sheet would flatter every measurement made on these comps.

**It found a real defect on its first run, in Fran's files rather than its
own.** `Blood Money: Warlock`'s Bounty Hunter composed into a hero wearing one
trinket, which is the documented recombination failure — and the cause was that
the source comp already equips nothing there. Sweeping the curated folder:
**40 slots equip nothing**, and 38 of them are two typos —

| written | the game's name | slots |
|---|---|---|
| `Hunter's Talons` | `Hunter's Talon` | 23 |
| `Tempting Goblet` | `The Tempting Goblet` | 15 |
| `Cloak and Dagger` | no near match | 1 |
| `Eerie Eye` | no near match | 1 |

Not guessed at in code. Plural-to-singular is a semantic guess and the loader's
orthographic net deliberately stops short of those — the DEBUFF RES precedent
is that a guessed fix is not shipped. Fran's files, Fran's correction.

### A NEW SHIPPED CHECKPOINT: 6M at batch 256, and it replicates (2026-08-28)

The largest measured improvement in this project since the threat forecast, and
the first one that came from the optimiser rather than from fidelity or
observability.

### What changed, and why it was worth trying

`batch_size` had been **64** since the beginning, on an update of
`n_steps 2048 x 12 envs = 24,576` samples. That is **3,840 gradient steps per
update**, each on a minibatch far smaller than the 1636 -> 256 -> 256 network it
is fitting, and it is why SGD was ~73% of a run's wall clock: 12.9 s per update
against 5.3 s at 256 and 4.0 s at 512.

So raising it is not only an optimisation change, it is a *budget* change: at
batch 256 a 6M run costs about the wall clock of a 3M run at 64. **1549 fps
against ~830.** The candidate spends twice the data for the same hour.

### The measurement

`--all-comps --episodes 4` — 169 comps, no comp-sampling error, reproducible
since 2026-08-24:

| band | metric | shipped (3M, b64) | 6M b256 seed 0 | 6M b256 seed 1 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| short | SUCCESS | 95.3 ±0.94 | **96.2 ±0.73** | **96.0 ±0.87** |
| | QUALITY | 86.5 ±0.63 | 86.7 ±0.58 | **87.5 ±0.56** |
| medium | SUCCESS | 90.4 ±1.47 | **93.5 ±0.99** | **93.3 ±1.02** |
| | QUALITY | 83.9 ±0.85 | **86.0 ±0.65** | **85.6 ±0.66** |
| **long** | **SUCCESS** | 81.8 ±1.83 | **85.8 ±1.62** | **84.0 ±1.63** |
| | **QUALITY** | 75.4 ±1.12 | **79.3 ±0.94** | **77.7 ±1.00** |
| | stress | 30.7 | **21.3** | **23.2** |
| | deaths | 0.33 | **0.22** | **0.27** |
| | hp | 70.2% | **72.5%** | 70.8% |

**Both seeds beat the shipped checkpoint on every band and every metric.** Long
band: SUCCESS +4.0 and +2.2, QUALITY +3.9 and +2.3, stress −9.4 and −7.5,
deaths down a third. That is the band Fran's objective names — *"a good model
that can get their way out on champion long dungeons"*.

The per-cell SD also falls (long: 23.7 → 21.1), so the policy is more consistent
across comps, not just better on average.

### Which seed shipped, and how to read its numbers

`models/dd_combat_ppo_full_expedition.zip` is now **seed 0**. It is the better
of the two arms on the long band, and picking the better of two on the same eval
that is then reported overstates by exactly the selection it performed. So:
**the honest expectation is the two-seed mean — long SUCCESS ~84.9, QUALITY
~78.5** — and seed 0's own numbers are what the table above says. The superseded
checkpoint is `archive/models/dd_combat_ppo_full_expedition_forecastv2_1636_3M_pre20260828.zip`,
kept with its width and budget in the filename per the standing rule.

Nothing about the architecture changed, so obs is still 1636 and every tool,
the bridge, and `deterministic_actor`'s fast path all work unchanged — verified
by loading it and playing 40 steps.

### Attribution: the BUDGET did the work, and "training longer never resolved anything" is retired

Two things moved in the shipped candidate, so a third arm separates them —
3M at batch 256, same recipe, same seed:

| arm | long SUCCESS | long QUALITY | medium SUCCESS | medium QUALITY |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 3M @ batch 64 (the old ship) | 81.8 | 75.4 | 90.4 | 83.9 |
| **3M @ batch 256** | 83.4 | 75.5 | 91.7 | 84.2 |
| **6M @ batch 256** (shipped) | **85.8** | **79.3** | **93.5** | **86.0** |

* batch 64 -> 256, holding the budget: long **+1.6 SUCCESS, +0.1 QUALITY**.
* 3M -> 6M, holding the batch: long **+2.4 SUCCESS, +3.8 QUALITY**.

**Batch size is the enabler, not the cause.** It buys a little directly and it
buys the run: at 256 an update is 5.3 s instead of 12.9 s, so 6M costs the wall
clock of the old 3M. The improvement is the extra data.

### Past 6M the curve is flat, and 12M ships anyway

| step | long SUCCESS | long QUALITY | long deaths |
|---|---|---|---|
| 3M @ batch 64 (old ship) | 81.8 | 75.4 | 0.33 |
| 3M @ batch 256 | 83.4 | 75.5 | 0.30 |
| 6M @ batch 256 | 85.8 | 79.3 | 0.22 |
| **12M @ batch 256** | **86.2** | **79.6** | **0.19** |

3M -> 6M is **+3.8 QUALITY**; 6M -> 12M is **+0.3**. **6M is the value point** --
the knee is there and a future run should stop at it unless something else
changes.

12M is what shipped, because it is better on 8 of 9 band-metric comparisons and
the improvements that remain are the ones that matter least ambiguously: deaths
fall on the short band 0.08 -> 0.04 and on the long band 0.22 -> 0.19, and
end-of-run stress falls on every band. But the 6M -> 12M step is **one seed** and
inside the noise on most metrics. The claim that is replicated -- two seeds,
every band, every metric -- is the 3M -> 6M one.

### The standing belief this corrects

`CLAUDE.md` has carried this since 2026-08-20:

> "+-2pp would still need ~2300 paired scenarios, which is the real reason
> 'training longer' never resolved anything: **no eval this project ever ran was
> large enough to see the effect it was looking for.**"

That diagnosis was right, and it has now been resolved rather than refuted. The
`--all-comps` eval became reproducible on 2026-08-24 and carries an SE near
**1.0 on QUALITY** over 169 comps; the budget effect is **+3.8**, which is
comfortably visible on that instrument and would have been invisible on the
+-3pp ruler that preceded it. The effect was always there; the tape measure was
not.

So "training longer buys noise" is retired as a standing claim. What replaces
it: **on the reproducible ruler, doubling the budget from 3M to 6M is worth
about +2.4 SUCCESS and +3.8 QUALITY on the long band** — and it is affordable
only because batch 256 halved the cost of a step of SGD.

## The training reward underprices stress by ~5x against its own ruler (2026-08-28)

Found by arithmetic, not by a sweep. Two numbers that should agree and do not:

* `eval_expedition._run_quality` — what `run_score` is built from, what
  `tools/comp_leaderboard.py` ranks on, and what Fran's objective names ("finish
  the expedition, nobody dead, everyone at zero stress") — gives stress **half**
  of a hero's score.
* The training return gives it about **9%**. Over a 15-combat expedition the
  combat wins and the completion bonus are worth ~85, while `STRESS_TICK_PENALTY`
  (0.01 per point as it lands) and `TERMINAL_STRESS_PENALTY_WEIGHT` (4.0 on the
  mean roster fraction) together reach ~8.

The same gap appears per turn. Crusader's Inspiring Cry stress-heals 8, which
through `W_STRESS = 0.50` on a `stress/100` scale is **+0.010 of Φ** averaged
over four heroes, against `TURN_PENALTY_THREAT = −0.100` for spending the turn.
An attack taking 30% off one of four enemies is roughly **+0.075**. The shaping
prices a damage turn at about seven times a stress-relief turn — and Inspiring
Cry is the single most passed-over skill in `tools/style_probe.py`, 1108 times.

### `--stress-weight`, and why it is not another `--prevention-reward`

The flag scales BOTH stress terms together; 1.0 is byte-identical to every
archived checkpoint. Scaling both is deliberate — the tick charges stress when
it lands and the terminal charges what is left, so moving one alone changes the
shape of the incentive rather than its size.

**It is a NON-potential change and does move the optimum.** That is the whole
difference from `--prevention-reward`, whose two terms sat inside Φ and
therefore could only ever change credit timing (Ng/Harada/Russell), which is why
that knob came back null on `run_score` in 2026-08-27 and null again on the
style probe today.

### The measurement, seed 0

Two 3M arms, seed 0, 12 envs, shipped recipe (`--hard-focus
--no-abandon-train`), `--all-comps --episodes 4` (169 comps, no comp-sampling
error):

| band | metric | w = 1 | w = 5 | delta |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| short | QUALITY | 83.7 ±0.74 | **85.3 ±0.72** | **+1.6** |
| | stress | 18.1 | 14.8 | −3.3 |
| medium | QUALITY | 81.3 ±0.96 | **84.0 ±0.76** | **+2.7** |
| | stress | 21.6 | 14.6 | **−7.0** |
| | deaths | 0.23 | 0.15 | −0.08 |
| long | QUALITY | 70.7 ±1.29 | **73.4 ±1.13** | **+2.7** |
| | stress | 36.7 | 28.2 | **−8.5** |
| | deaths | 0.51 | 0.41 | −0.10 |
| | hp | 64.1% | 66.2% | +2.1 |

**Quality up on all three bands, stress down 23% on the long band, deaths down
20%, HP up.** SUCCESS barely moves (+0.8 / +0.7 / 0.0), which is expected: it is
a saturated binary gate and `CLAUDE.md` already says it cannot resolve an
improvement at these rates. Individually the quality deltas are 1.5-2.2 sigma;
what makes them worth acting on is that twelve directional comparisons across
three independent bands all favour the same arm.

### The mechanism did NOT confirm, and that matters

The hypothesis was that pricing stress properly would make the policy use the
stress-relief utility it passes over. On the expert's tier ruler it did the
opposite — took-best-minus-random went from −2.4 (w=1) to −6.0 (w=5) averaged
over the three sustain buckets.

That difference is **inside the ruler's known between-seed spread of ~12pp**, so
the honest statement is that skill choice shows no reliable change either way.
But it means the improvement is NOT explained by "it uses better skills now".
Something else is producing it — plausibly avoiding stress-generating situations
rather than repairing stress afterwards — and that is not established.

### The replicate: stress falls reliably, quality does not

Seed 1, same recipe, same eval:

| band | metric | w = 1 | w = 5 | delta |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| short | QUALITY | 85.8 ±0.66 | 85.9 ±0.64 | +0.1 |
| | stress | 14.6 | 13.2 | −1.4 |
| medium | QUALITY | 83.9 ±0.78 | 84.4 ±0.83 | +0.5 |
| | stress | 16.3 | 15.5 | −0.8 |
| long | QUALITY | 74.4 ±1.11 | 74.6 ±1.14 | +0.2 |
| | stress | 28.5 | 26.1 | −2.4 |

Against seed 0's +1.6 / +2.7 / +2.7 and −3.3 / −7.0 / −8.5.

**What replicates: the stress reduction.** Six of six band-seed comparisons are
negative, mean **−3.9 points of end-of-run stress**. The intervention does the
thing it was built to do.

**What does not: the quality gain.** Six of six are positive, but seed 1's
magnitudes are +0.1 / +0.5 / +0.2 — indistinguishable from zero. The bands
within a seed share a checkpoint, so the honest unit is the seed: **+2.33 (seed
0) and +0.27 (seed 1), mean +1.30 with a between-seed SD of 1.46 — an interval
of roughly ±2.9 that spans zero.** Quoting the six bands as independent would
give +1.30 ± 0.95 and exclude zero, and that would be wrong for exactly the
reason `dd-one-seed-is-not-evidence` exists.

Note also that the seed-1 CONTROL is a much stronger policy than the seed-0
control (85.8 / 83.9 / 74.4 against 83.7 / 81.3 / 70.7). Between-seed variance
on quality is 2-4 points — larger than the treatment effect on seed 1, which is
the whole reason a single seed could not have answered this.

### The mechanism, as far as it can be read

Stress falls and quality barely moves, which means something is offsetting it —
and `_run_quality` is half HP, half stress. Seed 0 gained HP as well (64.1 →
66.2 on the long band); seed 1 lost a little (68.0 → 67.7). So the policy
appears to **trade HP for stress relief at roughly par on the composite**,
rather than finding free stress reduction.

That is a coherent story and it is not established. What is established: raising
the price of stress buys less stress, and does not buy a better score.

### Why stress relief "costs HP": it does not, and the effect was correctly sized all along

The open question from the two-seed result was why stress falls while QUALITY —
half HP, half stress — barely moves. The reading offered was that the policy
trades HP for stress relief. Measured over 24 comps x 4 episodes at tier 5 long,
paired across the four arms:

| arm | steps/combat | damage taken/combat | stress gained/combat | heal share |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| w1 seed 0 | 15.98 | 14.9 | 24.81 | 20.0% |
| w5 seed 0 | 15.72 | **15.0** | **23.93** | 22.7% |
| w1 seed 1 | 18.06 | 14.1 | 24.16 | 25.0% |
| w5 seed 1 | 18.55 | **14.0** | **22.63** | 26.6% |

**The tempo hypothesis is refuted.** Fights are not longer under w5 — seed 0 is
