slightly shorter, seed 1 slightly longer — and **damage taken per combat is
identical to a tenth of a point** on both seeds. There is no HP cost to find.

What there is: stress gained per combat falls by ~1.2 on both seeds, and the
heal share rises by 1.6-2.7pp. The policy does exactly what the reward now asks.

**And the arithmetic closes.** −1.2 stress per combat over ~15 combats and four
heroes is ~−4.5 points of mean end-of-run stress, which is what the eval
measured (−3.9). On `_run_quality`'s 200-point stress ceiling that is ~2%, and
stress is half the score, so it is worth **~1 quality point** — against the
+1.30 the two seeds actually produced.

So the earlier "trades HP for stress relief" line was over-reading seed 1's
−0.3pp of HP, which is noise. The honest account is simpler and more useful:
**`--stress-weight` works exactly as designed, and the effect it can produce is
inherently about one quality point.** Even driving long-band stress from 28 to
zero would be worth ~7 points, and no policy gets that for free.

That closes the lead rather than opening one. The knob is correctly built,
correctly sized, and not a route to a materially better checkpoint — which is
worth knowing before anyone spends two more seeds on it.

### Verdict

`--stress-weight` stays, default **1.0**. It is a real lever on a real quantity
— useful if Fran ever wants a policy that finishes cleaner rather than faster,
since his objective names zero stress explicitly — but on two seeds it does not
improve `run_score`, and it must not be turned on by default on this evidence.

The measured misalignment that motivated it is still true (stress is ~9% of the
training return against 50% of `_run_quality`). What this run shows is that
closing the gap by scaling the existing terms does not convert into score. If
anyone returns to it, the question to ask first is why stress relief costs HP —
that is where the trade is being made.

## Replicating the one-plan finding over five checkpoints, and correcting its headline (2026-08-28)

`tools/style_probe.py`'s first result was reported off ONE checkpoint. Four more
were then probed — the two `--prevention-reward` arms and their two controls,
which are two seeds of one recipe — and the replication changes what can be
claimed. Some of it strengthens; the part I stated most precisely does not
survive.

### What REPLICATES, and more strongly than first claimed

**The availability-controlled heal take-rate does not rise with sustain. On four
of five checkpoints it FALLS.**

| checkpoint | LOW | MID | HIGH | HIGH − LOW |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| shipped (forecastv2) | 20.4% | 21.5% | 23.8% | +3.4 |
| burnfix seed 0 | 32.5% | 24.9% | 22.3% | **−10.2** |
| prevention seed 0 | 28.2% | 24.4% | 23.6% | −4.6 |
| burnfix seed 1 | 19.4% | 14.4% | 16.9% | −2.5 |
| prevention seed 1 | 28.2% | 22.1% | 18.6% | −9.6 |

Mean −4.7pp. Given a heal on the board, the policy takes it at a rate that is
independent of — or slightly *inverse* to — how much the party was built to
heal. The original claim was "flat"; five checkpoints say flat is the optimistic
reading. **One plan, and it is not the comp's plan.**

### What replicates in DIRECTION only

Within-category skill choice against a uniform-random baseline over the same
legal actions, in points above random:

| checkpoint | LOW | MID | HIGH |
|---|---|---|---|
| shipped | +3.6 | −3.8 | −10.3 |
| burnfix seed 0 | −2.1 | −10.7 | −13.7 |
| prevention seed 0 | −3.2 | −9.3 | −6.5 |
| burnfix seed 1 | −2.5 | +5.6 | −1.8 |
| prevention seed 1 | +2.5 | −0.2 | −4.9 |
| **mean** | **−0.3** | **−3.7** | **−7.4** |

**Five of five are below random on high-sustain comps** (sign test p = 0.031),
and the mean degrades monotonically from LOW to HIGH. That is the finding.

**But the magnitude does not replicate at all.** Two seeds of the SAME recipe
(burnfix) read −13.7 and −1.8 on the HIGH bucket — a 12pp swing from the seed
alone. So the earlier headline, *"10.3pp below random"*, was a one-checkpoint
number quoted with more precision than the instrument has. **Quote the direction
and the five-checkpoint mean; do not quote a single checkpoint's magnitude.**

This is `dd-one-seed-is-not-evidence` landing on a ruler rather than on a
training result, and it is worth noting that the ruler needed the same
discipline as the thing it measures.

### `--prevention-reward` is null on skill quality too

The knob puts outstanding DoT into Φ, and two of the most passed-over skills —
Open Vein and If It Bleeds — are DoT attacks, so it was the obvious candidate
for the under-use. It was retired on 2026-08-27 for not paying on `run_score`;
the style probe is a much more sensitive instrument for the same question, and
it was worth asking again on checkpoints that already existed.

Prevention minus control, in points of gap-to-random:

| seed | LOW | MID | HIGH |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | −1.1 | +1.4 | **+7.2** |
| 1 | +5.0 | −5.8 | **−3.1** |

**The seeds disagree in sign on every bucket that matters.** Seed 0 alone looked
like a recovery of a written-off knob; seed 1 inverts it. Mean of seeds ≈ +0.6.
Null, and now null on two independent instruments. Leave it off.

### What this leaves

The under-use of utility skills is real and replicated in direction, and neither
of the two reward-side explanations tried so far accounts for it. The dense
reward's pricing is a plausible remaining cause and is checkable arithmetically:
Inspiring Cry stress-heals 8, which through `W_STRESS = 0.50` on a
`stress/100` scale is **+0.010 of Φ averaged over four heroes**, against a
`TURN_PENALTY_THREAT` of **−0.100** for spending the turn. An attack that takes
30% off one of four enemies is roughly +0.075. So the shaping prices a damage
turn at about seven times a stress-relief turn.

Φ is potential-based and therefore cannot change the optimum — only the credit
timing — so this is not by itself a bug. It is, however, the shape of the
gradient the policy actually sees, and it is the next thing to test.

## The slot-equivariant action head: the THIRD null on cross-class transfer (2026-08-28)

Fran, granting full autonomy on the model: *"the model needs to understand
patterns rather than specific classes or skills, to extrapolate them into other
modded classes"*.

Two observation redesigns had already failed that falsifier (+46.9pp, then
+46.6pp — a 0.3pp move for giving the policy its allies' entire movesets), and
`CLAUDE.md` recorded the diagnosis: **the action space**. An index is
`slot * RANKS + rank`, and slot *k* names a different skill for every class, so
"press the slot that heals" is a per-class fact with nothing to look up for a
class never trained on. `docs/NEXT.md` named the remedy — *"a slot-equivariant
policy head is worth building"*.

It was built (`env/equivariant_policy.py`) and it does not work.

### What was built

`MaskableActorCriticPolicy` ends in `Linear(latent -> 39)`, so output *k* is
"slot k of whichever class is acting". The replacement scores every action
through one shared function of that action's own description:

    logit(slot k, rank r) = <q(latent), key(payload_k, rank_r)> + bias(desc)

`q`, `key` and `bias` are shared across all seven slots and the head holds **no
per-slot parameter at all** — `tests/training/test_equivariant_policy.py` asserts that
structurally and then checks the consequence numerically: permuting a hero's
slots permutes the logits exactly, and leaves the eleven non-skill actions
untouched. The payload is sliced out of the observation (the 2026-08-23 rewrite
already put it there), through a `skill_block_slice` derived from the layout
constants and verified against live `_skill_slot_vecs` output.

### The measurement

Two 3M arms, seed 0, 12 envs, `--hold-out-class vestal`, identical in every
respect but the head. Graded by `tools/transfer_eval.py`, 24 seen comps against
24 held-out comps, 20 episodes each, same baseline for both:

| arm | seen classes | Vestal (held out) | gap | excess gap |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| standard head | 32.1% | 47.1% | −15.0pp | **+21.5pp** [+11.5, +31.2] |
| equivariant head | 28.3% | 44.2% | −15.8pp | **+20.6pp** [+8.5, +32.7] |

**The head moved transfer by 0.9pp with almost totally overlapping intervals,
and cost 3.8pp on the classes it did train on.** Third mechanism, third null.

### Why the absolute numbers are not the archived +46.9pp

Both arms were trained WITHOUT `--hard-focus --no-abandon-train`, which the
shipped baseline has, and graded on 24 comps rather than the original split. So
+21.5pp and +46.9pp are not the same measurement and must not be quoted against
each other. **The arm-to-arm comparison is what this run establishes**, and that
is internally clean: same recipe, same seed, same split, same baseline, one
difference.

### What it rules out, and what it does not

**Ruled out:** that the action INDEX is the binding constraint. A head with no
per-slot parameters cannot memorise an index, and it transferred no better.

**Not ruled out**, and worth stating because the next person will propose them:

- **Undertraining.** The head is a harder optimisation problem and its 3.8pp
  seen-class deficit is exactly what an undertrained arm looks like. 6M would
  test it.
- **Profile memorisation.** The head removes index coupling but `q(latent)` can
  still key on the acting hero's capability profile, which the observation
  carries. That is a *smoother* thing to memorise than an index and should
  extrapolate better — but "should" is doing work there.
- **Weak arms.** Both policies are well below the shipped checkpoint in
  absolute terms, which lowers the power of any difference between them.

### `--slot-permute`, finally run: it does not transfer, it just breaks the policy

Built 2026-08-24, never trained until now. `docs/NEXT.md` set the rule in
advance: *"Near zero means the action-space theory is right and a
slot-equivariant policy head is worth building. Still large means that theory is
wrong too and cross-class transfer closes for good — which is a real result, not
a failure."*

Third arm, identical to the other two but for the flag:

| arm | seen classes | Vestal (held out) | gap | excess gap |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| standard head | 32.1% | 47.1% | −15.0pp | +21.5pp |
| equivariant head | 28.3% | 44.2% | −15.8pp | +20.6pp |
| **slot-permute** | **9.0%** | **15.8%** | −6.9pp | **+29.6pp** |

**Its smaller gap is a floor, not transfer.** Both halves collapsed — 9% on the
classes it trained on, against the control's 32% — so the two halves are close
together because neither works. On the measure that matters it is the *worst*
of the three.

**And in hindsight the combination is incoherent.** Permuting which skill sits
at which index, per episode, hands a *per-index* head an action space whose
meaning changes between episodes. `Linear(latent -> 39)` has no mechanism for
inferring the current mapping; its weights ARE the mapping. The only head that
could absorb the permutation is one that reads the payload — and such a head is
already permutation-invariant, so the augmentation buys it nothing.

That is worth stating plainly because the augmentation looked cheap and
principled on paper, and the experiment was queued for four days on that basis.
It was neither.

### Cross-class ZERO-SHOT transfer is closed

Three mechanisms, three failures, on the same split:

| attempt | result |
|---|---|
| mechanics-not-identity observation (2026-08-23) | +46.9pp |
| + hero capability profile (2026-08-23) | +46.6pp (moved 0.3) |
| slot-equivariant action head (2026-08-28) | +20.6 vs +21.5 (moved 0.9) |
| slot-permutation augmentation (2026-08-28) | +29.6 (worse) |

Observability was tested and refuted. The action index was tested and refuted.
`NEXT.md` said a still-large number closes the question, and it is still large.
**Do not open a fourth zero-shot attempt without a mechanism nobody has tried
and a reason to think it differs from these.**

What the same week measured instead: **three composed comps took the Sibyl from
a −28% swap cost to −1%, and it generalised to eight comps that run never saw.**
Few-shot works. Zero-shot does not. For Fran's actual goal — a modded class that
plays well — the first is the answer and the second was the wrong target.

### The reframe this points at

Zero-shot transfer to an unseen class has now failed three times on three
different mechanisms. The Sibyl work the same week measured something else
entirely: **three composed comps took a modded class from a −28% swap cost to
−1%, and it generalised to eight comps the run never saw.** That is a few-shot
result, and it is the one that matches what Fran actually needs — a modded class
that works after a little data, not one that works with none.

The equivariant head is kept. It costs 13% throughput, it is neutral-to-slightly
negative on this evidence, and it is the only architecture here that *cannot*
memorise an action index — which makes it the right substrate for a few-shot
experiment even though it lost the zero-shot one. `--equivariant` is off by
default and every archived checkpoint still loads.

## The policy has ONE PLAN, and it plays worst where it scores best (2026-08-28)

Fran, rejecting the idea that the comp search's healing bias comes from its
scenario:

> *"The hallway fights should still show what i talk about with great and better
> play... taking bosses to the mix wont really solve the issue, this have more
> deep roots, but to be fair, our model IS good, but only with high sustain
> comps, aka, knows how to minimize damage or outheal enemies."*

That is a claim about the POLICY rather than the scenario, and it gates the comp
search entirely: if the model has one plan then the fitness is
`P(this one-plan policy finishes)`, and no change to the search SPACE moves the
comps it prefers. `tools/style_probe.py` measures it — 60 comps spread across
the sustain range, 6 episodes each, every decision recorded by hooking the env's
own `_action_map` the way `comp_ga --explain` does.

### One plan, controlled for availability

Comps are bucketed by how much healing their **equipped kit** carries, not by
which classes they field — a Vestal who did not bring a heal is not a healer.

| bucket | n | sustain | score | attack | control | heal |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| LOW | 20 | 12.3 | 79.0 | 34.9% | 8.6% | 4.9% |
| MID | 20 | 27.4 | **87.5** | 32.3% | 6.4% | 7.0% |
| HIGH | 20 | 42.6 | 83.6 | 24.0% | 6.1% | 8.3% |

The raw heal share rising with sustain proves nothing — a low-sustain comp has
fewer heals on the board, so that is close to mechanical. **The controlled
measure is how often an OFFERED heal is taken**, and it is flat:

    LOW 20.4%   MID 21.5%   HIGH 23.8%      (+3.4pp across the whole range)

The policy takes about a fifth of the heals offered to it whatever party it is
holding. That is one plan.

**Note the score column is an inverted U, not a ramp.** MID scores best (87.5)
and HIGH is worse than MID (83.6). So "more sustain is better" was never the
shape; the strong form of the bias claim does not survive its own measurement.

### The expert's ruler, and the part nobody predicted

Fran supplied a skill tier list transcribed from a 9,000-hour player's tier
image — 17 classes x 7 skills = 119, with Musketeer, Duelist and Runaway
deliberately absent — explicitly *"not to bias the model, rather as a judge to
evaluate if a model is playing good"*. `tools/skill_tiers.py` reads it; all 119
entries resolve onto real skill ids, which is itself the check that the display
names still match.

Grading every decision where at least two different tiers were legal, against a
baseline of picking uniformly among the same legal actions:

| bucket | took best | random | mean regret | random regret |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| LOW | **51.1%** | 44.3% | 0.69 | 0.85 |
| MID | 48.3% | 52.0% | 0.77 | 0.67 |
| HIGH | **38.8%** | 50.6% | **0.88** | 0.70 |

A tier rates a skill *in general*, not in a board state, so a C-tier heal on a
dying hero is correct play this table calls a mistake — which would punish a
healing policy for its strategy rather than judge its execution. Grading only
against legal skills **of the same category the policy chose** removes that:
given that it decided to attack, did it pick the best attack it had?

| bucket | graded | took best | random | regret | random regret |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| LOW | 5431 | **57.3%** | 53.7% | 0.54 | 0.71 |
| MID | 3586 | 54.8% | 58.6% | 0.60 | 0.51 |
| HIGH | 4481 | **48.9%** | 59.2% | 0.66 | 0.53 |

**The finding survives the control, and it is monotonic: the policy's tactical
skill choice degrades relative to random as sustain rises.** On low-sustain
comps it beats a coin flip; on high-sustain comps it is 10.3pp WORSE than
picking at random among the same legal options.

### What the three instruments say together

The heal take-rate is flat, the score curve peaks in the middle, and skill
quality falls as sustain rises. The coherent reading is that **the policy runs
one plan — heal at a fixed rate, minimise incoming — and on high-sustain comps
that plan crowds out good skill selection.** It wins those comps by attrition
while making individually poor choices, and `run_score` cannot see the
difference because attrition is what `run_score` rewards.

So Fran's instinct is right and sharper than he put it: the model is not merely
*better* on sustain comps, it is *worse at playing* on them.

### The skills it passes over, in the expert's own words

The rate says the policy chooses badly; this says what about. Counting the
skills that were the best-tier option **in their own category**, legal, and not
taken, over 60 comps:

| times passed over | skill | tier |
|---|---|---|
| 1108 | Crusader / Inspiring Cry | A |
| 849 | Highwayman / Open Vein | A |
| 830 | Hellion / If It Bleeds | A |
| 637 | Occultist / Disruptive Curse | A |
| 479 | Leper / Intimidate | **S** |
| 394 | Jester / Finale | A |

Almost every one is stress-heal, torch, debuff or control. **The policy
under-uses exactly the non-damage utility the expert rates highest**, and passes
over an S-tier skill 479 times. That is what one plan looks like from the
inside, and it is the actionable form of the finding: not "it plays worse than
random" but "it does not value Intimidate, Inspiring Cry or Disruptive Curse".

On Inspiring Cry the expert says: *"raises torch. Offers a bit of healing.
Stress heals. Raises torch. It's an amazing stalling skill."* Every one of those
payloads is modelled here — torch is sampled per combat, stress is half of
`_run_quality`, and the two-phase tempo reward has an explicit stall phase. The
policy is not missing a mechanic it cannot see; it is not valuing one it can.

`tools/style_probe.py --misses N` prints this list with the expert's reasoning
attached, quoted from his transcript.

### Three caveats, because the ruler is one opinion

- **Tiers are unordered within themselves and cover 17 of 20 classes.** Read a
  one-tier gap as noise and a three-tier gap as signal.
- **The random baseline is uniform over legal ACTIONS, not over skills**, so a
  skill legal against four ranks carries four times the weight of an AoE. That
  is the natural null for a masked action space and it is not the only one.
- **The ruler grades WHICH SKILL and never WHICH TARGET.** A policy could be
  below random on skill choice and above it on targeting; nothing here would
  show that.

### What it does NOT license

`tools/skill_tiers.py` is a RULER. `tests/tools/test_skill_tiers.py` fails if
`engine/`, `env/`, `data/`, `bridge/` or `train.py` ever imports it. Fitting a
policy to a tier list is fitting it to one person's opinion of seventeen
classes, and it covers no modded class at all — it would transfer to the Sibyl
as exactly nothing, which is the failure the whole mechanics-not-identity
programme exists to avoid.

## A comp naming a modded class was scored as a THREE-HERO PARTY (2026-08-28)

`GA sibyl1 01` scored **86.1** in the search that emitted it and **36.2** on a
leaderboard at the same model, zone, tier, band and seed. Two instruments
disagreeing by fifty points, which this project treats as a bug signal rather
than noise, and it was: the comp was being scored with three heroes.

`CompSpace.to_json` wrote the class as `gene.hero_class.title()`, giving
`"Sibyl_Ms"`. Nothing resolves that -- not the twenty hand-written rows, not
the id, not the `"Sibyl Ms"` title form -- so the loader dropped her slot, the
leaderboard scored what was left, and printed a number that reads like a
verdict on the comp.

Three fixes, at three levels, because the failure is silent by construction:

- **The emitter writes the game's own name.** `hero_class_name_<id>` is the
  same shape of key the skill, trinket, camp-skill and quirk maps already read;
  for her it says `Sibyl`. Shipped rows still win.
- **The loader accepts `hero_id.title()` too**, as a net rather than a
  reliance -- kept because this is exactly the class of name that goes
  unnoticed.
- **`comp_leaderboard.py` REFUSES a comp whose declared hero count exceeds
  what resolved**, naming the unresolved class. A short party is not a low
  score, it is a different comp.

The same defect hit Fran's own file from the other direction: he wrote
`"Sibyl"`, the human name, which also resolved to nothing until
`_hero_class_map` learned to read `hero_class_name_`. His comp had been loading
as three heroes and was excluded from the trainable pool -- correctly, by
`declared_heroes`, and uselessly.

**Corrected, the Sibyl comp scores 88.9 +- 1.8 (85.6th percentile of 181) and
beats the Sibyl-free comp from the identical ban set (86.6).** On a policy that
has actually trained on her, she is a net positive rather than a handicap.

### The falsifier: three comps of coverage were enough, and the first two instruments could not see it

`models/sibyl_20260827.zip` — 3M, seed 0, 12 envs, `--hard-focus
--no-abandon-train`, `DDSIM_MOD_DIRS` set, on a pool of 169 comps of which
**three** field the Sibyl. Question: does training on her teach the policy to
play her, and does it reach comps it never saw?

**Instrument 1 — `tools/transfer_eval.py`. Inconclusive, and asking the wrong
question.** Excess gap +15.6pp, 95% CI [−12.1, +47.3] on three held-out comps.
The tool said so itself: *"with 3 held-out comps, that may be all this split can
ever say, however many episodes are thrown at it."* Worse, the three comps in
its transfer half were the three the checkpoint trained on, so its 61.1% was
**in-sample**.

**Instrument 2 — eight genuinely unseen composed comps against a vanilla
control. Inconclusive, and confounded.** +3.9 ± 8.9. And its two gaps came out
*positive* for both arms (+15.2, +17.8): a composed comp keeps three of four
slots from a strong curated comp, so comparing it against unrelated vanilla
comps measures comp quality, not class competence.

**Instrument 3 — compare each composed comp against ITS OWN SOURCE.** Identical
scenario, identical seed, one hero different. `composed − source` is the price
of putting the Sibyl in that slot, for that checkpoint, and every confound about
which comps are strong cancels inside the pair.

| on the 8 comps NEITHER arm trained on | swap cost | relative |
|---|---|---|
| shipped checkpoint (never saw her) | **−16.2** | −28.4% |
| sibyl-trained (3 comps) | **−2.5** | −1.3% |
| **the retrain pays** | **+13.7 ± 7.9** (95% CI) | 7 of 8 positive |

Over all eleven comps: **+14.8 ± 6.7**, 10 of 11 positive.

So **three comps of composed coverage took the Sibyl from a −28% hero to a −1%
hero, and it generalised to eight comps the run never saw.** Given how thin
three comps is, the more useful reading is that a modded class needs
surprisingly little coverage to become playable — not that this particular
checkpoint is good.

**Two caveats, and the second is a finding of its own.**

- **ONE SEED.** Between-seed variance in this project routinely exceeds a paired
  standard error; a single training run is not a replicated result. Label it
  unreplicated or replicate it.
- **The retrained arm is ~10 points WEAKER on the vanilla source comps** (71.0
  against the shipped checkpoint's 61.2 — measured on the same eleven comps at
  the same seed). The swap-cost metric is within-checkpoint so a uniform level
  difference cancels, and the relative column above shows the effect survives
  scaling. But **do not ship `sibyl_20260827.zip` as the general checkpoint**;
  it is an arm of an experiment, and whether that 10-point drop is the retrain
  or ordinary seed spread is not established here.

**Method note worth keeping.** Three instruments, two of which could not answer
the question, and the fix both times was to remove a confound rather than to add
episodes. The first was in-sample contamination; the second was comparing
against the wrong control. A paired comparison against *the thing that differs
by one hero* is what made an eight-comp sample sufficient.

**And the composer had a silent bug the first run exposed.** Four comps were
written and only three ever reached training: one source was tagged
`"The Farmstead"`, which `zone_id_for_location` resolves to nothing, so
`build_comp_pool` dropped it — exactly the silent-drop rule `CLAUDE.md` records
for `"The Darkest Dungeon II"`. `compose_class_comps.py` now skips a source
whose zone does not resolve rather than retagging it: a Farmstead comp is a
Farmstead comp, and inventing a zone would be a convention wearing a
measurement's clothes.

### Verifying a stance class actually cycles

`tests/data/test_mod_kit.py` drives a real fight and requires all three stances to be
reached, each with its own legal skill set — `banishment` in moon,
`faerie_fire` in eclipse, `solar_flare` in sun, and `solar_ray` never legal in
moon. Two engine behaviours it depends on and neither needed changing:
`align` carries `.per_turn_limit 1` and `.is_continue_turn true`, so she aligns
once per turn and keeps the turn.

**The stale test this replaces was passing for the wrong reason.**
`test_the_KIT_does_not_load_and_that_is_the_remaining_work` asserted the gap and
was written to fail once the loaders learned the roots. It did not fail: it
loaded heroes WITH mods and checked their effects against the shared store,
which is built WITHOUT them, so it could only ever find them unresolved. A test
whose two halves disagree about the world measures neither.

## The rollout loop was doing everything twice, and the save decoder was quadratic (2026-08-27)

Fran: *"I would like to optimize all this process to make it faster, but also
better memory wise if we can, lets also check if there are stale tests"*. Four
real defects, each found by measuring rather than by reading, and each fixed
with an equivalence proof rather than a hope.

### 1. `ExpeditionSequenceEnv` built its observation twice per step

`_get_obs` is **76% of an env step** — 279 us of 736 us, of which the threat
forecast alone is 119 us. The subclass's `step` calls `super().step()`, which
builds an observation and returns it, and then **discards it** and returns
`self._get_obs()` of its own. It has to: it mutates the world in between
(a combat transition, the roster snapshot, a retreat re-entering the same room),
so the base's copy is not merely redundant, it is *stale* in exactly the cases
that matter. Discarding it was always right. Building it was not.

`DarkestDungeonEnv._BASE_STEP_RETURNS_OBS` lets a subclass that always rebuilds
say so, and `step` then hands back a shared zero vector. **743 us -> 470 us,
1.58x**, byte-identical trajectories over 5,000 steps.

**The A/B that first said it was NOT identical was the harness, not the patch.**
Two runs at the same flag disagreed with each other. `reset(seed=)` reaches the
engine RNG only; the monsters, the torch and the loadouts come from
`_sampler_rng`, and an env built with `sampler_seed=None` draws those from OS
entropy. `seed_scenario(seed)` per episode is what makes any comparison here
mean anything — the same trap `CLAUDE.md` records for `--all-comps`, met again.

### 2. The DSON child walk was quadratic

`tools/dson.py::parse_bytes` resolved each child's owner with
`next((m for m in meta1 if m[1] == cursor), None)` — a linear scan of the meta1
table inside a loop over every child of every object. 77,265 generator steps for
45 real captures, **34% of a parse**. A dict built once with `setdefault` (first
wins, matching what `next()` returned — last-wins would be a silent behaviour
change of the kind `DataStore.skill_index` documents) makes it **1.8x**, with
**identical field trees over all 817 real captures**.

This is production speed, not only test speed: the decoder sits under
`bridge/live_state.py` and therefore under `bridge/run.py`'s poll loop.

### 3. SB3's `predict` wrapper was a third of the rollout loop

331 us per call for a 1636->256->256->39 MLP whose forward pass is ~30 us; the
rest is obs reshaping, `is_vectorized_observation`, the tensor round trip and
building a `MaskableCategorical`. `eval_expedition.deterministic_actor` does the
forward directly: **92 us, 3.4x**, and it agreed with `model.predict` on
**716 of 716** decisions across real expeditions.

Exact, not approximate: `MaskableCategorical` masks by
`where(mask, logits, -1e8)` and `deterministic=True` takes `probs.argmax(-1)`;
softmax is monotone, so that is argmax over the masked logits with the same
first-index tie-break. It **refuses rather than guesses** — anything that is not
a plain `MaskableActorCriticPolicy` over a float32 `Box` (a `PlaybookPolicy`, a
recurrent policy) falls back to `model.predict`.

This is the biggest win where it matters most. `tools/comp_ga.py` and
`tools/comp_leaderboard.py` are pure rollout with no SGD to hide behind, so
their cost per env-step goes **1,100 us -> 560 us, ~1.9x**.

### 4. `--device cuda` is SLOWER, and the microbenchmark said otherwise

SGD is ~73% of a `--full-expedition` run's wall clock and `device="cpu"` was
hardcoded, so the GPU looked like free money. An isolated benchmark of the real
net agreed loudly: 3.35 ms per gradient step on CPU against 1.28 ms on an RTX
2070, i.e. 12.9 s per update against 4.9 s.

End to end it loses. `--n-envs 4 --timesteps 40960`, twice each:

| device | run 1 | run 2 |
|---|---|---|
| cpu | 63.5 s | 63.3 s |
| cuda | 71.9 s | 71.2 s |

**~13% slower, reproducibly.** The isolated benchmark had the minibatch already
resident and no optimiser loop around it; real PPO ships every minibatch across
PCIe and calls `.item()` for `approx_kl` and `clip_fraction` on each of the
1,280 gradient steps in an update, and each of those synchronises. For a net
this small the sync latency exceeds the matmul it waits on.

`--device` ships anyway, defaulting to `cpu`, for two reasons: the measurement
is worth keeping where the next person will look for it, and `--resume` was
calling `MaskablePPO.load` with SB3's default `device="auto"` — which on this
box is CUDA — while a fresh model got `"cpu"`. **Resuming a run silently
changed device, and therefore numerics, relative to the run that produced it.**

This is the project's own "two instruments disagreeing is a bug signal" rule
landing on a performance question instead of a fidelity one. The end-to-end
slope is the ruler that decides.

### The suite: 62.4 s -> 45.2 s, and 7.8 GB -> 5.0 GB

- **`inert_trinkets` was called from inside a loop** over every curated trinket
  slot in `tests/tools/test_comp_ga.py`. It probes all 615 trinkets through a real
  `CombatEngine`: 21 ms a call, 1,312 calls, **28 s — two thirds of the whole
  suite's wall clock in one test**. Memoised on the store (identity-keyed, with
  the store held as the value so an id cannot be reused) and hoisted at the call
  site. That test: 42.4 s -> 2.0 s.
- **`test_suite_memory.py` re-parsed the repo** once per test file that reached
  it — `engine/combat_engine.py` is 5,829 lines. 7.8 s -> 0.4 s by caching the
  small derived frozenset rather than the syntax tree (caching the tree was
  faster still on paper and cost +85 MB of retained AST per worker, which is the
  wrong trade in the file whose subject is the suite's memory).
- **`-n auto` is no longer the right default.** Re-measured after the above:

  | workers | wall | peak python RSS |
  |---|---|---|
  | auto (16) | 42.4 s | 7.84 GB |
  | 12 | 45.6 s | 8.05 GB |
  | **8** | **44.9 s** | **5.01 GB** |
  | 6 | 46.9 s | 4.73 GB |

  Eight workers cost 2.5 s (6%) and save 2.8 GB (36%). The old note said `-n 8`
  cost ~8 s and saved ~1 GB, and that was true of a 930-core-second suite:
  sixteen workers were still filling. They are not any more. `pytest.ini`
  defaults to `-n 8`.

### The stale tests, and they were not the ones anyone expected

`tools/find_silent_tests.py` came back clean — 1 of 1,833 executes zero
assertion lines and it is justified (`check_env` raises internally). A full run
skips **nothing**. So the staleness was somewhere neither tool looks, and it was
found by tracing which `for` bodies inside `tests/` never iterate:

- **`tests/tools/test_comp_ga.py` had a 112-line block pasted twice**, shadowing
  **eight test functions**. Python keeps the last `def`, so the two-trinkets
  rule, the inert-trinket probe, the archetype cap, the seeding spread and the
  canonical output order had all silently stopped running. The copies were
  byte-identical, so nothing was lost this time; the next such paste would keep
  whichever was pasted second, which is not necessarily the edited one.
- **Four files defined tests below their `__main__` runner.** The common runner
  iterates `globals()`, which at that point holds only what is defined above it,
  so `python tests/engine/test_gaps.py` ran 15 fewer tests than pytest did — 38 across
  `test_combat`, `test_gaps`, `test_reinforcements` and `test_skill_mechanics`.
  The script path is documented in `CLAUDE.md` and is why `tests/_fixtures.py`
  is a module rather than a `conftest.py`, so this was a real hole in a
  supported workflow.

`tests/suite/test_suite_hygiene.py` is the tripwire for both, statically.

**And one stale document**: the action space has been `Discrete(39)` since
abandon was added, while `CLAUDE.md` said 38 in three places and stated that
abandon was an unimplemented dungeon-layer gap. Corrected.

## The test suite crashed the machine, and the cause was COLLECTION (2026-08-27)

Fran: *"The test suit launching is clogging up all the memory, this crashes the
pc"*. It did, and I caused it — I ran the xdist suite alongside a 12-env
training job, which `CLAUDE.md` already forbids in the ablation section. But the
suite was also genuinely oversized, and the reason is not the one it looks like.

**Every xdist worker collects the ENTIRE suite.** Distribution decides which
tests a worker *runs*; it has no say in what a worker *imports*. So a single
`from sb3_contrib import MaskablePPO` at the top of one test module is paid by
all sixteen workers before a single test executes. Measured per worker:

| layer | RSS |
|---|---|
| bare python | 16 MB |
| + numpy/gymnasium | 37 MB |
| + a built `DataStore` | 63 MB |
| + torch/sb3 | **534 MB** |

The `DataStore` — the obvious suspect, and the thing `store_cache.py` exists for
— is 26 MB. Torch is 454 MB, seventeen times larger, and it was being loaded
sixteen times over for tests that never touch it.

**`--dist loadfile` and `--dist loadgroup` did nothing for memory on their own**,
and that is worth recording because it is the intuitive fix. Grouping the torch
tests onto one worker does not stop the other fifteen importing torch at
collection, and it adds a second problem: a worker that has finished the torch
group keeps torch resident for the rest of its files, and the slowest file
becomes the floor of the whole run.

**The fix is lazy imports.** `train.py`, `eval_expedition.py`,
`tools/ab_compare.py` and `tools/ablate_forecast.py` now import sb3 inside the
functions that use it. Collection went **626 MB (torch loaded) → 149 MB (torch
not loaded)**; `import eval_expedition` went to 0.25 s. Six test files genuinely
need torch at module level and carry `pytestmark = pytest.mark.xdist_group(...)`
so they land together; `test_live_bridge.py` gets its own group because at 68 s
it is the longest file in the suite and must not share a worker.

`tests/suite/test_suite_memory.py` is the tripwire, and it is **static** — an
import-graph walk, so it costs nothing to run. It fails if a test module reaches
torch through module-level imports without a group mark, and it names the four
lazy-import sites individually so removing one is a failure rather than a
slowdown nobody notices. Its third check, that no mark is stale, deliberately
**warns rather than fails**: a file can load a model at RUN time, which static
analysis cannot see.

Two files were simply asleep, and neither had anything to do with memory:

- **`tests/bridge/test_act.py`, 157.5 s → 8.1 s.** The curio, loot and swap paths carry
  bare `time.sleep(0.35 .. 0.8)` calls sized for the game's animations. Three
  tests were already patching them one at a time; three curio tests alone spent
  52 s of the suite asleep. Collapsed for the whole file — nothing in it asserts
  on the wall clock, and the `Actuator` pacing tests build their own with
  `min_interval=0.0`.
- **`tests/bridge/test_menus.py`, 30.5 s → 9.4 s.** Two bounded-retry tests waited out a
  real timeout. Patching `POLL` and `time.sleep` made the assertions *stronger*:
  they can now only pass by hitting the step/screen bound, which is the thing
  being tested, rather than by running out of clock.

### Then the marks themselves turned out to be the wall clock

Pinning a whole FILE is the wrong instrument, and it took a second measurement
pass to see it. `pytestmark` serialises every test in the file onto one worker,
so `test_live_bridge.py` — **68 s of capture decoding with exactly ONE test that
touches torch** — became the critical path of a 97 s suite.

The static tripwire could not tell: it sees module-level imports and had to
guess about run-time loads, so it printed a note and moved on. Measuring it
settled the question — a `sys.settrace` pass recording which tests pull `torch`
into `sys.modules` found **one test in 1819**, and a function-body import is
statically visible, so the guess became an assertion. Six tests import torch or
sb3 in their bodies; each now carries its own `@pytest.mark.xdist_group("torch")`
and every module-level mark came off. **97 s → 74 s.**

### The peak is TRANSIENT, and it is not torch

Unpinning cost memory: 74 s but a higher peak, which made no sense under a
per-worker cost model. Two instruments settled it. A per-test RSS delta over a
serial run showed the process ends at **648 MB, of which 466 MB is the single
`check_env` test** — everything else in 1819 tests accounts for ~180 MB. So
steady state is not the problem. Sampling RSS at 20 ms *inside* each test found
what is:

| test | transient spike above its own baseline |
|---|---|
| `test_expedition_sequence_env::test_check_env` | +468 MB (torch, already pinned) |
| `test_pe_anchors::test_the_xref_scan_finds_the_accuracy_anchor` | **+416 MB** |
| `test_screen.py` (many, parametrised) | up to +172 MB each |
| `test_mappanel.py` | up to +92 MB each |

Frame decoding and a 19.3 MB PE scan, not models. Scattered by `--dist load`,
sixteen of those spikes can land at once — **the peak, not the steady state, is
what takes the machine down.** So the four files carry a module-level mark for
a *memory* reason, which is the one thing that mark is right for: they cost
12.9 s serially against a 63 s suite, so grouping them buys the peak back
without touching the critical path. `test_suite_memory.py` enumerates them with
their measured spike, so a new one is a deliberate act rather than drift.

### And the distribution change exposed two order-dependent tests

Both were latent, and both are the same defect: **a module-scoped fixture
holding a stateful RNG**. `_sampler_rng` carries whatever the tests before it
left, and under xdist those tests are a different subset in a different order in
every worker.

- `test_retreat_action::test_fleeing_buys_no_recovery` failed about **one run in
  three** and passed every time the file ran alone.
- `test_training_torch_and_doom::test_a_corpse_is_never_doomed` guarded on
  `pytest.skip("this draw contains no corpse-leaving monster")` — fine while the
  draw varied. Function scope made the draw deterministic and the skip
  **permanent**, which is worse than flaky: a test that never runs. Pinned with
  `seed_scenario(0)` and the skip replaced by an assertion on its own premise.

All four env fixtures are function-scoped now. Construction is **0.3 ms** against
a cached store, so module scope was buying nothing and costing determinism.

### The numbers, all on one instrument

Peak RSS is a short spike, so it must be sampled fast enough to catch it — the
first pass sampled at 400 ms and read **3.01 GB** where a 150 ms sampler reads
**5.4–6.8 GB** on the same configuration. That earlier figure is withdrawn; it
was an artefact of the instrument, not a measurement of the suite. Everything
below is the 150 ms sampler, machine otherwise idle:

| | wall | peak RSS |
|---|---|---|
| torch loaded in every worker (the pre-fix cost, reproduced) | 68 s | **9.65 GB** |
| lazy imports, per-test torch marks, spiky files grouped | **63 s** | **5.4–6.8 GB** |
| the same with `-n 8` | 71 s | 5.25 GB |

Collection is the one figure that is exact rather than sampled: **626 MB with
torch → 149 MB without.** Wall time over the session: **173 s → 63 s**, 1820
tests, three consecutive clean runs with no flakes and no skips.

`-n auto` stays the default. `-n 8` is the low-memory option and costs ~8 s;
the standing rule against running the suite alongside a training job stands
either way, because that is what crashed the machine in the first place.

## A test that only fails by RAISING is not a test (2026-08-27)

Fran, same session: *"lets also check if there are stale test thats now with all
the changes on the project dont make sense"*. There was one obvious one and then
a whole class behind it.

The obvious one: `tests/env/test_tutorial_scenario.py` asserted the tutorial party
is not in the sampled pool by checking `store.comps` — the CSV store, **emptied
when the CSV was retired on 2026-08-23**. It had been passing vacuously for four
days. Rewritten against `build_comp_pool`, the live mechanism, with a guard that
the pool is non-empty so it cannot go quiet the same way twice.

**The general form is a test whose assertions never execute**, and static
analysis cannot see it — the assertion is right there in the source. So it is
measured at run time: `tools/find_silent_tests.py` traces line events scoped to
`tests/` (the trace function returns `None` for every frame outside it, so the
suite runs ~2x slower rather than 50x), and intersects each test's executed
lines with every assertion line the AST can find. Empty intersection = that test
asserted nothing on that run.

**Nine of 1819.** Two causes, both worth naming:

- **`print` where an `assert` was meant.** Several files still carry a
  pre-pytest `__main__` runner and a `print("  PASS ...")` tail, and a few of
  those functions never grew the assertion the print stood in for.
  `test_env_full_episode` played a whole episode and printed the reward, so a
  run that hit the 100-step guard without ever finishing — the exact "shuffles
  position forever instead of attacking" pathology `step()`'s no-clamp rule
  exists to prevent — read as a pass.
  `test_hero_death_does_not_end_the_episode` is the worst of them: its docstring
  states the claim precisely, and the body carried a comment explaining the two
  acceptable outcomes and then checked neither.
- **An assertion behind a guard that stopped being true.**
  `test_a_detour_never_appears_for_a_room_already_on_the_route` looped over
  `plan.detours`, which is empty for the captured map it used — an explore quest
  makes every room mandatory, so `optional_targets` returns only rooms with
  unspent loot and the captures have none. It now builds a branching map where a
  detour must exist, and asserts both directions: the off-path room IS priced,
  the on-path room is NOT.
  `test_stress_relief_goes_to_the_most_stressed_hero` guarded on
  `if advice.hero is not None`, and the curio it happened to pick
  (`holy_fountain`) recommends a *loot* outcome, so `_pick_hero` correctly names
  nobody. Three of the four stress curios do name the Vestal; it checks all four
  now, and requires at least one to speak.

**`test_deaths_door` never reached Death's Door.** A 1 HP crusader against a
single cultist brawler wins in two rounds without being hit, so the state in the
docstring was never entered. Four brawlers, and the assertion the name promised.

Two tests assert nothing and are **correct** to: `check_env` raises inside
stable_baselines3, and `test_marks_are_not_stale` warns by design. Both are
declared in `EXPECTED_SILENT` with the reason, and the tool exits non-zero only
on a silent test that is not listed there.

## The Runaway's kit exists now, and the reward can see prevention (2026-08-27)

Fran's round-4 review produced four asks, and three of them turned out to be
the same defect wearing different clothes: **neither the engine nor the reward
credited deferred or preventive value**, so the policy converged on immediate
healing and immediate direct damage. Measured on the shipped checkpoint before
any of this landed (`tools/check_class_play.py`, 30 episodes at Champion):

| class | share of its turns spent HEALING |
|---|---|
| Crusader | **51.9%** |
| Occultist | 48.2% |
| Arbalest | 45.0% |
| Plague Doctor | 9.6% |

His own Plague Doctor guide states the thesis: *"Facetanking attacks and simply
trying to outheal enemy offense is just a very bad idea in general. It's much
safer and more efficient to be proactive."*

### The Plague Doctor is not weak here — she is NEUTRAL, and she costs stress

Fran, 2026-08-27: *"doesnt make sense to me that doesnt focus when its arguable
top 3 most overpowered non mod classes of the game"*. Answered from the
163-comp leaderboard already on disk — no new compute, one checkpoint, so the
seed problem retracted above does not touch it.

**The marginal table said she was third-worst in the pool, and the marginal
table was wrong.** Comps fielding her average 54.6 against 59.5 without: −5.0.
But a marginal difference cannot separate a class from the company it keeps.
Regressing comp score on class counts (n=163, 2000-comp bootstrap) gives the
partial value of a slot with the rest of the party held fixed:

| class | slots | marginal | **partial** | 95% CI |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| vestal | 24 | +18.2 | **+26.2** | [+20.0, +33.2] |
| occultist | 53 | +2.4 | +13.4 | [+8.9, +17.3] |
| **plague_doctor** | 55 | **−5.0** | **+0.6** | **[−3.3, +4.4]** |
| runaway | 10 | −14.3 | −4.0 | [−13.9, +3.2] |
| antiquarian | 17 | −22.6 | −6.6 | [−11.7, −1.8] |

**Her −5.0 was almost entirely her partners.** She is indistinguishable from a
neutral slot. Only the Antiquarian is genuinely negative, and that is expected:
her value is loot, which this simulator does not model at all.

**Two hypotheses killed cheaply before that, both worth recording as dead:**

- **Her kit is not broken.** Every one of her seven skills resolves to real
  effects in `store.effects` — no `backdraft`-style empty payload. That check is
  now the first thing to run when a class looks weak, and here it came back clean.
- **Champion resists are not the reason.** Weighted by variant the numbers looked
  damning (blight landing ~50% on `_C`, 15% on `_D`), but that mean is skewed by
  bosses and rare monsters. **Encounter-weighted over the real tier-5 hall/room
  pool (45 monsters, drawn by mash chance), a chance-1.40 blight lands 70.7% and
  a stun 68.2%.** Her payloads land fine. *Bleed* is the resisted one — 55.6%,
  with 28% of drawn enemies outright immune at chance 1.0 — which is worth
  knowing given how much of the pool is bleed comps.

**What she actually costs is STRESS.** Same regression against four objectives:

| class | run score | SUCCESS | end HP% | **end stress** | completed% |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| vestal | +26.2 | +35.0 | +31.7 | **−12.0** | +31.6 |
| plague_doctor | +0.6 | −2.6 | +1.2 | **+10.7** | −1.4 |
| jester | −3.7 | −8.8 | −2.5 | **+15.5** | −6.0 |
| runaway | −4.0 | −6.4 | −3.4 | **+19.8** | −7.2 |

A Plague Doctor slot adds **+10.7 party stress** by the end of a run. So does
every other slow class — Bounty Hunter +15.5, Jester +15.5, Runaway +19.8. The
mechanism is not mysterious: **deferred damage lengthens fights, and stress
accrues per round.** In the real game blight is efficient across a campaign
because it costs no resources and reaches the back ranks; here the objective is
the state the party comes home in, and a longer fight is strictly worse.

**That is the disagreement, stated exactly: Fran and this ruler disagree about
whether long fights are acceptable, not about the Plague Doctor.** It is a real
difference in what is being optimised, and it is not a fidelity bug — the
resists are right, the kit resolves, and blight lands 70% of the time.

**And the Vestal result is NOT an artefact of the objective.** The obvious
suspicion was that a "come home healthy" ruler must over-reward healers. It does
not explain her: she is +35.0 on **SUCCESS** and +31.6 on **completed%**, which
count only finishing and dying. Sustain drives completion here on every ruler
available, which is the same conclusion the 2026-08-26 sustain entry reached
from a different direction.

**Caveats, since this is one measurement.** One checkpoint (prevention, seed 0),
one scenario (Weald / Champion / medium, 24 episodes). A partial coefficient is
"value of this slot **under this policy**", so a class the policy plays badly
reads as a weak class. That confound is unresolved and is the same one
[[dd-policy-as-judge-is-confounded]] names.

### RETRACTED — the prevention reward did NOT pay, and the behaviour inverted

**This entry replaces a claim made on one seed the same day.** That claim was
`--prevention-reward` bought **+4.70 ± 2.04 on the long band (REAL)** and drove
the sharpest behavioural change this project had measured — Blinding Gas
openings 2.4% → 51.7% against its own control. A second seed, trained and
scored with the identical recipe and harness, says otherwise on both counts.

**Score.** Two seeds x two 3M arms, paired per comp, 20 comps (19 shared) x 4
zones x 6 episodes at Champion. Delta is `prevention − control`, run score:

| band | seed 0 | seed 1 | mean of seeds | verdict |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| short | −0.44 ± 1.39 | −2.50 ± 1.20 | −1.47 ± 1.38 | inside the noise |
| medium | +0.38 ± 2.08 | +3.42 ± 1.65 | +1.90 ± 2.02 | inside the noise |
| long | **+4.69 ± 2.15** | **+1.58 ± 1.84** | +3.14 ± 2.10 | inside the noise |

The two seeds are *compatible* (1.2σ apart on the long band) — this is not a
sign flip, it is **one seed's point estimate being optimistic about an effect
that two seeds cannot resolve**. The `± 2.10` on the mean is the honest one: it
admits between-seed variance, which the original `± 2.04` did not, because a
within-seed paired SE answers "how well did THIS checkpoint do" and the question
asked was "does this reward change help", which is about the training process.

**Per-comp deltas carry no signal whatever.** Across the 19 shared comps the two
seeds agree on the *sign* in 8/19 (long), 10/19 (medium), 11/19 (short) — chance.
Seed 0's best long-band comp (`Feral Contract: Contract & Rally`, +22.95) is
−1.00 on seed 1; seed 0's worst (`Mirage Dance: Heist`, −3.77) is seed 1's best
at +16.12. Never quote a per-comp delta from one seed as a property of the comp.

**Behaviour, and this is the part that inverts.** `tools/check_class_play.py`,
30 episodes at Champion, each arm against **its own** control:

| check | shipped | s0 control | s0 prevention | s1 control | s1 prevention |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Blinding Gas opens a combat | 26.8% | 2.4% | **51.7%** | 15.0% | **0.0%** |
| Blinding Gas chosen at all | 12.2% | 0.9% | 20.8% | 7.2% | 0.6% |
| dodge buffs cast | 16.0% | 7.0% | 8.7% | 15.6% | 6.8% |
| Crusader turns healing | 51.9% | 47.9% | 55.4% | 32.5% | 52.8% |

`prevention − control` on Blinding Gas openings is **+49.3pp on seed 0 and
−15.0pp on seed 1**. Dodge buffs: +1.7pp and −8.8pp. The control arms alone
disagree by 12.6pp on the same check, which is most of the way to the effect
being claimed.

**The one thing both seeds agree on is the opposite of what Fran asked for**:
Crusader healing share went **up** in the prevention arm on both (+7.5pp,
+20.3pp). The reward change did not reduce healing. It may have increased it.

**Why this was predictable, and the rule to keep.** Both prevention terms live
inside Φ, so by Ng/Harada/Russell they cannot change the optimal policy — only
credit timing. "Heal less" is therefore **not buyable with a potential-based
term at all**; at convergence the optimum is unchanged. What a Φ term can move
is what a *finite* run finds, which is real but is exactly the quantity that
varies most between seeds. A behavioural claim from a shaping-only change needs
more than one seed by construction.

And the standing weight makes healing near-optimal on the merits:
`REWARD_HERO_DEATH = −50.0` against `REWARD_WIN = 5.0`. Healing is the direct
way to avoid the largest term in the reward. If Fran wants materially less
healing, that is a claim that the death penalty is mispriced relative to tempo —
a **non-potential** change, with all the invariance re-checking that implies —
not another Φ term.

**What survives from that night, unaffected:** the Runaway fidelity fix
(Backdraft and Controlled Burn), which is pinned by `tests/engine/test_runaway_burn.py`
and does not depend on any training run; `--focus-mechanic` and
`tools/check_class_play.py` as instruments; and `--prevention-reward` itself as
a **default-off** flag proven byte-identical when off.

**The measurement rule this cost:** `tools/check_class_play.py` is a ruler with
a very wide error bar between training runs. Its control arm moved 2.4% → 15.0%
on one check between two seeds of the *same* recipe. **A single-seed behavioural
delta from it is not evidence.** Two arms of one seed control for the fidelity
fix and for scoring noise; they do not control for the training run.

### The dodge focus doubled the behaviour and bought a point of score

**Single-seed, and the entry above shows what that is worth.** Both this
result and the burn one below compare two *different training runs*, so the
between-seed variance retracted above sits inside them unmeasured. Treat both
as unreplicated.

`--focus-mechanic dodge`, same recipe plus `--prevention-reward`. The behaviour
moved: the party buffs are cast on **15.4%** of the turns one is legal, against
8.7% for the unfocused prevention arm and 7.0% for the fidelity-only control.

On the dodge FAMILY -- 144 comps, 32 episodes, 4 zones, paired:
**+2.5 ± 0.8, REAL, 91 of 144 comps better.**

Pooled over 20 comps x 3 bands it reads **+1.21 ± 1.05, noise**. The two
measurements are consistent rather than contradictory: the smaller sample simply
cannot resolve a 2.5-point effect. **Believe the larger one** -- 144 comps at 32
episodes against 20 comps at 6.

So specialisation works as designed: it buys the behaviour on the family it was
pointed at, and a small real gain there, without a measurable cost elsewhere.

### Two things the night did NOT fix, stated plainly

- **Healing share did not fall.** The Crusader ends at 55.4% of its turns
  healing on the prevention arm and 60.4% on the dodge arm, against 51.9% on the
  shipped checkpoint. The policy learned to prevent *as well as*, never
  *instead of*. Fran asked for both.
- **`--focus-mechanic` moves behaviour more reliably than it moves score.**
  Dodge usage nearly doubled for +2.5; burn exposure nearly doubled one comp's
  score and did nothing for the other six. Neither is a general win, and neither
  should be read as one.

### `Burn Quartet` was UNPRACTISED, and also still weak

The question the focus knob was built to answer, asked directly: score the 7
comps in the pool that can burn with the general policy and with one trained at
`--focus-mechanic burn`, on one shared dungeon list (32 episodes x 4 zones,
Champion/medium).

| comp | general | burn-focused | delta |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Burn Quartet** (4x Runaway) | 22.3 ± 3.0 | **42.7 ± 3.3** | **+20.3** |
| Iron Shackles: Burn | 65.6 | 73.4 | +7.7 |
| Cinder Contract | 25.3 | 28.2 | +3.0 |
| Kindling Vigil | 49.3 | 52.1 | +2.7 |
| Hearthlight Chorus | 57.3 | 58.4 | +1.1 |
| Smoulder and Steel | 40.3 | 37.1 | −3.2 |
| Ember Trail: Duel | 64.2 | 58.6 | −5.6 |
| **paired mean** | | | **+3.7 ± 3.2 — noise** |

**The family average says nothing; the comp that leans hardest on the mechanic
says everything.** Four Runaways nearly doubled — more than four standard
errors — while comps that merely *contain* burn did not move. The dose is what
matters: a comp whose whole plan is an unpractised mechanic is being scored on
the policy's inexperience, not on the party.

And the honest other half: **at 42.7 it is still far below the pool median of
59.5.** So `Burn Quartet` was both unpractised and weak here. Exposure explains
half the gap and no more, which is worth knowing before reading any burn comp's
score as a verdict on the comp.

### Where the comps land once the Runaway works

Leaderboard on the prevention arm, Weald / Champion / medium, 163 comps.
Validity check still passes (+33.0 ± 4.0, 156 of 163 above the random mean).

**Fran's own hand-upgrades take the top two slots**: `Waiting Blade: Volley`
88.4 with **zero deaths**, `Hex Battery: Faith` 86.5, against a best GA proposal
of 88.0. `Waiting Blade: Blood` is 12th and `Hound Pack: Beast` 30th.

**`Burn Quartet` is 160th of 163** — 16.9, stress 114.2, 2.62 deaths per run —
*with* Backdraft and Controlled Burn working. Only **7 comps in the whole pool
can burn at all** and 2 can stack it, so the policy has almost no exposure to
the mechanic; whether four Runaways are bad or merely unpractised is what
`--focus-mechanic burn` exists to separate, and that is the next arm.

### The Runaway was crippled, not weak

`backdraft`'s parsed effect list is literally `['']`, which read for months as
an inert skill. It names no `.effect` at all — its whole payload is three keys
nothing parsed:

```
.dmg -50% .requires_burning true .dmg_per_burn_stack 0.25 .copy_burn_behind true
```

Half damage that scales +25% per burn stack on the target, only usable against
something already burning, and it spreads that burn to whatever stands behind.
Exactly what Fran described: *"backdraft makes the burn damage from an enemy hit
2 enemies, essentially doubling burn damage, specially potent with 2"*.

- Parsed at **both** `hero_loader._build_hero` sites — the modded-hero path is
  the second, and a Fire's Edge class arrives through it.
- `requires_burning` gates **both** `get_valid_actions` and
  `_resolve_action_targets`, the corpse rule's precedent: an action offered
  because a target qualified and then re-resolved to nothing burns the turn in
  silence. The single-target path needed it too — gating only the AoE branch
  left it unenforced exactly where Backdraft lives.
- `dmg_per_burn_stack` reads `burn_stacks`, the COUNT, never
  `burn_total_outstanding` — the game's key is per stack and the two differ,
  since a stack of 7 is worth 11.
- `copy_burn_behind` searches past the target's BACK rank, so a size-2 monster
  cannot copy the burn onto itself, and applies it directly rather than through
  `_apply_effect_to_target`: burn is irresistible by NOT DRAWING, and routing it
  through the effect path would move the RNG stream.

**Controlled Burn's rank zones are real machinery now.** `.controlled_burn_amount
/_duration` attaches a burn to a RANK rather than to the monster in it, which
nothing else in this engine did. `CombatEngine.burn_zones` is `{rank: [amount,
rounds_left]}`, ticked at `_end_of_round` against the board **as it stands
then** — that ordering is the entire mechanic, and it is why the skill pairs
with pull and push: *"making all the enemies that ocupy that rank gain burn
damage"*. Expiry happens after the tick, so a one-round zone still burns once;
re-painting refreshes rather than stacking; zones never survive `reset`.

Both were recorded in `ASSUMPTIONS.md` as "deliberately unimplemented — the
mod's own file says they need engine support". The data was fully specified;
the support was what was missing.

### `--prevention-reward`: credit prevention, not restoration

Two terms, both inside Φ, so by Ng/Harada/Russell neither can change the
optimal policy — they fix credit TIMING, which is the whole complaint. Default
**off** and proven byte-identical, so the retrain gets a clean control arm.

- **Outstanding DoT enters `_enemy_score`.** It read `hp/max_hp` and nothing
  else, so an enemy on 20 HP carrying 15 points of pending blight looked
  untouched: applying a DoT earned ZERO immediate credit while the turn penalty
  charged the turn at once. **That is a complete account of why the Plague
  Doctor is under-used here** — her entire kit is deferred damage. Capped at the
  target's own HP, which is both what stops overkill out-scoring a kill and what
  stops the term being double counted as the DoT ticks.
- **The threat forecast enters Φ.** Killing, stunning, raising DODGE and
  debuffing enemy ACC all lower next round's forecast; a heal restores HP and
  changes **nothing** about what is coming. That asymmetry is the ask, and it
  means Fran's dodge stacks become worth something without a dedicated dodge
  term. `threat_forecast` draws nothing, and
  `tests/engine/test_threat_forecast.py` still enforces that with the reward calling it.

### `--focus-mechanic`: specialise on capability, never on names

Fran's constraint governs the design: *"we want to fine tune this model so it
learns 'The mechanics of Darkest Dungeon and the importance of certain numbers'
rather than making a player that follows the same plan always, as this wont
enable the use of modded classes and will hurt our scalability on the future"*.

So a family is what a comp CAN DO, read off its **equipped** kit's effects: a
party fielding Solo, Protect Me, Invigorating Vapours or Guard Dog is a dodge
party whoever is in it, including a modded class that appears in no list. Same
argument that removed the 20-class one-hot from the observation.

**A family means BUILT AROUND it, not merely capable of it.** At a threshold of
one skill, 144 of 163 comps are "dodge comps" and 130 are "bleed comps" — that
is not a focus, it is the pool. `FOCUS_MIN_SOURCES = 2` gives dodge 98, bleed
77, blight 40. Burn has only 7 comps that can do it at all and 2 that stack it,
so `FOCUS_MIN_POOL` relaxes a thin family to 1 rather than shipping a two-comp
"focus" that is really a smaller training set wearing a knob's clothes.

### The class guides are a RULER, and only a ruler

`tools/check_class_play.py` grades the policy against the 21 hand-written
per-class guides in `metadata/HEROES/Heroes/`. Fran's instruction is explicit:
*"only use this to verify and iterate"*. **Nothing in it may become a reward
term, an action mask, or a training target** — a policy that follows a per-class
script is precisely the un-scalable player he does not want, and it would break
the modded-class transfer the whole observation design exists for.

Baseline on the shipped checkpoint: Blinding Gas opens 26.8% of the combats
where it is legal on her first turn and is chosen on 12.2% of her legal turns;
the dodge buffs are cast on 16.0% of the turns one is legal; 12.5% of DoT
applications land on a target already dying to one.

## Sustain is a property of the KIT, and the search could not reach it (2026-08-26)

Fran hand-upgraded two GA comps and both beat the originals they were built
from. Measured paired, 64 episodes, the Weald at Champion/medium, identical
dungeon lists, **same classes in the same ranks -- the kit is the only thing
that changed**:

| | score | stress | deaths | HP | one-offs |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| GA wealdban 01 | 71.6 ± 3.2 | 39.1 | 0.42 | 68% | 0 |
| **Waiting Blade: Volley** | **89.1 ± 1.4** | **11.9** | **0.06** | 83% | 7 |
| GA weald3 03 | 88.2 ± 1.2 | 21.0 | 0.03 | 88% | 0 |
| **Hex Battery: Faith** | **93.5 ± 0.7** | **14.0** | **0.02** | 96% | 5 |

**+17.5 ± 3.5** and **+5.3 ± 1.4.** The first is the largest effect anything in
this project has measured, fidelity fixes included, and it comes from trinkets
and quirks alone.

His claim, and the measurement agrees: *"having arbalest with both CC trinkets
and crusader with healing trinkets and quirks should enhance tremendously the
sustain of the overall comps, this shows that you can get vestal-like healing
trinkets without vestal"*.

**This sharpens the Vestal finding rather than replacing it.** The earlier
measurement said she lifts the mean by 13.6 and the ceiling by 1.3. The sharper
statement is that **SUSTAIN** lifts both, and sustain has two routes -- a healer
class, and a kit. The search knows exactly one of them, which is why every
lineage converges on the same answer.

### Trinket SETS were unreachable by construction

`Trinket.set_id` is the game's own field and `CombatEngine` has counted worn
sets and applied their buffs since long before this
(`combat_engine.py:334`). All 20 classes can complete exactly one set -- the
Crimson Court pair for the vanilla eighteen, plus `duelist_set1`, `rw_set1`
and `sb_set1`. Fran's Arbalest wears the complete `cc_arb` pair (his
"Bedtime Story" resolves to `cc_set_arb_sharpshooters_string_wax`).

**The search had no concept of a set.** Its only pair operator redraws two
trinkets from ~150 wearable, so landing both halves is about **1 in 11,800**
per draw -- it had never assembled one and never would. And it could not
hill-climb to one either: a hero wearing HALF a set is strictly worse than one
wearing two good unrelated trinkets, so every path to a set goes downhill
first. That is exactly the argument `trinket_pair` was added on, one level up.

Fixed with a `trinket_set` operator -- both halves at once or nothing -- plus
an explicit offer once per hero per round inside `polish_trinkets`, since two
random draws per hero per round would never see it. `trinket_set` takes its
0.06 weight from `trinket` and `trinket_pair`, which is where the redundant
draws were.

**The patch was written while a search was running and applied only after it
finished.** A `ProcessPoolExecutor` worker that respawns mid-run re-imports the
module, so editing it underneath a run risks half the generations being scored
by different code -- the same discipline as "no engine edits mid-A/B".

### Promotion is a `mv`, and it happened twice

Both upgrades went into `curated_comps/`; the pool moved 158 → 160 and the size
tripwires fired both times, which is their job. `Hex Battery: Faith` is now the
highest-scoring comp in the whole pool at **92.6**, above every GA proposal from
any round -- **the hand-upgrade beat the machine.** Fran's own caveat is worth
keeping beside that: *"the upgrade is not always optimal"*. A promoted comp is
his current best guess, not a gold standard to fit the search to.

## The one-off trinket rule was a MISREADING, and the Vestal is a convergence artefact (2026-08-26)

Two corrections from Fran's round-3 review, and the second one answers a
question this project had been treating as a fidelity problem.

### The trinket rule is PER ID, which is the game's own

`UNIQUE_TRINKETS_PER_COMP = 1` — "at most one `limit`-bearing trinket across all
eight slots" — was recorded on 2026-08-22 as *"stricter than the game's per-id
rule, and his call"*. It was not his call. Shown a comp of his own wearing three
different Ancestrals, he said:

> *"the problem is not 1 ancestral trinket per comp, rather several copies of
> the same ancestral trinket"*

You own one Ancestor's Candle, so one hero wears it — and another may still wear
Ancestor's Map at the same time. The cap was over-restrictive for four months of
searching and it cost the space its strongest gear. His own upgraded comp,
`Hex Battery: Faith`, wears **five** different one-offs.

Three changes, and the third is what actually opened the space:

- `enforce_trinket_budget` now caps each trinket **id** at its own `limit`
  (420 trinkets carry 1, two carry 2 and 3, 193 carry 0 = unlimited), rather
  than capping the comp. `duplicate_one_offs` reports violations.
- `grant_one_off` is a **floor**, not a ceiling: it seeds one so the search
  starts with strong gear in hand.
- **`draw_trinket` can now offer a one-off at all** (`ONE_OFF_DRAW_RATE`, 0.20).
  It could not before, because the comp was capped at one and `grant_one_off`
  placed it — so no amount of searching could ever build a party *around*
  several Ancestrals. Comps now carry 1-5 of them, none duplicated.

**Order matters in that draw and one ordering broke a contract.** Putting the
one-off roll ahead of the synergy prior made `--synergy-bias 1` stop meaning
"every draw is a synergy trinket", which is what keeps the pre-2026-08-22 arm
reproducible. The order is synergy → one-offs → class pool → uniform: matching
the kit's own payload is a stronger reason to wear a trinket than "it is boss
gear".

**And `random_genome` was hand-rolling a subset of `finish`.** Harmless while
the ordinary draw could not produce a one-off; a duplicate-Ancestral generator
the moment it could. It goes through `finish` now, which is the single place
every comp-level invariant lives.

### The Vestal lifts the MEAN by 13.6 and the CEILING by 1.3

Every emitted comp fielding a Vestal was read here as either a true property of
the game or a fidelity problem. It is neither. Measured over the curated pool in
the Weald, Champion, medium:

| | comps | mean | best |
|---|---|---|---|
| with a Vestal | 22 (14%) | 74.2 | 86.6 |
| without | 136 (86%) | 60.6 | 85.3 |

She appears in **45% of the top 20 and 0% of the bottom 20** — and the best
Vestal-free comp is **1.3 behind** the best Vestal comp, inside the noise.

**So she is the reliable local improvement, not the ceiling.** A hill-climbing
search converges on whatever raises the mean, so every lineage finds her in the
first generations and never leaves; the other basin is just as high and much
narrower to reach. That is a property of the SEARCH, not of the simulator, and
it is why the answer is a ban list rather than a fidelity investigation.

### `--ban-class`, and why it is a search strategy

`--ban-class vestal,occultist,...` removes classes from the space outright.
Fran's method, and it is sequential: emit a comp, ban the four classes it used,
search again. Each round is forced into an unexplored part of the roster, so a
BATCH covers the roster instead of recombining the same six strong classes.

- Enforced in `CompSpace.classes`, and repaired by `enforce_ban` **first** in
  `finish` — a banned hero is not a hero whose rank or kit is worth repairing.
- **A curated seed fielding a banned class is DROPPED, not repaired**:
  repairing keeps three quarters of a comp the search was told not to explore.
- The ban list is validated against the real class list, like
  `--hold-out-class`: a typo would ban nothing and the run would look like a
  successful experiment.

## Stress weighs 2:1 in the SEARCH, and only in the search (2026-08-26)

Fran's call, after the first run came home at **38.8 mean stress against 19.8
for his own top eight**, level on HP — and the ranking could barely see it,
because quality reads stress against the 200 ceiling and so a 19-point gap is
worth about 4.7 points of score, under the search's own noise. He chose "weight
stress 2:1 over HP" over rescaling to the 100 Resolve Check, which keeps the
shape of the metric and changes only what it cares about.

**`eval_expedition._run_quality` keeps the declared 50/50.** Every archived
QUALITY number was measured on it, and changing it would silently restate all
of them. The weighting is therefore a property of the **consumer**, which is
only possible because the two halves are now carried unmixed:
`run_quality_components` returns `(hp, calm)` already averaged over the roster,
`ExpeditionOutcome.quality_parts` carries them per episode,
`RolloutResult.weighted_run_scores(w)` / `weighted_qualities(w)` re-read them,
and `SEARCH_STRESS_WEIGHT` (2/3) is where the search asks for its own.

Three rules that must not be regressed:

- **`weighted_run_scores(0.5)` must reproduce `run_scores` exactly.** That is
  what makes the change inert for every other caller, and a test pins it.
- **The reported `qual` column is re-weighted too.** `score` is the mean of
  `quality × progress`; printing the 50/50 quality beside a 2:1 score shows a
  product that does not multiply out.
- **A dead hero contributes zero to BOTH halves.** The denominator stays the
  roster, not the survivors — otherwise raising the stress weight would make
  "let the stressed hero die" a better move, which is the exact failure the
  roster denominator exists to prevent.

An outcome carrying no components falls back to the mixed `quality`, so an
older pickled result still reads.

## Fran's round-2 review: the search built the comp in the wrong ORDER (2026-08-26)

He reviewed the eight emitted comps and the verdict was the same shape as round
1 — the SPACE was wrong, not the ranking — but this time one of his notes was a
description of a pipeline bug he could not have seen the code for:

> *"it feels the model chose the trinkets first before having the skill loadout
> and base comp ready, the order should be 1º comp + 1-2-3-4 slots, 2º skills
> for the comp, 3º Trinkets+Quirks"*

That is exactly what the code did. Before this change:

| stage | what it searched |
|---|---|
| 1 | classes and ranks, with the whole kit **redrawn every episode** so skills are averaged out |
| 2 restage | **best of `--restage-kits` (6) whole-party random kits** — the only moment skills are ever chosen |
| 2 order | all 24 orderings, exhaustively |
| 2 trinkets | `--polish` rounds of hill-climbing |

A four-hero party has ~35⁴ ≈ **1.5 million** skill combinations. Keeping the
best of six draws is indistinguishable from drawing at random, and skills were
never touched again — while trinkets got dedicated hill-climbing. The build
order is now his: **ranks → skills → trinkets**, both in `CompSpace.finish` and
in the polish loop, where it is a *dependency* rather than a preference (which
rank a hero stands in decides which skills it can perform; which skills it
performs decides which trinkets can fire).

### 23% of equipped skills could not be used from the hero's own rank

The measurement that made the rest of it concrete. Over the eight emitted
comps, **30 of 128 equipped skills (23%)** launched from no rank their hero
could reach, 22 of them on heroes with no self-move in their own kit:

- **Hand of Light on a rank-4 Vestal in four of the eight comps.** Fran named
  this one by eye: *"can't be used at all from the positions that we'll try for
  her"*.
- Comp #6 had **7 of 16 dead** — its rank-1 Houndmaster carried Hound's Rush
  *and* Lick Wounds, both launch-from-2/3/4. It ranked 3rd of the eight.

**The fitness cannot see this, and that is why it persisted.** The policy plays
whatever is legal, so a dead slot costs nothing directly — only the better skill
that could have been there, an opportunity cost smaller than the search can
resolve. Now `CompSpace.dead_skills` / `repair_dead_skills` remove it
statically, in `finish`, so it survives the `position` mutation and crossover.
Self-move is what makes an off-rank skill legal, as a **fixpoint**: Duelist's
Advance from rank 4 opens 3, then 2, then 1, which is what makes Point Blank
Shot legal on a back-rank Highwayman. Enemy pushes are deliberately not counted
— Fran's rule is that off-rank coverage matters in proportion to the comp's own
self-move and the rest is a surprise contingency that camping and scouting
mostly prevent.

### `viable_ranks`: the game's own rank rules, derived rather than written down

Repairing skills was not enough, because some of the offenders were not bad
loadouts but **impossible ones**: a Leper at rank 4 has one skill that launches
from there. A rank is viable for a class when at least `SKILLS_PER_HERO` of its
skills launch from it, and read off the shipped data that reproduces what every
player knows:

| | viable ranks |
|---|---|
| Arbalest, Musketeer | 3, 4 |
| Leper, Hellion, Crusader | 1, 2 |
| Vestal, Plague Doctor, Jester, Grave Robber | 2, 3, 4 |
| Shieldbreaker, Abomination, Highwayman | 1, 2, 3 |
| Houndmaster, Man-at-Arms, Occultist, Bounty Hunter, Flagellant, Antiquarian, Duelist, Runaway | all four |

That agreement is the check that the derivation measures the right thing. It is
enforced at the **structural** stage, where Fran puts ranks, and `repair_ranks`
prefers a **swap** over redrawing a class because a swap preserves the class
multiset — the comp's identity, and what the hall of fame keys on.

### Trinket coherence: the game states the condition, so it is a static check

> *"using ranged trinkets with a full melee highwayman doesnt make sense"*

Three buff `rule_type`s are decided by facts the comp has already fixed by the
time trinkets are chosen: `in_rank` (92 trinkets carry one), `rangedonly` (36)
and `meleeonly` (31). A trinket whose **every** buff is switched off by those
tests is a worn slot doing nothing — the same class of waste as an unperformable
skill, invisible to the fitness for the same reason. `trinket_is_dead` /
`repair_dead_trinkets` run last in `finish`, after ranks and skills are settled.

Deliberately not judged: `lightbelow`/`lightabove` (the torch is a property of
the run), `monsterType` (the region decides it — a region-pinned search *could*
judge it, filed rather than guessed), and unconditional buffs. And an unknown
buff id is **never** called dead: that means the loader missed something, not
that the trinket is inert.

### Two things I got wrong, and the hypothesis that failed

**The leaderboard's validity check was confounded, and it was published as
proof.** "Curated 63.0 vs random legal 24.2, gap +38.8" was offered as evidence
that the fitness measures comp quality. The shipped checkpoint trained on those
158 comps with the randomisation knobs **off**, so it has never seen any other
loadout or trinket in training; the random control is unfamiliar in every
dimension at once. The gap contains comp quality **and** policy familiarity and
that experiment cannot separate them. Fran's reading — *"we're just evaluating
which of the comps the model knows to use best"* — is the correct one. The clean
test, not yet run, is to re-measure the same gap on `knobs_20260825`, which
trained with randomised loadouts and kits.

**A low usage rate is not evidence against a skill.** `--explain` showed the
Vestal playing Illumination on 0.8% of turns and Hand of Light on 0.1%, and that
was read here as an indictment. Fran corrected it: niche skills are rare *by
design* — Battlefield Medicine matters against bleed and blight and in the stall
phase after Blinding Gas, Disorienting Blast against size-2 enemies and heavy
backline dealers. The indictment rests on the **swap deltas** and the **rank
legality**, not the usage table.

**FAILED HYPOTHESIS: region-pinning does not make skill choice measurable.**
The argument was good — a skill strong in one region and weak in three averages
to nothing, and Fran gives the examples (Adder's Kiss dead in the Weald's blight
resistance, Houndmaster bleeds good there, Smite worthless with no unholy). It
does not survive measurement. `--explain` on the same comp, same 64 episodes,
