pinned to the Weald: **sigma 1.3 against 1.4 for all four zones**, and the swap
deltas stayed inside 2σ. Region-pinning is still worth doing on Fran's authority
— a comp for one region is a different object from a compromise — but not on
this argument, and it does not rescue the skill search.

**What replicated instead:** *Guard Dog* is the top-ranked swap in both runs
(+2.9pp all-zones, +2.6pp Weald), the only one to clear 2σ in either. Fran had
independently named it the comp's biggest miss, with the mechanism — a
dodge-built Houndmaster guarding the front rank dodges nearly everything. The
instrument and the expert agreeing on one specific call, from different
directions, is worth more than either alone.

**So the constraints are doing the real work and the skill hill-climb can only
choose between options that are already sensible.** A skill swap is worth 1-3pp
against a ~1.3pp standard error at 64 episodes; resolving 1pp would need roughly
seven times the episodes per candidate, which the polish budget cannot pay.
`polish_skills` therefore requires a margin of `SKILL_STEP_SIGMAS` (1.0)
standard errors before taking a step: the max of ~48 candidates that are all
inside the noise is about two sigma of upward noise every round, and that
winner's curse is what would make the stage actively harmful rather than merely
weak.

## The comp search ranks the OBJECTIVE now, and the scenario probe was measuring the wrong half (2026-08-26)

Three changes, one finding each. All of them are about the comp layer; none
touches `engine/`.

### 1. The fitness was not the objective, and quality alone is exploitable

`Score.fitness()` ranked on **SUCCESS** — completed every combat AND nobody
died — with stress reaching the ranking only through a shaping term
deliberately bounded *below one episode*. Fran's stated objective since
2026-08-24 is finish the expedition, nobody dead, everyone at zero stress,
which is exactly what `eval_expedition._run_quality` already computes and what
`bridge/rollout.py` was discarding.

**Quality on its own is worse than SUCCESS, not better.** `_run_quality` scores
the party's state at episode end *unconditionally*, so an episode that **stalls
out at combat 2 of 14** with everyone healthy scores ~90 — above a party that
cleared all fourteen and came home at half HP. A search ranking on raw quality
would have selected comps for their ability to do nothing safely.

So the ranked quantity is gated on progress, per episode:

```
run_score = (quality / 100) * progress
progress  = 1.0                            if the expedition completed
            combats_reached / num_combats  otherwise
```

A wipe is already 0 (every hero dead ⇒ quality 0). A retreat does not count as
completion, so a retreat-heavy run is capped by how far it got.
`tests/bridge/test_rollout.py::test_a_healthy_stall_out_must_score_below_a_bloodied_completion`
asserts the trap and the gate in the same function.

**The exploit exists in the real comp folder, not just in the test.** Fran's
own "Money Quartet" — four Antiquarians, a gold-farming comp — reads
**quality 54.1 with progress 19.8%** over 64 episodes: one death, the
survivors at 59% HP, and it never finishes an expedition. Its `run_score` is
**9.0**, last of 158. On raw quality it would have outranked nothing useful and
still sat mid-table among comps that clear all fourteen fights. `byZone` says
6-11% everywhere, so this is not a zone effect.

Consequences that must not be regressed:

- **The per-episode values are carried, not their means.** `RolloutResult` now
  holds `qualities` and `progresses` as tuples. A `Score` is built from one
  `RolloutResult` per zone and a mean-of-means is wrong the moment two zones
  run different episode counts — and the spread is needed anyway, because
  `run_score_se` is a **sample SD over √n**, not the binomial form. The
  existing `standard_error` is still the binomial SE and is still SUCCESS's.
- **`by_zone` carries the RANKED quantity at index 1.** `emit._best_zone` picks
  the comp's `location` tag off `max(by_zone, key=…[1])`, and that field is
  what `ExpeditionEnv` routes by — tagging the zone by one metric while
  ordering the comps by another makes the label disagree with the ranking. The
  success rate rides along at index 3 so `zone_line` can still print both.
- **The shaping term still has a job and was kept.** `run_score` is 0 for any
  total wipe however far the party got, so `combats_frac` inside the shaping is
  what orders two total failures. Its bound genuinely weakens for a continuous
  headline — `success` is a rate, so 1/N was the smallest possible gap and the
  bound was absolute; no bound can be absolute now. The test was renamed to
  what it can still guarantee: a gap of **one episode's worth** of headline can
  never be overturned by shaping.
- **`score_structure` and `score_genome` were two hand-copied weighted means.**
  They are now one `_score_from` over one `_WMEAN_ATTRS` tuple, with a test
  that every name in it is a real `RolloutResult` field — a typo there would
  have contributed 0.0 forever, silently.

### 2. Fran's own comps beat random legal ones by 28.8 points — the fitness is valid

New `tools/comp_leaderboard.py`. It scores every comp in the trainable pool on
one scenario, paired with the search (same `Scenario`, same `--seed`, so a
curated comp and a GA candidate face the identical dungeon list), and it exists
for two reasons — the bar, and a **validity check on the fitness**.

The check is the point. These are comps Fran has played in the real game and
vouches for; if they do not beat comps drawn at random from the same legal
space the search draws from, the fitness is not measuring what makes a comp
good and no amount of search fixes that. Measured on the shipped checkpoint,
tier 3 long, four zones, 16 episodes per zone — **158 curated comps against 24
random legal ones, same scenario and same seed**:

| | run score |
|---|---|
| curated (n=158) | **84.1** (median 85.8, min 9.0, max 94.9) |
| random legal (n=24) | 55.3 |
| gap | **+28.8 ± 4.1** |

**155 of 158 curated comps beat the random mean.** Between-comp SD is 10.2
against a mean within-comp SE of 1.4, so the ruler resolves real differences
between them at that episode count. PASS, and by a margin nobody needs to
squint at.

### 3. The scenario probe ranked corners on the population the search LEAVES

`--scenario-probe` has been required "after any retrain" since 2026-08-22
because a corner where the policy always wins or always loses ranks nothing.
It probed **random candidates only** — which is what generation 0 looks like,
and is exactly half the run. A search *converges* into the curated region, so a
corner that ranks random comps beautifully and then saturates on good ones
ranks noise for its last generations, which are the generations that decide
what gets emitted.

That is not hypothetical. On the shipped checkpoint the two arms **invert**:

| corner | random SD | top-24 curated SD | headroom above the best curated comp |
|---|---|---|---|
| tier 3 medium | 9.8 | 1.2 | 3.0 |
| tier 3 long | **19.0** | 1.7 | 4.0 |
| tier 5 medium | 13.0 | 7.1 | 11.6 |
| tier 5 long | 4.6 | **12.9** | 19.1 |

The random arm names tier 3 long by a wide margin. On good comps tier 3 long
collapses to an SD of 1.7 with 4.0 points of room left above the best comp in
the pool — a search told to beat the curated bar there is trying to resolve a
gap smaller than five times its own error bar.

Fixed: the probe scores **both** arms, normalises each by **its own** mean
standard error (a spread smaller than the error bar is not a spread, and the
two arms carry very different error bars at the same episode count — a comp
that finishes every run has almost no variance, one that dies halfway has a
great deal), and ranks a corner by the **worse** of the two. It also prints
`head`, the distance from the best curated comp to a perfect 100.

Re-run with a representative curated sample rather than the top 24, it names
**tier 5 medium** (worse-arm SD/SE 4.2, headroom 20.1) — which is the corner
the tool has defaulted to since 2026-08-21 and which the 2026-08-24 note
declared dead. It was dead **on SUCCESS**: 11.6% there, a floor. On `run_score`
it reads 26.0 random / 60.0 curated and separates both arms. That is the
clearest single argument for the fitness change: the corner did not change, the
ruler did.

**Every comp score taken before 2026-08-26 is a different ruler**, twice over —
the fitness changed, and Fran re-exported `curated_comps/` the same day (100
comps added, 94 deleted, the pool 152 → 158).

### The search MATCHED the top of the pool and did not beat it — and it is worse on stress

Run 2026-08-26: tier 5 medium, all four zones, 32 candidates x 12 generations,
`--emit 8`, `--model-b knobs_20260825.zip`. Scored back against all 158 curated
comps on the identical scenario and seed (`comp_leaderboard --extra`, so the two
populations face the same dungeon lists):

| | run score |
|---|---|
| best curated (`Hound Pack: Faith`) | **89.2 ± 1.3** |
| best proposal (`GA_strongest_01`) | 87.9 ± 1.0 |
| paired gap | **−1.2 ± 1.6 — inside the noise** |

The proposal sits at the **98.7th percentile** (beats 156 of 158) and is level
with the top of the pool. It does not exceed it. State that plainly rather than
quoting the percentile alone.

**The proposals are systematically worse on STRESS, which is the criterion Fran
actually named.** Over the 8 proposals against the top 8 curated comps:

| | stress | deaths | final HP |
|---|---|---|---|
| top-8 curated | **19.8** | **0.076** | 85% |
| 8 proposals | 38.8 | 0.234 | 85% |

**That is the ruler, not the search.** `_run_quality` scores stress against the
**200 ceiling**, so the 19-point stress gap above is worth only ~4.7 points of
quality — less than the search's own generation-to-generation noise. The 200
denominator is deliberate and documented (the reward's `combat_end_condition`
clamps at 100, and a ruler must not go blind where the runs it grades end up),
so this is a **weighting decision for Fran**, not a bug: if "everyone home at
zero stress" is to be selected for, the quality weights have to say so. Recorded
rather than changed — changing the objective on his behalf is exactly the thing
the comp layer's review loop exists to prevent.

**Every proposal fields a Vestal**, and 9 of the 32 slots are Vestals. That is
the healer clustering the food/district work already explained: in-combat
healing is 48.4% of all HP restored over a long run, against 4.7% from camp and
4.7% from food. The search is rediscovering a structural fact about the
simulator, which is a point in its favour and also a reason not to read
"8 comps, 8 Vestals" as a preference.

**The `--model-b` column is worth reading.** Seven of the eight score lower on
`knobs_20260825.zip` than on the headline model, one by **−19.2**
(`GA_strongest_07`, occultist / musketeer / houndmaster / vestal). That is the
"easy for one policy" failure the flag exists to catch; `GA_strongest_08` is the
only one that holds (+0.6), and `GA_strongest_01` and `06` are the next most
robust (−2.8, −3.2).

### The re-export, and what it broke in the tests

Twenty-eight tests failed on it, and every one had pinned a comp by **writing
its name down**. A re-export deletes names — the old ones are not even kept as
aliases: `"Holy Hymn: Twin Heist"` and `"Dark Ritual: Heist"` both resolve to
`None` through `comp_loadout_by_name`. Fixed by resolving comps by **shape**:
`tests/_fixtures.py::comp_with_classes(*hero_classes)` returns a loadout
fielding exactly that class multiset, exact matches before supersets, sorted
for determinism, raising rather than returning `None`. The class multiset is
what those tests were ever about; the name was an implementation detail of the
export.

Four that were not renames, and are worth keeping separate:

- **`test_threat_forecast.py` built its board by hoping the sampler offered
  one.** It reset an expedition at a fixed seed and asserted properties of
  `swine_wretch_A`; a different comp pool draws a different scenario from the
  same seed. It constructs the board directly now. A test about one monster
  must not depend on the sampler ever having drawn it.
- **`test_ablate_forecast.py` found a saturating feature, not an inert one.**
  SECURED is `_clamp01(heal throughput / incoming damage)`, so any party whose
  healer out-paces the board reads 1.0 before *and* after the intervention —
  which the re-export made the common case. Exempted, but only that index
  (derived from the layout constants, never written down) and only after
  checking it did not leave the ceiling either.
- **`test_fires_edge.py` was pinning a rule that no longer exists.** Its claim
  was that a Fire's Edge comp is *unreachable* from `pick_representative_comps`
  — true when those comps had no CSV row. The CSV was retired on 2026-08-23 and
  that function now delegates to `build_comp_pool`, so they are drawn like any
  other comp. Rewritten to pin what is still true and still worth pinning: the
  eval and training see **one** population, not two.
- **A Workshop mod could crash the whole hero load.** `HeroLoader._build_hero`
  did `SkillType(rec.get("type", "melee"))`, which raises on a `.type` the enum
  does not know. Vanilla ships none; a newly-subscribed class (`iriscrewmate`)
  writes `.type ""`. It falls back to MELEE now — a discovered mod must never
  be able to break the load, the same contract that keeps it from shadowing a
  shipped id.

## Where the remaining headroom is: `check_playbook` on the ship candidate (2026-08-25)

`models/forecastv2_20260825.zip`, 36 episodes x 12 comps, seed 1.

### PLAYBOOK 3.3 — recovery on a secured kill: **17.2% → 34.5%**

With every living enemy stunned and the party needing recovery, the policy now
spends **34.5%** of those turns healing, against 17.2% before this line of work.
Doubled. Still not "most of them", which is what the expert says, and 45.5% of
those turns are still attacks.

This is the largest behavioural movement measured, and it is the behaviour the
threat forecast was supposed to enable. It is consistent with the forecast's
+6.2pp being real rather than a scoring artifact.

### Fights are SHORT, which bounds the whole stall discussion

    combats=288   rounds: mean 2.9  median 2  p90 5  max 21
    lasting >3 rounds: 23.6%   >5: 7.6%   >8: 4.2%

Three quarters of combats are over in three rounds. That bounds how much the
stall-to-recover play can ever be worth — and it is the most likely reason
removing `RECOVERY_TURN_BUDGET` did not visibly move anything on its own: the
opportunity is rare. `PLAYBOOK 3.5`'s point about buffs needing long fights
lands the same way.

Worth holding against the +5.5 discounted return that a one-off 30-stress
relief was measured to be worth: the prize is real, the *occasions* are not.

### Two live defects, both policy rather than reward

**Corpse attacks cost kills.** A corpse is on the board on 35.2% of hero turns
and blocks a living enemy on 60.3% of those. The policy attacks one on 10.2% of
the turns it could — and in **39.1% of those a living enemy within the same
skill's reach would have died to that hit** (conservative: `dmg_min` before
modifiers, so the kill was never in doubt). 59 kills traded for corpse damage.

**Unjustified heals persist, and the reward is not at fault.** n=278, target at
a median 82% HP, 54% above 80%, mean 3.6 HP actually restored. Realised step
reward: unjustified **−0.0635** against justified **+0.2183** — a −0.28 gap. The
reward *can* tell them apart and prices them correctly, so the policy is
leaving reward on the table. Per the tool's own reading, more training on this
reward is the fix, not a reward change.

### What the policy does RIGHT

**It avoids doomed targets.** With an enemy already dying to its own DoT, only
41.6% of attacks are aimed at it, against **56.4% for an indifferent
attacker** — actively steering away. That is what `_is_doomed` exists for and
it is working.

## The knobs buy robustness and cost deployment — both measured (2026-08-25)

The five randomisation knobs were argued for **generalisation to parties the
policy has not seen**. The standard eval cannot test that: it presents every
comp with its own real loadout, which is the narrow deployment distribution.
So the −8.6pp they cost there answered a different question from the one they
were built for.

`tools/compare_arms.py --rand-loadout 1.0` asks the intended one. Both arms get
identical random 4-of-7 loadouts, drawn at the call site from the scenario seed
(see the unreachable-knob note in `CLAUDE.md` for why the env knob cannot do
this). `knobs_20260825` against `forecastv2_20260825`, 608 paired long-band
scenarios:

| | real loadouts (deployment) | random loadouts (robustness) |
|---|---|---|
| SUCCESS | 70.4% vs **78.9%** — **−8.6pp**, p<0.0001 | **57.6%** vs 52.6% — **+4.9pp**, p=0.0224 |
| QUALITY | 69.1 vs **73.8** — −4.77 | 60.5 vs 61.6 — −1.10, CI [−3.65, +1.44] |
| deaths/run | 0.590 vs **0.383** | 0.860 vs **0.798** |

**The sign flips.** The knobs were not a mistake; they optimise a different
distribution, and which one is right depends on what the model is for.

### Three things in the numbers worth reading

- **Success up while deaths are also up** (0.860 vs 0.798). SUCCESS requires
  zero deaths, so the knobs arm has *more clean runs AND worse disasters* — a
  bimodal outcome, which is why QUALITY nets to nothing. Neither metric alone
  says this; they have to be read together.
- **Both arms crater** under random loadouts: ~79% → 57.6%/52.6%. A random
  legal 4-of-7 is worth about **25pp less** than the curated choice. That is
  independent evidence that the curated comps' skill selections carry real
  value, measured without reference to anyone's judgement about them.
- **QUALITY and SUCCESS disagree here**, and that is the second time tonight.
  The first was the ablation, where a quality CI nearly produced the opposite
  conclusion. Read both, always.

### What to do with it

**Keep the knobs off for a shipping checkpoint.** Fran plays curated comps with
their real loadouts; that is the deployment distribution and the knobs cost
8.6pp on it.

**Turn them on if the goal changes** to advising on arbitrary or experimental
parties — which is a plausible future direction, and now has a number attached
rather than an argument.

## P(death) is null — and so is every refinement of the forecast (2026-08-25)

`models/pdeath_20260825.zip` — 3M, same flags, observation 1636 → 1644 for
`p_deaths_door` and `p_death`. Against `forecastv2_20260825` on the identical
608 long-band scenarios:

| | forecastv2 | pdeath |
|---|---|---|
| SUCCESS | 78.9% | **78.9%** |
| QUALITY | 73.8 | 73.3 |
| deaths/run | 0.383 | 0.38 |

Indistinguishable. The feature is well-motivated — `time_to_secure` showed the
mean forecast reads 100% safe at round 0 on every tier, so it genuinely could
not express "this hero might die" — and the tail computation is correct
(verified 0.0000 healthy / 0.23–0.27 at 3 HP / 0.075–0.090 at Death's Door).
It just does not change what the policy does.

### The pattern across the night, which is the real finding

| change | result |
|---|---|
| the threat forecast itself | **+6.2pp, p=0.0010** |
| crit stress (stress calibration 1.97 → 1.07) | null, p=0.51 |
| P(death) / P(Death's Door), the tail | null |

**The coarse signal is what paid; every refinement of it is null.** The policy
appears to extract "there is danger here, aimed roughly there" and to be
insensitive to precision below that. Two independent refinements — fixing a 2×
scale error, and adding a quantity the mean structurally cannot represent —
both moved nothing.

So: **stop refining the forecast.** The remaining headroom is elsewhere.

### A structural limit worth recording

Widening the observation makes the previous checkpoint **unloadable**, so a new
feature can never be tested by comparing against the previous generation —
`compare_arms` died on `Unexpected observation shape (1644,) ... please use
(1636,)`.

`--blind-forecast` works only because it zeroes at **source**, keeping the
width identical. That is the general method: **to test a new feature, train a
control at the SAME width with only that feature zeroed.** Comparing across a
width change is not a comparison at all.

The fallback used here — band aggregates over identical `_cell_scenario` seeds
— is valid and cheap, but it cannot give a per-scenario McNemar, so it can only
resolve large effects. It was enough to call a null.

## The five randomisation knobs cost 8.6pp at 3M (2026-08-25)

`models/knobs_20260825.zip` — 3M, seed 0, 12 envs, `--hard-focus`,
`--no-abandon-train`, plus **all five** randomisation knobs for the first time:
`--loadout-random-p 0.5 --kit-random-p 0.5 --cross-region-p 0.3
--resolve-sampling --boss-quest-p 0.1`.

Against `forecastv2_20260825` (identical but knobs off), paired over 608
long-band scenarios:

| | knobs | knobs off | delta |
|---|---|---|---|
| SUCCESS | 70.4% | **78.9%** | **−8.6pp** |
| QUALITY | 69.1 | **73.8** | **−4.77**, CI [−7.02, −2.53] |
| deaths/run | 0.590 | **0.383** | **+0.21** |
| McNemar | 42 / 94 / 472 tied | | **p < 0.0001** |

### This qualifies "train harder than you deploy", it does not refute it

The two changes that *did* pay under that banner — `--hard-focus` and
`--no-abandon-train` — **reweight the scenario**: harder bands, harder tiers,
and withholding an exit so the policy cannot opt out of the states it needs to
learn. The distribution of *parties* was untouched.

The five knobs instead **randomise the party and the routing**: a random legal
4-of-7 instead of the comp's own four, a random trinket pair and quirk sheet, a
comp routed to a region it was never built for, and 10% boss quests. At a fixed
3M budget that spends capacity on a much wider space, and the eval measures the
narrow one the model is actually deployed on.

So the distinction worth carrying: **reweighting toward hard scenarios helped;
randomising the party hurt.**

### What this does NOT establish

- **One seed, one budget.** Wider distributions plausibly need more than 3M.
  This says nothing about 6M or 10M.
- **Which knob.** All five were turned on together, so the damage is not
  attributed. `--cross-region-p` (a Cove comp in the Ruins) and
  `--kit-random-p` (which destroys trinket/skill synergy) are the natural
  suspects, and `--boss-quest-p 0.1` puts 10% of training on a fight the eval
  never presents.
- **Robustness.** The knobs arm was evaluated on the REAL distribution, which
  is the right test for shipping. It is *not* a test of whether that arm
  generalises better to unseen comps — which is the thing the knobs were
  argued for, and is Fran's actual goal of a model that plays any comp he
  picks. Evaluating both arms under a randomised eval env is the open
  follow-up.

**Do not turn the knobs on for a shipping run at this budget.** They remain
interesting for a robustness study with its own ruler.

## The threat forecast PAID: +6.2pp and 0.15 fewer deaths on the long band (2026-08-25)

The first observability change in this project that improved the policy rather
than merely reaching it. Two prior attempts — the mechanics-not-identity
rewrite and the hero capability profile — came back at +46.9pp and +46.6pp
excess gap, i.e. changed nothing.

### The control arm

Two 3M runs, identical seed, flags, `--hard-focus`, `--no-abandon-train` and
stall fix, differing **only** in whether the 19 forecast features are visible.
`--blind-forecast` zeroes them at their **source**, so the observation width,
every other index and the action space are untouched.

Paired over 608 long-band scenarios, **each arm evaluated in the env it was
trained for** (`tools/compare_arms.py`):

| | sighted (v2) | blind | delta |
|---|---|---|---|
| SUCCESS | **78.8%** | 72.5% | **+6.2pp** |
| QUALITY | **73.8** | 69.2 | **+4.57**, CI [+2.39, +6.74] |
| deaths/run | **0.385** | 0.539 | **−0.154** (−29%) |
| McNemar | 83 / 45 / 480 tied | | **p = 0.0010** |

Standalone all-comps shows where it does **not** act: short 93.9 vs 93.8,
medium 92.1 vs 91.1 — indistinguishable. Long is 78.9 vs 72.5 with stress 34.8
vs 41.0. That is the right shape: those bands are near-saturated and a
one-round lookahead cannot matter where the party is never under sustained
pressure.

### Calibrating the forecast's stress bought nothing

`v2` (crit stress included, calibration ratio 1.07 at Veteran) against `v1`
(which under-predicted stress ~2×), paired over the same 608:

    succ 78.9% vs 80.3% (−1.3pp) · quality +0.86 CI [−1.34, +3.06]
    McNemar 52 / 60 / 496 · p = 0.5085

Indistinguishable — and v1 was the **handicapped** arm (see the confound
below). The likely reading is that the policy uses the forecast *relatively*
rather than on its absolute scale, so a 2× error in one component barely moves
the decision. Same shape as the food constant: **correct on fidelity grounds,
null on outcome.** Keep it, do not quote it as an improvement.

### Two measurement bugs, both caught by instruments disagreeing

**1. The comparators ran full 7-skill movepools.** `eval_expedition` resolves
each comp's real 4-of-7 loadout and passes it; `play()` in `compare_arms` and
`ablate_forecast` did not. Both arms shared the flaw, so the comparison was
internally valid while measuring the wrong world — and it **diluted** the
effect, because a party holding all seven skills has more ways to react and
less need to see danger coming. The first paired run read +3.0pp (p=0.14)
against +6.4pp from the standalone evals on nominally identical scenarios;
that disagreement is what exposed it. Fixed; the same run then read +7.7pp.

*Internally valid is not the same as measuring the intended distribution.*

**2. `forecast_20260825` was evaluated off-distribution.** It finished at 01:40;
crit stress landed at 03:19 and roughly doubled the forecast's stress outputs.
Every evaluation of that checkpoint afterwards fed it numbers it had never
seen, so its +7.7pp is **confounded** — probably understated, since
off-distribution input usually hurts, but "probably" is not a sign. The clean
+6.2pp above uses `v2`, which trained on the code it was evaluated with.

This is why the ablation's earlier `p=0.0132` is **superseded rather than
re-run**: the control arm is the definitive form of that experiment and the
ablation was only ever its proxy.

### What to ship

`models/forecastv2_20260825.zip` — tied with v1 on outcome, and the only
sighted checkpoint whose training distribution matches the current code.

## The forecast retrain: used, p=0.0132 — and a small gain nobody can attribute yet (2026-08-25)

`models/forecast_20260825.zip` — 3M, seed 0, 12 envs, `--hard-focus`,
`--no-abandon-train`, 1h02m. Two changes in one run: the threat forecast in the
observation (obs 1613 → 1636) and the removal of `RECOVERY_TURN_BUDGET`.

### The result

All-comps, 1,824 episodes, on the reproducible ruler:

| band | succ (base → new) | qual (base → new) | deaths |
|---|---|---|---|
| short | 94.4% → 93.9% | 86.6 → 85.4 | 0.06 → 0.09 |
| medium | 90.1% → **92.1%** | 83.0 → **84.2** | 0.19 → **0.13** |
| long | 76.0% → **78.9%** | 71.2 → **72.2** | 0.51 → **0.45** |

Retreats fell 4.5% → 3.1%. **No single band delta clears its own SE** (~1σ
each). What makes it more than nothing is that deaths and retreats fall on the
same bands quality rises on — independent measurements agreeing. Read it as
*probably a small real gain, 1–3pp on the long band*, not as an established
effect.

### The ablation says the features are READ

`tools/ablate_forecast.py`, written before the run: the same checkpoint
evaluated twice on identical paired scenarios, once with the nineteen forecast
indices zeroed.

| band | succ | blinded | discordant (sighted / blind) | p |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| short | 94.4% | 92.4% | 14 / 8 | 0.2863 |
| medium | 91.1% | 87.8% | 19 / 9 | 0.0872 |
| long | 76.3% | 72.7% | 36 / 25 | 0.2000 |
| **pooled** | | | **69 / 42** | **0.0132** |

This is the first observability change in this project that the policy
demonstrably uses. The mechanics-not-identity rewrite and the hero capability
profile both came back at +46.9pp and +46.6pp excess gap, i.e. changed nothing.

### Two things it does NOT establish, and they matter

- **Use is not value.** Zeroing is off-distribution: a policy can be hurt by
  nonsense input it would never have been *helped* by. The ablation proves the
  features reach the decision, not that a policy trained without them would be
  worse.
- **The run changed two things.** The gain is not attributable between the
  forecast and the stall-cap removal without a control arm — one more 3M with
  the forecast features present but permanently zeroed, everything else equal.

### A defect in the harness, found by reading its own output

The first version put a 95% CI on **quality** and left **success** as a bare
point estimate — and success is where the signal was. The auto-verdict
therefore printed "the policy learned to IGNORE the forecast" off a quality
interval of [−0.44, +2.19] while the success column showed +2.0/+3.3/+3.6pp in
the same direction on all three bands.

Success is a paired BINARY outcome and wants McNemar, which this project
already uses in `ab_compare` and `agreement`. Added, and the verdict inverted.

The two columns were never in conflict: a ~3pp success drop implies roughly
`0.03 × (85 − 30) ≈ 1.6` quality, against a measured +0.93 ± 1.32. **Quality is
simply not sensitive enough to resolve a 3pp success change at n=304** — worth
knowing before quoting a quality CI as evidence of no effect anywhere else.

## The all-comps eval was never reproducible, and `--all-comps` did not fix it (2026-08-24)

`reset(seed=)` reaches the **engine** RNG only. The torch level, which monsters
are drawn, and whether a quest is a boss quest all come from `_sampler_rng`,
which is built once in `__init__` and never reseeded — and
`eval_expedition.py` constructs its env with `sampler_seed=None`, i.e. OS
entropy.

Two runs of one command on one checkpoint, back to back, no code change between
them:

| band | run 1 | run 2 |
|---|---|---|
| short | 91.8% | **93.8%** |
| medium | 91.8% | 91.4% |
| long | 78.0% | 79.3% |

**The part that was not known** is that `--all-comps` does not fix this.
`CLAUDE.md` already warned that `--full-expedition` is not reproducible and that
`tools/ab_compare.py` exists because of it, and `--all-comps` was then
introduced to remove **comp sampling** error. It did exactly that and left
**scenario seeding** untouched — so it reads as the trustworthy ruler while
carrying ~2pp of invisible run-to-run error, which is in one way worse than the
flaw it replaced, because that one was documented.

Two numbers quoted the same day are inside it and must not be requoted: the
step-cap change at "+1.2pp overall" and the food change at "−0.2pp overall".

**Fix**: a `sampler_seed` reset option, which `eval_expedition` fills from each
cell's own `_cell_scenario` hash. Verified bit-for-bit identical across two runs
over 152 comps × 3 bands × 2 episodes.

**It is opt-in rather than derived from `seed`, and that is load-bearing.** Each
`SubprocVecEnv` worker is given `sampler_seed + i` precisely so the workers face
*different* scenarios; deriving the sampler from a per-episode `seed` would
collapse them into N copies of one episode — the exact narrowing `CLAUDE.md`
warns about under `--n-envs`. Training is byte-identical, and
`tests/training/test_eval_reproducible.py` pins that as well as the reproducibility.

## The QUALITY ruler: SUCCESS is a gate, not the goal (2026-08-24)

Fran restated the objective: "complete an expedition with all heroes at 0
stress, without anyone dying". `eval_expedition` measured half of it —
`success = completed and zero_death` — and printed `final_stress` only in a
per-cell detail block that `--all-comps` suppresses.

It had also run out of resolution. On the fixed ruler the shipped checkpoint
reads **94.4% short / 90.1% medium**, so two of three bands are saturated and
cannot show an improvement, while the state the party comes home in is
unmeasured and nowhere near its ceiling:

```
  short: succ 94.4%  qual 86.6   (stress 13.9  hp 81.9%  deaths 0.06)
 medium: succ 90.1%  qual 83.0   (stress 17.6  hp 77.4%  deaths 0.19)
   long: succ 76.0%  qual 71.2   (stress 35.2  hp 65.1%  deaths 0.51)
```

`_run_quality` scores each run 0–100 over the **roster**: per hero, 0 if dead,
else half for HP and half for stress. Three rules:

- **Averaged over the roster, not the survivors.** Averaging over survivors
  would let a policy raise its score by letting the most stressed hero die,
  which is the exact opposite of the objective. A dead hero costs its whole
  25-point share.
- **The three components print beside the composite**, because the weights are
  a declared convention. A composite that can hide a component moving the wrong
  way is how a heal metric broke a five-arm monotonic climb earlier this month.
- **Stress is scored against the 200 ceiling, not the 100 Resolve Check.** The
  reward's own `combat_end_condition` clamps at 100 and is therefore flat above
  it; a *ruler* must not go blind exactly where the runs it grades end up.

## The monster brain's weights are readable without drawing (2026-08-24)

`_select_enemy_skill` and `_select_enemy_target` each built a
`(candidates, weights)` vector and then threw it away on one `weighted_choice`.
Those weights are graded against the running game at **46/46** by
`tools/brain_oracle.py`, so they are the best available prediction of what an
enemy does next — and nothing could read them.

Split into a weighting half (`_enemy_skill_weights`, `_enemy_target_weights`)
and a thin drawing wrapper. The weighting half **draws nothing**: a redundant
roll would move the RNG stream and invalidate every trace comparison after it,
the same discipline `.dot_irresistible` follows by *skipping* its roll rather
than rolling against a zero resist. Pure refactor; `brain_oracle` unchanged.

`CombatEngine.threat_forecast()` is what it was for: per hero, expected damage,
expected **stress**, and P(targeted) for the coming round, through the hit rule
`tools/hit_oracle.py` validates at 155/155. `_threat_ratio`'s own docstring said
a precise answer "needs the monster brains" — this is that answer.

**`swine_wretch_A` is the case that shows the gap it closes**: a 1–1 damage band
and a single skill, `vomit`, that is really a stress attack. The forecast reads
0.24 HP and **2.55 stress** per hero; `_threat_ratio`, which knows only the
damage band, calls that board harmless. Stress is the currency the objective is
denominated in and nothing in the observation carried it.

Four declared simplifications are in the docstring. The one that matters: damage
is the **mean** of the band, so this is a forecast and not a guarantee — what
kills a hero at 12 HP is the top of the band times the crit multiplier.

`tests/engine/test_threat_forecast.py` replays three seeded episodes with and without
calling it and requires identical trajectories.

## Food was refused in exactly the state that predicts a death (2026-08-24)

### The diagnosis came first

32.5% of Champion long runs wipe, and the combat that takes the party's **first**
death is predictable from the state it is entered in — 50 runs, n=33 first
deaths:

| entering the combat | took the 1st death | took none |
|---|---|---|
| party HP | **66.3%** | **85.2%** |
| mean stress | 33.3 | 16.8 |
| worst hero | 56.5 | 34.1 |
| someone at Death's Door | 0.15 | 0.00 |
| surprised | 24.2% | 11.9% |
| threat ratio | 0.28 | 0.15 |

Every dimension separates about 2×, and the first death lands **29% through the
run** — early, so this is not end-of-run exhaustion. Surprise is an independent
draw the party cannot influence; the buffer it walks in with is the only thing
it can.

### The cause was an invented constant

`FOOD_ALLOWANCE_PER_COMBAT = 2.0` was a declared approximation of Fran's own
"it's like 4 food per 2 combats or so". At Champion it blocked **82.8%** of all
refused meal-stops — **46 of them with the party under 75% HP** — while the run
ended carrying **26.6 of its 46 food**. The party was denied food precisely when
it was in the state above.

An earlier hypothesis that the *hunger reserve* was the blocker was **wrong, and
the measurement said so**: the Mill disables hunger at Champion, so the reserve
is zero there and blocked nothing.

### The game ships its own limit

`rules.json max_provisions_before_full = 4` — a per-**hero** cap, not a rate,
and unparsed until now. `data/rules.py` and `data/types.py` read it and
`_deliberate_eat` bounds one stop at that many meals. Three real limiters remain
and none is invented: the ration itself, the hunger reserve below Champion, and
`EAT_TOPUP_FRACTION`.

### What it moved, and what it did not

Over ~750 combat entries at Champion long: the HP the party carries **into** a
fight went **79.5% → 85.4%** and food left **25.4 → 2.3**. That lands on the
85.2% the first-death table says separates a survivable fight from a fatal one.

It is kept on **fidelity** grounds — a shipped constant replacing a declared
convention, the same class as districts, burn and surprise — and explicitly not
on a win-rate claim. The all-comps SUCCESS deltas measured for it on the day
were taken on the un-reproducible ruler above and are not evidence either way.

## Withholding the exit during training is worth +23pp at Champion (2026-08-24)

`models/noabandon_20260824.zip` — 3M, seed 0, 12 envs, `--hard-focus`,
`--no-abandon-train`. Trained with `ACTION_ABANDON` masked off, **evaluated with
it enabled**. Best checkpoint this project has produced.

### The result

All-comps eval, 1,824 episodes:

| band | end-state | no-abandon-trained | delta |
|---|---|---|---|
| short | 89.5% | 92.8% | +3.3pp |
| medium | 85.4% | 88.7% | +3.3pp |
| **long** | **68.1%** | **77.0%** | **+8.9pp** |
| overall | 81.0% | 86.2% | +5.2pp |

Per-tier on the long band (60 episodes per tier), which is where it really shows:

| tier | SUCCESS (end-state → no-abandon) | abandoned | wiped |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 93.3% → **98.3%** | 6.7% → 0.0% | 0% → 1.7% |
| 3 | 71.7% → **90.0%** | 26.7% → 1.7% | 0% → 5.0% |
| **5** | **33.3% → 56.7%** | 65.0% → 11.7% | 0% → 23.3% |
| all | 66.1% → **81.7%** | 32.8% → 4.4% | 0% → 10.0% |

Abandon usage across the whole eval fell 16.3% → **3.7%**, and when it fires it
is now at 66% of the way through with **1.01 deaths already taken**. Deaths rose
0.08 → 0.25 per expedition, which is the honest price of playing on instead of
quitting — and the long band still gained 8.9pp net.

### Why it works, and what it says about the previous measurement

The end-state reward made a death cost most of a run and handed the policy a
legal exit, and it concluded — correctly, in the narrow sense — that the safest
way never to lose a hero is to leave. Over 180 long runs it recorded **zero
wipes, zero deaths, zero retreats**, and failed 32.8% of the time purely by
declining to play.

The paired counterfactual then said those abandons were individually correct:
**43 of 43 Champion-long abandons wiped when forced to play on.** That number
was real but it was a **lower bound, not a ceiling**, and the entry that
recorded it said so: the forced arm is off-distribution, because a policy
trained with an exit never learns the states past it. Withhold the exit during
training and the same runs become winnable — Champion long goes 33.3% → 56.7%.

**The general lesson is about the exit, not about abandonment.** Giving a policy
a safe terminal lets it opt out of exactly the states it most needs to learn.
Withhold it while training, restore it at deployment.

### A mislabelled number, corrected before it was quoted

The Champion counterfactual printed **"SUCCESS now 93%"**. That is
`1 - abandon_rate`, which silently counts wipes and truncations as wins. It read
as near-correct on the end-state checkpoint only because that one never wiped,
and was badly wrong the moment a checkpoint played on. The true figure is
**56.7%**. The line now prints "not-abandoned" and says what it excludes.

### What is left at Champion long

43.3% still fails, and it is no longer one thing: **23.3% wipe, 11.7% abandon,
8.3% truncate.** Truncation is new and is a pure loss (party alive, clock gone),
which makes the step budget worth a look. The death spiral also persists in the
wipes — first death at 33% through, wipe at 48%, median gap 2 combats, only 11%
in the same combat.

## The end-state objective: deaths fall 5x, and the heal metric finally breaks (2026-08-24)

The retrain that followed the reward redesign, and the first intervention this
project has made that moved every number it was aimed at.

`models/endstate_20260824.zip` — 3M, seed 0, 12 envs, `--hard-focus`, graded on
the 1,824-episode all-comps eval.

### What moved

| arm | deaths/expedition | old-succ (walked out alive) |
|---|---|---|
| food (districts on) | 0.39 | 84.0% |
| districts-trained | 0.49 | 80.6% |
| **end-state** | **0.08** | **92.4%** |

Per-band SUCCESS: short 89.5%, medium 85.4%, long 68.1% (overall 81.0%).

**The SUCCESS column is NOT comparable to the arms above it and must not be
quoted as if it were.** Three things changed under it at once: abandoned runs
count as failures by definition, the food rate limit made the game harder, and
the action space went 38 → 39 so no earlier checkpoint can even be loaded on
this ruler. The columns that *do* carry across are deaths and old-succ, and
both moved sharply. Quote those.

### Why the old reward produced the old behaviour

A 15-combat run paid `15 x 8 + 10 = 130`; a hero death cost `-20`. So a death
was **15% of a run**, and marching on with three heroes strictly beat turning
back. The policy learned exactly that, and the measured signature was the whole
long-band failure mode: the first death lands 48% of the way through, **2.07
more follow it**, and **81% of wipes happen two to three combats LATER** rather
than in the fight that took the hero. Sustain was spent as well — runs ended
with 2.7 of 46 food.

Nothing about that was a policy error. It was correct play under a reward that
priced progress above survival, which is what Fran said out loud: "If the model
is just focusing on progressing, it wont reach far."

### The heal metric broke its climb, and not by being targeted

Five arms of monotonic rise in unjustified heals — 51.9 → 57.9 → 59.7 → 65.0%
— through a reward fix, an observation feature, a 9x food increase and a full
districts package. This arm reads **58.6%**, and the composition is better
rather than merely smaller:

* stall/recovery heals 28.1% → **31.0%**
* PLAYBOOK 3.3 recovery spend 14.3% → **23.2%** (best measured here)
* total heals 16.0% → **12.3%** of hero turns

Worth recording *how* that happened: nothing targeted the heal behaviour. What
changed was **what winning means**. Four separate attempts to move this number
by acting on healing directly all failed; changing the objective moved it on
the first try. The standing note to treat `check_playbook` 3.2 as the
mismeasured thing should be re-read in that light — it may simply have been
measuring a policy optimising the wrong objective all along.

### Abandon: the collapse resolved itself with training

At 400k the gated policy abandoned ~65% of runs at 30% of the way in. The
trained checkpoint abandons **16.3%**, at **62% of the way through**, with
**0.36 deaths already taken** — i.e. it reserves the exit for runs that are
genuinely lost and pushes on otherwise.

That is the answer to the open worry recorded on the commit: the exit was not
too cheap, the policy was too weak to know better. **The gate was still
necessary** — ungated, a 400k probe abandoned 75% of runs a median 12% in at
74% party HP with 0.00 deaths and not one wipe — but no further tightening was
needed, and tightening it again on the strength of the 400k number would have
been tuning against a transient.

### The abandoned runs were genuinely lost — `tools/abandon_audit.py`

Answered the same day, and by counterfactual rather than by description: the
"62% of the way through with 0.36 deaths" figures *look* like cutting losses,
but they are equally consistent with a policy that has learned to bail on runs
it could have won. So the audit runs the same checkpoint twice on the same
seeds — once with abandon allowed, once with it masked off — and because
`deterministic=True` makes the policy a function of state, both arms produce
identical trajectories up to the moment the first one quits. The second then
plays on **from exactly that position**.

Over 300 episodes, 50 abandoned (16.7%, matching the eval's 16.3%, so the
population is representative):

| forced to play on | |
|---|---|
| **wiped** | **40 / 50 (80%)** |
| completed | 10 / 50 (20%) |
| deaths taken | **1.32 per run** |

Position at the moment it quit: **45.5% party HP, 47.7% through the run, 6.0
combats still to go**, 53.9 mean stress, 0.10 heroes already dead, 22 food
left. That is the situation the mechanic was argued for, found by the policy on
its own.

So four in five were lost, and quitting five runs trades one completion for
avoiding four wipes and ~5.3 hero deaths. Under this reward that is plainly
correct — and it is most of why deaths fell to 0.08.

**The 20% is the honest caveat**: one run in five was winnable, so there are
points on the table. Whether that trade is right depends on what a dead hero is
worth against a completed quest, which this simulator cannot say — there are no
quest rewards and no roster replacement cost modelled.

**A harness trap worth knowing, because it fails silently.** The first run of
this audit reported "100% of runs abandoned", every scenario long/Champion. The
cause was building a fresh env per episode with `sampler_seed=0`: `_sampler_rng`
restarts identically each time, so all 300 episodes drew the SAME comp, band and
tier. It produced entirely plausible-looking numbers from one repeated scenario.
Reuse the env and reseed the sampler per episode — the pattern
`tools/ab_compare.py` already uses, and now `tools/abandon_audit.py` too.

### Still open

The long band at 68.1% is the only place with headroom left; short and medium
are at ceiling and cannot resolve an improvement.

## Districts, and the food nobody could spend (2026-08-24)

Two changes that arrived together because measuring either one exposed the
other. Fran's ask; the town has always been out of scope, and for nine of the
nineteen districts that was the wrong call — **a district's payoff is not paid
in town.** They grant ordinary buff ids that `_apply_buff_to_stats` already
cashes, so the effect lands in combat and this simulator was fielding parties
without it.

### What it is worth

36 paired long/Champion scenarios (same comps, same seeds, same dungeon list),
on `models/headroom_20260824.zip` — a checkpoint that has never seen any of it,
so every number here is a **lower bound** on the trained effect:

| arm | SUCCESS | deaths | wipes | end HP | food left |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| none | 16.7% | 1.22 | 80.6% | 23.6 | 19.5 |
| district buffs only | 30.6% | 1.17 | 58.3% | 41.1 | 14.4 |
| full districts | 27.8% | 1.25 | 66.7% | 38.1 | **46.0** |
| buffs + eating | 27.8% | 1.03 | 55.6% | 44.2 | 8.2 |
| **full + eating** | **36.1%** | 1.08 | 58.3% | 46.6 | 2.7 |

Paired deltas vs none: buffs **+13.9pp** [+2.6, +25.2], full+eat **+19.4pp**
[+2.5, +36.3]. Only the first clears its interval at n=36; the rest are
directionally consistent and not separated. That is still the largest fidelity
effect this project has measured — for comparison, the eval's own noise is
~3pp and no single mechanic fix has ever moved more than ~5pp.

### The trap, and Fran's correction

Read literally, **"full districts" scored WORSE than "buffs only"** (27.8% vs
30.6%), because `the_mill` carries `DistrictDisableBuffData .disabled_type
"hunger"` and switching hunger off left the party carrying **46 of 46 rations
to the end of the run**. The first reading of that was that the Mill is a
tradeoff. It is not, and Fran said so: districts are overwhelmingly positive,
and *fewer hunger checks means more food for healing and camping* — which is
powerful, and more so with the Granary.

He was right, and the arm that proves it is `full+eat` vs `buffs+eat`: **36.1%
against 27.8%**. The Mill is a gain. What the "full is worse" row actually
measured was a **missing mechanic in this simulator**, not a property of the
district.

### The missing mechanic: nothing ever spent food on purpose

`FOOD_AMOUNTS` was corrected to the real 18/24/36 earlier the same day, and the
hunger check was implemented — but the check was food's **only** consumer. A
long run ended with 18.5 of 36 unspent, and a Champion run with all of it. The
thing a real player does between rooms — click food, heal the party — did not
exist.

Measured cost of that omission, over 24 long/Champion runs taking **345 HP** of
damage:

| source | HP restored | share of damage |
|---|---|---|
| in-combat healing | 167.1 | **48.4%** |
| camping | 16.1 | 4.7% |
| food (hunger check) | 16.1 | 4.7% |
| **camp + food** | **32.2** | **9.3%** |

So the simulator gave a party ~9% of its damage back through the two channels
that do not require a healer, and ~48% through the one that does. **That is
what made a dedicated healer structurally mandatory here**, and it is the real
source of the Holy Hymn clustering in the comp search — an adaptation to a
world where food did nothing, not a discovery about comps. Fran flagged exactly
this: players clear max difficulty deathless with no healing skills, on DODGE,
Guard, Block and food.

`_deliberate_eat` is the fix. One declared convention, `EAT_TOPUP_FRACTION`
(0.90) — no shipped key states when a player eats — with a reserve held back
for the expected remaining hunger checks, since starving costs 20% max HP
against the 5% a meal restores. Camping's own share is separately low because
long runs make **0.88 of their 2 campfires**; that is a scheduling defect and
is not fixed here.

### Two readings, both evidenced rather than declared

**`hero_type_tags: []` means EVERY hero, not none.** This is the *opposite* of
the `hero_classes: []` rule on camping skills, so it cannot be inherited from
there. `conservatory_of_steel` settles it: one building ships BOTH an empty-tag
entry (three buffs) and a `conservatory_of_steel`-tagged entry. Read empty as
"no hero" and three buffs are unreachable dead data sitting beside a working
example of the other form. The Granary is the same shape — its eat bonus is
tagged `[]` and **no hero anywhere carries a `granary` tag**.

**Every hero carries exactly one district tag** (plus `light`/`heavy` and
`religious`/`non-religious`, which no district reads), all 20 classes, DLC
included. So `hero_type_tags` is a class gate wearing a tag's clothes, and a
district's hero buff reaches a predictable 1–5 classes.

### Two wiring bugs the work uncovered

- **`hp_heal_received_percent` ignored its sub type.** `districts_granary_1` is
  that stat with `stat_sub_type "eat"` — food only. Ungated, the Granary would
  have silently become **+15% to all healing received, a Vestal's included**.
  Same class of bug as `combat_stat_add` carrying its real axis in the sub
  type. `CombatantState.eat_heal_received_mult` is now its own field.
- **`provision_hp_heal` was a literal** in `env/expedition_sequence_env.py`,
  with a comment naming the rules.json key it was copied from. Now parsed.

### The tier gate is economic, not mechanical

Fran's call: a real campaign cannot afford these early, so a fully-districted
Apprentice party is a scenario that never occurs. Crimson Court and Fires Edge
districts from **Veteran** (tier 3); Color of Madness districts — the Mill
included — from **Champion** (tier 5). Keyed on the `dlc/<package>/` directory
the file was read from, so a district cannot drift brackets by being renamed.

Tiers 1–2 are therefore byte-identical to the pre-districts behaviour, which
makes them a **control group** when reading the retrain — the same role the
short band plays for camping.

### The Granary always gives its maximum

Shipped range is 4–10 and the game rolls it, but the roll is rerollable from
the provision screen, so a player who wants the maximum takes it. Fran's call:
model the maximum, not the roll, because modelling it would add variance no
real player is exposed to.

### Consequences

Comp scores from before 2026-08-24 are **a different ruler** at Veteran and
Champion, the same way the status-effect change was on 2026-08-22. Checkpoints
still load — the observation is unchanged. `DISTRICTS_ENABLED` reproduces the
old distribution for an A/B arm.

`tests/data/test_districts.py` pins the loader (10 tests), including that an
unrecognised `District*BuffData` type fails loudly rather than being silently
dropped.

## Unjustified healing is an OBSERVATION gap, and the reward was right all along (2026-08-24)

N3. 51.9% of the shipped checkpoint's heals land on a target at a **median 94%
HP**, restoring 2.4 HP. The obvious suspects were the reward and training
volume. Both are wrong, and the arithmetic settles it.

**The reward already prices these correctly.** A marginal heal of ~3.4 HP on a
~35 HP hero is worth `(3.4 / 35) / 4 = +0.024` in Φ, against a
`TURN_PENALTY_THREAT` of `-0.10`. So the reward scores an unjustified heal at
about **-0.076** — and the realised step reward for exactly those heals measures
**-0.0819**. Those agree to within a rounding error. The policy is not being
paid to do this; it does it anyway.

**Why it cannot tell.** The comparison was not recoverable from the
observation:

* the hero slot carried HP as a **fraction**, `hp / max_hp`;
* the skill slot carries a heal as an **absolute amount over 30**.

Judging overheal means comparing an absolute heal against an absolute deficit,
and that needs the target's max HP — which appeared **nowhere** in the vector.
Two heroes at the same HP fraction and different max HP were indistinguishable,
though one has three times the room to heal into.

This is structurally identical to `_is_doomed`, whose own note reads: *"the
comparison needs the target's absolute max HP, which the observation never
carried, so 'is this kill already secured?' was not recoverable from the vector
at all."* Same missing quantity, same shape of fix.

`HERO_HEAL_HEADROOM` is the hero's absolute HP deficit on the **same /30 scale**
the heal payload uses, so the two are directly comparable and the policy no
longer has to infer a hidden divisor. Appended after the capability pools, so
every index below is untouched. A hero on Death's Door reads FULL headroom
whatever the HP says — matching the exception `_is_wasted_heal` already makes,
because a feature that disagreed with the legality rule would teach the opposite
of what the mask permits.

### A hypothesis of mine that the measurement killed

I claimed `check_playbook`'s heal-reward comparison was confounded because a
justified heal fires when the party is taking the most damage. **Measured: the
opposite.** Justified-heal steps lose *less* party HP (0.74 against 1.25).

The comparison is confounded, just not that way. The real confounders:

| | unjustified | justified |
|---|---|---|
| in the recovery phase | **0%** | **58%** |
| stress gained on the step | 1.28 | **2.35** |
| net HP restored | 3.43 | **4.22** |

58% of justified heals happen where a turn costs `-0.01` instead of `-0.10`, and
they carry a point more stress. The tool's own comment — *"both buckets carry
the same confound, so the DIFFERENCE is the readable part"* — assumes a
symmetric confound and there isn't one. Its "the reward cannot tell them apart"
conclusion should not be quoted.

### What the retrain tests

Whether the policy uses it. A feature it ignores is worth nothing, so the
falsifier is **`check_playbook`'s 51.9%**, not a win rate — and the corridor
eval only has to show the observation change cost nothing.

## Modded classes load, and are shells (2026-08-24)

`CLAUDE.md` said modded heroes cannot load because "no `.info.darkest` exists
anywhere in the install". Wrong diagnosis. Fran has **five class mods
subscribed** — Exanimate, Trophy Hunter, Navigator, Tempest, Sibyl — their hero
files exist, and they parse with the **unmodified** parser. They live in
`steamapps/workshop/content/262060/`, outside `GAME_DIR`, where nothing ever
looked. And `HERO_DIR_MAP` is an explicit list of the 20 shipped ids, so even
pointed at the right directory the loader would skip a class it had not been
told about.

`HeroLoader._load_mod_heroes` **discovers** classes under the roots named in
`DDSIM_MOD_DIRS`. All five load with the vanilla shape — 7 skills, 5 weapon and
5 armour tiers, real stats — and every one is a **stance hero** (Sibyl's are
`moon`/`eclipse`/`sun`). One of them fights a real combat through to a winner.

**Off by default**, because every archived measurement, every comp in the pool
and every checkpoint assumes a 20-class roster. Two traps are documented on the
method and pinned by tests: a discovered id **can never shadow a shipped
class** (a mod replacing the Crusader would otherwise silently become the
Crusader in curated comps that mean the real one), and **the store cache does
not key on this variable** — pair it with `DDSIM_NO_STORE_CACHE=1` or the first
run may serve a store built without the mods.

### The honest half: they are shells

Loading the hero is not loading the class. `effects/`, `shared/buffs/`,
`trinkets/` and `raid/camping/` are still read from `GAME_DIR` only, and every
mod ships its own copies of all four. Measured across the five discovered
classes: **669 of 684 effect references resolve to nothing — 97.8%**. Zero
Sibyl trinkets and zero Sibyl camp skills load.

So a modded hero arrives with correct stats, skills, stances, ranks and damage,
and **no payloads whatsoever** — no stuns, no marks, no buffs, no bleeds. The
same inertness the Runaway's burn had before burn was implemented, and exactly
the failure this project has learned to distrust: something that looks like it
works because nothing errors.

`tests/data/test_mod_heroes.py` pins both halves, and its kit test is written to
**fail** once those loaders learn the same roots — that failure is the signal to
delete it.

### Why this is being stopped here rather than finished

Extending five more loaders is real work, and it serves the goal this session
already argued down: cross-class transfer has nothing to transfer to, and if a
modded class loads the right answer is to *train on it* rather than transfer to
it. Training on it needs the kit, the localization (`*.loc2`, not the
`*.string_table.xml` the loader reads), and curated comps that field it.

What has been bought is the diagnosis and a working first step, both cheap to
resume. What has not been bought is a playable modded class, and this entry
exists so nobody believes otherwise.

## The boss dose-response: 25% is better than 50%, and the prediction held (2026-08-24)

The endurance reading predicted, **on the record before the arm finished**, that
`--boss-quest-p 0.5` would land well short of doubling 25%'s +3.3pp and might
plateau, because exposure teaches recognition and recognition is not the
constraint. It did worse than plateau. It reversed.

| dose | corridor | vs control | boss quests | vs control |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0% (`control_20260824`) | 70.6% | — | 41.7% | — |
| **25%** | 70.4% | **-0.1pp** [-2.4, +2.2] | **45.0%** | **+3.3pp** [-0.2, +6.9] |
| **50%** | **65.4%** | **-5.2pp** [-7.6, -2.6] | 41.1% | -0.5pp [-3.9, +3.0] |

**Half the training on boss quests is worse than a quarter on both rulers.**
The corridor loss is real — interval clear of zero — and the boss gain is
entirely gone.

### Why, and it is obvious in hindsight

**A boss quest is a normal expedition plus a boss at the end.** Seven or so
corridor fights, then one boss. So corridor competence is most of boss
performance, and the two rulers are not independent: degrade corridors by 5.2pp
and the boss column loses roughly what the boss training bought it.

That also explains the shape of the 25% result — a real boss gain at *zero*
corridor cost is exactly what a small dose should produce, and it is the dose
where the two effects have not started fighting.

Combined with the static finding that a boss party carries **2.66x the HP at
identical damage per round**, the picture closes:

- bosses are hard because the fight is **long**, not because it is unfamiliar;
- exposure buys recognition, which was never the binding constraint;
- and past a small dose, exposure costs the corridor skill that boss runs are
  mostly made of.

### What this settles

**Stop tuning `boss_quest_p`.** The optimum is at or below 25%, the ceiling on
that lever is a few points, and the 29pp gap is not going to close this way.
A third arm at 0.1 might find a slightly better point, but the lever's whole
range is now bracketed and it is small.

**The remaining lever is resource management over long fights**, which is the
same defect already measured from the other direction: 51.9% of heals
unjustified, 23.4% of secured-kill turns spent recovering, and deaths per
expedition 0.78 -> 1.87 on boss runs. Over a fight 2.66x longer than a corridor
pack, those compound. That is N3, and it just acquired a second independent
reason to be the next thing worked on.

**Neither boss arm ships.** 25% is inside the noise on corridors and its boss
gain grazes zero; 50% is a clear regression. `control_20260824` stays live.

## A boss fight is an ENDURANCE problem, not a recognition problem (2026-08-24)

Written **before** the `--boss-quest-p 0.5` arm finishes, so the prediction below
is on the record rather than fitted afterwards.

Static comparison of every distinct encounter party in the game, boss rows
against hall/room rows, at each monster's top variant:

| | corridor | boss | ratio |
|---|---|---|---|
| party HP | 73.8 | **196.4** | **2.66x** |
| best-skill damage per round | 18.5 | **18.5** | **1.00x** |
| party size | 3.3 | 2.3 | 0.70x |
| actions per round | 3.3 | 2.9 | 0.88x |

**A boss deals no more damage per round than a corridor pack. It has 2.66x the
HP.** So the fight is not harder per turn — it is roughly two and a half times
LONGER at identical incoming pressure, and the party therefore absorbs roughly
two and a half times the total damage before it ends.

That reframes everything measured about bosses this session:

- It explains why **exposure barely helped**. 25% of training bought +3.3pp and
  closed an eighth of the gap. The policy is not failing to recognise a boss;
  it is running out of party over a long fight.
- It matches the failure mode already measured: on boss runs `old-succ` tracks
  SUCCESS in both columns and **deaths per expedition go 0.78 -> 1.87**. Not
  stalling, not fleeing to safety — dying.
- It explains why the **large-monster penalty vanishes** on boss runs (2.8pp
  against 15pp in corridors): reach and rank matter less when the binding
  constraint is total damage absorbed over many rounds.
- And it connects to the standing policy defects. Over a 2.66x longer fight,
  resource management — when to heal, when to spend a secured turn recovering —
  compounds. Those are exactly the behaviours measured at 51.9% unjustified
  heals and 23.4% recovery spend.

### The prediction

If bosses are an endurance problem rather than an unfamiliarity problem, then
**`--boss-quest-p 0.5` should NOT roughly double the +3.3pp**. It should land
meaningfully short of +6.6pp, and may plateau near where 25% did, because more
exposure teaches recognition and the binding constraint is not recognition.

If instead it comes in near +6.6pp or better, this reading is wrong: exposure
does scale, bosses were simply under-trained, and the answer is more boss
volume rather than better resource management.

Either way the next move follows from the answer rather than from taste, which
is the point of writing it down first.

## Training against bosses: no corridor cost, and a weak real gain (2026-08-24)

The first boss arm. `--boss-quest-p 0.25`, 3M, seed 0, 12 envs — every other
setting identical to `control_20260824`, so it is a one-variable comparison.
Graded on both rulers, 456 paired cells each.

| ruler | control | boss arm | paired delta | 95% CI |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **corridor** | 70.6% | 70.4% | **-0.1pp** | [-2.4, +2.2] |
| **boss quests** | 41.7% | 45.0% | **+3.3pp** | [-0.2, +6.9] |
| boss, long band | 30.9% | 36.7% | **+5.8pp** | [-0.3, +12.2] |

**The corridor cost is zero.** -0.1pp overall, and every band inside ±2.0. That
was the real risk — a quarter of training spent elsewhere could easily have
eaten the 90% of play that is corridors, and it did not.

**The boss gain is real in direction and weak in evidence.** +3.3pp overall with
a lower bound of -0.2, and +5.8pp on the long band with -0.3. By the ruler
recorded in `NEXT.md` this passes — it gains on bosses without losing on
corridors — but "gains" is a point estimate whose interval grazes zero, and it
should be quoted that way.

**The number that matters more: the gap barely moved.** Corridor 70.4% against
boss 45.0% is still a **-25.4pp** hole, where before training it was -28.9pp.
A quarter of training spent on boss quests closed about an eighth of the gap.
That is worth stating plainly, because the cheerful reading of "+3.3pp, no cost"
hides it: bosses are not merely unfamiliar, and exposure alone is not obviously
going to close them.

Two readings, and the next arm distinguishes them:

- **Dose.** 25% was not enough exposure, and the effect scales. Then 50% should
  give roughly double and the gap should keep closing.
- **Ceiling.** Something about boss fights is hard for reasons exposure does not
  address — the parties are smaller (mean 2.3 against a corridor's 4), 16 of 29
  contain a size-2+ monster, and 12 of 29 have `life_link`ed adds whose
  behaviour the policy has to learn separately. Then 50% plateaus, or starts
  costing corridors.

`--boss-quest-p 0.5` is running to tell them apart. It is the cheapest question
available: same budget, one knob, and the answer changes whether boss
competence is a training-volume problem or a modelling one.

## The slot-permutation probe is INCONCLUSIVE, and for a knowable reason (2026-08-24)

The theory: cross-class transfer fails because an action index is
`slot * RANKS + rank` and slot *k* names a different skill per class, so the
policy memorises `(class, slot)` and has nothing to look up for a class it never
saw. `--slot-permute` shuffles which skill sits at which index per episode, so
an index cannot be memorised. Two 3M arms, matched to the earlier pair (seed 0,
6 envs, no knobs), one with `--hold-out-class vestal`.

**The excess gap collapsed — and so did everything else.**

| | seen classes | Vestal comps | gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| held-out, permuted | 2.7% | 11.5% | -8.8pp |
| baseline, permuted | 3.4% | 8.3% | -5.0pp |

**Paired excess gap -3.8pp, 95% CI [-13.7, +4.2]**, against the standing
**+46.6pp**. The old gap is far outside this interval, so it is genuinely gone.

**But it is gone because both arms floored out, not because transfer works.**
`tools/transfer_eval.py` runs at tier 5 medium, and the scenario probe run
hours earlier found tier 5 medium is a **floor at 3.1%** for this generation of
checkpoint. Both arms score ~3% on seen classes there. You cannot measure a gap
between two policies that can both do nothing. The earlier +46.6pp pair scored
14-16% on seen comps in the same scenario.

**Permutation costs a great deal of general competence.** Same 3M, same seed, no
knobs, 456 paired cells:

| band | unpermuted | permuted | delta | 95% CI |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| short | 81.9% | 69.7% | **-12.2pp** | [-16.1, -8.4] |
| medium | 75.3% | 58.9% | **-16.4pp** | [-21.5, -11.5] |
| long | 54.4% | 34.7% | **-19.7pp** | [-24.0, -15.6] |
| overall | 70.6% | 54.4% | **-16.1pp** | [-18.6, -13.6] |

That is the second-largest effect this project has measured, after the boss gap,
and it is entirely expected in direction: removing a stable index mapping makes
the control problem much harder. 3M steps is evidently not enough to relearn it.

### What this does and does not settle

**Does not settle the action-space theory.** The experiment lost its dynamic
range before it could answer. A fair test needs the permuted arms trained to
comparable competence first — and at -16.1pp after 3M, "comparable" may cost
several times that budget, which is exactly the kind of spend this project's
own guidance says to justify before starting.

**Does settle that slot permutation is not free.** Anyone reaching for it as a
cheap augmentation should know it costs ~16pp at 3M.

**And the split is not like-for-like anyway**: 131 seen / 21 transfer comps
against the earlier 124 / 17, because 12 curated comps were added between the
two runs. Recorded so the -3.8pp is never quoted against the +46.6pp as if the
same measurement had been repeated.

The cheaper remaining option for the same question is the third one listed when
this line opened: **sort the skill slots by payload** so an ally heal always
occupies the same index across classes. That gives the policy a stable mapping
*and* a class-independent one, where permutation removes the stability without
replacing it.

## The comp search's scenario had stopped ranking anything (2026-08-24)

`CLAUDE.md` has required a "four-way probe after any retrain" since 2026-08-22
and never named a command for it, so it stayed folklore and was never run for
the current checkpoint. `tools/comp_ga.py --scenario-probe` makes it executable:
it spreads a fixed set of random candidates across the four (tier, band)
corners and ranks them by the SPREAD they produce, because spread is the whole
property being bought. A scenario every candidate wins scores 1.0 across the
board and ranks noise; one every candidate loses ranks nothing at all.

**Shipped checkpoint, crypts, 8 candidates x 4 episodes:**

| tier | band | mean | min | max | SD | verdict |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 3 | medium | 68.8% | 25.0% | 100.0% | 29.1 | usable |
| **3** | **long** | **65.6%** | 0.0% | 100.0% | **32.6** | **widest spread** |
| 5 | medium | 3.1% | 0.0% | 25.0% | 8.8 | floor |
| 5 | long | 0.0% | 0.0% | 0.0% | **0.0** | **useless — every candidate identical** |

**The polarity has completely inverted since the 2026-08-21 retrain.** Then,
tier 3 medium and tier 3 long were both ceilings at 100%, tier 5 long a floor,
and **tier 5 medium was the only corner that separated candidates** — which is
why it became the default. On the current checkpoint tier 5 medium is a **floor
at 3.1%** and tier 5 long ranks literally nothing, while both tier 3 corners are
comfortably usable.

So the GA's shipped default has been unable to rank comps since the checkpoint
changed, and every comp it emitted would have been ordered by noise. Use
`--tier 3 --band long` until the next retrain, then re-run the probe — which is
now one command instead of a paragraph of advice.

This is also the clearest single statement of how much weaker the current
checkpoint is at endgame difficulty: 65.6% at tier 3 against 0.0% at tier 5, on
the same comps in the same zone.

## Bosses measured for the first time: -28.9pp (2026-08-24)

37 boss encounter rows and **60 monsters reachable only through them** — the
Collector, Brigand Cannon, the Hag's cauldron, Necromancer forms — and until
today not one had ever appeared in an eval or in training. `boss_quest_p` has
been `0.0` in every checkpoint this project has ever produced, and the corridor
eval contains no boss quests by construction. So `life_link` (a boss's adds die
with it), the untargetable cauldron and the summon machinery were all built,
tested against the data, and never once exercised by a policy.

`eval_expedition.py --boss-quests` forces the last combat of every episode to be
the region's boss. `ExpeditionSequenceEnv` already honoured an explicit
`boss_quest` in its reset options, which is why this is three lines rather than
a new knob.

**Shipped checkpoint, 152 comps x 3 bands, boss quests against corridors:**

| band | corridor | boss quest | delta | 95% CI |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| short | 81.9% | 47.7% | **-34.2pp** | [-40.8, -27.5] |
| medium | 75.3% | 46.4% | **-28.9pp** | [-35.4, -22.9] |
| long | 54.4% | 30.9% | **-23.5pp** | [-29.4, -17.3] |
| overall | 70.6% | 41.7% | **-28.9pp** | [-32.6, -25.1] |

**This is by a wide margin the largest effect ever measured in this project.**
For scale: the large-monster split is -15pp, the reward fix was +4.3pp on the
long band, and the eval's own noise is ±2pp. A boss costs more than everything
else put together.

It is also the least surprising, which is the point — the policy has never seen
one. The number is not evidence that boss modelling is wrong; it is the size of
a training gap, and it is now measurable, which it was not this morning.

**Report it separately, never merged with the corridor number.** A boss run is a
different question, and averaging the two would hide both. Note also that the
deltas *shrink* with expedition length (-34 short, -23 long) because a long
corridor run is already hard — the boss is a smaller share of what kills you.

**Not paired in the strict sense**: the corridor arm ran 4 episodes per cell and
the boss arm 2, so the cells share comps and bands but not episode counts. At an
effect size of 29pp with a ±3pp interval that changes nothing, and it is
recorded rather than glossed.

**The boss content itself is intact, checked rather than assumed.** Of the 66
distinct monsters across the 37 boss rows: **0 missing** from the store, **0**
without a brain id, **0** whose brain id is absent from the brain library. 15
carry no skills and every one of them is scenery — `cauldron_empty_A..C` (the
Hag's, already implemented as untargetable), `nest_A..C` (the Crow), and
`pew_small/medium/large_A..C` (the Prophet's church pews). No actual boss is
skill-less. So the -28.9pp is not a loading hole leaking into the number; it is
the training gap at full size.

### What actually kills them, from the same run

| | corridor | boss quest |
|---|---|---|
| SUCCESS | 70.6% | 41.7% |
| old-succ (reached the end alive, fights skipped or not) | 71.3% | 42.0% |
| expeditions that fled at least one fight | 13.1% | **28.5%** |
| combats fled per expedition | 0.37 | **0.74** |
| **deaths per expedition** | **0.78** | **1.87** |

**`old-succ` tracks SUCCESS almost exactly in both columns** (71.3 vs 70.6;
42.0 vs 41.7), which rules out the two failure modes that are not deaths: the
party is not stalling out on the step budget and it is not surviving by
skipping fights. It is dying. **Deaths per expedition more than double.**

The retreat rate doubles too, so the policy is *trying* to disengage — it just
does not save the run. That is the shape of a party that meets something it has
no answer to rather than one that plays a known fight badly.

One more thing worth knowing: **the large-monster penalty nearly vanishes on
boss runs** — 43.2% vs 40.4%, a 2.8pp split against 15pp in corridors. The boss
dominates whatever else is standing there, so the corridor-tuned "large monsters
are hard" effect stops being the thing that decides the fight.

The obvious next step is a `--boss-quest-p` training arm, and it needs its ruler
decided first: a boss-trained checkpoint will look *worse* on the corridor eval,
so grading it there alone would punish the thing being bought.

## `--slot-permute`: the one-run probe of why transfer fails (2026-08-24)

Two observation redesigns failed the held-out-class falsifier by the same
+46.6pp, and the second one — giving the policy its allies' entire movesets —
moved the number by 0.3pp. Observability was never the blocker.

**The action space is.** An index is `slot * RANKS + rank`, and slot *k* names a
different skill for every class: an ally heal sits at slot 3 for Vestal and
Occultist, 4 for Crusader, Plague Doctor and Antiquarian, 5 for Arbalest and
Musketeer. "Press the slot that heals" is therefore a per-class fact the policy
memorises, and for a class it never trained on there is nothing to look up — no
matter how well the observation describes the skill sitting there.

`--slot-permute` shuffles which skill occupies which index, **fixed per
episode**. Across training the policy sees every skill at every index, so an
index cannot be memorised and the choice has to come from the slot's own payload
features. Paired with `--hold-out-class` it is a one-run test of the theory: if
transfer does not move, the action-space explanation is wrong too and the line
closes cheaply.

Per EPISODE, not per step — per step would make the action space
non-stationary inside a single decision sequence, which is a different and
incoherent experiment.

### The refactor it required, and the failure it protects against

The slot order was read at **two independent sites** — `_rebuild_action_table`
and `_skill_slot_vecs` — coupled only by both happening to call
`engine.get_hero_full_skill_ids`. Permute one and not the other and the policy
reads slot *k*'s features while index `k*RANKS + r` fires slot *j*'s skill, and
**the action mask cannot report it**: the mask is built from `_action_map`
alone, so it stays perfectly self-consistent while describing the wrong skill.
Both now go through one `_slot_order`, and a test walks real episodes asserting
the two agree under both settings.

### Two bugs found while wiring it

- **The flag was echoed but not wired.** `train.py` printed
  `slot_permute=True` in its distribution line while **no env ever received
  it** — the smoke test looked like a pass. Three construction sites needed it:
  the vec env, the `MaskableEvalCallback` eval env (a mismatch there would grade
  a permuted policy on an unpermuted board), and `--play`/`--check`. A test now
  asserts the forwarding, not just the echo.
- **`tools/playbook_policy.py` hardcoded `PASS_ACTION = 36` and
  `RETREAT_ACTION = 37`** as bare literals — the only place outside
  `env/gym_env.py` that duplicated the action layout. It now imports
  `ACTION_PASS` / `ACTION_RETREAT`, so widening `MAX_SKILL_SLOTS`, `RANKS` or
  `PROVISION_ACTION_ORDER` can no longer make it return a skill action where it
  means "pass".

Default off, and `test_off_by_default_and_byte_identical` pins that an unset
flag leaves the order exactly as every archived checkpoint expects.

## The reward fix shows up in BEHAVIOUR, and moved one number the wrong way (2026-08-24)

`tools/check_playbook.py` on the shipped checkpoint against the one it
replaced, same flags (`--episodes 36 --comps 12 --comp-seed 1`). The Φ reset
was charging the party for entering a fight healthy, so the prediction was that
removing it would show up as *more recovery*, not just a better win rate.

| | pre-fix | post-fix |
|---|---|---|
| PLAYBOOK 3.3 — secured-kill turns spent recovering | 17.2% | **23.4%** |
| PLAYBOOK 3.4 — heal share of recovery-phase turns | 15.3% | **24.8%** |
| PLAYBOOK 3.2 — heals justified by stall/recovery | 29.1% | **40.0%** |
| PLAYBOOK 3.2 — heals justified by NOTHING | 58.6% | **51.9%** |
| corpse attacks, as a share of chances | 11.9% | 9.6% |
| combats lasting >3 rounds | 10.8% | **20.4%** |

Every recovery metric moved the predicted way, and unjustified healing fell
6.7pp without being targeted. Combats also got materially longer (mean 2.1 ->
2.6 rounds), which is the same story from the other side: a party that is
allowed to recover survives to fight more rounds. **Three independent rulers now
agree the fix was right** — the eval (+4.3pp long), the tutorial rig (98% ->
100%), and behaviour.

### One number went the wrong way, and it is not dismissible
