The 3.2 discrimination line **flipped sign**:

| | unjustified heal | justified heal | delta |
|---|---|---|---|
| pre-fix | -0.1656 | **-0.0491** | -0.1165 |
| post-fix | **-0.1921** | -0.2592 | **+0.0671** |

By the tool's own reading, `unjustified >= justified` means the reward can no
longer tell the two apart, and no amount of further training will fix a
behaviour whose reward does not distinguish it. Before the fix it could.

**The confound, stated because it is large.** A justified heal happens when a
hero is badly hurt, which is to say in a worse position — the party is taking
damage, Φ is falling, and the step's realised reward carries all of that, not
just the heal. Now that Φ is no longer pinned to zero at the combat boundary,
those surrounding losses reach the step reward where before they were partly
washed out. So the flip may be the measurement getting *more* faithful rather
than the reward getting worse. The tool already warns its third column is not a
control; this comparison has the same disease one column to the left.

Not resolved here. Recorded as the first thing to look at if unjustified healing
stops falling — and the honest note is that the fix that improved three rulers
made a fourth harder to read.

## The retrain, graded three ways: the reward fix did the work (2026-08-24)

Two 3M arms on the fixed reward, identical but for the five randomisation
knobs, against the checkpoint they replace. All three graded on
`--all-comps --episodes 4` — **456 paired cells**, identical scenarios in every
run because `_cell_scenario` hashes `comp|band|episode`, so these are paired
deltas with real intervals rather than three independent means.

| | short | medium | **long** | overall |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| live `baseline_caps` (old reward, knobs off) | 80.4% | 77.5% | 50.2% | 69.4% |
| **`control_20260824`** (new reward, knobs off) | 81.9% | 75.3% | 54.4% | 70.6% |
| `big_20260824` (new reward, knobs ON) | 81.7% | 77.0% | **56.2%** | 71.7% |

| paired delta | long | overall |
|---|---|---|
| **reward fix** (ctrl − live) | **+4.3pp** [+0.0, +8.4] | +1.2pp [−1.0, +3.4] |
| **knobs** (big − ctrl) | +1.8pp [−2.3, +5.8] | +1.1pp [−1.0, +3.2] |
| **both** (big − live) | **+6.1pp** [+1.8, +10.4] | +2.3pp [+0.1, +4.4] |

**The reward fix did most of it, in exactly the band it was aimed at.** +4.3pp
on the long band — the band that collapses, and the one the per-combat Φ reset
was charging the party for recovering in. The interval's lower bound sits on
0.0, so on this ruler alone it is at the edge; it is corroborated independently
on the one exactly reproducible harness in the project, where the same change
took **The Old Road from 98.0% to 100.0%** with deaths/run 0.02 → 0.00.

**The knobs are not distinguishable from zero.** +1.8pp on long [−2.3, +5.8],
+1.1pp overall [−1.0, +3.2] — neither clears its interval — and they cost
**6pp on the tutorial rig** (100.0% → 94.0%, 10.5 steps → 15.8). Half of that
arm's training had a randomised kit and the Old Road is a fixed two-hero party,
so losing specialisation there is the expected direction, but it is a real cost
against no measurable gain.

**Shipped: `control_20260824`**, Fran's call. It wins the reproducible rig
outright, captures the long-band gain, and the knobs add nothing that pays for
their specialisation cost.

**The argument on the other side, recorded because the measurement cannot
settle it:** the eval pool is the same 152 comps the policy trains on, so an
eval on it is structurally incapable of showing a generalisation benefit. The
knobs are the only thing that ever exposes the policy to a randomised kit, and
`bridge/` plays parties Fran assembles by hand. If that is the use case,
`big_20260824` is the better bet on principle and the eval will never say so.
Kept in `models/` rather than archived, for that reason.

### Method note: pairing is what made this readable

Only ~80 of 152 cells per band differ between any two arms. Comparing two
independent means would have given ~1.7σ on the long band and no usable
interval on the others; the paired deltas above come from the same cell facing
the same tier, length and seed in both runs. That property is a direct
consequence of the `--all-comps` change made the day before, and it paid for
itself on its first use.

## The eval stops sampling comps (2026-08-23)

Comp-to-comp spread is the dominant error term in the full-expedition eval, and
it dwarfs episode noise. Measured over 12 comps x 3 bands x 50 episodes:

| band | mean | comp-to-comp SD | SE from 12 comps | 95% CI |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| short | 84.2% | 7.3 | +-2.1pp | [80.1, 88.3] |
| medium | 77.0% | 12.1 | +-3.5pp | [70.2, 83.8] |
| **long** | **56.8%** | **16.2** | **+-4.7pp** | **[47.7, 66.0]** |

Individual comps ran **30% to 88%** on the long band — the band the policy is
worst at and the one every remaining question is about. Reaching +-2pp by
sampling harder would need ~252 comps and only ~152 exist, so the term cannot
be beaten by widening the sample. It has to be removed.

`--all-comps` evaluates the whole trainable pool in sorted order. **152 x 3 x 4
is the same 1,824 episodes as 12 x 3 x 50**, with no comp-sampling error left
in it. `--comps` and `--comp-seed` are ignored under it; both default off, so
every archived invocation still means what it meant.

Three things it had to change beyond the comp list:

- **Each cell draws its own scenario** (`_cell_scenario`, hashed from
  `comp|band|episode`). The shared `sampler_rng` was consumed SEQUENTIALLY
  across the whole nest, so changing the comp count moved the tier and length
  of every cell after it — and Fran adds curated comps regularly (144 -> 156 on
  this day alone), so under the old scheme every addition silently reshuffled
  the scenarios of every later cell. A cell is now reproducible on its own and
  the pool can grow without moving anything.
- **The cell count is printed up front.** `tools/summarize_eval.py` reports
  `cells=N`, and a 456-cell run compared against a 36-cell archived log is two
  different rulers.
- **The per-cell detail block is suppressed** under `--all-comps` (456 lines),
  replaced by a per-band summary that reports the comp-to-comp SD and standard
  error directly — the numbers this whole change is about. `--detail` restores
  it.

### First run, and it moved the long band by 6.6pp

152 comps x 3 bands x 4 episodes, on the live checkpoint:

| band | success | SE | 12-comp estimate it replaces |
|---|---|---|---|
| short | 80.4% | +-1.84pp | 84.2% +-2.1 |
| medium | 77.5% | +-1.99pp | 77.0% +-3.5 |
| **long** | **50.2%** | **+-2.53pp** | 56.8% +-4.7 |

The long band moved **6.6pp** — inside the old +-4.7pp interval's reach, which
is the point: that interval was wide enough to hide it, and the new one is
nearly twice as tight for the same 1,824 episodes.

**Why all-comps-with-few-episodes is the right split of a fixed budget**, and
not merely a different one. The variance of the mean is
`comp_var / n_comps + episode_var / N_total`. The second term is pinned by the
total episode count however it is divided; only the first responds, and it
shrinks with the number of comps. So for any fixed budget, maximising comps and
minimising episodes-per-cell is optimal. 152 x 4 beats 12 x 50 for free.

**One label was wrong in the first version and is corrected**: the summary
called its dispersion "comp-to-comp SD". At 4 episodes a cell's rate is
quantised to {0, 25, 50, 75, 100}%, so that figure carries binomial episode
noise on top of the real spread between comps and reads far higher (31.2pp on
long against the 16.2pp measured at 50 episodes). It is now labelled cell SD
and says what is in it. The SE beside it was always right — it is the standard
error of the mean over cells and absorbs both sources.

**A claim that was made and is false**, recorded so it is not re-derived: that
`random.sample` switching branch at `k=n` makes a 152-comp draw disagree with a
12-comp draw at the same seed. It does take a different branch — selection-set
versus partial-shuffle, on CPython's `setsize` heuristic — but the two agree on
their first 12 picks for this population anyway. Checked rather than assumed,
after it had already been written into a docstring. A wide `--comps` really
would give a superset; it just would not fix the sequential RNG, which is the
actual reason this flag exists.

## The camping sweep, part 3: a camp buff had no sign and no target (2026-08-23)

Two defects stacked on the same branch, and the first was invisible because
nobody had asked what a camp `buff` actually contains.

**Sign.** `_effect_value` gave every `buff` effect a flat `+W_BUFF`. Most camp
buffs are good; two are not. The Runaway's `play_with_fire` grants **+10%
damage received** and her `cauterize` grants **-20% debuff resist**. Both were
scored as benefits, so the greedy spend could buy a debuff with points that had
somewhere better to go. The sign is now read off the buff's own `stat_type`,
using the same "received axis" rule the observation uses for its stat buckets —
and the trap that rule exists for is real here: `campingStressResistBuff` is
**-0.30** on `stress_dmg_received_percent`, so a naive `amount >= 0` test would
call the best defensive buff in the game a debuff.

**Target.** `_pick_individual` had branches for both heal types, both damage
types and both DoT cures, and none for `buff` — it fell through to
`candidates[0]`, the frontmost living hero. Now routed by what the buff does:
a harmful one goes to whoever can best absorb it (mirroring the existing
`stress_damage_amount` rule), a survivability buff to the hero closest to
dying, an offensive one to the biggest hitter. **Only the routing is
convention** — which side a buff falls on is data. 261 equipped slots, and an
unresolvable buff id keeps the old fall-through exactly.

### The schedule change that was tried, measured, and reverted

The sweep's other finding was that **three quarters of the second campfire is
never spent**: on 40 long expeditions, 33 runs reach the first anchor (combat
5) and only 10 reach the second (combat 10), for 0.93 fires against a budget of
2.

The obvious reading is that a fixed index is itself the convention — the real
game has no schedule, a player camps when worn — so discretion was implemented:
the anchor kept as a deadline, pullable forward up to 2 combats when
`_party_needs_recovery()` holds, designed so a camp could only ever happen
earlier or at the same combat, never later.

**It measured worse and was reverted.**

| | anchors only | pulled forward |
|---|---|---|
| camps per long run | 0.93 | 0.90 |
| party stress removed per camp | **31.1** | 10.6 |
| mean final stress per hero | **21.4** | 24.7 |

`_party_needs_recovery` is true almost immediately — any living hero under 85%
HP or over 30 stress — so the window fired nearly every camp two combats early,
on a party that was not yet worn enough for the recovery to land. The
anti-waste scoring in `resolve_camp` then had less to buy, and total recovery
delivered went DOWN. The count did not even rise, because the runs that die
before combat 10 mostly die before combat 8 too.

So the even-spacing anchor stands, and the conclusion flips: **the low reach is
not a scheduling bug, it is the policy dying.** Front-loading it would have
been tuning the model to flatter a weak policy, which is the thing this project
refuses to do everywhere else. Recorded here so the idea is not re-proposed —
and note the change was cheap to test precisely because a camp probe already
existed.

## The camping sweep, part 2: `.chance` was scored but never rolled (2026-08-23)

`_effect_value` scales a candidate's expected value by `effect.chance` --
`return total * effect.chance`, which is correct for ranking a greedy spend --
and that was the **only** place the field was read. `_apply_effect` fired every
effect unconditionally.

Ten shipped effects carry a chance below 1.0, across **131 equipped comp
slots**, and the error runs in both directions:

| skill | intended | what happened |
|---|---|---|
| `gallows_humor` | 75% an ally is relieved of 20 stress, 25% they gain 10 | both every time: a guaranteed net **-10** per ally |
| `abandon_hope` | two independent 50% hits of 10 and 5 | always **15**, double its 7.5 expectation |
| `quarantine` | two 50% heals of 20 and 15 | always **35**, double its 17.5 |
| `weapons_practice` | a 75% third buff | always granted |

Measured after the fix, over 400 campfires x 3 allies, Gallows Humor from 60
stress: **56.1% healed only / 18.9% both / 18.8% neither / 6.2% hit only**,
mean 47.51 against a predicted 47.5. Before it, every ally landed on exactly
50, every time.

**DECLARED READING: one roll per TARGET, and a skill's rows roll
INDEPENDENTLY.** Nothing in the data marks Gallows Humor's heal and its stress
damage as exclusive, so an ally can legitimately get both or neither -- which
is where the 18.9% comes from. The alternative reading, a single roll choosing
between the two, would need a marker the files do not carry.
**Falsifier:** camp with Gallows Humor in the real game and count how often one
ally takes the relief *and* the hit. Independent rolls predict ~18.75% of
allies; exclusive rolls predict 0%.

**No draw is taken at chance >= 1.0** -- the `dot_irresistible` precedent. 185
of the 195 shipped camp effects are certainties, and rolling against them would
shift the RNG stream for every party in the game without changing one outcome.
A test pins that a campfire of certainties costs at most the single ambush draw.

### What the rest of the sweep found, so it is not re-swept

The camping data is small and is now fully enumerated: **7 skill keys** (`id`,
`level`, `cost`, `use_limit`, `effects`, `hero_classes`,
`upgrade_requirements`) and **6 effect keys** (`selection`, `requirements`,
`chance`, `type`, `sub_type`, `amount`) across 6 files and 150 records.

- **`level` is 0 on all 150 records** -- inert, correctly unparsed.
- **`upgrade_requirements` is town gold** (1750 per skill) -- out of scope with
  the rest of the town.
- **`configuration.class_specific_number_of_classes_threshold: 4`** is the only
  unparsed key in the family, and it is inert here. The real distribution is
  1 skill with no classes, 72 with one, 4 with two and 3 with nineteen, so the
  threshold cleanly separates class-specific from shared -- a split our strict
  `hero.definition_id in skill.hero_classes` membership test already produces.
- **A camp buff lasts exactly one battle, and that is correct.**
  `_camp_buffs_by_slot` is reset at the top of every transition, carried in
  `CarryOverHeroState.camp_buff_ids`, and re-applied once at the next combat's
  reset. Checked rather than assumed -- `duration` has no JSON counterpart, so
  the lifetime lives entirely in the consumer.
- **`loot`, `remove_disease`, `reduce_torch`, `camp_restore_torch`** remain
  genuinely inapplicable: no loot, no disease, no torch decay over travel.

Still open, and deliberately not changed:

- **Stress urgency saturates at 100.** `_urgency(1 - min(1, stress/100))`
  prices a hero at 190 identically to one at 100, though the heart attack is at
  200. It is a convention weight rather than game data, so it is recorded here
  rather than tuned blind.
- **An `individual`-target camp BUFF still lands on the frontmost living
  hero.** `_pick_individual` has no branch for `buff` and falls through to
  `candidates[0]`. This was first written up as a 2,018-slot bug; that count
  was wrong -- only 23 of 107 buff rows are `individual`-target, which is **261
  equipped slots**, and `candidates[0]` is the frontmost LIVING hero, a
  defensible target for a defensive buff. Undeclared convention rather than
  defect, and there is no non-invented rule for who most wants +ACC.

## The camping sweep, part 1: a mortality debuff was permanent (2026-08-23)

Camping skills were the **one in-scope data family the engine audit never
swept** — `NEXT.md` says so outright, ranked last because they sit outside the
combat loop. Fran guessed at them independently while reading the
long-expedition collapse. The first thing the sweep found is a one-directional
fidelity bug.

`_apply_recovery_tier` gives a hero who survives Death's Door a sticky tier of
debuffs — `mortality` is -2 ACC, -5% DMG low and high, -1 SPD, -stress resist;
`heart_attack` is the heavier set. It is carried between combats in
`CarryOverHeroState`, and **every write to `mortality_tier` raises it**: the
applier itself guards with `MORTALITY_TIER_RANK[new] <= [current]`. Nothing
anywhere lowered it.

The real game removes them at a campfire and the shipped data says which
skills do it: `stand_tall` and `dark_ritual` carry
`remove_deaths_door_recovery_buffs`, **94 equipped slots** across the comp
pool. `engine/camping.py` filtered that effect out, with a comment saying it is
"handled by the engine's own mortality tiers". The tiers handle *applying* it.
Nothing handled removing it — so one Death's Door visit cost a hero those stats
for the rest of the expedition, paid ten or more times over on a long run.

Four things the fix had to get right:

- **The clear is the only thing that lowers the tier**, so it is a public
  `CombatEngine.clear_mortality_tier` rather than a private helper:
  `engine/camping.py` holds no engine reference and takes it as a callback, the
  same way it already takes `apply_buff`.
- **It reads the tier it is undoing.** The two tiers have different buff lists;
  reversing the `mortality` list off a `heart_attack` hero would leave the
  stats wrong in both directions.
- **The exact deltas are recorded at apply time** (`CombatantState.
  mortality_deltas`). Reversing a `combat_stat_multiply` on an int-typed stat
  is lossy — damage 10 applies to `int(10*0.95) = 9` and divides back to
  `int(9/0.95) = 9` — so without the recorded delta a hero lost a point of
  damage on **every** apply/clear cycle. `ActiveBuff.applied_delta` solves the
  same problem for duration-tracked buffs; the mortality tier is deliberately
  not ActiveBuff-tracked because it outlives the combat. A test asserts the
  round trip is exact rather than close.
- **Targeting.** Both carriers are `individual`, and `_pick_individual` had no
  branch for this type, so the fall-through would have spent the skill on the
  lowest-index living hero — usually one with no tier at all. It now picks the
  heaviest tier, and `_effect_value` scores 0 on an unmarked party so the
  greedy spend will not buy a useless clear.

`W_MORTALITY_CLEARED = 0.50` is a convention like every other weight in that
heuristic, priced between a DoT instance and a campfire of healing: the debuff
is small per combat but permanent, so a long run pays it many times.

**Not wired into `bridge/camp.py`**, which runs `resolve_camp` on a clone for
advice and already passes no `apply_buff` either — so buffs and clears are both
inert there. Pre-existing and consistent, but it means the advisor's camp
valuation still under-counts both.

## The potential function was being washed out once per combat (2026-08-23)

`DarkestDungeonEnv.step` pinned `phi_after = 0.0` whenever `engine.done`. In a
single combat that is correct -- the combat end IS the episode end. In an
expedition `engine.done` fires **8-18 times per episode**, and
`ExpeditionSequenceEnv.step` immediately un-terminates all but the last. So the
potential was being zeroed at every internal transition.

Pinning the endpoint makes one combat's shaping telescope to

```
g*0 - phi_start + (g-1)*sum(intermediate)  ~=  -phi_start
```

**Two consequences, and the second is worse than the first.**

- **A healthier party is charged more.** About **-0.33 per combat boundary**
  between a healthy and a hurt party, **-4.9 over a long expedition**, against
  a -0.10 threat-phase turn penalty and a -20.0 hero death. That lands exactly
  on the defect `tools/check_playbook.py` measures: PLAYBOOK 3.3 says spend a
  secured fight's turns on recovery, and the policy spent **17.2%** of them
  that way. The reward was charging it for the play the playbook asks for.
- **Nothing inside the combat reached its shaping total.** With the endpoint
  pinned, the HP term, the stress term, guard, riposte and `W_ENEMY_STUNNED`
  all cancel over the fight; only `phi_start` survives, as a penalty. The whole
  potential function -- every term the last year of tuning added to it -- was
  contributing nothing per combat except that penalty.

It also broke the invariance argument this project rests on. `CLAUDE.md` states
the argument covers the Φ terms and names exactly **two** deliberate
non-potential exceptions; this was an undeclared third. And the
expedition-level potential already had it right --
`_compute_expedition_potential` zeroes only at the true end -- so the
per-combat one was the odd one out rather than the precedent.

**The fix** is a predicate, `_is_episode_terminal()`, so the base class keeps
asking `engine.done` and the subclass can answer for the episode instead of the
combat. `ExpeditionSequenceEnv` overrides it, mirroring `step`'s own transition
block:

- a party **wipe** ends the episode wherever it happens;
- the **last combat** ends it -- but only if resolved. A retreat from the last
  combat does NOT, because a fled room stays on the map and the party re-takes
  it, the same rule that stops a retreat being a paid skip. `step` sets
  `terminated = False` there, and a naive `last_combat` test gets this wrong.
- everything else is internal and the episode continues.

**Deliberately not covered: step-budget truncation.** It is decided after
`super().step()` returns, from a `_step_count` the predicate cannot see the
final value of. The expedition-level potential zeroes on truncation because it
is computed where the flag exists; this one cannot. Worth ~2% of episodes, and
recorded rather than faked.

**No checkpoint trained before this is comparable, and `--resume` is invalid
across it.** This is a reward change, so it lands in the batch BEFORE the next
training run rather than after it.

`tests/env/test_potential_reset.py` pins the predicate's truth table, that the
single-combat envs are unchanged, that the predicate actually reaches
`phi_after` (a predicate nothing reads is the failure mode this week kept
finding), and the arithmetic property itself: pinned, a combat's shaping total
is identical whether it ends well or badly; unpinned, the ending reaches the
total.

Unrelated, same commit: `tests/env/test_expedition_env.py`'s comp-pool tripwire
goes **141 -> 152**. Fran added 12 curated comps; the tripwire exists to be
bumped deliberately when the folder changes.

## The capability profile did nothing, and "put identity back" was never available (2026-08-23)

The repair `CLAUDE.md` named for the failed transfer falsifier -- a hero
capability profile instead of a class one-hot -- was built, and it moved the
number by 0.3pp.

| held-out-Vestal, 3M vs 3M, paired | excess gap | 95% CI | on Vestal comps |
|---|---|---|---|
| mechanic obs only | +46.9pp | [+37.2, +56.9] | 17.6% vs 62.3% |
| **+ `_hero_capabilities`** | **+46.6pp** | [+37.2, +56.3] | 8.3% vs 53.4% |

17 of 17 comps negative both times; arms level on SEEN comps both times
(15.0% vs 13.5%). Two follow-up explanations were tested and both are dead:

- **Did holding out the Vestal starve the pool of healers?** No. 92% of the
  held-out pool still fields an ally-healer, against 93% of the full pool.
- **Did the policy never learn the heal action?** No. On Vestal turns the
  held-out arm heals **20.3%** of the time. The baseline heals **58.6%** and
  attacks 30.7% where the held-out arm attacks **61.4%**. It reaches the action
  and does not value it -- it plays her as a damage dealer.

So the blocker is not observability. The payload was legible before the profile
and the ally view is legible after it, and neither changed the behaviour.

### The recommendation that came out of this was wrong, and is retracted here

Both this CHANGELOG's earlier entry and `tools/transfer_eval.py` concluded "the
class one-hot was doing real work after all", and putting it back was offered as
the cheap fix. **It cannot work, by construction.** `--hold-out-class` removes
the class from the comp pool entirely, so that class's one-hot bit is `0` on
every step of every episode of training -- the weights behind it see gradient
only from weight decay. At eval it fires for the first time, an untrained input
on an out-of-distribution pattern. **Identity carries strictly less about an
unseen class than mechanics do, never more.**
`tests/env/test_skill_mechanics.py::test_a_held_out_class_is_unreachable_by_ANY_identity_encoding`
pins the premise, and the tool no longer prints the claim.

The apparent counter-evidence does not survive either. The Duelist run
(`4958e48`) scored **+5.0pp, CI [-9.6, +19.6]** *with* the one-hot, which looked
like identity transferring. It differs from the Vestal runs in three variables
at once -- the class, the held-out comp count (**3 vs 17**), and the encoding --
and its interval spans zero. It is an underpowered split, not a control.

### What this settles, and what it does not

**Settled:** the mechanic encoding does not give zero-shot transfer to an unseen
class. Fran's hypothesis is falsified and should not be re-quoted as open.

**Also settled:** no encoding change fixes it. Adding observation features has
now failed twice, and the one remaining "obvious" alternative is disqualified
above.

**Not a reason to revert the rewrite.** On seen classes -- which is the entire
real game, 20 of 20 classes -- the two arms are level. The rewrite costs nothing
on the actual goal and closed 89.3% of hero skills' payloads being invisible.
What it does not buy is the modded-class transfer it was argued for.

## A filed contradiction dissolves: the Flagellant's Rapturous (2026-08-23)

`ASSUMPTIONS.md` recorded `override_trait_type_ids` as **open, and contradicting
an existing decision**: `CLAUDE.md` says never to add a Flagellant-only
`berserk` branch, and the Flagellant's own file carries what looked like exactly
the mechanism for one. Investigated, and both statements are true about
different things:

* **`is_generated: False`** says berserk is never produced by the RANDOM DRAW.
  `_draw_trait`'s `is_rollable` filter was right and is untouched.
* **`overstressed_modifier: .override_trait_type_ids berserk
  .override_trait_type_chances 1.0`** says this hero does not use the random
  draw at all.

The rule the contradiction was filed against forbids a *class branch*. Reading a
record off whatever hero carries one is the opposite of that, and it is what the
rule's own wording ("never by hero class") asks for. `grep -rl
override_trait_type` over the whole install returns his file and the four
`Darkest.exe` copies — one carrier, and a test asserts that count so a second
one is a re-read rather than an extension.

**What was actually broken:** the Flagellant drew one of the seven ordinary
afflictions. Rapturous IS berserk (`DMGL25 DMGH25 DEF-20 SPD3` — the wiki's
"+25% DMG, −20 DODGE, +3 SPD"), and its `buff_ids` carry **neither** the −15
resists nor the MAXHP−10 that every ordinary affliction includes in its own
list. So the simulator was making him strictly WORSE when overstressed than the
real game does, and his six act-outs and seven reaction rows never fired at all.

Four things the wiring had to get right:

- **The record was not even parsed** — absent from `HeroLoader` entirely, so
  "unread" understated it the same way `stall_round_reset_threshold` did.
- **`is_virtue` comes from the named trait**, never from assuming an override is
  an affliction. Berserk happens to be one; nothing in the key says it must be.
  A test pins the virtue path.
- **No draw is taken for a single-entry override.** The `dot_irresistible`
  precedent: rolling against a foregone conclusion gives the same outcome and a
  different RNG stream, invalidating every seeded comparison after it. The
  shipped data has exactly one entry, so no existing trace shifts — the test
  reads `SeededRNG.call_count` directly rather than through a defaulting
  `getattr`, which is how two dead observation features shipped this week.
- **An id naming no loaded trait resolves to an EMPTY override**, falling back
  to the ordinary Resolve Check rather than leaving the hero with no outcome.

**The declared reading is that the override replaces the WHOLE outcome**, so he
can never be virtuous — the record is named for overstress rather than for
afflictions, and its `_ids`/`_chances` pair matches
`.random_monster_class_ids`/`_chances` four entries away in the same field
table, where chances are weights over a draw that always happens. The other
reading, and its falsifier (count virtues on a Flagellant run to 100 stress
under `-randomseed`; any virtue at all kills this one), is in `ASSUMPTIONS.md`.

Frequency caveat, stated rather than discovered later: afflictions fire on ~0.6%
of env steps. This is fidelity, not win rate.

## The comp search goes two-stage: structure, then kit (2026-08-23)

The GA searched classes, ranks, skills, trinkets, quirks and camp skills in one
genome. The trinket axis is both the widest (~98 unlimited choices per hero)
and the noisiest -- **24pp of measured spread** -- so a strong class multiset
drawn with a lucky trinket set outranks a better one drawn with a poor set, and
the fitness cannot tell those apart. Fran's call: split it.

**Stage 1 ranks the STRUCTURE only** -- the class multiset and the formation --
and REDRAWS every hero's kit once per episode, so kit luck integrates out
instead of being attributed to the structure. **Stage 2 is the existing
order-then-trinket polish, unchanged**, on stage 1's winners. `--polish` and its
three-seed discipline are untouched; the 7.5pp the polish is worth is not being
thrown away, it is being spent on structures that earned their ranking.

Default ON. `--no-structure-first` restores the single-stage search.

Five things that had to move with it, each a way it would have looked fine and
been wrong:

- **The hall of fame keys on `structural_key`, not `archetype`.** `archetype`
  is the order-INSENSITIVE multiset, deliberately, so two reorderings cannot
  both be emitted. Stage 1 spends **45% of its mutations on `position`** -- key
  the hall on the multiset there and every reordering it finds is discarded on
  the way in. Emission still dedups on `archetype`.
- **The kit operators are switched off in stage 1.** `STRUCTURAL_MUTATIONS` is
  `position` and `class` only: a trinket mutation whose result the fitness is
  about to redraw spends a generation to change nothing.
- **Common random numbers on the kit axis, and it is WEAKER than the dungeon
  one.** Every candidate's episode *k* draws from the same stream
  (`_kit_rng(seed, k)`), but two comps of different classes draw from different
  trinket pools, so the same stream does not hand them the same items -- only
  the same sequence of decisions. Kit variance is reduced here, not eliminated
  the way `Rollout.evaluate` eliminates dungeon variance. Stage 1 therefore
  buys less ranking power per episode than the old fitness did; that is the
  price of not confusing structure with kit luck.
- **One `evaluate` call per episode, and the seeds fan out with a stride.**
  `evaluate` derives its own per-episode seeds from one scenario key, so K
  episodes with K different kits need K calls. `_episode_seed` multiplies by
  1024 so a tuning seed's range can never run into the held-out seed's -- a
  collision there would put a tuned dungeon into the number that gets
  reported.
- **The handoff draws `--restage-kits` kits per surviving structure and picks
  on the TUNING seed.** A stage-1 genome's carried kit is arbitrary, so the
  polish needs something worth hill-climbing from; picking it on
  `validate_seed` would be the same overfit the held-out validation exists to
  prevent, one step earlier. `zlib.crc32`, never `hash()` -- Python randomises
  str hashing per process, so a tuple-hash seed draws different kits on every
  run of the same command and nothing looks wrong.

`--self-check` gained a structural leg. It is the more likely of the two to
drift, because a non-reproducible kit draw moves the score without touching the
genome -- invisible to the existing check, which never calls the new fitness.

## The hero capability profile: a falsifier came back NO, and this is the repair (2026-08-23)

The mechanic-only observation shipped earlier today on the argument that skill
payloads carry everything the 20-class one-hot did. **The held-out-class
falsifier tested that and it failed decisively.** Held-out-Vestal vs baseline,
both 3M steps, identical flags, paired per comp:

| | held-out arm | baseline |
|---|---|---|
| on Vestal comps | **17.6%** | 62.3% |
| on SEEN comps | 16.7% | 14.4% |

**Paired excess gap +46.9pp, 95% CI [+37.2, +56.9]**, all **17** held-out comps
negative (-17 to -75), not one neutral. The arms are level on seen comps, so
this is not a general-competence difference — it is specifically the class the
policy never saw.

**The diagnosis was already written in the code.** `_hero_slot_vec`'s docstring
named the cost when the one-hot came out — "it cannot reason *my Vestal can heal
me next turn*" — and named the fix: a capability profile, never identity. The
enemy slot got exactly that on the same day (`_enemy_capabilities`, the
monster's whole moveset max-pooled into the payload vector). The hero slot did
not, so three of the four ranks reached the policy as bare stat blocks.

`_hero_capabilities` closes it. Hero slot 56 -> 191, observation 1065 -> 1605.
Four decisions, each of which was a wrong answer first:

- **Three pools, not one.** ALLY / SELF / FOE. Measured on the single-pool
  version: a Vestal read `heal 0.283` and a Leper `heal 0.333`, so the policy
  could not tell a healer from a self-sustainer — which is the one fact a party
  needs about its back rank. The file already argues this case for stat buckets
  ("the policy is never asked to infer an XOR against `targets_allies`"); the
  pools follow the same rule.
- **The split reads two fields.** A two-way split on `targets_allies` alone was
  built and thrown away: that flag is the `@` prefix on `.target`, so it is
  **False** on a SELF-only skill, and Leper's Solemnity, Leper's Revenge and
  Withstand and Houndmaster's Lick Wounds all landed in the FOE pool — the
  observation claiming those heroes can heal the monsters. Self-only skills
  carry an **empty** `.target`, and that is what separates them.
- **A heal in the FOE pool is a lifesteal.** Vestal's Judgement is aimed at an
  enemy and heals her. The payload indexes carry a side for stat buckets
  (`T_` / `S_`) but not for heals, so which pool a heal lands in is the only
  thing saying who gets the HP. Recorded rather than "fixed".
- **Read the equipped 4-of-7, not the class's seven.** `--loadout-random-p`
  varies exactly this, and a Vestal who did not bring a heal must not read as a
  healer. Memoised on `(equipped ids, skill_tier)`; the tier belongs in the key
  or a rank-0 party reads with rank-5 numbers because a maxed one was profiled
  first.

**Arbalest and Musketeer produce the identical profile, and that is the result,
not a collision to fix.** Their seven skills are renamed copies carrying the
same numbers (`aimed_shot` for `sniper_shot`, `patch_up` for
`battlefield_bandage`). Under the one-hot they were two unrelated indices the
policy had to learn separately; under a capability profile they are the same
hero. `tests/env/test_hero_capabilities.py` asserts it is the **only** collision in
the roster — every other class separates.

**No checkpoint trained before this loads**, and the question is not closed: the
paired held-out-class run has to be repeated with the profile in. A win-rate
delta cannot answer it — the seed-to-seed spread (~1.4pp) is larger than most
effects this project measures, and the excess gap is the statistic that is not.

## The full data sweep: seven more mechanics wired (2026-08-23)

The effect-key audit below was one family. This is the same method applied to
**every** family the game ships -- hero and monster `.info.darkest`, trinkets,
quirks, monster brains, traits, buffs, provisions, camping, `rules.json` -- plus
a directory-level check for whole subsystems nothing reads.

**There is no hidden subsystem.** Every unread top-level directory is
presentation (`fonts`, `fx`, `shaders`, `overlays`, `panels`, `video`,
`scrolls`) or front-end flow (`fe_flow`, `game_over`, `raid_results`, `maps`,
`mods`). What was left were keys, and the filter that makes them readable is the
same one as always: rank by what a real expedition **equips or meets**, not by
how many definitions exist.

### Trinket SET BONUSES -- the biggest of them

Four `*.sets.trinkets.json` files ship (Crimson Court, Shieldbreaker, Duelist,
Runaway) and **38 `SET_*` buffs were already being loaded into `store.buffs`**.
Nothing read `set_id` or the set files, so the binding between them did not
exist and the buffs could never reach anyone. **23 of the 564 hero slots in the
comp pool wear a complete pair** -- Vestal x13, Highwayman x5, Man-at-Arms x3,
Houndmaster, Arbalest -- and got nothing for it.

- **The payout threshold is `worn >= 2`, and every shipped set is a pair.** A
  test asserts the pair-ness rather than trusting it, so a mod with a
  three-piece set fails loudly instead of paying out early.
- **`CombatantState.trinket_ids` is new.** The buffs were already flattened into
  the stat block at equip time; sets and procs need the ids themselves.

### Affliction REACTION act-outs

We modelled `combat_start_turn_act_outs` -- an afflicted hero refusing their own
action. The sibling table `reaction_act_outs` is the afflicted hero refusing
what is done TO them, and it ships real numbers: `block_heal`, `block_buff`,
`block_item` and `block_combat_retreat` at **0.33** on depressed, irrational,
masochistic and paranoid; `block_move` at 0.33 on those plus abusive and
fearful.

- **`comment_*` rows are dropped at load** -- their payload is a `BarkStress`
  voice line, not behaviour.
- **The roll is drawn only when an affliction actually carries that kind**, so a
  party with no afflicted hero consumes no RNG and every existing seeded trace
  is untouched. Two tests pin that.
- **Self-targeting is exempt**: refusing your own Adrenaline Rush is not the
  mechanic, and it would let an afflicted hero soft-lock their own turn.
- Frequency caveat, stated rather than discovered later: afflictions fire on
  ~0.6% of env steps, so this is fidelity, not win rate.

### `stall_round_reset_threshold` -- and it was not even parsed

Absent from **both** `data/rules.py`'s map and the `Rules` dataclass, so
"unread" understated it. Declared reading, from the naming pattern: the other
two thresholds on this counter are `stall_round_stress_effect_threshold` (3) and
`stall_round_summon_effect_threshold` (4), both meaning "at this counter value,
do this". So the counter **cycles** -- stalling charged +5 party stress every
round from the third onward forever, and now charges it on two rounds in four.
The other reading (4 consecutive non-stalling rounds to clear) is recorded in
`ASSUMPTIONS.md` because it points the opposite way.

Consequence worth knowing: on the accelerated path the counter reads 1, then 6,
summons, and is cleared **inside the same call**, so 6 is no longer observable
afterwards. `test_an_accelerated_stall_counts_one_then_five` now asserts the
spawn instead of the intermediate value.

### Three narrow ones

- **`attack_skill_additional_effects`** -- trinket procs on a landed hit. 28
  equipped slots across Keening Bolts, Thirsting Blade and Sunstone Searing.
  Routed through `_resolve_effect`, so a proc obeys the same `.chance` roll and
  resist subtraction as any other effect. The other **fourteen** proc hooks
  (riposte kill, on-dodge, was-hit, turn-end...) have zero pool carriers.
- **`.refresh_after_each_wave`** -- exactly two skills are both per-battle
  limited and wave-refreshed: Abomination's Transform and Runaway's Controlled
  Burn. A reinforcement wave gives their uses back.
- **`mode: .battle_start_effect_ids`** -- the Duelist opens every fight with
  Riposte and a self-debuff, in either stance. **This consumes two `.chance`
  draws before initiative is rolled**, which reordered two `test_fires_edge`
  fixtures that had been relying on the seed to make her act first. The fixture
  now pins her explicitly, which is what its own comment always claimed.

### A correction: the brain stress gates are INERT

`can_target_afflicted` / `can_target_virtued` / `can_target_not_overstressed`
were first scored at "14.5% of hall/room encounter slots". That counted monsters
whose brain **mentions** the key. **All 40 shipped carriers set all three to
`true`**, which is the default, so not one desire is gated by them. Mentioning a
key and being changed by it are different measurements, and only the second is
worth anything. Parsed and branched anyway -- three lines, free, and a mod can
set it false -- with a test recording the inertness so it is not re-reported as
a live gap.

### What the sweep confirmed was already right

Recorded so nobody re-runs it: every buff `stat_type` equipped by a pool trinket
is named in `combat_engine.py`; all six camping-effect keys across the base file
and all four DLC files are read; the in-combat provision set is correct;
`_apply_stun_recovery` already models the +50% stun-resist buff; and
`does_count_towards_stall_penalty` has **zero loadable carriers**, which confirms
the monster-side audit really was exhausted.

Two non-findings worth their line. **`carrion_eater_large_A`** is referenced by 8
Warrens hall/room rows and exists **nowhere else in the entire install** -- cut
content still named by the encounter tables, so those rows field one monster
fewer. Not our bug. And the DLC merge comment claims base wins on id collision
while `.update()` actually lets DLC win -- there are **zero** real collisions for
trinkets or quirks, so it is harmless, but the comment is wrong.

## Five effect keys the data ships and nothing read (2026-08-23)

Found by a **mechanical audit**, not by playing: enumerate every `.key` any
shipped effect file uses, keep only the effects this simulator actually FIELDS
(named by a hero skill or a loadable monster), then rank by live carriers. That
last filter is what makes the list readable -- the raw diff is 60-odd keys, most
of them Crimson Court, town or curio content, and the filtered one is 25.

The same discipline the buff sweep used: **rank by what a real expedition
equips, not by how many definitions exist.** It is why `.torch_increase` (24
fielded carriers) came out ahead of `.curio_result_type` (838), and it is the
only reason the important one was visible at all.

### The torch was an input nothing could move

`CombatEngine.torch_level` was assigned once in `reset()` and never written
again. Meanwhile:

| carrier | delta |
|---|---|
| Crusader Bulwark of Faith | **+24** |
| Crusader Inspiring Cry, Vestal Illumination | +10 |
| Runaway Hearthlight | +8 |
| Arbalest Flare, Musketeer Skeet Shot | +7 |
| Runaway Firefly / Controlled Burn, Vestal Dazzling Light | +6 |
| Shuffler Howl | **-25** |
| Crone Embrace the Dark, Cultist Incantation | -10 |
| Occultist Hands from the Abyss | -5 (self-inflicted) |

**104 of the 141 comps in the pool equip at least one of the nine hero
skills.** What makes this worth more than the carrier count is that everything
*downstream* was already correct -- the 234 `lightbelow`/`lightabove`
conditional buffs, the surprise draw, the darkness band table, the loot. The
subsystem was complete and being fed a constant.

Three rules:

- **`.torch_increase` and `.torch_decrease` both ship POSITIVE magnitudes**
  under oppositely-signed key names, so the sign is ours (`Darkness 3
  .torch_decrease 25` means -25).
- **Clamped to `[0, 100]` at both ends.**
- **`.target "global"` is the BOARD, so it fires once per ACTION.** Added to
  `_split_group_effect_names`' once list; per-target, Flare would pay +7 for
  every enemy it hit. 23 of the 24 shipped `global` effects also carry
  `.apply_once` -- the one that does not is why this is keyed on the target
  and not on that flag.
- **No conditional-buff resync is forced.** `_sync_all_conditional_buffs`
  already re-evaluates every living combatant once per action, so the
  light-gated buffs pick the change up on the same schedule as every other
  state change on the board.

### Four more, each the defining skill of its class

- **`.steal_buff_stat_type`** -- Flagellant's Suffer pulls an ally's bleed and
  blight onto himself. Without it the skill was a mark-swap that left the DoT
  where it was. The guard that matters is `eff_target is not performer`:
  extending then clearing the same list would delete the DoT rather than move
  it.
- **`.untag`** -- Arbalest/Musketeer's Flare clears a mark off the whole ally
  side; Suffer clears it off the ally it takes from; Swine Prince's obliterate
  reads it.
- **`.unstun`** -- Flare and Skeet Shot only.
- **`.bonus_action_next_turn` + `.requires_kill_target` + `.initiative_change`**
  -- Duelist's Coup de Grace grants a bonus action **on a kill**. Implemented
  as the free-action path `.is_continue_turn` already uses (the turn simply
  does not end) rather than by inserting a second entry into the initiative
  queue, so `.initiative_change 1` on the same effect is subsumed. Bounded by
  the enemies left alive, since the gate needs a fresh kill each time.

**The kill gate is checked AFTER the `.chance` roll, deliberately.** Coup de
Grace was already drawing for this effect while the payload did nothing, so
gating before the roll would have shifted that skill's RNG stream for no
behavioural gain. No shipped effect pairs `requires_kill_target` with any other
payload -- a test pins that count, because the position stops being unobservable
the moment a second carrier appears.

### What the audit found was already clean

Worth recording so nobody re-runs it: every buff `stat_type` equipped by any
trinket in the comp pool is named in `combat_engine.py`; all six camping-effect
keys across the base file and all four DLC files are referenced; the in-combat
provision set is correct (a torch is not usable mid-fight); and
`_apply_stun_recovery` already models the +50% stun-resist buff, so Fran's
"stun four enemies and next turn they resist" was never missing.

Still unread and deliberately so: `.curio_result_type` (curios are out of
scope), `.summon_can_spawn_loot`, the eleven disease flags, and
`.controlled_burn_*` (already documented as unimplemented). One real leftover:
`rules.json`'s **`stall_round_reset_threshold = 4`**, missed when the other four
stall keys were wired.

## The comp CSV is retired, and four stall keys get read (2026-08-23)

Two unrelated pieces of one session; both remove a guess.

### `COMPS/Comps <hash>.csv` no longer decides anything

Fran re-exported `metadata/COMPS/curated_comps/` -- 105 files deleted, 107
added, 32 modified, 66 of the 138 carrying a new `alias` field holding their old
name. The format did not change and nothing crashed, which is what made it
dangerous:

- **`--comp-seed 1` resolved 1 of its 12 comps**, `--comp-seed 0` resolved 3.
  The other nine silently lost their skills and trinkets. Every archived eval
  baseline was already incomparable before anyone noticed.
- **The tutorial party vanished by name.** `The_Old_Road.json` now carries
  `teamName: "Ragged Band: Heist"` with `alias: "The Old Road"`, so
  `store.comp_loadouts["The Old Road"]` returned `None` -- taking
  `data/tutorial_loader.py` and `tools/eval_tutorial.py`, the one exactly
  reproducible ruler here, with it.
- 113 of the CSV's 145 rows named comps that no longer existed.

Fran's call was to stop using the CSV at all, and the codebase had been arguing
for that for months: `CLAUDE.md`'s own "prefer `store.comp_loadouts` over
`store.comps`" exists because the CSV alphabetises formations (and `reset()`
assigns `position = index`, so that silently reformed every party) and
deduplicates duplicate-class rosters (three Crusaders trained as one).

`build_comp_pool(store)` is the single source now, iterating the JSON.
`ExpeditionEnv._build_hero_pool` and `eval_expedition.pick_representative_comps`
both delegate to it -- the latter used to keep a hand-synced copy, which is how
the two drift. The pool went 135 -> 142.

Three things fell out of it:

- **`_fires_edge_comps` is gone.** It existed for exactly one reason: a comp
  fielding a Duelist or Runaway could not be given a CSV row without shifting
  which comps every archived `--comp-seed` picks. No CSV, no row to withhold,
  no archived seed left to protect.
- **`PINNED_COMP_NAMES` had to be said out loud.** The tutorial party was kept
  out of training only by having no CSV row. It is matched by `alias` as well as
  by name, because the rename is precisely the case that would leak it.
- **`DataStore.comp_loadout_by_name`** resolves a comp by current name or alias.
  Aliases are deliberately NOT extra keys in `comp_loadouts`: anything iterating
  that dict to build a pool would draw the same comp twice.

Four fixture comps were renamed too, resolved by ROSTER rather than by guessing
at titles -- `Riposte Style (Hag)` -> `Holy Hymn: Twin Heist`, `All Good Bounty`
-> `Blood Money: Volley`, `Ascetic Movement` -> `Cross Pack: Blood`, `African
Vapors` -> `Curious Coin: Sand`. The pool-shape tests are property-based now (a
non-alphabetical formation exists; >= 30 duplicate-class rosters survive) with
one named example each as a readable anchor, so the next re-export does not
break them.

**What is NOT recovered: comparability with any eval run before 2026-08-23.**
That was lost when the comps were renamed, not when the CSV was dropped.

### Four stall keys, and an honest accounting of what wiring them changed

Nothing, yet. Recorded because "we implemented the real rule" is worth less than
knowing whether it moved anything.

`stall_extra_round_size_threshold`/`_amount` make the enemy side of the stall
test a SIZE measure rather than a head count; `stall_round_accelerated_only_
increment`/`_consecutive_increment` replace a hardcoded threshold whose own
comment admitted it was a guess. The size rule is **inert in shipped content**
-- all 61 size-2+ monsters also carry `disable_stall_penalty`, which
short-circuits first -- and the accelerated arithmetic lands on the same round
the guess did. Both are pinned by tests, including a tripwire that fails if a
large monster ever ships without the disable flag.

What the same pass CONFIRMED was already correct, from real data rather than
transcription: the 2-real-actions reset, the 1-2 hero exemption, Vulnerability
Hex (`disruptive_curse`) as the only attack with `is_stall_invalidating False`,
Jester's Solo as the only non-attack with it `True`, the accelerating-monster
set, and the +50% post-stun resist that makes "stun all four, then eat four
attacks" a real cost here. Two further readings are left open in
`ASSUMPTIONS.md` rather than guessed at -- one of them has effects in
*opposite* directions depending on which way it is read.

## The observation learns mechanics instead of memorising skills (2026-08-23)

Fran, after reviewing what the GA was producing: the model should learn that
**+DODGE is good** -- it avoids damage, avoids crits, and avoids the stress a
crit brings -- rather than that "Invigorating Vapors is good". Then a modded
class built around dodge plays correctly with no retrain. He listed the
vocabulary he expected it to reason in: stuns, debuffs, stalling, stress
healing, guard, self-healing, team-healing, buffing, damage, DoT, armour pierce.

He was right, and by more than he guessed. The skill slot was 10 floats --
`exists, legal_now, targets_allies, is_heal, is_aoe, atk, dmg_mod, crit,
is_stall_invalidating, is_continue_turn` -- and a census found **125 of 140
hero skills (89.3%) carry at least one payload none of them describes**:

| carried by | mechanic |
|---|---|
| 60.7% | stat buff/debuff (including +DODGE) |
| 17.1% | self-reposition |
| 12.9% | use limit |
| 10.7% | mark |
| 10.0% / 8.6% / 5.7% / 2.1% | bleed / stun / blight / burn |
| 8.6% | push/pull |
| 6.4% | heal AMOUNT (only a yes/no was seen) |
| 5.7% | stress heal |
| 2.1% each | guard, riposte, cure |

Two skills of one class were therefore separable **only by the 20-class one-hot
plus the slot index**. The policy was memorising `(class, slot)` pairs. A modded
class has no one-hot slot at all, so it transferred nothing -- which is exactly
the complaint. `data/types.py::EffectDefinition` already parsed all of it; the
observation threw it away before the policy saw the board.

This also reframes two complaints that looked separate. The sub-optimal trinket
picks and the unusable GA comps are **downstream**: the GA's fitness is
`P(this policy completes the run)`, so a policy that cannot see "this skill
applies stun" makes a fitness that cannot value a stun trinket either.

### What changed

`SKILL_MECHANIC_FEATURES` (45) appended after the original 10, so every index
below still means what it meant. Memoised per `(skill_id, level)` -- static per
skill, and rebuilt 7x per step without it. Observation 345 -> 581 (589 for
`ExpeditionSequenceEnv`). Rollout speed is unchanged at ~2590 fps.

The **20-class one-hot is gone**, which was Fran's pick over keeping it or
replacing it with a capability profile. Stated cost: the policy can no longer
tell which class the other three ranks are, so "my Vestal can heal me next
turn" is not recoverable from the hero block. If that matters the fix is a
capability profile, not identity.

`_threat_ratio()` is the new 8th global: enemy damage per round over party HP,
stunned enemies excluded. Fran's framing is that enemies out-damage heroes, so
stalling against four is death and against one is free -- and that comparison
was between two numbers the observation did not carry. The stall timer had been
observable since the reinforcement work, but unreadable without it.

### Four things the build got wrong first, all caught by tests

- **The heal AMOUNT was missing.** Vestal's Divine Grace carries no healing
  *effect* -- the amount is on the skill level -- so her signature skill read as
  a completely empty payload. Found by a test asserting every payload feature is
  reachable by some shipped hero skill; a feature nothing can set is a wiring
  bug, not a rare mechanic.
- **`combat_stat_add`/`combat_stat_multiply` carry the axis in the SUB type.**
  Keying the bucket map on `stat_type` alone collapsed 1085 buffs -- most of the
  60.7% -- into one meaningless bucket.
- **`stress_dmg_received_percent` (183 carriers) was unmapped**, along with
  healing and damage-taken percentages and the attacker-side chance bonuses. Four
  buckets were added after a census of what the game actually ships, and
  `_RECEIVED_IS_BAD` flips the two whose shipped sign is inverted: +20% stress
  damage *received* is a debuff.
- **`SkillLevelData.extra_damage_vs_*` is dead in shipped data.** All eight
  fields are zero on every hero skill AND every monster skill; the real mechanic
  travels as buffs with `monster_type_condition`. Two features were removed
  rather than left permanently zero.

Sign convention, and the reason target and self are separate 12-bucket blocks:
every bucket reads "better for whoever RECEIVES it", so a Weakening Curse is
negative-to-target and Bolster positive-to-self and the policy is never asked to
infer an XOR against `targets_allies`.

**No checkpoint before 2026-08-23 loads.** The falsifier Fran chose is
**transfer to a held-out class** -- train with one class removed, measure it
cold -- not a win-rate delta, whose seed-to-seed spread (~1.4pp) is wider than
most effects measured here. That harness is the next piece and is not built yet;
until it is, this is a change with a rationale and no result.

## What the project is

A Python turn-based combat simulator for Darkest Dungeon, exposed as a Gymnasium environment for RL training (`sb3_contrib`'s `MaskablePPO` — action masking is required, see "Gym env" below for why). Not a game — a faithful numeric reimplementation of the game's combat math, driven by two data sources described below.

## Hard dependency: local Darkest Dungeon install

Combat math is NOT re-derived from CSVs — it's parsed from the actual game's data files at a **hardcoded absolute path**:

```
D:\Program Files (x86)\Steam\steamapps\common\DarkestDungeon
```

This path (`GAME_DIR`) plus the CSV/repo root path (`CSV_DIR`, `F:\Personal_Fran\Juegos\Videogames\Indies\Darkest Dungeon\SIM`) are hardcoded at the top of `env/gym_env.py` and duplicated at the top of every test file in `tests/`. **Nothing in `env/` or `tests/` runs without the game installed at that exact path on this machine.** If you move the repo or the game install, update `GAME_DIR`/`CSV_DIR` in `env/gym_env.py` and every `tests/test_*.py` file.

## Two-source data model — the central design decision

CSVs (`HEROES/`, `SKILLS/`, `ENEMIES/`, `TRINKETS/`, `QUIRKS/`, `CAMP_SKILLS/`, `COMPS/`) are a **metadata index only** (roster, skill→hero mapping, human-readable references). They are **insufficient for simulation math** (max-level only, comma-decimal crit mods, free-form effect strings). All numeric truth comes from the game install:

| What | Source (under `GAME_DIR`) |
|---|---|
| Combat constants (crit, stress, death's door, stun, dodge, stall, torchlight) | `shared/rules.json` |
| Buff definitions | `shared/buffs/base.buffs.json` |
| Skill per-level atk/dmg/crit/effects (5 levels each) | `heroes/<hero>/<hero>.info.darkest` |
| Monster skills + damage ranges per variant | `monsters/<monster>/<monster>_<A\|B\|C>.info.darkest` |
| Effect templates (push, pull, bleed, blight, stun) | `effects/base.effects.darkest` |
| Trinket→buff-id mapping | `trinkets/base.entries.trinkets.json` + `dlc/**/*.entries.trinkets.json` (DLC merge, see below) |
| Quirk→buff-id mapping | `shared/quirk/quirk_library.json` + `dlc/**/*quirk_library.json` (DLC merge, see below) |
| Monster AI | `raid/ai/base.monster_brains.json` (165 brains, drives real skill/target selection — see below) |
| Enemy encounter comps per zone/tier | `dungeons/<zone>/<zone>.<tier>.mash.darkest` (real hall/room/boss/stall/named groups — see below) |
| Skill/camp-skill/trinket/quirk display-name → internal-id resolution | `localization/*.string_table.xml` + `dlc/**/localization/*.string_table.xml` (English section; used only by `CompLoadoutLoader`, see below) |
| Provisions (item defs, mechanical effects, hero-class starting freebies) | `inventory/base.supply.inventory.items.darkest`, `effects/base.effects.darkest`, `campaign/provision/provision.json` (see below) |
| Reinforcements (fixed stall summon, tier-matched) | `effects/base.effects.darkest`'s `stall_summon_A/B/C` (see below) |
| Hero voluntary move distance | `combat_move_skill:` record in each hero's `.info.darkest` (see below) |
| Per-class on-crit passive | `crit:` record in each hero's `.info.darkest` (see below) |
| Death's Door 3-tier recovery debuffs | `deaths_door:` record's `.recovery_buffs`/`.recovery_heart_attack_buffs` (see below) |

`DataLoader.load_all(csv_dir, game_dir) -> DataStore` (`data/data_loader.py`) is the single entry point that binds both sources. `DataStore` (`data/types.py`) holds typed dicts: `heroes`, `enemies`, `skills`, `buffs`, `effects`, `trinkets`, `quirks`, `rules` (`CombatRules`), `camping_skills`, `comps`, `comp_loadouts`, `enemy_comps`, `provisions`, `hero_class_starting_provisions`.

## Real per-comp skill loadouts (`data/comp_loadout_loader.py`)

A `HeroDefinition` always carries its full movepool (~7 skills) — nothing in `GAME_DIR` narrows that down, because which 4 a player actually equips is a save-file choice, not base install data. Found while investigating why a trained model's Highwayman had `take_aim` available at all: `COMPS/Comps/<name>/<name>.json` (peer to the `COMPS/<hash>.csv` used for `store.comps`, scraped from a comp-planning tool, 493 folders on disk) is a *second*, richer per-comp data source with each hero's real 4 equipped skills plus camp skills, 2 trinkets, and quirks — nothing else in the project had this. `CompLoadoutLoader.load_all(csv_dir, game_dir, store) -> list[CompLoadoutEntry]` parses it, wired into `DataLoader.load_all` as `store.comp_loadouts` (dict keyed by comp name, matching `store.comps` entries — 131/145 CSV comps have a match; comps with no JSON, e.g. modded-hero comps, fall back to full movepool downstream).

Display names in the JSON ("Hands from the Abyss", "Demon's Cauldron", "Tough") are resolved to internal ids via the game's own English localization string tables, not guessed — several skills were renamed after their internal id was set (Occultist's `bloodlet` displays as "Sacrificial Stab"; the `COMPS.csv`'s own `SKILLS.csv` even has this exact name typo'd as "Sacrifical Stab", underscoring why a name-based guess isn't safe). Hero-id parsing matches the longest known hero id as a prefix rather than splitting on the first underscore, since several hero ids contain underscores themselves (`man_at_arms`, `bounty_hunter`, `grave_robber`, `plague_doctor`) which a naive split would cut in the wrong place. `store.trinkets[id].name`/`store.quirks[id].name` are NOT used for trinket/quirk resolution — both are set to the id itself, not a real name (same shape of bug as `SkillDefinition.name=sk_id`, pre-existing, out of scope to fix here); trinket/quirk names are resolved via their own separate localization keys instead.

`CombatEngine.reset(..., hero_skill_ids: list[list[str]] = None)` consumes this — restricts `get_hero_skill_ids`/`get_valid_actions` for the matching hero to only the given skill ids, instead of the `HeroDefinition`'s full list. `hero_skill_ids=None` (default) is behaviorally identical to before this param existed, same convention as `hero_levels=None`. `env/gym_env.py`'s `reset(options={"hero_skill_ids": [...]})` and `ExpeditionEnv._sample_scenario()` (looks up the sampled comp's real loadout by name, matched to `hero_ids` by `hero_id` since CSV/JSON hero-list order isn't guaranteed to agree) both wire it through; `eval_expedition.py` mirrors the same lookup so evaluation matches whatever a comp's real loadout restricts it to during training.

**Design principle: prefer `store.comp_loadouts` (the per-comp JSON) over `store.comps` (the CSV) for anything about a comp's real hero identity/order/count — the CSV has repeatedly proven unreliable.** Two independent, confirmed bugs, both from trusting the CSV: (1) **formation order** — `store.comps`' CSV `heroes` field is **alphabetically sorted by hero class name**, not the real front-to-back formation, but `CombatEngine.reset()` assigns `position = index` whenever `hero_positions` isn't given, so hero_ids order **is** each hero's formation (verified: 0/129 checked comps had CSV order matching the real JSON formation, all 129 were alphabetical; e.g. "Pigpen Cleanup"'s real Hellion-front/.../Arbalest-back became Arbalest-front/.../Plague-Doctor-back). (2) **duplicate-class comps** — the CSV **deduplicates repeated hero classes to their unique names** (user-reported real bug: "Ascetic Movement" is really 3 different Crusaders + 1 Flagellant, each Crusader with a genuinely distinct real loadout, but the CSV's `heroes` field has only 2 unique class names, silently evaluating it as a 2-hero party the whole time — confirmed 37/131 loadout comps have a duplicate-class roster, e.g. "Prophet's Last Sight V2.0" is really 4 heroes with two Arbalests, not the 3-hero comp an earlier reading of the CSV suggested). Fixed in `ExpeditionEnv._build_hero_pool`/`_resolve_skill_ids`/`_resolve_trinkets_and_quirks` (mirrored in `eval_expedition.py`'s `pick_representative_comps`/`resolve_skill_ids`/`resolve_trinkets_and_quirks`): hero identity/order/**count** now comes directly from `store.comp_loadouts` whenever a loadout exists for the comp (132/145 pool comps) — not just as a same-multiset reordering fallback. Only the ~13 comps with no JSON match at all fall back to the CSV (alphabetical, deduplicated — unchanged, lower-fidelity behavior for those few). Skill/trinket/quirk resolution is now **positional** against `loadout.heroes`, not a hero_id-keyed dict — a dict lookup silently collapses a duplicate-class comp down to whichever entry was inserted last, applying the wrong loadout to the other copies of that class.

**Trinkets/quirks now auto-applied**: `HeroLoadout.trinket_ids`/`quirk_ids` (resolved by `CompLoadoutLoader`, see above) were captured but never wired into any `reset()` call until now. `ExpeditionEnv._resolve_trinkets_and_quirks`/`ExpeditionSequenceEnv` (reapplied at the start of every internal combat via `_apply_trinkets_and_quirks_for_current_roster`, since `CombatEngine.reset()` rebuilds a fresh `CombatantState` each combat but trinkets are permanent-for-the-expedition equipment) apply them through the pre-existing `CombatEngine.apply_trinkets_and_quirks`; `eval_expedition.py` mirrors this too. Only fires on the auto-sampled path for `ExpeditionEnv` (explicit `hero_ids` still bypasses all real-loadout enrichment, matching `test_explicit_options_bypass_sampling`'s existing contract) but on both paths for `ExpeditionSequenceEnv` (which already threaded `comp_name` through its explicit-options branch for other purposes). **Found and fixed a real gap while wiring this**: `store.trinkets`/`store.quirks` only ever loaded their single base-game file each — unlike `store.buffs`/`store.effects`, which already merge in `dlc/**/*.buffs.json`/`*.effects.darkest`. Any comp using a DLC-exclusive trinket (e.g. Color of Madness's "Thirsting Blade", `com_hel_thirsting_blade`, a real Hellion comp trinket) resolved a real id via localization but silently no-op'd in `apply_trinkets_and_quirks` since the id was never in `store.trinkets`. `DataLoader.load_all` now merges `dlc/**/*.entries.trinkets.json` and `dlc/**/*quirk_library.json` too, base wins on id collision (matches the buffs/effects merge convention) — except the Arena PvP DLC's trinkets file, which is genuinely encrypted/binary (not valid UTF-8, unlike every other plain-text game data file in the install) and is skipped rather than crashing the load; consistent with this project's existing PvP-exclusion convention (Daze, see ASSUMPTIONS.md). **Also found and fixed a real train/eval mismatch while auditing this path**: `eval_expedition.py --full-expedition`'s `run_full_expedition_episode` never resolved/passed `hero_skill_ids` at all (unlike the single-combat eval loop, which already did) — every prior full-expedition eval ran each hero with their full ~7-skill movepool instead of the real 4-skill loadout actually used during training. Both now flow through `comp_name`/`hero_skill_ids` passed into `ExpeditionSequenceEnv.reset()`'s options.

**Modded-hero comps excluded from training/eval**: 11 `COMPS/Comps` entries include one or more fan-made hero classes (Monk, Black Mage, Spartan, Falconer, Sisters, Cataphract, Thrall — distinct from the base-game class *mods*, shieldbreaker/musketeer/flagellant, which fully load) this project's data has no support for at all. `store.comps`' own `Heroes:` list for these simply omits the modded slot rather than including an unresolvable placeholder — e.g. "Holy Hymn: Monk" is really a 4-hero comp (Highwayman, Jester, Vestal, Monk) but resolves to only 3 real heroes, silently training/evaluating it as a smaller party than it's designed and balanced around. `CompsCsvLoader.load_modded_hero_comp_names(csv_dir)` detects these from `COMPS/Comps/<name> <hash>.md` (a Notion export per comp, sibling to the per-comp JSON folder) — each has an explicit `Modded Heroes: X` line, blank/absent for a fully real-class comp; matched by the `# <name>` header, not the hash-suffixed filename. Wired into `store.modded_hero_comp_names`, consumed by `ExpeditionEnv._build_hero_pool` and `eval_expedition.py`'s `pick_representative_comps` to exclude these comps outright (132/145 comps remain in the pool — see the JSON-vs-CSV design-principle note above for why this number moved around across fixes; 2 of the 11 modded comps were already excluded by the zone/hero-count filters regardless).

## Enemy encounter comps (`data/enemy_comp_loader.py`)

`EnemyCompLoader.load_all(game_dir) -> list[EnemyCompEntry]` parses `dungeons/<zone>/<zone>.<tier>.mash.darkest` for the 5 core zones (`crypts`=Ruins, `weald`=Weald, `warrens`=Warrens, `cove`=Cove, `darkestdungeon`=Darkest Dungeon, tier 6 only — it has no 1-5, it's entirely the 4 scripted story quests) using the same `DarkestParser` as everything else. Each `hall`/`room`/`boss`/`stall`/`named` line in these files is a real, game-authored enemy group with a `.chance` weight — not hand-curated, the same encounter tables the actual game rolls against. 1105 total entries across the 5 zones, wired into `DataLoader.load_all` as `store.enemy_comps`. `EnemyCompLoader.to_enemy_defs(store, comp)` resolves a comp's `enemy_ids` (in the file's own order = position 1..N) straight to `EnemyDefinition`s for `CombatEngine.reset(enemy_defs=...)`.

`ENEMY_COMPS/<zone>.json` (repo root, peer to `COMPS/`) is a **generated snapshot** for browsing — mirrors the role CSVs play for hero data (reference index, not combat-authoritative; `store.enemy_comps` parsed live from the game install is the real source of truth). Regenerate via `python -m data.generate_enemy_comps` (must run as a module — running it as a script path shadows the stdlib `types` module with this repo's `data/types.py` and crashes on import).

`.conditional.` mash files (rare, condition-gated special events — a 1-3% Shambler ambush, a Collector event gated on inventory value) and DLC zones/encounters (Crimson Court's Courtyard, Color of Madness's Farmstead, DLC-added encounters for the base zones) are deliberately not loaded — see ASSUMPTIONS.md. One real data quirk found and fixed: `warrens.2`/`warrens.4.mash.darkest` reference `carrion_eater_large_A`, but the monster's actual asset folder (and therefore its real id) is `carrion_eater_big_A` — corrected via `ENEMY_ID_ALIASES` rather than left silently unresolvable.

## Expedition length was normalised out of the observation (2026-08-08)

**The flaw**: `ExpeditionSequenceEnv._get_obs` exposed expedition progress only as
`combats_remaining_frac = remaining / total`. Normalising by the total erases the one
quantity resource pacing depends on — *how many fights are left*. The start of a 3-combat
run and the start of a 16-combat run both read `1.0`; halfway through each, both read
`0.5`. The correct play is opposite (spend freely vs. conserve), and the policy could not
tell the two apart.

**Why it only bit now**: this was latent and largely harmless while `EXPEDITION_LENGTH_BANDS`
was the invented short `(8, 11)` / medium `(10, 14)` / long `(14, 18)` — a 2.25x spread,
near-continuous, so one averaged pace was adequate everywhere. Correcting the bands against
real game data (see the dungeon-layout section below) moved them to `(3, 8)` / `(6, 10)` /
`(14, 16)`: a 5.3x spread, **bimodal**, with a gap at 11-13 combats the policy never trains
on. The same fraction now maps to wildly different remaining workloads.

**Measured consequence**, first retrain under the corrected bands, evaluated against
`eval_wikifix_3M` with identical `--comps 12 --comp-seed 1 --episodes 50`:

| band | Δ SUCCESS | comps improved |
|---|---|---|
| short (3-8) | **+4.7 pp** | 6/12 |
| medium (6-10) | -2.8 pp | 5/12 |
| long (14-16) | **-19.8 pp** | 2/12 |

The mean fell 61.8% → 55.8%, but the drop is entirely the long band — and the long band had
itself become *shorter* (14-18 → 14-16), so it should have got easier. The stress column
gives the mechanism: short-band stress collapsed (Shadowed Purse Strings 81.6 → 36.1, Dark
Ritual 116.7 → 48.9) while long-band stress exploded (Marked Prey: Snipe 105 → 176,
Shadowed Purse 158 → 185). That is one sprint pace, learned for the now-dominant short case
and then applied to 15 consecutive combats.

**Fix**: added `combats_remaining_abs = remaining / MAX_EXPECTED_COMBATS` (16.0, the longest
expedition the real game generates) alongside the existing fraction, taking
`EXPEDITION_CONTEXT_FEATURES` 7 → 8 and the observation 331 → **332**. Both are kept: the
fraction is the natural "how far along" signal, the absolute is the scale it was missing.

**This is a design change, not a fidelity fix** — it does not correct a divergence from the
real game, it gives the policy information it needs to learn. Worth keeping that distinction
when reading this file. It invalidates every earlier checkpoint (observation width changed);
retrain from scratch.

Tests: `tests/env/test_expedition_length_obs.py`, including one that pins the flaw directly
(`test_fraction_alone_cannot_distinguish_lengths`) so the absolute feature can't be dropped
as redundant.

**Caveat on attribution**: that retrain bundled this band correction with the shared-AoE-
hit-roll fix, so their individual contributions are not separable from this one data point.
The available evidence does not implicate the AoE change — the most AoE-heavy comps were
among the biggest *gainers* (`Dark Ritual: Ballad` +24/+32 short/medium, `Stun Control:
Blight` +22/+18) — but that is inference, not measurement.

## Game-trace oracle (`tools/dbwin_capture.py`, `tools/game_trace.py`) — reading the real game's own debug output (2026-08-08)

`Darkest.exe` (win64, **release** build) still contains its debug instrumentation. Mined from the binary: the full command-line flag table, including a deterministic-testing arsenal — `randomseed`, `alwayshit`, `alwaysmiss`, `alwayscrit`, `nevercrit`, `alwayshealcrit`, `alwaysdeathblow`, `alwaysapplyeffect`, `alwaysambush`, `battle_surprise_override`, `firstroombattle <monster>`, `allow_smaller_party`, `enable_autobattle`, `textsave`, `enabledebugmonsterbrainoutput`, `enabledebugqueueoutput`. The sink is `OutputDebugStringA` (the binary imports it and has no `AllocConsole`/`WriteConsole`), i.e. the Win32 debug channel — readable from pure Python by owning the `DBWIN_BUFFER` shared section. No debugger, no injection, no modification of the game. Enable the flags via Steam → Properties → Launch Options.

(`HKCU\SOFTWARE\Red Hook Studios\Darkest Dungeon\commandline` is **not** read by the game, despite looking like a settings key — it is only where the shipped `Darkest_command_line.vbs` remembers what you last typed before passing it as argv.)

**What the game actually emits in single-player combat**, confirmed across three real captures:

| Data | Sample |
|---|---|
| The entire RNG stream | `Random uniform: 0.6740028262 Seed: 1447410098` |
| Attack resolution | `Chance to hit: 0.925000` / `Roll to hit: 0.674003` |
| Damage roll | `Random int range A,B: 3 , 5 Value: 3` |
| Monster AI | `Desire: specific_skill`, `Skill chances:` / `Target chances:` vectors, `selected skill at index: N` |
| Turn structure | `Battle: state change to bs_before_turn_DOT` … (the full battle state machine) |
| Acting combatant | `check pCurrentBattleActor #N: <name>` |

`uniform == seed / (2**31 - 1)` held on **508/508** draws, so `Seed` is the raw 31-bit generator state, not a derived value. Combined with `-randomseed` that makes replay exact rather than merely distributional — the strongest form of oracle available short of decompiling.

**Calibrating the probe was itself a lesson.** The first version of `PROBE_PATTERNS` was mined straight from the binary's string pool and scored a misleading 4/11. Four of the patterns it looked for (`Initiative order!`, `Queue %d: %s: %.2f`, `Actor %d stress: %.5f`, `Actor %d buff %d turns duration`) live in the **multiplayer sync** path — their neighbours in the binary give it away (`MonsterBrain::ReceiveData`, `Reporting match`, `Local actor %d buff %d missing remote actor buff`). They need an MP session, so their absence says nothing about single-player fidelity. Re-scored against the same capture with patterns verified to fire in real SP combat: **12/12**. Don't treat a string's presence in the binary as evidence of which subsystem emits it.

`--selftest` emits one sample of every probe pattern from a *separate* process and asserts they all arrive. That exists so a negative `--probe` can be attributed to the game rather than to the capture path; without it a tooling bug and "the calls were compiled out" are indistinguishable. Two real capture-path bugs were caught this way and one by the user: missing `argtypes` on `UnmapViewOfFile`/`CloseHandle` (a 64-bit pointer marshalled as C `int`), and — the nasty one — allocating `SECURITY_DESCRIPTOR` at its **x86** size of 20 bytes when x64 needs **40** (4-byte header, 4 bytes of alignment padding, then four 8-byte pointers). `InitializeSecurityDescriptor` wrote past the buffer; the resulting heap corruption surfaced *nondeterministically*, passing in one run and failing with `ERROR_NOACCESS` (998) out of an unrelated `CreateEventW` in the next. The struct is now declared in full so ctypes computes the size, rather than the size being arithmetic in a comment.

Actor names arrive in the game's active language (a Spanish install logs `Cruzado`, not `Crusader`), so traces meant to be matched against hero ids want `-start_language`, or resolution through `localization/*.string_table.xml`.

## AoE attacks share ONE to-hit roll — found by the trace oracle (2026-08-08)

**The bug**: `_execute_action` and `_execute_enemy_skill` both looped over an AoE skill's targets calling `resolve_attack` per target, and `resolve_attack` drew its own `rng.random()` each time — an **independent** to-hit roll per target.

**The real game rolls once per skill use** and tests that single roll against each target's own hit chance. From `tools/game_trace.py --aoe` over captured combat:

```
2 targets | roll=[0.565382] chance=[0.585, 0.635]
2 targets | roll=[0.974187] chance=[0.600, 0.650]   <- both miss
2 targets | roll=[0.788809] chance=[0.620, 0.690]   <- both miss
3 targets | roll=[0.441106] chance=[0.625, 0.750]
3 targets | roll=[0.673395] chance=[0.625, 0.750]
```

**11/11 multi-target attacks shared one roll, zero counterexamples**, across 2- and 3-target skills. The per-target `Chance to hit` values genuinely differ (different dodge), so this is one roll tested against N chances, not a repeated log line. Sample limitation: no 4-target attack was captured, though the mechanic is consistent across every size that was.

**Only the to-hit roll is shared.** The same traces show a distinct `Random uniform` (the crit check) and distinct `Random int range A,B` damage rolls per target after each hit, so the fix is deliberately narrow: `resolve_attack` gained `hit_roll: Optional[float] = None`, and `CombatEngine._shared_hit_roll(targets)` returns a single draw for `len(targets) > 1` and `None` otherwise. `None` keeps the RNG stream byte-identical for every single-target attack, so this cannot perturb existing single-target behaviour.

**Why it matters**: this does not change any individual target's expected hit rate — it changes the *correlation* between targets. A real AoE tends to hit everything or miss everything; independent rolls average out. So it changes how swingy AoE comps are, not their mean damage, which makes it exactly the kind of variance-shaped gap that aggregate eval rates hide. Note `Dark Ritual: Ballad` — the worst comp in the eval at 2-44% SUCCESS — is an AoE/blight comp. Whether that is causal is untested; the retrain will show.
