Tests: `tests/engine/test_shared_hit_roll.py` (6), including a guard that two *separate* single-target attacks still draw independently, so the fix can't be over-applied.

## The bridge's observation channel is the save files, not the debug log (2026-08-09)

The debug channel emits no HP, stress, torchlight or buffs, which looked like a blocker for ever driving the real game. It is not: the game persists all of it, constantly, and `tools/dson.py` already decodes it.

**`persist.raid.json` and `persist.map.json` exist only while a raid is in progress and are deleted on the way out** — they cannot be inspected afterwards, which is why they were missed. `tools/save_watch.py --capture` snapshots each new version; 72 versions across one short raid are in `archive/traces/raidsnaps/`.

`persist.raid.json` carries: **`torchlight`**, `party` (a count followed by hero guids), `inbattle`, `camp`, `inventory`, `areatile`/`last_room_id`/`in_doorway`/`in_area` (traversal), `mash`, `loot`, and `combat_start_turn_act_out_counts`. Per-hero state stays in `persist.roster.json`, whose `actor` sub-record holds `current_hp`, `stunned`, `combat_ready`, `current_mode_id`, `health_damage_blocks`, `buff_group` and `actor_dot`, with `m_Stress`/`affliction_type_id`/`virtue_type_id` alongside.

**Granularity, which was the question that could have sunk the plan**: both files are rewritten every 1-5 seconds *during* a fight, not only at room transitions — verified across a Shambler fight where `persist.raid.json` moved 14064 → 18324 → 17436 → 19568 → 21160 → 16012 bytes. Turn-level observation is therefore possible. Torchlight decayed 100 → 94 → 76 → 58 → 46 → 34 across the raid and reset to 100 on a torch, so the values are live and correct.

**`current_hp` is absolute HP, valid only for heroes currently on the raid.** This took two wrong readings to settle. It first looked absolute (29.5, 24.0); then several heroes read exactly 1.0 at embark, so it was recorded here as a *fraction* — also wrong. Pairing a captured battle snapshot with the roster decided it: the party in that fight read 35 / 25 / 27 / 22, matching Leper / Bounty Hunter / Musketeer / Plague Doctor max HP exactly, and monsters read 11 / 21 / 15. The 1.0 readings belong to heroes left behind **in town**, whose `actor` record is simply not maintained. So: trust it for heroes in the raid party, ignore it for everyone else.

**`Field.as_float()` added to `tools/dson.py`.** DSON does not tag scalar types, so every 4-byte value decoded as an int — right for guids, ranks and name hashes, wrong for `m_Stress` (1115422720 = `0x42800000` = 64.0f) and `current_hp`. Deliberately not auto-detected: the game's class-name hashes reinterpret as plausible floats too (1181166609 → 14925.27), so a magnitude heuristic would corrupt genuine ints.

**Two mistakes worth recording.** The game crashed twice on dungeon entry while the first version of `save_watch.py` was parsing the saves in place, and that was blamed on the tool. It was not: the cause was a `-startafflicted afflict` launch option suggested here on a guess at the flag's syntax, which fires exactly at raid start. Clearing the launch options fixed it with the watcher still running. The tool was still made non-intrusive — it stats by default and copies before parsing — but the diagnosis was wrong first.

## The five dungeon layers get a number for the first time — and the first three attempts at it were flattering (2026-08-16)

Combat had a measured agreement rate (84% MATCH / 9% DIFFER / 6% `?` over 32 recommendations, third live run). **Route, torch, curio, camp and retreat had none, ever.** `Session.room` printed advice and published an `Update`, but only `Session.battle` called `_arm`, and `replay()` did not even dispatch to `room` — so five layers shipped with no contrast against anything, in a project where every live run has found a real defect and the one look Fran took at the out-of-combat block produced *"no se entiende qué pasillo te recomienda"*.

Closing that needed `Layers.block` to return its advice as **structure** (`RoomAdvice`) rather than text, for the reason the overlay already has: a claim recovered by a regex over `NEXT -> coAB  (corridor, 1 fight)` breaks the first time a label changes, silently, by scoring the wrong thing.

### The instrument, and why each rule is shaped the way it is

**The verdict is taken when the party leaves the area.** A room recommendation has no clock — the party can stand in a corridor for a minute — so walking out is the only reliable boundary.

**But endpoints cannot answer the torch, so the events are latched per poll.** The torch decays continuously while walking, so a relight followed by a walk reads as "never relit" if you compare the two ends. `latch()` runs on **every** tick, in a battle as well as out of one (the torch gets relit mid-fight, and that is still the room's recommendation being taken).

**Every layer is scored separately and they are never pooled**, because one number would let a route layer at 30% disappear inside combat at 84%.

### Three ways the first version flattered itself, all caught before it shipped

**1. Route came out 18 for 18, and it meant nothing.** From inside a corridor there is the far room and the way back; from a two-corridor room there is the other corridor and the way back. That is not a decision, and marking it scores the layer for the *shape of the dungeon*. `ways_forward()` counts the exits that are not where the party came from, off `DungeonMap.graph()` rather than off the `coAB` name convention so both readings of an id agree by construction. **16 of 18 route steps in `raidsnaps4` and 20 of 22 in `raidsnaps2` are forced**; they are counted and reported, never scored. What is left is 4 real choices, 3 MATCH.

**2. A torch decaying `7 -> 0` was being read as a deliberate shutdown.** Snuffing writes 0 in one step, but so does the last leg of a torch nobody relit — and reading that as compliance is the single most flattering mistake this layer could make, since advising darkness is its rarest call. The discriminator is that a snuff **crosses the whole darkest lit band in one step**, and 25 is `rules.json`'s own boundary: `darkness.range_table` ships pitch black as `(-inf, 0]` and the band above it as `(0, 25]`. `dark_ceiling()` reads it off the table; nothing writes it down.

**3. Four of five curio DIFFERs were not disagreements.** The captured route walks `coEF(sack) > rooE > coEI(crate) > rooI` into a dead end and comes back out, **using both curios on the return leg**. Scoring the first pass read the layer at 72% when the advice had in fact been taken. A curio walked past is now parked (`DEFER`, deliberately not a member of `VERDICTS` so it cannot be scored by accident) and settled by whether the party ever comes back; anything still parked when `summary()` runs is the disagreement it looked like.

### And one outright bug, of a class the combat marker was already guarded against

A pending room claim survived the **end of the raid**. Between two captures in one replay directory the party walks home, so the entire previous inventory reads as spent in a single poll — which produced

```
DIFFER curio  advised skeleton_key -> lost_luggage, spent 19, 8, 9, antivenom,
              bandage, bust, citrine, crest, deed, emerald, jade, ...
```

a disagreement invented entirely by the expedition ending. The combat marker has had the equivalent guard since it was written (the round number going backwards ends a fight); the room marker had none. `same_raid()` uses the two things that cannot happen inside one raid: the battle counter going backwards, and a guid **joining** the party (heroes leave it, so a subset is normal).

### What it measures, on captures that were already on disk

`replay()` now dispatches to `room` as well as `battle`, so `raidsnaps2` (193) and `raidsnaps4` (135) produce dungeon verdicts with nobody playing:

| layer | verdicts | MATCH | DIFFER | note |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| route | 4 | 3 | 1 | 36 further steps had one way forward and were not scored |
| torch | 6 | 6 | 0 | all relights; the shutdown call has no sample yet |
| curio | 13 | 12 | 1 | after the deferral rule; 5 of these were mis-scored before it |

The one real curio disagreement is `advised antivenom -> old_tree, spent holy_water, shovel`. **The samples are small and the torch column is not yet evidence of anything** — 6 relights at torch 0 is close to a forced call, the same way a corridor is. What the phase actually delivers is the instrument, and it now runs offline.

### The shutdown had never been recommended once, and the reason was structural

Chasing why the torch column held only relights turned up a dead branch rather than a rare event. `torchlight.advise` gates the shutdown on `about_to_win` — "darkness hands the monsters +12.5 accuracy, +25% damage and +5 crit, so the shutdown is only ever a free lunch on the turn that ends the fight". `Layers.block` passes `about_to_win=state.in_battle`. And `Session.room`, the only caller of `block` in the loop, runs **only when `not state.in_battle`**.

So the argument was **always False**, and PLAYBOOK 2.3's shutdown — the play `torchlight.py` exists for, and the only dungeon decision that is purely economic — was unreachable in `bridge/run.py`. Zero samples was the loop never asking the question, not the play being rare. Nothing in the module was wrong; the question was being asked from the one place that can never answer it.

`Session.battle` now asks it, with `about_to_win(engine, action)`: **one living enemy left, and it either dies to the acting hero's `dmg_min` or is already `_is_doomed` by its own DoTs.** That is the "secured kill" test `tools/check_playbook.py` already uses, so it is this repo's existing convention rather than a fresh one. The advice prints with the combat move because it is a decision about *that turn*, and it arms a torch claim like any other; the latch already runs on battle ticks, so a snuff is caught whenever it lands and the claim settles when the party walks out.

`Update` gained a `layer` field so the overlay's badge stays about the **combat** turn: without it a DIFFER on which corridor to walk would sit over the next combat recommendation as though that were the one that had been judged.

## The bridge closes: a real fight seeds the simulator, and the policy answers (`bridge/`, 2026-08-11)

The read side turned out to be complete at **turn granularity**, not just room granularity. Everything `CombatEngine.reset(...)` needs is in the two save files, in this repo's own ids:

| what | where | observed |
|---|---|---|
| torchlight, traversal tile | `raid.torchlight`, `raid.areatile` | 34.0, 3 |
| party in **formation order** | `raid.party.heroes` (count, then guids) | `[44, 7, 121, 118]` |
| party size at embark | `raid.party.start_heroes_size` | 4 — not `len(heroes)` once someone dies |
| round and **current turn** | `battle.round`, `battle.currentturn` | 0→5, resets per round |
| **full initiative queue** | `battle.initiative.{heroes,monsters}` | `14.007, 10.004, 9.005, 7.007, 5.003, 3.003, 2.002` |
| enemies in **rank order** | `battle.enemies[]` | reorders on death; `corpse_A` holds the rank |
| per-enemy HP / stun / turns taken | `…data.actor.{current_hp,stunned,performing_turn}` | 21 / 11 / 6 |
| per-hero HP, stress, affliction | `roster` → `raw_data` | 35/25/27/22, stress 42/70/62/40 |
| class, equipped skills, quirks, trinkets, `resolveXp` | `roster` → `raw_data` | `leper` → `chop, hew, solemnity, intimidate` |
| combats already fought | `raid.stat_database.BATTLE_COMPLETED.count` | real, per raid |
| party provisions | `raid.party.inventory` | bandage 6+1, antivenom 6+1, holy_water 5 |

Details that cost time to find and would cost it again:

- **`initiative` is a float32 in an int field**, the same `Field.as_float()` case as `m_Stress`. Heroes are keyed by `roster_guid`, monsters by `battle_guid` (which matches `enemies[].data.battle_guid`). Merging both maps and sorting descending reconstructs the one interleaved queue this engine models — **verified correct** against real play.
- **`battle.currentturn` must NOT be used to decide whose turn it is.** It indexes that queue correctly but is written *after* the turn it names has resolved, so following it advises one turn late. Whose turn is next comes from `actor.performing_turn` instead — see the section below, which is what live play found.
- **`affliction_type_id`/`virtue_type_id` are length-prefixed strings**, empty when neither applies — so an afflicted hero's trait id drops straight into `store.traits` and `CarryOverHeroState`. They are *not* the int name-hash the format uses elsewhere.
- **Food ships with an empty `id`** in the party inventory and is identifiable only by `type: "provision"`. Supplies also split across several slots (bandage 6 + bandage 1), so they must be summed, not first-matched.
- **`resolveXp` → resolve level is real data**: `campaign/roster/roster.variables.json` ships `resolve_level_thresholds: [0, 2, 8, 14, 24, 36, 48]`, seven entries for levels 0-6.
- **`persist.map.json` and the map layout are still undecoded**, so the one piece of expedition context the bridge cannot read is *how many combats this dungeon holds in total*. `bridge/advisor.py` defaults it to the medium band's midpoint and takes `--combats-total`. It shifts resource pacing only.

### The DSON name hash, recovered

`h = 0; for c in s: h = h * 53 + c`, mod 2³². Found by brute force from a real save, which is self-describing here — meta2 stores each field's hash and the data block stores its name, so the (name, hash) pairs are free. **200/200 matched on the first snapshot tried.** In `tools.dson.dd_name_hash`.

**Not every opaque int is one of these**: `raid.in_area` was the obvious candidate and matches none of the six real `dungeons/` ids, so it is an area *instance* id. Checked so nobody spends the time again.

### `reset(auto_resolve_to_hero=False)`

The one engine change. `reset()` normally walks the initiative queue and lets monsters act before handing over the first hero turn. Seeding from a fight already in progress, that has the engine **inventing enemy actions the real game already played**, on top of the very state being copied. The new flag stops reset short of the walk; every other caller keeps the default and is behaviourally identical, same convention as `hero_levels`/`provisions`/`carry_over`.

### Real initiative vs `SPD + d8` — the captures are an oracle

The snapshots record what every combatant actually rolled, so a core mechanic that had never been checked against the game can be, for free and with no play required. Across two rounds, 14 values, both sides: **every one lands within 8 of that combatant's speed.** The SPD term and the 8-wide spread are confirmed.

Two things this exposed:

- **Hero speed comes from the weapon tier** (`weapon_tiers[t].spd_bonus`), not `base_stats.speed` — which is what the engine already does. Checking against `base_stats.speed` makes 5 of 8 hero values look impossible when they are fine. A trap worth naming.
- **The offset is one high, and is deliberately left alone.** Real rolls land in `[spd, spd+7]`; `SeededRNG.roll(8)` returns 1-8. That is `+1` to **every** combatant equally, so no ordering and therefore no behaviour can differ — while changing it would shift every existing seeded trace for zero effect. Pinned by `tests/bridge/test_live_bridge.py::test_real_initiative_values_fit_the_engines_spd_plus_d8`.

### What the advisor does with it

`bridge/advisor.py` seeds the engine, hands it to `ExpeditionSequenceEnv` (the host env, so the observation is byte-identical to training — the base env's 324 is the wrong width for the full-expedition checkpoint's 332), and prints the move plus its runner-ups with probabilities. Against snapshot `00113`:

```
[R1 T5] Musketeer (rank 3, 27/27 hp, 62 stress)
  -> AIMED SHOT on rank 3 (Swine Drummer, 6 hp)   99%
     alt: PATCH UP on rank 1 (Leper)  1%
```

**Declared gaps in the seeding**: active buffs/debuffs/DoTs do not transfer (`actor.buff_group`/`actor_dot` are in the save; decoding their ids is a separate job), nor does monster AI intent or skill cooldown history. Trinkets and quirks *do* — they are real equipment with real stat buffs and a live roster carries several per hero.

## The acceptance test found the one defect it existed to find: the advisor was a turn late (2026-08-11)

Played for real, Weald, **Riposte Style (Hag)** (MaA / Highwayman / Highwayman / Vestal — the best-evaluated comp, and the one whose hero-side damage bands were already differentially verified against the game). Symptom, as reported: *"when it was the Man-at-Arms's turn it was still showing the Vestal's."*

**The queue was right; the clock was wrong.** `battle.currentturn` is written to disk **after** the turn it names has resolved. Reproduced offline against the capture, and the counters make it unambiguous:

| save write | `currentturn` | hero turn counters | advisor said | truth |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 00058 | 0 | all 0 | Highwayman(2) | correct, on time |
| 00066 | 0 | HW(2)**=1** | *(deduped)* | Vestal is next |
| 00108 | 1 | Vestal(49)**=1** | Vestal | **she had already played** |
| 00121 | 2 | HW(43)**=1** | Highwayman(43) | already played |
| 00128 | 3 | MaA(67)**=1** | Man-at-Arms | already played |

### The fix: `actor.performing_turn` is the clock

It is a per-combatant count of turns **taken** this battle — present on heroes (in the roster) and monsters (in the raid file) alike, and earlier misread here as a boolean flag. Anyone who has acted in round R has a counter of at least R, so:

> **next actor = the first queue entry whose `performing_turn < round`, skipping the stunned.**

That names the next hero in the *same write* that records the previous one finishing — a full save-write earlier, which is the difference between advice arriving before the turn and after it. Applied in three places that all previously keyed on `currentturn`: `LiveState.current_actor`, `bridge/seed.py::_sync_turn_pointer` (the engine pointer, or the observation goes to the wrong hero even when the printed name is right), and the advisor's poll key.

Pinned by `tests/bridge/test_live_bridge.py`: one test reproduces the real play order from the capture and asserts every named actor has *not* yet acted; one asserts the `currentturn` reading is demonstrably late, so nobody simplifies it back.

**Dataset caveat, learned the hard way**: the first capture (`archive/traces/raidsnaps/`) has no contemporaneous roster, because `--capture` did not snapshot one until this session. Its hero counters come from a later moment, so it *cannot* answer "whose turn was it" — and the counter rule correctly gives a meaningless answer there. Turn-identity tests use `raidsnaps2/` only.

### Second run: the same rule was still wrong at the end of a round

Reported as *"when there are two monster attacks in a row the text does not update properly"*. Two further defects, both in the same rule:

**1. A round that ends on a stunned combatant fell back to the stale index.** In `raidsnaps3/00174` the whole party and one monster have acted; the only combatant left is a Fungal Bloat that is **stunned**. Its slot is consumed without it acting and the round simply ends. The first fix excluded stunned combatants from the pending list, which left that list empty and fell through to `currentturn` — re-announcing the Man-at-Arms whose turn had already passed. **`None` is the correct answer**: the advisor holds its last output. Re-announcing a hero who has played is exactly what "does not update" looks like from the outside.

**2. A trailing turn counter blocked the queue.** A combatant stunned through a whole round never increments, so it comes back permanently one behind — the same Fungal Bloat sat at `0` while the party was at `2`. "Has it acted this round" therefore cannot be read from its counter in isolation, and a straggler near the front of the queue would be reported as "next" for the rest of the fight.

Fixed by using the property that makes the whole thing well-defined: **the queue is consumed in order, so whoever has acted is a PREFIX of it.** Scan forward from the last combatant known to have acted, not from the front:

```python
acted = [t.turns_taken >= round for t in queue]
last_acted = max((i for i, a in enumerate(acted) if a), default=-1)
for i in range(last_acted + 1, len(queue)):
    if not acted[i] and not queue[i].stunned:
        return queue[i]
return None                      # round ending on stun skips — hold
```

Three tests pin it, including a sweep asserting that **no snapshot in either capture ever names an actor that has already played or is stunned**.

### Third run: a corpse killed the seeding outright

The real cause of "it doesn't move on to whoever's turn it is", and not a turn-order problem at all. An audit of the whole capture showed the queue logic holding everywhere — the acted-set was a prefix in every snapshot and no actor was ever named twice within a combat. What was actually happening:

```
SeedError: unknown monster id(s): ['corpse_A']
```

A corpse is stored as `monster_class: "corpse_A"`, which is not a monster this repo loads, so `build_engine_from_live` **raised for the whole board** — and the advisor stopped producing any advice for the rest of the fight. It only appears after the first kill, which is exactly why every earlier capture (all mid-fight, nothing dead yet) ran clean and the tests passed.

The record keeps the original monster in `previous_monster_class`, stored as the format's **name hash** — the function recovered earlier the same day. `1184436572` inverts to `fungal_artillery_A`. That matters beyond labelling: a large monster's corpse occupies two ranks, and the engine takes occupancy from the definition's `size`. Unresolvable corpses fall back to a placeholder rather than refusing the board — a corpse of unknown origin still blocks its rank correctly.

Swept afterwards: **37 recommendation blocks across two full raids, zero seeding failures.**

### The save goes silent while enemies act, so the advisor now reads ahead

Reported first as "when there are two monster attacks in a row the text doesn't quite update". The save is only written when an action **resolves**, so a run of enemy turns produces no write at all — **17 seconds of silence** measured between two hero turns in `raidsnaps2/20260811-214142_00146` → `_00163`. Advising only for the current actor leaves the previous hero's move on screen for that entire window.

`LiveState.next_hero()` now returns the next hero *and* how many enemy turns land first, and the advisor always advises for that hero, labelling the wait:

```
[R2] after 2 enemy turns -> Highwayman (rank 2, 23/23 hp, 14 stress)
  -> POINT BLANK SHOT on rank 1 (Webber, 1 hp)   100%
```

The board does shift while the enemies act, so the count is shown rather than presenting a look-ahead as the current position. `build_engine_from_live(..., actor=)` exists for this: it seeds the engine pointing at the hero being advised rather than at whoever the queue says is up right now.

**How risky is the look-ahead?** Measured on both captures — 6 previews, and in all 6 the advised hero's rank was unchanged and the recommended skill and target were identical; only the confidence and the target's displayed HP moved. But the sample only contains single-enemy-turn gaps, and the concern is real in principle: **rank is not cosmetic**, since a skill's `launch` decides whether the advised hero can use it at all.

So the preview is labelled provisional, and names the specific risk when it exists. Which monsters can move somebody is real data — `move_self` / `move_target` / `push` / `pull` per skill level, plus effect `push_distance` / `pull_distance` — giving **46 of 246** monsters. In these fights the Swine Chopper qualifies and the spiders, cultists and fungal enemies do not, which is exactly why the previews held:

```
[R1] after 1 enemy turn -> Vestal (rank 4, 29/29 hp, 17 stress)
  (provisional; refreshes when they finish - Swine Chopper can shuffle ranks)
```

A preview is always superseded by a block computed from the real post-enemy board, with one legitimate exception pinned by test: the enemies being looked past can **end the fight**, so the previewed hero never takes a turn. That can only happen in a combat's last round.

### Fourth run: "several heals on targets that were already at full HP"

Behaviour, not plumbing — the advisor itself was reported as working well. It turned out to be **two independent causes**, and only one of them was the policy's fault.

**1. The bridge ignored the real gear ranks.** `CombatEngine.reset()` derives one gear tier from `hero_levels` (`tier_idx = level - 1`), a deliberate convention for sampled parties. A real roster does not work that way: resolve level and the two upgrade tracks are independent, and the save carries all three. A resolve-2 Vestal with rank-2 armour has **34** max HP; the convention gave **29**, and since `_apply_carry_over` clamps `hp = min(carry.hp, max_hp)`, her real 34 was truncated to 29/29.

Consequence: **a damaged hero could read as full**. Two of the four bad heals were exactly this — the Highwayman being healed was at 28/33 and 20/28, not full at all, so those recommendations were correct all along and only looked wrong. Fixed by applying the real `weapon_rank`/`armour_rank` as a delta against the tier reset() used (so torch modifiers survive), and by taking `skill_tier` from weapon rank, which selects the per-level row of every skill.

**2. Trinkets were read by slot number.** `trinkets.items` is keyed `"0"`/`"1"` with the id inside each slot — unlike quirks and skills, which are keyed by id. Reading the keys produced `['0', '1']`, which resolves against nothing, so **every trinket in every real party was equipped as a silent no-op**. `health_stone` is the one that bites: it raises max HP, and without it a Man-at-Arms reading 38.75 HP had nowhere to put it. HP is now written *after* trinkets, since they set the ceiling.

**3. The remaining heals were a genuine policy defect, now a dominated action.** Measured in the pure simulator over 12 expeditions: **20.8% of skill turns are heals, and a measurable share landed on targets already at full HP**. A pure heal that cannot restore a point of HP is strictly dominated — it restores nothing, carries no effect, and costs the turn — so `_is_wasted_heal` keeps it out of the legal set. The real game permits it; this is a deliberate narrowing, not a fidelity fix.

Deliberately tight, so nothing legitimate is lost: only skills whose *sole* payload is healing (Plague Doctor's Battlefield Medicine keeps `Cure`/`Cureself`, Occultist's Wyrd Reconstruction keeps its self-bleed); for an AoE heal every affected rank must be full; a hero on Death's Door is never "full".

**The trap that made the first attempt a no-op**: `.effect ""` parses to `['']`, not to an empty list, so `if sd.effects:` read every heal as "has effects" and disabled the rule entirely — on Divine Grace and Divine Comfort, the two skills it exists for. `CombatEngine._named_effects` now drops the blanks.

Measured at fixed policy, same 12 expeditions, same seeds: pure wasted heals **→ 0**, deaths **7 → 7**, steps **1306 → 1285**. No regression; the checkpoint simply picks its next-best action where the dominated one used to sit. A retrain would be needed to actually *learn* from the narrower action set.

### `--capture` filenames collided across runs

Files were named by elapsed seconds only, so a second capture into the same directory interleaved with the first and overwrote parts of it. That happened — one directory ended up holding two different raids, which briefly made a four-hero round look complete when it was two runs spliced together. Filenames now carry the run's start time; the mixed directory was split by mtime into `raidsnaps2/` (run 1) and `raidsnaps3/` (run 2).

### Two things the same run confirmed, both previously untested

- **Duplicate classes stay distinct.** The two Highwaymen were tracked separately throughout — different ranks, 77 vs 0 stress, separate recommendations. This is the bug family that has bitten this repo twice (a dict keyed by class collapses them); the bridge resolves positionally and holds up.
- **Live formation changes are tracked.** `Duelist's Advance` moved a Highwayman forward mid-fight and `raid.party.heroes` reordered accordingly, `[67,2,43,49]` → `[2,67,43,49]` → `[2,43,67,49]`. The party guid list is genuine live formation order, not a fixed embark roster.

## The save's buff table decodes: DoTs, guard and riposte now transfer (`bridge/seed.py`, 2026-08-12)

The bridge's one declared seeding gap — "active buffs, debuffs and DoTs do not transfer" — is closed. It turned out to be four mechanics, not two, and all four are fully modelled by the engine already: the whole gap was in the translation.

### `actor_dot` is a decoy — DoTs are buffs

The obvious home for bleed and blight is `actor.actor_dot`, which sits right next to `buff_group` in every combatant record. It is **empty in all 445 captured snapshots**, including fights where a hero visibly bled. Every DoT is a `buff_group` entry with `stat_type` 5. `tests/bridge/test_live_bridge.py` pins the negative result, because a later "fix" that reads `actor_dot` would get nothing and look correct.

### The buff-stat enum, recovered from named ids

`buff_group[].stat_type` is an int index into an enum the game ships only in the binary. It was recovered by taking the save entries that carry a real buff `id` and reading that id's `stat_type` **string** out of `shared/buffs/base.buffs.json` — a direct cross-reference, no inference:

| save int | engine `stat_type` | proven by |
|---|---|---|
| 2 | `combat_stat_add` | `on_crit_spd` (speed_rating +2) |
| 3 | `combat_stat_multiply` | `townFlagellationDMGLow/HighBuff` |
| 7 | `hp_heal_percent` | `on_crit_healbuff` (+25%) |
| 11 | `resistance` | `STUNRECOVERYBUFF` (stun +50%) |
| 30 | `stress_dmg_received_percent` | `STRESSDMG-10`, `BRAWLER_STRESSDMG_D1` |

**Do not extend this by assuming the enum is the alphabetical order of the stat types base.buffs.json uses.** That ordering reproduces 2 and 3 by coincidence and then misses 7, 11 and 30, because the real enum holds values no shipped buff uses. Attempting to read the order out of `Darkest.exe` also fails: the strings sit in a pooled table, not in declaration order.

`amount` is a float32 in an int slot, the same treatment `m_Stress` and `initiative` need.

### DoT amount is damage per tick, measured not assumed

A Highwayman carrying one `stat_type 5, amount 2, duration 3` entry went **30 → 28 → 26 HP** as the duration counted 3 → 2 → 1. A Man-at-Arms carrying two `amount 1` entries lost exactly 2 HP a round, and lost only 1 while one of those was paired with a `stat_type 1` entry. So `amount` is per-tick damage with stacks already summed, `duration` is ticks remaining — and `stat_type 1` deals no damage at all.

Bleed vs blight is not in the entry; `damageSourceType` carries it. 6 appears only in encounters whose monsters are `dotPoison`-only (spider_spitter/webber, carrion_eater), while 5 shows up in a `cultist_brawler`-only fight, which is `dotBleed`-only. Residual doubt: 4 snapshots of a 5 in a blight-labelled encounter, each explainable by a bleeder that had already died. It matters because `bandage` is only offered against bleed and `antivenom` only against blight.

### Guard sits on the guarded hero, pointing at its guardian

The natural reading — marker on the guardian, naming whom it protects — is backwards, and shipping it would have pointed the protection the wrong way. The game's own data settles it: Man-at-Arms `defender` fires `MAA Guard 1` (`.target "target"`, `.guard 1`) at the ally and `Defender N` (`.target "performer"`, `.protection_rating_add 0.15`) at itself. In the capture where a Man-at-Arms guards a Vestal, the +15% PROT is on the Man-at-Arms and the `stat_type 27` marker is on the Vestal.

### `stat_type 26` is riposte, not guard's other half

It looked like the guarded-side twin of 27, and counting killed that: **26 occurs 156 times against 27's 17**, in perfect lockstep with `riposte_on_hit_chance` and `riposte_on_miss_chance` (156 each). Riposte ships as a group of six entries sharing one `source` value, carrying exactly the five fields `RiposteState` holds — so an active riposte transfers whole, including its real per-weapon-rank numbers (two Highwaymen in one capture read −33%/+2% and −25%/+3%).

**The group must not go through `_apply_buff_to_stats`.** Its −33% damage and +2% crit belong to the riposte's counter-attack, not the hero's own attacks; applying it straight quietly took a third off every riposting Highwayman's damage. Caught by seeding the same snapshot twice, with and without the group, and diffing the stats. Grouping is by equal `source`, which needs no knowledge of what the source enum means.

### `duration -1` means "already applied by the equipment pass"

Permanent entries are trinket, quirk and town/district buffs — 224 inline plus 240 `townFlagellation*` across the captures — and `_sync_equipment` has already applied them. Seeding them again would double every one. Only entries with a positive duration are read, which also gives the correct *remaining* rounds: the save decrements them, so `STUNRECOVERYBUFF` reads 1 when base.buffs.json says 2.

### What is still unmapped, and why it is counted rather than guessed

`stat_type` 1 and 4 were never identified. 1 demonstrably deals neither HP nor stress damage while its duration ticks; 4's duration never ticked inside a capture. They are dropped and counted into `engine.seed_unmapped_buffs`, which the advisor prints — a wrong stat is worse than a missing one. Across the captures that is 49 entries against 354 buffs, 42 DoTs, 17 guards and 135 ripostes applied.

## Large monsters only answered to their front rank (2026-08-12)

Reported from live play: the advisor said "attack rank 3 (Fusilier)" while the Fusilier was standing at rank 4, with a size-2 Brigand Bloodletter holding ranks 1-2. Two independent bugs, one in the bridge and one in the engine, both from the same wrong assumption — that a monster occupies exactly one rank.

### The engine: `.position` was treated as the monster's only rank

`get_valid_actions` built `enemy_positions` from `e.position` alone, so a size-2+ monster was only reachable from the rank it stands on at the front. Consequences, all real:

- a skill whose `.target` is exactly that monster's **back** rank could not be aimed at it;
- a rank-3-only skill facing a large enemy on ranks 2-3 had **no legal target at all**;
- an AoE over ranks 3-4 missed a monster standing on ranks 2-3 entirely (`_resolve_action_targets` and the monster-side AoE both matched on `c.position in positions`);
- `_find_combatant_by_position` returned None for the back rank.

**61 of 246 loaded monsters are size 2 or bigger** (51 size-2, 8 size-3, 2 size-4) and **314 of 1105 real encounter comps contain one**, 72 of them with it somewhere other than the front. Measured over 80 sampled such comps × 4 heroes = 320 hero-turns: **+1218 legal actions restored, and 36 skills that previously had no legal target at all**. Some of the 1218 are the same monster reachable from either of its ranks, which is what the real game does; the 36 are the ones that were flatly wrong.

`CombatantState.occupied_positions` was itself capped at two ranks (`[position, position + 1]`) regardless of size, so the 8 size-3 and 2 size-4 monsters covered one or two ranks fewer than they really do. Now `range(position, position + size)`.

Note that `WIKI_MECHANICS.md` item 11 had already looked at the `c.position in positions` AoE filter and concluded "moot, not a bug" — correctly, for the question it was asking (can an AoE hit one monster twice?). It never asked the opposite question, which is the one that mattered.

### The bridge: a list index is not a rank

`LiveEnemy.rank` was documented as "index in `battle.enemies` == its rank". That holds only while every monster is size 1. The advisor keyed its display names by that index, so with a size-2 monster in front, **every name behind it shifted one rank forward** — which is exactly the reported symptom, entry 2's name (Fusilier) printed against rank 3 (Cutthroat).

The field is now `LiveEnemy.slot`, named for what it is; the save carries no rank of its own, and deriving the real one needs each monster's `size`, which needs the DataStore, which `live_state.py` deliberately does not load. So the translation happens in `seed.py` (via `reset()`'s size-aware slot cursor, which was already correct) and the advisor names monsters by the seeded combatant's own id rather than by any rank.

Regression tests: `tests/engine/test_combat.py` pins targeting, AoE and the property; `tests/bridge/test_live_bridge.py` pins the labelling against the real capture the report came from (`raidsnaps2/20260812-175803_00042`, Bloodletter / Cutthroat / Fusilier on ranks 1-2 / 3 / 4).

**The current checkpoints were trained under the old, narrower mask.** No reward term changed, so they still load and run, but they have never seen the restored actions on a large-monster board. That is a retrain argument, not a correctness one.

## Expedition length is in `persist.map.json` after all, and `content` is not the field to read (2026-08-12)

The advisor's last guessed input. `_num_combats` fed the full-expedition observation from a fixed default (the medium band's midpoint) because "decoding the map is a separate job".

**A tile that still has a fight waiting carries a `mash_index` into the zone's mash table; an empty one carries `-1`.** Winning the fight spends the entry, so `battles_completed + (tiles with mash_index != -1)` is the expedition's real length. Verified across every captured raid: the sum holds constant at 6, 6 and 7 while combats are fought.

The fourth run is the interesting one — it goes 4 → 5. That is a **camping ambush**: a real fight that was never on the map. So the number is a **floor** on the total, not a promise, and the test allows it to grow by one but never to shrink.

**`content` is the field anyone reaches for first, and it is wrong.** It is overloaded (values 0,1,3,4,6,7,8,10 observed) and it changes as tiles are merely explored: across one captured raid the party completed a fight and the `content == 1` count did not move, while the `mash_index` count dropped by exactly one. Same shape of trap as `actor_dot` looking like the home of DoTs — pinned by its own test so nobody re-derives it.

`--combats-total` still overrides, and an explicit value is no longer silently replaced by the map's.

## Making the large-monster fix falsifiable, and a measurement bug found doing it (2026-08-12)

`eval_expedition.py` now reports one machine-readable line: SUCCESS across a median split on **how much of each expedition was fought against size-2+ monsters**, plus how many chosen attacks aimed at a rank that was illegal before the fix.

Two things went wrong on the way, both worth keeping:

**The obvious split does not discriminate.** The first version flagged "did this expedition ever meet a large monster". Measured: **11 of 12 expeditions did** — unsurprising once an expedition runs 3-16 combats. A 92/8 split leaves the control column with no data. Dose, not presence, is the discriminating variable, and a median split balances the groups by construction whatever the distribution is.

**The eval command in `CLAUDE.md` is not the one the historical baselines used.** `eval_traits_3M.log` (the 64.4% every recent decision has been anchored to) has **36 cells**; the documented example produces **15**, and worse, `--comp-seed 0` selects **twelve entirely different comps** from the ones that log was measured on. The traits run used `--episodes 50 --comps 12 --comp-seed 1`. Comparing a new number against 64.4% with the default seed compares two different games.

### The control cell: the fixes are worth +2.1pp with no retraining at all

Same policy (`traits_3M`), same 12 comps, same 50 episodes per cell — only the engine differs:

| | SUCCESS mean | median | wipe | stall | stress |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| old engine (`eval_traits_3M.log`) | 64.4% | 70.0% | 21.3% | 1.9% | 58.6 |
| fixed engine (`eval_sizefix_baseline.log`) | **66.5%** | 72.0% | 20.3% | 1.6% | 55.9 |

The only engine deltas between the two are the wasted-heal narrowing and the large-monster rank family. In a project where 4x the training steps once bought ~1pp, two fidelity fixes buying 2.1pp for zero compute is the same lesson again.

**14539 back-rank actions across 1370 of 1800 episodes.** The restored actions are not a theoretical nicety — the policy reaches for them in three quarters of expeditions. Large-monster boards are also genuinely harder: SUCCESS 60.1% on the high-dose half against 72.4% on the low-dose half, which is the gap a retrain has to close if the fix is to be worth compute on top of correctness.

## The final-step save is not the checkpoint to evaluate (2026-08-12)

The retrain that followed the large-monster fix produced two checkpoints and they disagree by 3.1pp, which is larger than any fidelity fix this project has ever measured.

`train.py` saves at the last step. Its own EvalCallback also saves `logs/full_expedition/best_model.zip` at the best periodic eval. The 3M run's eval-reward curve, read from `evaluations.npz`:

```
-15.96, 64.88, -14.40, 8.66, 25.30, 18.28, 51.53, 66.82, 66.72, 62.21, -9.50, 64.50
peak 103.97 at 1.78M steps        last point 30.91
```

That is not a curve with a plateau to read off — it is noise with an envelope. **The final-step save lands wherever the noise happens to be**, and this run ended near a trough. Every past 3M-vs-3M comparison in this file inherits that: they were comparing final-step saves off curves like this one, so some of those deltas were sampling noise wearing a fidelity fix's name.

Measured on the same 12 comps / 50 episodes / `--comp-seed 1`:

| checkpoint | engine | SUCCESS | hi-large | lo-large | wipe | stall | stress |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| traits 3M, final save | old | 64.4% | — | — | 21.3% | 1.9% | 58.6 |
| traits 3M, final save | fixed | 66.5% | 60.1% | 72.4% | 20.3% | 1.6% | 55.9 |
| sizefix 3M, **final save** | fixed | 64.3% | 60.4% | 67.8% | 19.4% | 1.0% | 62.7 |
| sizefix 3M, **best_model** | fixed | **67.4%** | **62.5%** | 71.5% | 18.7% | 1.0% | 58.4 |

`models/dd_combat_ppo_full_expedition.zip` now holds the best_model; both saves are in `archive/models/`.

### What this says about H1, and a verdict that was issued too early

Read off the final save, the large-monster hypothesis looked falsified: the high-dose half sat still (60.1 → 60.4) while the low-dose half fell. Read off best_model it has the predicted signature — **high-dose +2.4pp (60.1 → 62.5) while low-dose gives back 0.9pp** — which is what "a change that only affects large-monster boards helped" is supposed to look like.

It is not decisive. At n≈850 per half the standard error is ~1.7pp, so +2.4pp is about 1.4 sigma. The honest summary is:

- **the engine fix is worth +2.1pp and that part is settled** — same policy, same comps, no compute (`eval_traits_3M` vs `eval_sizefix_baseline`);
- **3M of retraining on top is worth about +0.9pp**, within noise of the control cell, and only if the best checkpoint is the one kept;
- so the fidelity-beats-compute pattern held again, and the compute half of this exercise mostly bought the knowledge that the final-step save is the wrong artefact to measure.

## The simulator becomes a valuation, not just a trainer (`bridge/rollout.py`, `town_state.py`, `embark.py`, 2026-08-13)

The project had a combat **policy** and no **valuation**. Every decision outside combat — which of the four offered quests, which four heroes, push on or turn back — is the same question, *does this party survive that?*, and no heuristic answers it honestly. Meanwhile `ExpeditionSequenceEnv` plays a whole 3-16 combat expedition in about a second. So the answer is measurable: run the party forward N times and count. Zero training steps.

Deliberately the **same code path** as the eval table (`run_full_expedition_episode`, `ExpeditionOutcome`), so a rollout's P(success) and an archived eval's SUCCESS mean the same thing and sit on the same scale.

### What a real party needed that a scraped comp did not

`ExpeditionSequenceEnv.reset()` already took an explicit scenario, but everything it took described a *sampled* party. Four additions, each of which was a real difference:

- **per-hero resolve levels** (`hero_levels`) — `_original_hero_level` was one shared int. A real roster is four independent numbers.
- **gear ranks** (`gear_ranks`) — resolve level and the two upgrade tracks are three independent numbers in a save. The stat math was factored out of `bridge/seed.py::_sync_gear` into **`CombatEngine.apply_gear_ranks`** so the bridge and the rollout cannot drift apart.
- **explicit trinkets/quirks and camp skills** — a real party has no `comp_name` to resolve them by. Camp skills are not optional: leaving them empty removes the campfire's entire recovery from the medium and long bands, which would bias every comparison towards short quests.
- **an initial `carry_over`** (also plumbed through `DarkestDungeonEnv.reset`) — asking "should we retreat?" *is* asking about a party that is already hurt.

### A real bug found while wiring it: HP must be written after the ceiling moves

A Crusader with rank-5 armour at resolve 4 opened at **54/67 instead of full**. `_apply_carry_over` clamps `hp = min(carry.hp, max_hp)` inside `reset()`, against the still-resolve-derived ceiling; `apply_gear_ranks` and `health_stone` then raise it and the clamped value never catches up.

This is the same class as the bridge's "a damaged hero read as full", in the opposite direction — and worse, because it **compounds**: `_start_next_combat` re-clamps every combat, so a real party would bleed HP between fights it never took damage in. Fixed by writing HP last (`_apply_persistent_equipment_for_current_roster(hp_targets=...)`), mirroring what `bridge/seed.py::_sync_hp` already did against a live save. `None` leaves the sampled path byte-identical, and `tests/bridge/test_rollout.py` pins an explicit party expressed with the convention's own gear (`weapon_rank = armour_rank = resolve_level - 1`) against the comp path — the equivalence that licenses comparing a rollout to an archived eval.

### The Hamlet decodes, and availability is a field not a search

`bridge/town_state.py` is a pure save reader in `live_state.py`'s style (no `DataLoader`), reusing `_read_hero` unchanged — a roster entry is the same record whether the hero is in a dungeon or the Abbey. Four facts settled against the real save:

- **Availability is `roster.building_name` on the hero record** (empty = free), not a search of `persist.town.json`'s seventeen buildings and their activity slots. One field on one record, and it cannot disagree with itself. `roster.status == 1` separately marks the four currently selected to embark.
- **HP in town is not a real number.** A never-deployed hero reads `actor.current_hp` = 1.0, and a returning one keeps the last raid's value. The game restores HP on returning to the Hamlet, so nothing reads it: an embarking party is full by definition. **Stress is the opposite** — it persists, it is the binding constraint, and it is read.
- **Hero names are not in the save.** No `name`/`display_name`/`m_Name` on a roster record; the game generates them client-side. Heroes are identified by class plus guid.
- **Quest difficulty IS the mash tier.** Difficulty takes 1/3/5 and each zone ships `<zone>.conditional.1/3/5.mash.darkest` beside `<zone>.1..5.mash.darkest` — the same three numbers. So a quest's difficulty goes straight to the simulator as its tier.

The resolve cap is real game data, not a convention: `campaign/quest/quest.restriction.json`'s `resolve_level_threshold_table` `[2,2,3,4,5,99,99]` is indexed by difficulty, giving Apprentice 2 / Veteran 4 / Champion unrestricted — exactly the brackets the game shows. (Its own comment claims "COLs are QUEST LENGTH", but the shipped table is one-dimensional, so length varies nothing.) Quest length 1-4 maps to Short/Medium/Long/**Exhausting** per `localization/miscellaneous.string_table.xml`; `map_generator.darkest` defines only three sizes, so Exhausting — the hand-authored Darkest Dungeon maps — is priced as long.

### Candidate parties come from real comps, filled with real heroes

Thirty-two free heroes choose four is 35 960 parties. The obvious fix — inventing "needs a healer, needs a front-line" — is a convention masquerading as game knowledge. Instead the *shape* (the class list) comes from the 131 scraped human-designed comps, and **every hero filling it is the player's own**: their resolve levels, gear ranks, equipped skills, camp skills, trinkets and quirks. A two-stage screen (6 episodes over every fieldable shape, then a proper measurement of the finalists) keeps four quests under three minutes. The party currently selected in game is always measured, so "is my own pick any good?" lands on the same scale.

Measured against the real save on 2026-08-13: on the three Apprentice quests everything saturates at 100% and the tool says nothing useful, which is correct — those are not hard. The **Veteran warrens cleanse** is where it discriminates: options ranged from 60% down to 15% (2.65 expected deaths) across parties drawn from the same free roster, with the player's own selection at 55%.

### `reset(seed=)` does not reproduce a fight, and it changed the answer

Caught by the equivalence test flaking: `reset(seed=)` reseeds the combat RNG, but **which enemy comp each combat draws comes from `ExpeditionEnv._sampler_rng`, an independent generator that keeps advancing**. Two resets with the same seed therefore face different monsters — and since `reset()` auto-resolves any enemy turns ahead of the first hero, the party can even open on different HP. The same four heroes built twice compared unequal for reasons that had nothing to do with how they were built.

`Rollout.evaluate` now reseeds the sampler per episode from the **scenario, deliberately not from the party**, so every candidate is ranked over the *same* sequence of fights. This was not cosmetic: the first warrens table, drawn before the fix, read 45% for the player's party against 70% and 65% for two alternatives; with matched draws the same three sit at 55%, 60% and 60% — inside each other's error bars. **Most of that apparent gap was the luck of the draw**, and the tool was confidently wrong about a decision.

The same caveat applies to `eval_expedition.py`'s own table, where one module-level `random.Random(12345)` advances across every comp and band: comps in that table did not face identical enemy sequences either. Left as-is on purpose — changing it would break comparability with every archived eval the project's decisions are anchored to — but it is a real noise floor under those numbers, on top of the sampling error.

### Zone and tier are inferred from the enemy line, because the raid save has neither

`persist.raid.json` carries `in_area` (matching no real dungeon id), no difficulty, and an empty `mash` block in every capture. `persist.game.json` does hold `raiddungeon`, but it reads `"none"` outside a raid so it cannot be verified against the captures this repo has. The enemy line is the better source and is already on the board: the encounter tables are keyed by exactly (zone, tier). `infer_zone_and_tier` scores entries by **overlap** rather than equality, since a real fight holds corpses, reinforcements and the already-dead, and **breaks ties towards the lower tier** — overstating difficulty would make retreat look more necessary than it is, which is the one direction this must not err in.

### The retreat note

`bridge/advisor.py --survival` prints, under the recommendation, the simulated chance of finishing the remaining combats intact. Off by default: it costs a second env and checkpoint load, and it is cached on `(combat, party HP/stress)` so it recomputes when the position materially changes rather than once a second. **It uses its own env** — `_install` writes a seeded engine into the advisor's env in place, and sharing would overwrite the board about to be advised on.

`RETREAT_THRESHOLD = 0.5` is a convention and only labels a number that is printed in full either way. The percentage itself is a **lower bound**: it is the probability *this policy* completes the run, and the policy plays worse than a competent human, so it ranks options honestly but must not be read as odds.

## Acceptance run 5: bleed vs blight was read off the wrong field (2026-08-12)

A run flown specifically to settle one question — Holy Hymn: Riposte in the Weald, whose **only** DoT source is the Highwayman's `opened_vein`. That single-source property is what makes the result unambiguous.

**The finding: `damageSourceType` says who applied the DoT, not what kind it is.** The shipped code classified bleed vs blight from it. Two confirmed bleeds from this capture:

| DoT | on | `stat_type` | `damageSourceType` |
|---|---|---|---|
| `opened_vein` (`dotBleed 3`, duration 3) | an enemy | 5 | **6** |
| Rabid Dog's `Minor Bleed 1` | the Crusader | 5 | **5** |

The second is airtight: the only other monster on that board is `fungal_bloat`, which applies no DoT at all. Same kind, same `stat_type`, different `damageSourceType` — so the field tracks the source side (5 monster-sourced, 6 hero-sourced) and carries nothing about the kind.

Consequence of the old reading: the party's own Open Vein bleed was filed as a **blight**, which offers `antivenom` against a bleed and withholds `bandage`. Now classified by `stat_type` — 5 bleed, 4 blight — which also promotes `stat_type 4` out of the unmapped bucket, taking `seed_unmapped_buffs` to **0 across all 68 in-battle snapshots** of the run.

`stat_type 4 = blight` remains the weaker half: it appears only in encounters holding a `dotPoison` monster and never in a bleed-only one, but no capture yet contains a blight landing with a known single source. One sample still resists both readings (a Highwayman carrying a `stat_type 5` in a spider-only fight) and is left on the record rather than explained away.

### What else the run establishes

- **The turn-order rule holds 50 for 50 over a whole raid.** For every in-battle snapshot, the hero `next_hero()` names is the first whose `performing_turn` actually increments afterwards. The one apparent miss is a combat that *ended* on the predicted turn, so a combat boundary now ends the check rather than failing it. This is the defect the first acceptance run was reported for, measured rather than argued.
- **Expedition length from the map held exactly.** `battles_completed + remaining_battles` stayed at 6 for the entire raid, walking 0+6 → 5+1. No camping ambush this time, so the documented +1 caveat is still a caveat.
- **51 recommendations, zero seeding failures, zero holds**, 10 look-aheads correctly labelled provisional.
- **Still untested**: no size-2 monster appeared (bad luck in a zone where 35% of encounters hold one), so the large-monster rank fix remains validated only offline; and no guard marker was captured, though `DEFENDER` was recommended once.

### 45% of corpses were seeded as the wrong monster, from a sign bug

Found while adding the stall display, not by looking for it. `previous_monster_class` is a 32-bit hash; `tools/dson.py` decodes 4-byte fields as **signed**, while `dd_name_hash` is unsigned mod 2³². Every monster whose hash has the top bit set therefore arrived negative and matched nothing — **72 of 160 captured corpses, 45%** — and the "unresolvable corpse" fallback quietly placed whatever id `store.enemies` yielded first. `cultist_witch_A`, `fungal_bloat_A` and `cultist_brawler_A` were the three being lost.

It cost no crash, which is why it survived: a corpse still appeared, in the right rank, blocking. Its only mechanical contribution is the `size` deciding how many ranks it blocks, and the fallback happened to be size 1, so the board was right by luck rather than by construction — `next(iter(store.enemies))` is insertion-order luck and could equally have been a size-4 boss. The fallback now takes the smallest monster available, for the same reason.

### Two silent-failure bugs in the instrumentation, both mine

The run before this one produced no evidence at all, and neither tool said so:

- **`save_watch --capture` never created its output directory.** `shutil.copy2` into a missing directory raises, and the three-attempt retry loop swallowed it. Captures had always been written into directories that already existed, so it had never surfaced. Now the directory is created, the absolute path is printed at startup, and a copy that survives three attempts is fatal.
- **`--log` logged the run's bookends and not the recommendations.** A botched edit left the recommendation on `print` while only `-- raid over --` went through the logging path; `--help` looked correct, so it shipped. `tests/bridge/test_live_bridge.py` now drives the real `_watch` loop and asserts a recommendation body reaches the file.

## Buff durations are turn-scoped in the real game, and the debug channel never names a hero skill (2026-08-09)

From a human-played capture (`archive/traces/human_riposte_hag.log`, 5352 events, 59 skill executions, ~28 rounds, RNG integrity 1502/1502).

**Confirmed: buff duration is updated per TURN, twice, and there is no round-scoped update at all.** Across 2167 state transitions the complete state list contains exactly two buff-duration states — `bs_before_turn_update_buff_duration` (66 firings) and `bs_after_turn_update_buff_duration` (67) — against 28 rounds. The state machine *does* have round-level states (`bs_start_round`, `bs_before_start_round_stall`, `bs_before_start_round_check_for_death`, `bs_before_start_round_update_torch_settings`), and **none of them touches buff duration**. `CombatEngine._tick_buff_durations` runs from `_end_of_round`, i.e. once per round. This closes the "per-turn vs per-round" item as *confirmed divergent in scoping*.

**Still open, and NOT answerable from this channel**: whether a per-turn update decrements every combatant's buffs or only the acting one's. If only the actor's, "3 turns" ≈ 3 of that hero's turns ≈ 3 rounds and the engine is close; if everyone's, buffs expire several times faster. The state name cannot distinguish these.

**Hard limit of the oracle, worth recording so no one records more traces for it**: the channel instruments the monster AI and the battle state machine, not the player. `MonsterBrain:` blocks name monster skills, valid-skill lists, desires and target chances — but a grep for every hero skill in the equipped loadout returns **0 hits across the whole capture**. No hero skill is ever named. Therefore a human-played trace **cannot** report which skill a player chose, and cannot answer hero-side behavioural questions (riposte trigger rate, "was Bolster used", anything resembling behavioural cloning). It remains a good oracle for monster AI, RNG structure, state ordering and damage ranges.

`bs_after_turn_damage_reflect` exists as its own per-turn state (67 firings), so riposte/reflect resolution is a post-turn step in the real game — but with no skill names, the capture cannot say how often one actually fired.

### CORRECTION: `tools/game_trace.py`'s per-actor attribution is wrong, and so is anything built on it

`_RE_ACTOR` matches `check pCurrentBattleActor #N: <name>` and the module docstring calls it "naming who is acting". It is not. Those lines arrive **in groups of three, after a turn has already resolved** (`#1: Vestal, #2: Bone Rabble, #3: Bone Rabble`) — a candidate scan, not the acting combatant. Any "damage ranges by acting combatant" table produced by `--draws` attributes draws to whoever was named last and is therefore unreliable; the earlier version of this entry reported such a table as fact.

**What survives is the turn window**, which is unambiguous: `bs_turn_do_skill → bs_turn_presentation → [int range draws] → bs_after_turn`. Pooling bands per turn window and checking them against the union of what the party and the monsters could roll is attribution-free and is the correct way to use this data.

Done for the `Riposte Style (Hag)` capture (Man-at-Arms + 2× Highwayman + Vestal, weapon/armour rank 0, loadout recovered from `persist.roster.json`): **every hero-side band the real game rolled matches this engine exactly** — Man-at-Arms `5-9` (Crush/Defender/Bolster) and `2-4` (Rampart), Highwayman `4-8` (Pistol Shot/Duelist's Advance/Open Vein) and `8-15` (Point Blank Shot), Vestal `1-2` (Dazzling Light) and `3-6` (Judgement), plus Vestal's **heal** band `4-5` (Divine Grace) — an independent number from a different code path. 40 of 50 pooled draws are explained; the 10 that are not are monster-side bands not enumerated in that check, not hero-side mismatches.

## Overstress personalities came from a convention that contradicted a data file nobody had opened (2026-08-09)

**`shared/trait/trait_library.json` had never been read by this project**, and two docs asserted it did not exist. `_maybe_act_out`'s docstring said outright: *"the real per-personality act-out tables live in the game binary, not in any data file"*. `CLAUDE.md` and `ASSUMPTIONS.md` listed affliction/virtue personality types as deliberately out of scope. All three were wrong. The file ships 14 traits — 7 afflictions, 5 virtues, the Flagellant's `berserk` (Crimson Court) and `color_madness` (Color of Madness) — each with a complete `buff_ids` list and a `combat_start_turn_act_outs` weight table.

**Why it was worth chasing.** After camping, `camping_3M` reached 64.9% and the remaining gap was still the long band (44.5%), still failing by stress. The correlation across the 12 eval comps is nearly monotone — Riposte Style (Hag) 82% at mean stress 42.7, down to Shadowed Purse Strings ALT 4% at 153.2 — and `behaviour` sampling showed **22 hero deaths at mean stress 120**. Affliction starts at 100. Whatever the engine does at overstress is the mechanism that decides the band.

### Four divergences

**1. The simulator manufactured ~3.3x the real affliction stress.** Every act-out was charged as `bark_stress` (+6 to the whole party) at a flat `AFFLICTION_ACT_OUT_CHANCE = 0.33`. The *rate* was about right; the *outcome* was not. Real weights, per affliction:

| trait | act-out | of which `bark_stress` | the rest |
|---|---|---|---|
| fearful | 31.6% | **10.5%** (4/38) | change_pos, ignore_command |
| paranoid | 31.2% | 9.4% | change_pos, random_command, attack_friendly |
| selfish | 33.3% | 8.3% | change_pos, ignore_command, random_command |
| masochistic | 33.3% | 8.3% | change_pos, attack_self, mark_self |
| abusive | 29.4% | 23.5% | attack_friendly |
| depressed | 35.0% | 5.0% | + attack_self, mark_self |
| irrational | 33.3% | 5.3% | all six |

Mean bark share ≈ 10%, against the engine's 100%. Meanwhile the outcomes the real game actually uses — breaking formation, obeying the wrong order, hitting an ally — did not exist.

**2. Virtues had no per-turn effect at all.** `_maybe_act_out` returned `False` immediately for `is_virtuous`. Really every virtue rolls **25% per turn**: `stress_heal_self` (`HealStressVirtued`), `stress_heal_party` (`HealStressVirtuedParty`, `.healstress 4`), `buff_party` (`PowerfulPartyBuff`, +15% damage), `buff_random_party_member` (`FocusedAllyBuff`), `heal_self`. Every one of those names resolves in `effects/base.effects.darkest`, which `store.effects` already parses — they route through the normal `_resolve_effect` path.

**3. Virtues were missing six +25 resists.** Each virtue's `buff_ids` grants `STUN/BLIGHT/BLEED/DISEASE/DEBUFF/MOVERESIST25` *alongside* its `virtueXBuffN` pair. Only the pair was applied.

**4. Every affliction applied the wrong stat set.** The old code matched `base.buffs.json` ids against `^affliction(Name)Buff\d+$`. Those ids exist — but no trait references them (the virtues *do* reference their `virtueXBuffN` ones), so they are legacy:

| | engine applied | real `buff_ids` |
|---|---|---|
| Fearful | ACC−10, CRIT−8, DEF+5, PROT+10, SPD+2 | **DMG−25%**, DEF+10, SPD+2 |
| Abusive | ACC+10, DMG+10%, CRIT−8 | ACC−5, **DMG+20%**, DEF−15 |
| Irrational / Selfish | one buff of amount `0` → nothing | 13 and 12 buffs |

`AFFLICTION_RESIST_PENALTY`/`AFFLICTION_MAX_HP_PENALTY` were also hardcoded as a wiki-sourced baseline. They are entries *inside* each affliction's own `buff_ids` (`STUNRESIST-15`, …, `MAXHP-10`), so keeping the loop would have doubled them. Both constants deleted, along with `_personality_buff_ids`.

**Net direction is genuinely mixed**: 1-3 make the simulator gentler at overstress, 4 and the new disruptive act-outs make it harsher. That was accepted deliberately rather than split into two retrains.

### Two traps found while implementing

**Act-out weights must normalise over the rows valid for the acting hero's class.** `attack_friendly` ships as one row *per hero class* — Abusive has 19 of them at weight 1 each. Normalising over the raw table gives Abusive a 64.7% act-out rate instead of its real 29.4%, because 18 rows belonging to other classes count as live outcomes. `TraitDefinition.act_outs_for(hero_class)` filters first; the empirical distribution then reproduces the wiki's per-affliction totals exactly.

**The roll is once per turn, not once per `step()`.** The game's own field name is `combat_start_turn_act_outs`. A hero re-enters `step()` on every provision and on Abomination's Transform, so without `_act_out_rolled_for_index` (mirroring the existing `_dot_ticked_for_index` guard) a Courageous hero could farm party stress heals by chaining provisions.

### A separate, larger bug found on the way

**`combat_stat_add` / `defense_rating` was silently dropped — 95 entries, the 7th most common buff shape in `base.buffs.json`.** `_apply_buff_to_stats` matched only `"defense"` and `"dodge"`; the game's own name for dodge in a buff record is `defense_rating`. Every one of those 95 did nothing, from any source: trinkets, quirks, skill effects, and now traits (Fearful's `DEF10`, Abusive's `DEF-15`). Same bug class as the historic `dmg_min`/`dmg_max` and `.dotStress` key mismatches — a real mechanic disabled by a name the code never looked for. Fixed in both `_apply_buff_to_stats` and `_reverse_buff_from_stats`. **This rides along in the same retrain as the trait work**, so any score movement has two causes, not one.

### The draw pool is gated by the game's own flags, not by class

A first attempt gave the Flagellant `berserk` exclusively, reasoning that a DLC trait library implied a class-specific pool. The file says otherwise: `berserk` is **`is_generated: False`**, the same flag the `test` placeholder carries — it is never produced by a Resolve Check at all, but by a specific Crimson Court trigger. `color_madness` carries `generate_chance_modifier: -1.0` and is Farmstead content in the real game. `TraitDefinition.is_rollable` gates on both, leaving exactly the **7 afflictions and 5 virtues** the wiki documents. The `-1.0` semantics are not documented in the file; it is read as "never rolled by ordinary overstress" because that is what the real game shows, and it is declared as a reading rather than a certainty.

**Timing note**: this landed *after* the `traits_3M` retrain had already started, so that run drew from a 9-affliction pool including `berserk` and `color_madness`. Not re-run for it — the same session measured act-outs at 0.6% of env steps with 0.33 resolved personalities per expedition, so a two-entry change to the affliction pool cannot be worth 80 minutes of retraining. Flagged rather than quietly ignored.

### Scope declared

- `retreat_from_combat` carries weight **0 in every shipped trait**, so it can never be rolled — pinned by a test rather than implemented.
- `consume_item` is Color-of-Madness-only and this simulator has no inventory beyond provisions.
- An unrecognised act-out kind **blocks**, following `engine/camping.py::_requirements_met`. Treating it as a wasted turn would be inventing a mechanic and calling it game data.
- `VIRTUE_SELF_HEAL_MAX_HP_FRACTION = 0.10` is the one remaining convention: Vigorous's `heal_self` row names no effect and no shipped rule states an amount.
- The DLC Arena trait libraries are binary/encrypted, like the Arena trinkets file — skipped, not fatal.

### Affliction did not survive a combat — and the game's own camping data proves it should

`CarryOverHeroState` excluded `is_afflicted`/`is_virtuous`, documented as *"matches the real game: affliction is a per-battle mental state"*, and `tests/engine/test_carry_over_reset.py` pinned it with `assert hero2.is_afflicted is False`. **Both were wrong**, and the counter-evidence is in data this repo already loads: `zealous_vigil` and `turn_back_time` each grant an extra 15 stress heal gated on `requirements: ["afflicted"]`, and both are **camping** skills. Camping happens between combats. If an affliction cleared when the fight ended, those two effects would be unreachable by construction — the game would ship them dead.

Clearing it every room also meant a hero at 150 stress started each fight mentally fresh and drew a **new 25% virtue lottery ticket** the next time they took stress. Now carried as `affliction_id`/`virtue_id` and restored by `_restore_personality`, which re-applies the trait's buffs but deliberately does not call `_apply_affliction_effects` — that would draw a *new* personality, and a hero keeps the one they broke with.

### The result: correct, and it did not move the number

Measured at fixed policy before retraining (the technique that put the frozen-formation fix beyond doubt), same checkpoint and eval protocol, mean SUCCESS **64.9 → 65.4 → 65.1** and mean stress **65.1 → 63.7 → 65.9** across base → +traits → +persistence. Flat, with long-band stress ending *higher* (101.1 → 103.6).

The plan had committed in advance: *if long-band stress does not fall from 101.1, the 3.3x bark divergence was not load-bearing.* It did not fall.

**Why**: measured on the trained policy over 30 expeditions, act-outs fire on **0.6% of env steps**, only **4 of 30 episodes** produce any afflicted or virtuous hero at all, and median peak stress across a whole expedition is **50**. Not a wiring bug — 9 of the 9 heroes finishing at ≥100 stress had a resolved personality. Correcting a 3.3x error in something that touches 0.6% of play cannot move a headline. The eval's "long band mean stress 101" is a mean of *final* stress dominated by a few comps, and reading it as "most heroes spend the raid afflicted" was the mistake.

The fixes stay — they are correct and data-backed, and this file exists to stop correct-but-unrewarding fixes being reverted. The `defense_rating` fix is the one with real reach (21% of eval hero slots, always on) and is the more likely source of any retrain movement.

**Retrain confirmed the prediction**: `traits_3M` at 3M with `--seed 0` came in at **64.4%** against `camping_3M`'s 64.9% — flat. Mean stress fell 65.1 → 58.6, the only aggregate to move past noise, consistent with the bark-rate correction. But the long band split violently by comp: **both riposte comps lost exactly 30pp with their stress roughly doubling** (Riposte Style (Hag) 82 → 52, stress 42.7 → 97.8), while the two worst Stun Control comps gained +28 and +26 with stress collapsing (Blight 134.8 → 72.4). An untested hypothesis for the riposte collapse is recorded in `ASSUMPTIONS.md` — dodge that finally works may be anti-synergistic with a damage engine that needs to be hit — explicitly flagged as unverified.

Tests: `tests/engine/test_traits.py` (17), plus `test_an_affliction_survives_into_the_next_combat` replacing the assertion that pinned the old claim. Suite 396 → 414.

**Also corrected here**: `tests/tools/test_dson.py::test_last_party_is_a_count_followed_by_guids` asserted that the leading number in `last_party_guids` is followed by that many roster guids. A later read of the same live save returned `[4, -1, -1, -1, -1]`, falsifying it — the number is a **slot count** and `-1` marks an empty slot.

## Camping implemented — the long band was measuring a game that doesn't exist (2026-08-09)

**Why the scope changed.** After the frozen-formation fix, `rankfix_3M` reached 62.5% mean SUCCESS with stalling essentially gone (0.3%), and the whole remaining gap sat in one band:

| band | SUCCESS | wipe | stall | mean stress | firewood the real game grants |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| short | 80.7% | 9.2% | 0.0% | 38.1 | **0** |
| medium | 69.7% | 17.3% | 0.2% | 61.2 | **1** |
| long | **37.2%** | **45.5%** | 0.8% | **117.5** | **2** |

The failure mode is no longer dithering — it is dying of attrition, at a mean stress above the affliction threshold of 100. And the score tracks the firewood gradient exactly: the band the real game gives no campfire to was already matching, while the band it gives two to was missing every recovery. Camping had been listed as deliberately out of scope in both CLAUDE.md and ASSUMPTIONS.md; that stopped being defensible once the gap localised this cleanly.

**Firewood per length is 0 short / 1 medium / 2 long**, user-confirmed against the real game. `provision.json`'s `raid_starting_length_inventory_item_lists` carries firewood 0/0/1/2/4/0 across indices 0-5, which pins indices 1/2/3 to short/medium/long; index 4's four firewood is the Darkest Dungeon final quests, which this simulator does not model. **Short getting zero is load-bearing**: the short band cannot move because of camping, so it acts as a control group when reading the retrain.

**What was already there.** `CampingSkillDefinition`/`CampingSkillEffect` had existed in `data/types.py` since the loadout work, and `CompLoadoutLoader` already resolved each comp hero's `activeCampSkills` into `HeroLoadout.camp_skill_ids` — the definitions were simply never loaded, so all of it was inert. `_snapshot_and_advance_roster` also already had a precedent for auto-resolved between-combat recovery in the food auto-heal.

**Auto-resolved, not a policy decision.** `Discrete(37)` is documented as fixed semantics and the observation carries no camp state, so exposing camp choice would have meant growing both and bundling a learning-scope expansion into a fidelity fix. A fixed heuristic buys the correction alone and leaves a clean baseline to measure a policy-controlled version against later.

**Real data**: 64 skills with costs (1-5), use limits, class restrictions and typed effects from `raid/camping/default.camping_skills.json`; 12 points per camp and a 33% ambush chance from `rules.json`. `camp_rest_point_threshold` (6) is parsed but **deliberately unused** — no shipped rule states how much HP a generic "rest" restores, and inventing one would be a simulator convention masquerading as game data. All recovery comes from camp skills, which is where the real game's healing demonstrably lives.

**Convention, and flagged as such**: the spend heuristic's weights. Two calibration bugs were found and fixed before shipping it, both by inspection of real party states:

1. Scoring stress and HP point-for-point is nowhere near neutral — stress runs 0-200 and HP ~20-45. Flat scoring spent an entire campfire on stress and left a Bounty Hunter at 8/45 HP untouched. Recovery is now scored as a **fraction of the pool it protects**.
2. Fraction-scoring alone still bought a 5-point stat buff over a 2-point `first_aid` sitting unused in that same hero's kit. Recovery is now also **urgency-weighted** (`URGENCY_MAX`), so healing a hero at 18% HP outranks topping up one at 90%.

Verified across three party states: battered buys two `first_aid` for the two lowest-HP heroes plus stress relief; stressed goes all-in on stress; healthy spends on buffs and changes no HP or stress at all.

**Ambush matters and is included.** Without the 33% roll, camping is free healing and the correction overshoots in the other direction. An ambush adds one real combat, and the step budget grows with it — otherwise the hazard would be scored as the party's failure to finish. Note that `reduce_ambush_chance 1.0` (Zealous Vigil and four other effects) genuinely zeroes it, so a comp fielding a Crusader who brings it is never ambushed. That is faithful, and it is why the ambush-rate test has to strip camp skills to measure the base rate.

**Camp buffs cross the combat boundary.** They are defined to last until the end of the next battle, but camping happens *between* combats and `CombatEngine.reset()` deliberately drops all buffs. `CarryOverHeroState` gained `camp_buff_ids` — the one buff class that carries. Combat buffs still do not.

Tests: `tests/engine/test_camping.py` (13), including the positional-resolution guard for duplicate-class comps ("African Vapors" fields two Antiquarians whose camp kits differ), use limits, class restrictions, requirement gating, and that an unrecognised requirement blocks an effect rather than firing it. Suite 383 → 396.

**Requires a retrain**, and the honest framing: camping makes the game easier, so part of any gain is not the policy playing better. The point is that 37.2% was measuring a game the player never plays.

## The save format decodes (`tools/dson.py`), and damage is quantised earlier than the sim does it (2026-08-08)

A trace capture records the damage ranges the game rolled but never says which hero rolled them at what gear tier. That is in `persist.roster.json` — which despite the name is **not JSON** but DSON, a binary container (magic `01 B1 00 00`). `tools/dson.py` decodes it; hero records are whole DSON files nested inside a `raw_data` length-prefixed blob, so it recurses. Header offsets were confirmed by internal consistency (`meta1_off + meta1_size == meta2_off`, `meta2_off + 12n == data_off`) rather than assumed.

Recovered for `archive/traces/trace_diff.log`: party guids `[19, 7, 2, 1]`, all at `weapon_rank 0`/`armour_rank 0`, resolveXp 2-6 — Arbalest (sniper_shot, suppressing_fire, sniper_mark, flare), Plague Doctor (noxious_blast, plague_grenade, blinding_gas, battlefield_medicine, trinket snake_oil), Highwayman (opened_vein, pistol_shot, grape_shot_blast, take_aim), Crusader (smite, zealous_accusation, stunning_blow, bulwark_of_faith, trinkets health_stone + swordsmans_crest). **`last_party_guids` is stored back-to-front** — taken literally it puts the Arbalest at rank 1 and the Crusader at rank 4; the tactically coherent reading (Crusader/Highwayman/PD/Arbalest, ranks 1-4) is an inference, not something the save states.

**Where the game quantises damage, and where the engine does.** The game logs the **already-modified integer bounds** it rolls between:

| actor | ranges the game rolled | weapon tier 0 base | skill `.dmg` |
|---|---|---|---|
| Arbalest | **4-8** (x9) | 4-8 | sniper_shot 0.0 |
| Arbalest | **1-2** (x14) | 4-8 | suppressing_fire -0.8 → 0.8-1.6 |
| Plague Doctor | **1-1**, **1-2** | 4-7 | noxious_blast -0.8 → 0.8-1.4 |
| Crusader | **8-16**, **9-17**, 8-15, 9-18 | 6-12 | smite 0.0, + `swordsmans_crest` |

The Crusader's inflated range is not a mismatch: `swordsmans_crest` resolves to `TRINKET_meleeonly_DMGL_B1` + `TRINKET_meleeonly_DMGH_B1`, buffs that move the range endpoints themselves. The engine applies those the same way (`attacker.dmg_min`/`dmg_max` are the buffed stats). **The numbers broadly agree** — this is largely a negative result, and worth recording as one.
