The one structural difference is *when* the skill's own `.dmg` modifier is applied relative to quantisation:

```
game:  A,B = modified integer bounds        -> A + floor(u * (B-A+1))     discrete
engine: base = rng.uniform(dmg_min, dmg_max) -> * (1+dmg_mod) * mult ...   continuous
        ... -> * (1-prot) -> max(1, round(applied))                        rounded once, at the end
```

So the engine multiplies by `skill.dmg_mod` **after** drawing a continuous value and rounds once at the very end (`resolution.py:146,164,177`), while the game folds the modifier into integer bounds **before** drawing. Same mean to within a rounding step; different support and different variance, and the endpoints can differ by 1. Pinning the exact rounding rule (`round` vs `floor` vs `ceil` on the bounds) needs the torch level and full buff stack at capture time, which the trace does not record — so the rule is **not** claimed here, only the ordering.

Tests: `tests/tools/test_dson.py` (5, skipped when no save profile exists — it reads the live campaign, so assertions are structural only). Suite 378 → 383.

## The real battle state machine and RNG layering, from the trace oracle (2026-08-08)

Two captures (`archive/traces/game_trace.log`, `archive/traces/trace_diff.log` — party Crusader/Arbalest/Highwayman/Plague Doctor across Ruins, Cultists and Brigands) give the game's own turn structure. `tools/game_trace.py --states --draws`.

**Confirmed matching the engine** (recorded so they aren't re-investigated):

- **Round start ordering.** Game: `bs_before_start_round_stall → _actions → _update_torch_settings → _wave_logic_presentation → _check_for_death → bs_start_round → bs_before_roll_initiative → bs_roll_initative`. Reinforcements therefore resolve **before** initiative and join the order of the round they spawn in. The engine's `_end_of_round` calls `_check_stall()` (which owns `_spawn_reinforcements`) and only then `_compute_turn_order()`. Match.
- **DoT before stun recovery, at the affected unit's own turn start.** Game: `bs_before_turn_DOT → _DOT_check_for_death → _update_buff_duration → _update_stun`. Independently confirms the earlier "stunned combatants still take DoT" fix.

**Open divergence — buff durations tick per TURN in the game, per ROUND in the engine.** The game passes through `update_buff_duration` twice per turn (73 and 79 visits across 17 rounds); there is no `bs_before_start_round_update_buff_duration` anywhere in the 48 observed states, i.e. no global round tick exists. `_tick_buff_durations()` runs once per round over `self.heroes + self.enemies` at once. The observable consequence is *when within a round* a buff expires — whether a support buff cast on a slower ally is still up when that ally acts. **Not claimed as a bug**: the state names do not say whether the game ticks only the acting unit's buffs. Resolving it needs a capture where a known ACC buff (Man-at-Arms' Command, Crusader's Inspiring Cry) is applied and the buffed ally then attacks over the following rounds — the logged `Chance to hit` reveals the exact round it lapsed. The capture taken did not contain one.

**`bs_after_turn_actor_dot`** exists once per turn with no engine equivalent. Deprioritised: if it were a second full DoT tick, a 5/round × 3 bleed would deal 30 rather than the confirmed 15. Most likely bookkeeping for DoT applied *by* the action.

**RNG lines are logged once per layer, not once per draw.** The raw uniform, the bare int conversion and the A,B conversion all carry the **same seed** — one advance reported three ways:

```
Random uniform: 0.8884044886      Seed: 1907834115
Random int range: 3   Value: 2    Seed: 1907834115
Random int range A,B: 2 , 4 Value: 4  Seed: 1907834115
```

1798 logged lines → **1347 real advances**. The conversions are exact and verified **90/90** on real captures:

```
int range N     -> floor(u * N)
int range A,B   -> A + floor(u * (B-A+1))     [A and B both inclusive]
```

**Per-execution draw shape**: 44 of 64 skill executions are `UUUcUU` — three advances, then the `Chance to hit`/`Roll to hit` pair, then two more. **The to-hit roll is the FIRST advance but is only reported two advances later**, and the damage draw is the fourth. So the game draws eagerly where `resolve_attack` draws lazily (hit, then crit only on a hit, then damage). A differential harness cannot align draw-for-draw without modelling that; it must dedupe by seed first and attribute by state.

**Real damage ranges, ground truth for the engine's own range arithmetic** — Crusader 8-15/8-16/9-17/9-18 and 4-7/4-8/5-9, Highwayman 5-9, Arbalest 4-8, Bone Defender 2-4/3-5, Brigand Cutthroat 3-5, Brigand Fusilier 1-3, Bone Arbalist 3-7, Brigand Bloodletter 4-11, Cultist Brawler 2-4, Spitter 3-5. Checking these against the simulator needs the capture's exact hero loadout (weapon tier / resolve level / equipped skills), which was not recorded alongside it.

The AoE shared-to-hit-roll finding reproduces on this second, independent capture (pairs at lines 547/551, 1393/1397, 1947/1951, 3459/3463, 3560/3567 — identical rolls).

Tests: `tests/tools/test_game_trace_draws.py` (6). Suite 372 → 378.

## Monster formations never closed ranks — the frozen-formation bug (2026-08-08)

**The bug**: nothing in the engine ever moved an enemy on death. Grepping every write to a non-hero `.position` returned exactly five sites — the Move action, push, pull, a skill's own `move_self`, and `_collapse_hero_position_gap` (heroes only). An enemy formation was therefore **frozen for the entire fight**. Confirmed by direct probe:

```
start                             r1:fishman_harpoon | r2:fishman_harpoon | r3:fishman_shaman | r4:fishman_shaman
rank-1 dead, no corpse, +1 round  r1:(DEAD)          | r2:fishman_harpoon | r3:fishman_shaman | r4:fishman_shaman
  living enemies occupy ranks : [2, 3, 4]
  rank 1 empty and unblocked  : True
  formation advanced?         : NO - frozen
```

**The real rule** (user-confirmed, and the premise the corpse mechanic is built on): monsters advance into an empty rank ahead of them, so the **only gaps a monster formation can hold are at the back**. A corpse occupies a rank exactly like a living unit — that is precisely what makes it *block* — and it slides forward too, since a permanent front gap is something the real game never shows.

The old `_collapse_hero_position_gap` docstring asserted enemies were excluded "deliberately… their corpse blocks the rank until cleared". That conflated two different things: *a corpse blocks its own rank* (true) with *nothing ever advances* (false). It also made this file's own DoT entry vacuous — "finishing something with bleed/blight **opens the lane** that a normal kill does not" means nothing if no one ever walks through the lane.

**Why it was load-bearing rather than cosmetic.** No hero skill in the game reaches enemy rank 4 from every position, and the Abomination cannot reach rank 4 in **any** form:

| skill | form | launch (from hero rank) | target (enemy ranks) |
|---|---|---|---|
| manacles | human | 3,2 | 1,2,3 |
| vomit | human | 3,2 | 2,3 (AoE) |
| rake | beast | 2,1 | 1,2 |
| rage | beast | 2,1 | 1,2,3 |
| slam | beast | 3,2,1 | 1,2 |

So a surviving back-rank monster (a rank-4 `fishman_shaman_B` — 17 HP, a stress caster the policy targets last) was **permanently untargetable** by him. His remaining legal actions were Transform (`per_battle_limit 2`), Move, a provision, or **Absolution** — a self-heal with `atk 0`, no cooldown, no per-battle limit, and `is_stall_invalidating False`. The policy took Absolution, forever. From `battle_report.py`, both Abominations at full HP and zero stress:

```
> Abomination (rank 3, HP 41/41, stress 0) uses absolution -> self/none
> Abomination (rank 2, HP 41/41, stress 0) uses absolution -> self/none
```

The game's own stall penalty cannot catch this: `_check_stall` requires `alive_enemies <= stall_enemy_size_threshold` (2), and the stalemate holds at 3-4 alive. Correctly so — the real game wouldn't penalise it either, because in the real game the formation would have closed and the fight would have ended.

**Measured on the trained policy**, `--band long`, 10 episodes/comp, same checkpoint before and after:

| comp | Abominations | env steps/combat | truncation rate | combats completed |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Stun Control: Beast | 2 | **228.4 → 35.4** | 30% → **0%** | 13 → **43** |
| Stun Control: Blight | 1 | **94.9 → 22.9** | 20% → **0%** | 36 → **88** |
| All Good Hellion | 1 | 35.7 → 14.5 | 20% → **0%** | 68 → 75 |
| Riposte Style (Hag) *(control, 0 Abom)* | 0 | 18.6 → 16.0 | 0% → 0% | 114 → 119 |

Absolution went from **57% of every env step** in Stun Control: Beast to absent from its top four (attack is now 51%). Truncation went to 0% in 10 of 12 comps. This is the *old* policy, trained against the frozen engine — a retrain should do better still.

**How it was found.** Not by reading the engine. The eval's long band showed `Stun Control: Beast` at 2% SUCCESS with only 26% wipes, 70% stall-outs, 67 mean stress and 6.1 of 15 combats reached — a party that was alive and healthy and simply not finishing. Cross-tabulating stall rate against Abomination count over 12 comps × 2 independent retrains put Beast (the only 2-Abomination comp) first in both. A prior hypothesis — that free actions (`is_continue_turn` Transform, provisions) were burning env steps without consuming turns — was **refuted** by the same probe: free actions are 3-17% everywhere and were *lowest* in exactly the comps that stalled.

**Caveat on `Dark Ritual: Ballad`**: it completed *fewer* combats after the fix (27 → 17) at 0% truncation, i.e. it now wipes instead of dithering. Same pattern as the Riposte-ACC and stun/DoT fixes — more fidelity producing a harder, more honest number.

Tests: `tests/engine/test_enemy_ranks.py` (7), including the back-gaps-only invariant over every death subset, corpse blocking, corpse rot releasing the rank, size-2 slot accounting, and a guard that the real kill path (not just the helper) triggers the advance. Suite 365 → 372.

**Requires a retrain**: the enemy formation is an observation input and a targeting domain, so every existing checkpoint was trained against a different game.

## Dungeon layout / expedition length (`data/map_gen_loader.py`) — a data source the docs said didn't exist (2026-08-08)

**The bug**: `EXPEDITION_LENGTH_BANDS` (`env/expedition_sequence_env.py`) was short `(8, 11)` / medium `(10, 14)` / long `(14, 18)` combats, documented in both `CLAUDE.md` and `ASSUMPTIONS.md` as a necessary simulator convention because "dungeon layout/room-count generation is hardcoded in the game binary, not exposed in any data file — confirmed via exhaustive search."

**That was wrong.** `scripts/map_generator.darkest` ships with the game in plain text and states the counts directly: 44 `map:` records keyed by `(size, quest_type, dungeon_type)`, each carrying `.base_room_number`, `.base_corridor_number`, `.hallway_battle <min> <max>` and `.total_room_battles <min> <max>`. Combats in a raid = the two battle ranges summed (hallway battles and room battles are disjoint sets of fights).

**Why the earlier search plausibly missed it**: the file uses the **multi-line block layout** — `map:` alone on a line, then one `.key value` per following line — whereas every `.darkest` file the project had parsed until now puts a whole record on one line. Run this repo's own `DarkestParser.parse_file` on it and you get 44 records back with **every field silently dropped** (`{'_type': 'map'}` × 44), because the `.key` lines contain no `:` and are skipped, and the `map:` header has an empty body. It reads exactly like a file with no relevant content. Fixed by adding `DarkestParser.parse_blocks`, which rejoins each block into one logical line and delegates to the same `_parse_line` — both layouts therefore share one set of value-parsing quirks rather than growing a second, divergent parser. `tests/data/test_map_gen.py::test_block_parser_keeps_keys_the_line_parser_drops` asserts the exact contrast (44/44 records both ways; all fields lost via `parse_file`, all kept via `parse_blocks`) so the silent-empty-record mode can't come back unnoticed.

**Measured gap** (real = envelope across all quest types and zones per size):

| band | invented convention | real (`map_generator.darkest`) |
|---|---|---|
| short | 8–11 | **3–8** |
| medium | 10–14 | **6–10** |
| long | 14–18 | 14–16 |

Short and medium ran roughly **twice** the real number of combats; the inversion is the severe part — a simulated "short" expedition contained *more* fights than a real **medium** one. Since `ExpeditionSequenceEnv` carries HP/stress/Death's-Door across combats, expedition length is directly an attrition dial, so every training run and every eval table produced to date was scored against a materially harsher-than-real campaign. This is a plausible contributor to the persistent wipe rate (23.3% at the best checkpoint, `eval_wikifix_3M`), though that link is inference, not yet measured — the retrain will settle it.

**Consequence**: this changes the episode distribution, so `--resume` is invalid across it and **the model must be retrained from scratch**; pre-existing eval logs are not comparable to post-change ones and a fresh baseline is required. All prior checkpoints/logs were moved to `archive/` rather than deleted for exactly this reason.

Multi-value numerics (`.hallway_battle 2 4`) reach the loader as the **string** `"2 4"` — `_parse_value` leaves it alone since it matches neither the int nor the float pattern. `MapGenLoader._pair` splits it into an inclusive `(min, max)`. `MapGenLoader.combat_count_bands` collapses the 44 records to one band per size (the simulator has no `quest_type` concept — verified, zero references — so a size's band is the envelope across every quest type and zone), and **forces `short` → `medium` → `long` insertion order** rather than inheriting the file's: callers index these positionally (`list(EXPEDITION_LENGTH_BANDS)[ep % 3]` in `tools/analyze_behaviour.py`, `list(...keys())` in `eval_expedition.py`), so a reordering upstream would silently relabel every eval column.

Kept as a module-level dict parsed at import time (one small file, no `DataStore` needed) so the public API that `eval_expedition.py` and `tools/analyze_behaviour.py` import is unchanged; also exposed as `store.map_gen_settings` for anything wanting the full per-quest/per-zone detail. A hardcoded fallback matching the derived values guards a missing game install.

**Still genuinely convention**: the provision quantities (`provision.json` has only per-length purchase *limits*, not recommended loadouts) and `TIER_TO_HERO_LEVEL`. The lesson generalizes though — "confirmed via exhaustive search that no data file exposes X" is only as strong as the parser used to search, and this project had a parser with a silent-failure mode for an entire file layout.

## The dungeon map decodes: `persist.map.json` -> a graph (`bridge/dungeon_map.py`, 2026-08-14)

The third thing the bridge can read, and the input both route advice (PLAYBOOK 2.2) and
curio advice (PLAYBOOK 2.4) need. Built after the expert interview fixed the build order
to "expedition layer first, town last". No engine change, no training.

**The graph is in the area names.** Areas are `rooA`, `rooB`, `coAB` — a room is
`roo<letter>`, a corridor is `co<letter><letter>` naming the two rooms it joins. Nothing
else in the file stores connectivity, so a decoder that ignores the names has no map.

**The file uses two different id conventions, and mixing them finds nothing.**
`entrance_id`/`final_room_id` are the 4-char area name packed **little-endian ASCII**
(`1097822066` -> `rooA`); `curio_prop`/`trap` are `dd_name_hash`. Inverting the wrong one
returns silence, not an error. `final_room_id` reads `'none'` until the quest goal room is
known. The hash side inverts cleanly once decoded **unsigned** — 22/22 distinct curios and
2/2 traps in `archive/traces/raidsnaps*/` resolved against `curios/curio_props.csv` and
`props/trap_definitions.json`. `poison_cloud` arrives as `-702901882`, the same
signed/unsigned trap that cost the corpse decoder 45% of its lookups.

**`content` is the live state; `curio_prop` is the identity.** A used curio keeps its
`curio_prop` and drops to `content = 0`. 1232 of the captured curio tiles are already
consumed, so a decoder that lists "what loot is left" by the id field re-advertises all of
them.

**The content enum, and how each code was pinned.** `scripts/map_generator.darkest` names
the vocabulary — corridors get battle/trap/obstacle/curio/hunger, rooms get
battle/curio/treasure plus guarded variants. Most codes fall out of co-occurrence (a
battle tile always carries a `mash_index`, a trap tile a `trap`, and the guarded room
codes resolve to `heirloom_chest`/`locked_strongbox` for treasure against
`eldritch_altar` for curio). **4 vs 8 needed a separate argument**, both being
corridor-only with nothing to key on: every `hallway_hunger` range in the generator has a
minimum of **1** while 33 of 44 `hallway_obstacle` ranges start at **0**, and across the
30 distinct captured maps code 8 is never absent while code 4 is absent in 5. Hence
4 = obstacle, 8 = hunger.

**An unrecognised code is reported, not guessed at**, and the test suite fails on it.
That is how code 9 (unguarded room treasure) was found: it appears **6 times in the whole
corpus** against 361 of its guarded twin, so a survey of the first 120 snapshots missed it
entirely and only the full-corpus test caught it. `room_curio` presumably has a code that
has still never been captured.

**`mash_index == 0` is a real index.** A throwaway survey wrote
`t.get("mash_index") or -1`, which reclassified the 59 battle tiles at index 0 as having
no mash table — and that fiction was briefly written up as "quest/boss fights the game
scripts rather than rolls". It is not: every battle tile in the corpus carries
`mash_index >= 0`. `has_battle` keys on `content` regardless, because that is the field
that says whether the fight is still there and it makes the guarded room codes count as
the battles they are. `tests/bridge/test_dungeon_map.py` pins the zero case so the truthiness bug
cannot come back.

**`knowledge` is tri-state** — 1 unknown, 2 scouted, 3 visited — confirmed by watching it
climb 1 -> 3 area by area across a run's ordered snapshots. `explored_fraction()` is
deliberately labelled an estimate: an explore quest wants 90% of *something*, and which
denominator the game counts is unconfirmed until a live run pins it.

## Curio policy — the expert rule as a falsifiable prediction (`bridge/curios.py`, `data/curio_loader.py`, 2026-08-14)

PLAYBOOK 2.4's curio rules, implemented **without hard-coding any of them**. Every
outcome is scored from `curios/curio_type_library.csv`, resolving each concrete result
against the real effect and quirk definitions, so the expert rules become predictions the
module can be wrong about. It was wrong about one, and so was the prose written alongside
it.

**The format is a spreadsheet dump, not a data file.** One block per curio, with two
unrelated tables sharing the same rows: column 2 runs a vertical key/value list
(`ID STRING`, `REGION FOUND`, `TAGS`) while column 4 simultaneously runs the fixed
result-type ladder (`Nothing`/`Loot`/`Quirk`/`Effect`/`Purge`/`Scouting`/`Teleport`/
`Disease`) with weights beside it. Neither table can be read without ignoring the other.
**The weights are the truth and the `% CHANCE` column is stale** in several blocks — it is
the author's own arithmetic, left behind by later edits.

**`curio_prop` in the save is a prop name; the library is keyed by type.** They are equal
for most curios and not for all, so `curios/curio_props.csv` supplies a mapping that is
not optional.

**Deliberately not wired into `DataLoader.load_all`.** The engine does not simulate
curios; loading this on every env construction would be pure cost. `bridge/curios.py`
loads it lazily and caches.

### Three things the tables settle that the rule alone gets wrong

- **`Loot: JOURNALONLY` is not loot** — a journal page, no resources. It is
  `stack_of_books`' only loot result, and scoring it as loot makes books look worth
  opening.
- **A matching provision is not automatically good.** 4 of the 46 item interactions hurt:
  `stack_of_books` and `bas_relief` answer their item with `Stress 5`, `decorative_urn`
  answers a shovel with the negative quirk `guilty_conscience`, and the Shambler's altar
  answers a torch with a boss. `bas_relief` is the counterexample to a blanket "dig
  whenever you have a shovel".
- **The result TYPE does not settle polarity.** `Effect` is hostile on `stack_of_books`
  and helpful on `confession_booth` (a stress heal). This was written up backwards first
  — the docstring asserted `confession_booth` was a trap, and the test built on that claim
  failed against the data. The polarity model, which reads the concrete effect definition,
  was right; the prose was the wrong part. All 30 effect names a curio can name resolve in
  `effects/base.effects.darkest`, so the model never has to guess.

### Where the data disagrees with the expert, and why it is left open

PLAYBOOK 2.4 says books and libraries are net negative, don't touch. The tables back that
for `stack_of_books` only (**-0.11**); `bookshelf` (**+0.07**) and `pile_of_scrolls`
(**+0.19**) come out slightly positive, both because they can roll Scouting. All three sit
near zero, so "not worth the turn" survives and "net negative" does not.

**Resolving it needs magnitudes** — how much a `Stress 3` costs against what a loot draw
is worth — and no shipped file provides them. Inventing weights would be a convention
wearing game-data clothes, so the scoring stays +1/0/-1 per result and
`tests/bridge/test_curios.py::test_only_one_of_the_book_curios_is_actually_negative` records the
disagreement instead of forcing either side.

### Skipping is advice, not a guarantee

**Every quirk carrying a `curio_tag` is `is_positive: False`** — 20 compulsions at 0.2 to
0.6 chance — so a hero can interact without being asked. The advice names them
(`kleptomaniac` on any `Treasure` curio, and the tag `All` matches anything) rather than
pretending the choice is always the player's.

### Not covered

Trap disarm, PLAYBOOK 2.4's last row. Disarm is the `resistance`/`trap` stat, built from
class base plus quirks plus trinkets; neither `CombatantState` nor `live_state.py` carries
it. `sacrificial_stone`'s only interaction names the item `"provision"`, which no
inventory file defines — reported as unusable rather than as something to go buy.

## Route policy, and two things the raid save was believed not to contain (`bridge/route.py`, 2026-08-14)

PLAYBOOK 2.2 over the decoded dungeon graph. Building it turned up two fields that
correct documented invariants.

**`raid.in_area` is the party's current area, as packed little-endian ASCII.** Same
convention as the map's `entrance_id`, not `dd_name_hash`. `CLAUDE.md` recorded it as
"matches no real dungeon id" — true of the hash, and it hid the answer. All 395 paired
captures resolve to an area that exists; 388 of them to one the map already marks visited,
and the other 7 sit on the entrance **one write early**, because the raid file lands before
the map file on a room transition.

**`raid.raid_instance` carries the entire active quest** — `id`, `type`, `dungeon`,
`difficulty`, `length`, `goal_ids`, and the completion reward down to the trinket — in
**399 of 399** captures. This directly contradicts the zone/tier invariant, which said the
raid save had neither and that both must be inferred from the enemy line.
`bridge/rollout.py::infer_zone_and_tier` is not wrong, but it is now the fallback rather
than the only option, and the save wins on disagreement. It also means `embark.py` can
price a quest by its actual trinket reward, which is PLAYBOOK 1.2's second criterion.

**`goal_ids` is a length-prefixed string list**: a 4-byte count, then per entry a 4-byte
length and a NUL-terminated name — `explore_all_rooms`, `battle_all_rooms`,
`survive_short`. The objective is stated in plain text, so it never has to be guessed from
the quest type. Read as ints (which is how it arrives when the blob happens to parse as
one) it is unreadable.

### The planner

Rooms are nodes, corridors edges. Targets come from the goal: every unvisited room for
`explore_all_rooms`, every room still holding a fight for `battle_all_rooms`, the goal room
alone for `kill_boss`. An unrecognised goal falls back to **every unvisited room** — the
safe superset, which can waste moves but cannot miss the objective.

Order is an **exact** shortest cover (Held-Karp over subsets). A real map is 7-9 rooms, so
exact costs a few hundred thousand operations and removes the question of whether a bad
route is the planner's fault or the map's.

**A party standing in a corridor is planned from both ends and the cheaper wins.** Picking
an end arbitrarily silently adds a move whenever it guesses wrong, and a corridor is where
the save gets written most often — on one real capture it was the difference between 3
moves and 4.

**"Minimise walking the same place twice" has a load-bearing qualifier.** Real dungeons
branch, and a target down a dead end has to be walked back out: `00022` needs 2 revisits to
reach `rooC` off `rooH`. A first test asserted zero revisits across the corpus and failed
on exactly that. The planner reports backtracking rather than producing an impossible walk.

**Detours are priced, not decided.** PLAYBOOK 2.1 values a treasure at "usually one extra
fight for a very high reward"; how a player trades moves and fights against loot is a
judgement no shipped file contains. So each optional target reports `+N moves, +N fights`
and nothing else — `Detour` deliberately has no value field, and a test asserts it stays
that way. On a boss quest, unvisited rooms join the optional list, since PLAYBOOK 2.2 makes
exploring a choice there rather than a requirement.

## Camping policy, and two timing rules that looked principled and were wrong (`bridge/camp.py`, 2026-08-14)

PLAYBOOK 2.5. **What** to spend the 12 points on was already solved by
`engine/camping.py`; this reuses that spender verbatim rather than growing a second copy.
**When** to burn a campfire is the decision the simulator never had to make, because it
camps on a fixed schedule.

**`build_party_from_live` (`bridge/seed.py`) exists because camping happens between
fights**, which is exactly the state `build_engine_from_live` rejects — it raises "not in
a battle", and 171 of the captures are in it. The new builder does the hero half only,
fielding one throwaway monster because `CombatEngine.reset` needs an enemy line, and
keeps the HP-written-last ordering: armour rank and `health_stone` both raise the ceiling,
so an HP written before them reads a full hero as damaged.

### Two dead ends, both caught by tests

- **Carried stress against the stress a campfire could shed.** The ceiling was measured on
  a copy with HP full, which forces the whole budget onto stress — while the real spend
  also buys HP and buffs. On a live capture the two disagreed by a factor of four: it
  announced "carried stress already exceeds what a campfire can shed, none of it is
  wasted" over a camp that actually shed **15 stress of a possible 70**.
- **Whether the spender exhausts the 12-point budget.** This looked airtight — the engine
  already refuses to buy wasted recovery, so leftover points ought to mean a wasted fire.
  A **fully healthy party still spends all 12**, on buffs, because `W_BUFF` is positive.
  Budget exhaustion measures nothing at all.

### What it does now

Two gates, both borrowed. First, the fire must deliver actual HP or stress — buffs alone
do not count, and zero needs no threshold. Second, it must be the fire that
`ExpeditionSequenceEnv._camp_schedule` places at this point in the run: that function
already spreads F fires evenly over N combats, and combined with `FIREWOOD_PER_BAND`
(0 short / 1 medium / 2 long, real data) and the save's own combats-fought count,
PLAYBOOK 2.5's "mid-run" falls out for a medium quest with nothing tuned. The pseudo-abbey
clause is the exception: last firewood with the run nearly over, camp regardless, because
the stress it sheds is for the *next* expedition.

**The ambush is reported, not priced** — 33% is real, but trading it against recovery is a
judgement no shipped file states, the same reason `bridge/route.py` prices detours without
valuing them.

### A duplicate-class scare that was not a bug

The first output showed `first_aid` twice for "highwayman", and `first_aid` has
`use_limit` 1. The party had **two Highwaymen** — one use each, entirely legal — and the
class-only label hid it. `CampAction.line` now prints the rank. This is the third time
duplicate classes have caused a wrong reading in this repo; the other two were real bugs.
Note also that `skills_used` records the **performer**, not the target, so a `pep_talk`
listed under one hero may well be healing another.

## The reward tests were replaying a different random episode every run (`tests/`, 2026-08-14)

`test_episode_return_bounded_by_terminal_dominance` failed once inside a full-suite run and
then passed five times in isolation and twice more paired with each new test file — the
exact signature of cross-test pollution, and it is not that.

**`env.reset(seed=)` seeds the ENGINE's RNG, not the action space.** Gymnasium leaves
`action_space._np_random` unseeded until something asks for it and then draws it from OS
entropy, so `env.action_space.sample()` produces a different action sequence on every
construction. Two freshly built envs, both `reset(seed=1)`, sampled `[25, 20, 24, 0, ...]`
and `[33, 7, 9, 7, ...]`. Every test that drives an episode with `sample()` was therefore
measuring a different episode each time, and the bound it asserts is only *usually* met.

Fixed by seeding the space alongside the env at all three sites — `tests/env/test_reward.py`'s
`_make_env`, `tests/env/test_env.py`, `tests/env/test_expedition_env.py`. The tests keep their
meaning and become reproducible; the reward suite now passes six consecutive runs and the
full suite 601/601.

Worth remembering when writing any future test that plays random actions: **seeding the
env is not seeding the policy.**

## Torch policy — the loot model was in rules.json all along (`bridge/torchlight.py`, 2026-08-14)

PLAYBOOK 2.3's shutdown: hold radiant, then drop the torch to nothing on the turn that
ends a fight so its loot pays more. This was written up twice in this repo as needing a
loot model the simulator does not have. **It does not.**

`shared/rules.json`'s `darkness.range_table` prices darkness in both directions, over five
brackets:

| torch | gold | treasure draws | monster atk/dmg/crit | stress chance/dmg | scouting | surprise |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 76-100 | — | — | — | — | +15 | monsters +25 |
| 51-75 | — | — | 0 / 0 / +1 | +5 / +10 | +7.5 | monsters +15 |
| 26-50 | +10% | +4 | +5 / +10 / +2 | +10 / +20 | — | heroes +15 |
| 1-25 | +15% | +8 | +10 / +15 / +3 | +25 / +30 | — | heroes +25 |
| 0 and below | **+30%** | **+12** | +12.5 / +25 / +5 | +35 / +40 | — | heroes +40 |

`DarknessBand` had been **dropping the loot, scouting and surprise columns** on the way in,
documented as "dungeon exploration/loot mechanics with no analog in this single-encounter
combat simulator". True of the engine, and it is still true — the engine reads only the
combat columns and its behaviour is unchanged — but the bridge needs them, so they are
parsed now instead of the bridge re-reading rules.json to recover numbers the loader
already walked past.

### The brackets exclude their lower bound, and the engine had it backwards

`darkness.lowest_excluded` is `true`, which yields the tiers 76-100 / 51-75 / 26-50 / 0-25
— **the exact list `CombatEngine._get_darkness_band` recited in its own docstring while
implementing `band.lower <= torch < band.upper`**, i.e. putting 75 in the radiant band.
Now `band.lower < torch <= band.upper`.

Two consequences, and the second is why it was worth changing:

- **Inert for training and every archived eval.** They field `torch_level=100`, and the
  two readings differ only at exactly 75 / 50 / 25 / 0. Comparability with archived
  numbers is preserved.
- **Not inert for the bridge.** `bridge/seed.py` passes the real torch out of the save
  into `engine.reset(torch_level=...)`, and PLAYBOOK 2.3 parks it at exactly **75** on
  purpose. Under the old reading the advisor simulated a radiant fight while the game ran
  a dim one — different monster crit, different stress.

Also newly exposed and worth knowing: **`camp_restore_torch` is 100**, so a shutdown on
the fight immediately before a campfire is free. PLAYBOOK 2.3 does not mention that case.
And `ambush_torch_reduction` is **-100**: a camp ambush zeroes the light for you.

### The one number that is not game data

**No shipped file states how much light one torch restores.** Not
`inventory/base.supply.inventory.items.darkest` (stack limit and price, no effect), not
`base.effects.darkest` (its `torch_increase` entries belong to curios and hero skills), not
anything under `raid/`. `TORCH_RESTORE = 25.0` is expert-measured — 4 torches take a dark
run back to full — and is labelled as such at the constant.

### Cost is marginal, not absolute

The shutdown costs what it takes to get back to **where you were**, not back to 100. A
party already down at 25 pays one torch to undo it, not four; charging the full relight
would talk the policy out of a play that is nearly free. The reported gain is marginal for
the same reason: from 25 a shutdown adds +15% gold, not +30%, because the first half is
already being collected.

And the shutdown is only ever offered when the fight is about to end. Every bracket that
pays loot also hands the monsters accuracy, damage and crit — a test asserts that holds
across the whole table — so darkness before the kill is buying loot with risk that has not
been earned yet.

## Retreat, re-framed: two bills in gold instead of a probability threshold (`bridge/retreat.py`, `data/town_costs.py`, 2026-08-14)

`advisor.py --survival` recommended pulling out below `P(finish intact) < 0.5`. That
threshold was never mis-calibrated so much as **mis-framed**: PLAYBOOK 2.6 does not compare
a probability to a number, it compares the cost of repairing four heroes against repairing
one plus losing the dungeon. Every exchange rate that needs is shipped game data.

| what | gold | source |
|---|---|---|
| one point of stress | **22.2** un-upgraded, **10.0** fully upgraded | `abbey.building.json`: `cost_upgrades` 1000/850/700 against `stress_upgrades` 45/56/70 |
| a whole party pulling out | 20 x 4 = **~1,776** | `rules.json` `combat_retreat_stress` = 20 |
| a dead rank-4 hero | **~36,000** | `upgrades/heroes/<class>.upgrades.json`, weapon/armour/skill ranks summed |
| a generic quest | **3,000** gold + 3 deeds + a trinket | `raid.raid_instance.completion_reward` |

**A death costs ~20x a full-party retreat and ~12x the quest it is risked for.** That
single ratio is the whole of PLAYBOOK 2.6's second clause, and it explains the shape of
expert play the probability framing could not: retreat is *rare* because stress is cheap,
and *decisive* when it happens because a hero is not.

A fresh recruit is free from the stage coach (PLAYBOOK 0.1), so what dies is not "a hero"
but the gold sunk into their ranks — which is why the framing changes sign on party
quality. Same 1.0 expected deaths, same quest: retreat with a levelled party, push on with
rank-0s. A threshold on `success_rate` cannot express that at all. A real capture makes
the point on its own: its Man-at-Arms is rank 0/0 and carries **0 gold** of investment.

Live example, on a captured raid: `P(finish intact) 67%, expected deaths 0.8` — and the
verdict is still *push on*, because the party is cheap and the quest is worth more. The old
rule agreed, for the wrong reason; with a levelled party the same 67% flips.

### Also fixed on the way

- **`rules.json`'s `combat_retreat_*` were not parsed.** `combat_retreat_stress` (20),
  `combat_retreat_chance` (0.7) and `combat_retreat_bonus_chance_per_attempt` (0.05) are
  read now — the engine still ignores them, since retreat is not in the action space, but
  the advice must not read as though pulling out were free *or* guaranteed.
- **`bridge/torch.py` shadowed the real `torch` package.** Running any script inside
  `bridge/` puts that directory first on `sys.path`, so importing a checkpoint died with
  `ModuleNotFoundError: 'torch' is not a package`. Renamed `bridge/torchlight.py`, which is
  the save's own field name. Same trap `data/types.py` carries against the stdlib `types`.
  `tests/bridge/test_retreat.py` now fails if any module in `bridge/` takes a name that shadows a
  stdlib or common third-party package.
- **The advisor prefers `raid_instance` for zone and tier**, falling back to
  `infer_zone_and_tier` only when there is no quest record.

### Deliberately not priced

Heirlooms and the reward trinket. Heirlooms buy building upgrades and have no gold price;
PLAYBOOK 1.2 values a trinket by whether one of the comps equips it, not in coin. Both are
reported beside the total rather than folded into it with an invented rate.

`shuffle_is_worth_undoing` covers PLAYBOOK 2.6's *first* reason — retreat and re-enter to
undo a catastrophic shuffle — by running the same dungeon twice, once per formation, so
the answer comes out in deaths and stress rather than from a formation heuristic.

## The run loop, and recovering what the player actually did (`bridge/run.py`, 2026-08-14)

Two problems, one module.

**The bridge had become seven command lines.** `advisor.py` polled for combat; `route.py`,
`curios.py`, `torchlight.py`, `camp.py` and `retreat.py` each answered one question about
one captured snapshot, when asked. That is workable while developing a layer and unusable
while playing: it makes the human decide *which layer to consult*, which is most of the
decision. `run.py` polls once and dispatches on where the party is — in a battle, the
combat move plus the retreat bill; between rooms, position, route, curios here, torch,
campfire — into one timestamped log.

**And no acceptance run had ever recorded which recommendations were taken.** Five live
runs found five real defects, every one of them reconstructed from memory afterwards, so
"the advisor said rank 3 and I did something else" was testimony rather than evidence. The
debug channel cannot close this: it instruments the monster AI only, and a grep for every
equipped hero skill across a 445 KB capture returns **0 hits** (see the trace-oracle
section). There is no behavioural-cloning data to be had that way.

The **save diff** recovers most of it. Between two writes one hero usually acts, and what
changed says what kind of turn it was: a provision stack shrinking is an item, a live
enemy losing HP *or vanishing from the enemy list* is an attack on that enemy, a hero
gaining HP is a heal, stress coming off or a buff appearing is support. That fixes the
kind and the target — what PLAYBOOK-level disagreements are about — and never the skill,
since the plain-damage skills are indistinguishable in a diff and `Battle Random int
range A,B` only separates the few with unique damage bands.

### Ranking the signals, and the 23 turns that measured it

The first version treated two signals firing at once as ambiguity and marked it `?`.
Replaying the 193 captures in `archive/traces/raidsnaps2/` gave **MATCH 21% / DIFFER 29% /
`?` 50%** — an instrument half blind. 23 of the 28 unresolved turns carried the same
reason, `several signals at once (move, attack)`, and all 23 were **Duelist's Advance**:
the lunge family moves the performer *and* attacks in one turn, as Judgement damages an
enemy while healing the Vestal. That is one skill, not two events.

Signals are now ranked — **attack > heal > move > support**, since nothing but an attack
takes HP off an enemy on a hero's turn — and the ones not chosen are reported alongside
rather than hidden. Same captures: **MATCH 46% / DIFFER 46% / `?` 7%**.

The ranking is fixed and derived from the game's skill design. It is deliberately *not*
"pick whichever signal the advice predicted": that resolves every ambiguity towards
agreement, which collapses the DIFFER rate while the tool still looks like it works. An
instrument built to find disagreements must not lean that way, so what genuinely cannot
be attributed still resolves to `?` — two heroes advancing their turn counters between one
pair of writes, an item and a skill in the same window, or no change at all.

**A miss leaves no trace in the save, so `?` is a real verdict and is printed as a rate**
rather than folded into agreement. Model-free across every consecutive in-battle pair in
the capture set: 3 of 56 real turns, **5.4%**. `tests/bridge/test_run.py` asserts it stays under
20%, which is the regression guard for the whole attribution scheme — the move/attack
conflation degraded it silently, because `?` is not an error.

### Three things that shipped a wrong reading once each

- **A rank change is only evidence of a move when no enemy acted in between.** 46 of 246
  monsters reposition, so a push reads exactly like a chosen move.
- **A vanished guid is a kill.** A killed monster leaves the live enemy list for the corpse
  list; without that, every killing blow reads as "an enemy whose HP did not change".
- **Names do not identify a target.** A real Weald encounter fields **three Carrion Eaters
  on identical HP** (`20260811-214142_00122`, guids 305/306/307), so `advised Carrion
  Eater, played Carrion Eater` reads as a tooling bug when it is a genuine disagreement
  about which one to hit. Labels carry the save-list slot. That slot is **not a rank** — a
  size-2 monster covers two of them.

Heal and support are one family for the verdict: a heal on a full-HP ally and a pure buff
produce the same diff, so telling them apart would claim more than the evidence carries. A
MATCH there is a claim about *who* was helped. This is what catches Defender being played
instead of the advised Rampart — guard leaves no HP trace at all, only a buff.

**Provisional advice is never marked.** `recommend` looks ahead past pending enemy turns so
the screen is not blank while they play, and labels that block provisional; scoring it
would score a prediction about a board that had not happened yet.

`--replay <dir>` runs a whole capture directory through the loop offline. That is how all
of the above was measured *without anyone playing* — a live run is expensive and
unrepeatable, and the captures were already on disk.

One process note: `path.replace("raid", "roster")` is wrong for these captures, because
the directory is named `raidsnaps2`. It silently yields a missing roster, an empty party,
and a scan that reports 92 pairs and 0 turns. Use `replace("raid.json", "roster.json")`.

## The overlay — the advice was in the wrong place (`bridge/overlay.py`, 2026-08-14)

Every recommendation this project has ever produced was correct and behind the game
window. What that costs is not reading time, it is **looking away**, and looking away
degrades both the play and the notes — the only two products a live run has. So the HUD
draws the recommendation on top of the game with a ring on the rank it points at.

It renders `bridge/run.py`'s `Update` records and decides nothing. The seam exists
specifically so the overlay never parses the printed block back into structure: a marker
keyed on a regex over `-> SKILL on rank 3 (...)` breaks the first time a label changes,
and breaks **silently**, by pointing at the wrong rank. `Session.publish` swallows a
front-end failure for the same reason — the log is the record, the screen is a
convenience, and the loop must outlive its display.

### Two window styles, and why one is not enough

`-transparentcolor` keys one unused colour out of the window, which makes everything not
drawn on see-through. That alone is wrong: the parts that *are* drawn on stay
mouse-opaque, so the ring around an enemy swallows the click aimed at that enemy — the
overlay would block the exact action it was drawn to recommend. `WS_EX_TRANSPARENT` makes
the whole window ignore the mouse; `WS_EX_NOACTIVATE` stops it stealing focus from the
game on repaint. If the `ctypes` call fails the overlay says so loudly rather than
carrying on as a click-eating rectangle.

**The game has to be borderless, and DD has no menu item for it.** Over exclusive
fullscreen a layered window is usually not composited at all, and the overlay simply does
not appear. The setting exists but is not in the UI, which took a look at the binary to
establish: `shared/options/options.value_definitions.json` defines `resolution` (and its
six allowed sizes) and nothing else about the window, `persist.options.json` — plain JSON
in `Documents/Darkest/`, not DSON — stores only `fullscreen`, `resolution` and
`monitor_number`, and no localization file mentions borderless at all. But the binary's
SDL setup block, sitting next to `SDL_CreateWindow` and `plinth_app.cpp`, carries the
option names `vsync`, `fullscreen`, **`full_as_borderless`**, `window_pos`,
`monitor_number` and `size` — and the embedded default command line is
`+appname "Darkest Dungeon" +shortname "darkest" -vsync 1`. So it is a launch option in
that same `-name value` form:

```
Steam -> Properties -> Launch Options:   -full_as_borderless 1
```

Note this is a **different option system** from the `-enabledebug*` family used by the
trace oracle: those strings live around `0xab74c0` near `Darkest::ParseArguments`, these
around `0xaac2e8` in the plinth/SDL layer.

**The game's own resolution is irrelevant to the overlay.** The layout is calibrated in
screen pixels and DD scales its 16:9 layout to fill, so a game set to 1280x720 on a
2560x1440 monitor puts the ranks in exactly the same screen positions as one set to
2560x1440. A change of *desktop* resolution or of aspect ratio does invalidate it, which is
what `Layout.screen` is checked against at startup.

And the consequence that had to be designed around rather than worked around: **a
click-through window cannot receive a keystroke either.** The `<Escape>` binding tkinter
offers is dead precisely when the overlay is working as intended, which would have shipped
a HUD with no way to close it. The key is polled globally with `GetAsyncKeyState`, and it
is **F10** rather than Esc — Esc is DD's own menu key, and DD binds nothing on F10. The Esc
binding is kept anyway, for the degraded case where the `ctypes` call failed and the window
*is* focusable.

Two smaller things a first attempt would have burned itself on. The command is typed in a
terminal, which leaves the terminal in front of the game — and the calibration window is a
45% tint over whatever is behind it, so it would have been nine clicks on a picture of a
terminal. Hence `--delay` (6 s, counted down in the console) before `--calibrate` and
`--test` open. And an `overrideredirect` window is not given focus by the window manager,
so Backspace and Esc did nothing until the first click landed; `focus_force()` fixes it.

### Calibration is nine clicks, on purpose

Four hero ranks, four enemy ranks, one card anchor, saved to
`bridge/overlay_layout.json`. Interactive rather than derived from the window size,
because DD's formation is laid out by the art and not by a grid — the sprite centres move
with the aspect ratio, so a formula is quietly wrong at 21:9. The ninth click earns its
place too: the alternative is guessing a corner and finding out mid-fight that the card
covers the torch meter. A half-finished calibration is rejected outright (`Layout.load`
returns None unless both rows have 4 points) — it would otherwise render happily with
every marker on the missing row silently a no-op.

**Rank 1 is the FRONT rank on both sides**, matching the engine, the advisor and the
game's own tooltips. Reversing it mirrors every marker onto the opposite formation and
nothing downstream would object, which is why `tests/bridge/test_overlay.py` pins it along with
the 4/4/1 slice of the calibration points.

### Threading

Tk must be driven from the thread that created it, so the poll loop runs as a daemon
thread handing `Update`s over a `queue.Queue` that the UI drains on a 120 ms timer. That
is also what keeps a `--survival` rollout — seconds of simulation — from freezing the
display. The UI drains to the *latest* update rather than replaying the backlog: a queued
recommendation is about a board that has already moved.

Two testing notes worth keeping. The render tests open a real Tk window instead of mocking
the toolkit, which passes just as happily when the toolkit is what broke. And they share
**one** root for the module: creating and destroying a Tk root per test skipped a random
case roughly one run in two, the kind of flake that later gets "fixed" by deleting the
test.

## The first live run with the whole loop — four defects (2026-08-14)

One real expedition, overlay up, everything logged. Tally as played: **MATCH 10 / DIFFER 3
/ `?` 4** over 17 resolved turns. Four defects, none of them where the report pointed.

**Reported: "the decision it recommends is not the one that's due."** The obvious suspect
was the turn pointer, which has caused exactly this before. It was not: across every
usable in-battle capture (97 of 99; the other two have an unpaired roster), the queue never
names a hero the party does not contain and `next_hero` never points outside it — pinned in
`tests/bridge/test_run.py`. The cause was presentation. `recommend` reads ahead past pending enemy
turns so the screen is not blank through a ~17 s stretch with no save write, and **13 of 77
advisories in the capture set are that look-ahead**. The terminal block leads with
`after 2 enemy turns ->`; `Session._battle_update` dropped that lead and published only the
move, so the overlay showed a preview as an instruction. The count and the repositioning
enemies now travel on the `Update`, the headline leads with `2 ENEMY TURNS FIRST, then …`,
and the ring is greyed and dashed — or **withheld entirely** when a pending enemy can move
somebody, since 46 of 246 monsters can, and a confident circle on the wrong enemy is worse
than no circle. Verified on the captures: 12/12 look-aheads now labelled, 2 of them with
the ring suppressed, and 0 current advisories falsely labelled.

**"One fight left" is not "the run is over."** `torchlight.advise_from_live` inferred
`last_combat_of_run` from `remaining_battles <= 1` and then *asserted* the consequence:
"the run's last fight — there is nothing left to walk through", relight priced at 0
torches. In the run there were **five moves and eight unexplored areas** still ahead.
Walking five corridors pitch black is not free — it costs scouting and raises the ambush
chance on every one of them. It now takes `moves_left` and claims free only at
`remaining <= 1 and moves_left == 0`; `bridge/run.py` feeds it from the route plan it has
already computed. Unknown does **not** infer it: without the route the two cases are
indistinguishable, so it charges the relight, which still recommends the shutdown whenever
the torches are in the pack — just with its real price on it.

**The out-of-combat block was keyed on the raw torch level**, which burns down continuously
while the party walks, so all fifteen lines reprinted for every single point — six
identical blocks in one corridor, burying the combat log interleaved with it. Keyed on the
darkness **bracket** now: none of the six layers reads the raw number, what changes their
answer is crossing a band.

**Ctrl-C threw away the tally.** It lands inside Tk's mainloop, so `overlay.run()` raised
and `main()` never reached `session.summary()` — the run finished, the raid ended, and the
one number the session exists to produce was lost. Printed from a `finally` now.

Two observations from the same log, not defects: the policy recommended `PASS` at 100%
twice with one or two enemies left (the stall behaviour PLAYBOOK 3.4 covers, and one of the
DIFFERs is Fran moving instead), and `1 buff(s) not mapped` appeared repeatedly — that
counter is `engine.seed_unmapped_buffs`, working as designed, counting rather than guessing.

## A corpse cannot be attacked — an open fidelity gap (found 2026-08-15)

Raised from live play, and correct: with one enemy left behind a corpse, destroying the
corpse collapses the enemy line and pulls the survivor into reach of heroes who could not
otherwise touch it. **The simulator cannot express that play**, so the policy never learned
it.

The cause is one expression in `CombatEngine.get_valid_actions`:

```python
enemy_positions = sorted({p for e in self.enemies if e.is_alive() ...})
```

and `CombatantState.is_alive()` is `not is_dead and not is_corpse`. Corpses are therefore
absent from every target pool, for every skill, always.

The game data is unambiguous that they are targets.
`monsters/corpse/corpse_A/corpse_A.info.darkest`:

```
stats: .hp 7 .def -20% .prot 0 .stun_resist 200% ...
battle_modifier: ... .can_be_random_target False ... .can_be_missed False .can_be_guarded False
```

A 7-HP, −20%-dodge target that **cannot be missed**. `.can_be_random_target False` only
stops the *monster AI* picking one at random; it says nothing about a player-directed
attack. `.accelerate_stall_penalty True` on the same record is already honoured by the
engine, which is a second sign the file is read as a real combatant elsewhere.

Everything else about corpses is modelled and correct: they spawn only from a monster with
a `death_class:` record (125 of 254), never on a crit or a DoT kill, they occupy and block
their ranks, they slide forward with the formation, they rot after 3 rounds, and
`is_clear_corpses` skills remove them. The observation carries an `is_corpse` flag per
enemy slot, so the policy can *see* them — it simply has never been offered the action.

**Not fixed here, and deliberately.** Closing it means giving the corpse its own stat block
so it can take damage and be destroyed, which changes the legal action set — the mask gains
options the checkpoint has never seen, and an untrained logit on a newly-legal action can
make behaviour worse rather than better. It is a fidelity fix gated on a retrain, with a
ready falsifier: attacks-on-corpses should be non-zero and concentrated in exactly the
position that motivated it (one enemy left, out of reach behind a corpse).

## The pre-retrain bundle — three fidelity gaps closed together (2026-08-15)

Everything here **invalidates every existing checkpoint** and was done in one batch for
exactly that reason: two of the three change the observation width or the legal action set,
so they cannot be shipped one at a time without a retrain each. `models/*.zip` and
`logs/*/best_model.zip` from before this date will not load.

### 1. Training had never once fought in the dark

`ExpeditionSequenceEnv._start_next_combat` called `engine.reset(...)` **without
`torch_level`**, and `CombatEngine.reset` defaults it to `100.0`. Neither did
`ExpeditionEnv`'s sampling path. So every combat of every expedition ever trained here was
fought at full radiant light.

Two separate costs, and the second is the larger:

- Torch **is** in the observation (`env/gym_env.py`, `engine.torch_level / 100`). Held
  constant, that feature carried zero information — one of 332 inputs that was provably
  useless.
- The policy is **off-distribution in the dark**, where `rules.json`'s `darkness.range_table`
  changes player crit and dodge, monster ACC, crit and damage, and both stress chance and
  stress damage. `_apply_torch_modifiers` implements all of it; training simply never
  reached it.

Not a corner case. Over **206 in-battle snapshots captured from real play**, only 68% were
radiant: 19% black, 11% dim, 2% dark, min 3, p25 62, median 89, p75 95. Roughly a third of
real combats happen under modifiers the policy had never seen.

Fixed with `TORCH_LEVEL_DECILES` (`env/expedition_env.py`) — the deciles of that measured
distribution, `(3, 25, 34, 62, 83, 89, 92, 94, 99, 100, 100)`, interpolated inside each bin
so the draw is continuous rather than 11 spikes. Drawn from **`_sampler_rng`**, the scenario
RNG, so a scenario reproduces its torch along with its comp and encounters — which is what
lets `Rollout.evaluate` compare two parties against the same dungeon instead of against
different luck. Drawn **per combat, not per expedition**: the deciles are already the
marginal over real captures, so the player's own relighting is baked in, and nothing in
`Discrete(37)` can act on the torch, so the within-expedition correlation would buy the
policy nothing while requiring a traversal model this simulator does not have.
`options["torch_level"]` pins the opening combat, which is what a rollout asked from
mid-dungeon needs — it knows the *current* torch off the save and nothing about the rest.

Caught immediately by a test that had nothing to do with torch:
`test_carry_over_opens_a_damaged_party` began reading `max_hp` 31 instead of 34, because a
lone Vestal opening at 40 stress now sometimes started in the black, where the darkness
stress multipliers carried her to afflicted during `reset()`'s auto-resolved enemy turns —
and **every affliction carries `MAXHP-10`**. The test pins `torch_level=100.0` now; the
behaviour it found is correct.

### 2. "This kill is already secured" was not in the observation at all

Raised from live play: the policy finished an enemy that was already dying to a bleed,
wasting the turn (1 of 47 attacks in the run that raised it).

The reason it is not simply a bad policy: the quantity was **not recoverable from the
observation vector**. HP fraction was there, and a normalized DoT total per kind was there,
but comparing them needs the target's *absolute* max HP, which the vector has never carried.
The policy was being asked to infer a comparison from two independently normalized numbers.

Added as an explicit per-enemy-slot flag (`_is_doomed`, `ENEMY_SLOT_SCALARS` 14 → 15):
outstanding bleed + blight ≥ current HP, summed across both kinds, gated on `is_alive()` so
a corpse is never flagged.

**The falsifier needed a measurement that did not exist**, so `tools/check_playbook.py`
gained a `DOT` block: of the attacks made while a doomed enemy is on the board, how many are
aimed at that enemy. Note this is **not** PLAYBOOK 3.3 as the tool measures it — 3.3's
"secured" is *every living enemy stunned* (baseline 60.4% attack, 18.7% recovery, over 4083
hero turns in 334 combats), a related but distinct situation. Conflating the two would read
a movement in one as evidence about the other.

### 3. A corpse is a target now

Closes the gap recorded above. Three parts:

- **The corpse carries the corpse stat block, not the husk of what died.**
  `DataStore.corpses` (`HeroLoader._load_corpses`) loads `corpse_A..D` and
  `corpse_large_A..D` into their own dict — deliberately *not* `store.enemies`, which is the
  pool encounter sampling and every diagnostic iterate. The slain monster names its own
  variant via `death_class: .monster_class_id`, already parsed into
  `EnemyDefinition.corpse_id`, so the difficulty scale comes out right: 7/10/14/14 HP for
  `corpse_A..D`, 15/21/29/30 for the size-2 `corpse_large_A..D`. Without this the corpse kept
  the dead monster's HP and "attack the corpse" would have been a multi-turn job against a
  full-health body — an action nobody would ever take, i.e. a fix that fixes nothing.
- **It cannot be missed.** `resolve_attack` forces `hit` when the target is a corpse, per
  `battle_modifier: ... .can_be_missed False`. The roll is still drawn so the RNG stream
  stays aligned with any other attack.
- **Damage and nothing else.** `_strike_corpse` bypasses guard redirect, riposte, crit stress
  relief, on-crit passives, skill effects and movement. That is not a simplification: a
  corpse resists stun, debuff and move at 200%, never takes a turn (so a bleed or blight on
  it can never tick), carries `crit: .is_valid_effects_target false`,
  `.can_relieve_stress_from_crit False`, `.can_relieve_stress_from_killing_blow False` and
  `.can_be_guarded False`. Routing it through the full per-target machinery would have
  invented every one of those.

`_can_damage_corpse` narrows the offer to skills with a damage payload (`heal_min is None`
and `dmg_mod > -1.0`), on the same strictly-dominated argument as `_is_wasted_heal`: Plague
Doctor's Blinding Gas is `.dmg -100%` and can do *literally nothing* to a corpse.
`_resolve_action_targets` had to move in step, or an AoE offered because a corpse stood in
its ranks would re-resolve to zero targets at execution time and silently burn the turn.
`_find_combatant_by_position` gained `include_corpses`, **off by default**, so monster AI
targeting and push/pull resolution keep meaning "living".

**And a potential term, or the action would have been learned away.** A corpse is `is_dead`,
so `_enemy_score` short-circuits to `ENEMY_DEAD_SCORE` and its HP never enters Φ — breaking
a corpse would read to the policy as a turn spent for nothing, a pure turn-penalty loss.
`W_BLOCKING_CORPSE` scores corpses that stand *in front of a living enemy*, the only kind
that costs anything, since that is exactly what puts a survivor out of a front-rank skill's
reach. Potential-based like the rest of Φ, so by Ng/Harada/Russell it cannot change the
optimal policy: if clearing a blocking corpse is not worth a turn, this mis-times the credit
and buys nothing.

### How often any of this comes up

Measured over 2730 hero turns of random-policy full expeditions, after the change:

| situation | share of hero turns |
|---|---|
| a corpse on the board | 21.9% |
| a corpse **legally targetable** (was 0.0% for the life of the project) | 15.2% |
| a corpse **blocking** a living enemy | 13.9% |
| an enemy already doomed by its own DoT | 6.6% |

### The retrain, and what it settled (6M steps, `--seed 0`, from scratch)

**The comparison needed a harness first.** Every archived eval was measured when training and
eval both ran at torch 100 *by construction* — the bug fix 1 closed. Diffing the new model's
sampled-torch number against them would compare two different tasks. So the bundle run was
evaluated in both conditions, with `_sample_torch_level` monkey-patched to a constant for the
radiant column (a comparability harness, not a shipped mode):

| checkpoint | radiant-pinned (comparable to baselines) | sampled torch (the real task) |
|---|---|---|
| `best_model` (peaked at 1.07M steps) | 64.2% | 58.3% |
| **final save (6M)** | **66.7%** | **61.9%** |
| prior `eval_traits_3M` (3M, radiant by construction) | 64.4% | — |

**66.7% vs 64.4%** on equal terms: +2.3pp, inside the measured ±4pp noise floor, so the honest
claim is *no regression* rather than an improvement — while the same checkpoint also holds
61.9% on a task that now includes the dark, which the 64.4% never measured. Mean party stress
tells the same story: 58.6 at baseline, 65.1 for the new model in radiant, 84.9 sampled.

**Falsifier 2 (doomed targets): passes, and needed a null to be readable.** "40% of attacks go
to the doomed enemy" means nothing on its own, so `check_playbook.py` now also computes what an
*indifferent* attacker would score — the share of living enemies that are doomed at that moment.

| | policy | indifferent | verdict |
|---|---|---|---|
| final 6M, sampled torch | 40.4% | 59.8% | avoiding by 19.4pp |
| final 6M, radiant | 40.3% | 58.8% | avoiding by 18.5pp |
| `best_model`, sampled torch | 39.1% | 55.4% | avoiding by 16.3pp |

The policy actively steers off targets that are already dead. That behaviour did not exist
before — the quantity was not in the observation.

**Falsifier 3 (corpses): passes.** 130 attacks aimed at a corpse, **11.5%** of the turns one was
on the board. Not learned away. The situation is also far more common than the pre-retrain
random-policy estimate suggested: a corpse is present on **25.3%** of hero turns and is
**blocking a living enemy in 66.7%** of those.

**PLAYBOOK 3.3 improved sharply**: turns spent on recovery with the kill already secured
(every living enemy stunned) went 18.7% → **46.5%** radiant / 33.3% sampled.

**PLAYBOOK 3.2 regressed, and darkness is only half of it.** Unjustified heals went 47.4% →
66.5% sampled, and re-measured on equal terms it is still **58.8%** radiant, with total heals up
from 12.3% to 16.3% of turns. So roughly 8pp of the move is the harder task and ~11pp is real:
a policy trained under darkness heals more, and carries that habit into radiant fights where
the metric — which is fixed at "not Death's Door, not below 34% HP, not a recovery phase" —
correctly calls it unjustified. Not fixed here; recorded as the open one.

**A new anomaly.** Longest single combat: **220 rounds** with sampled torch, against a max of
16 in the radiant re-run — which read as darkness-specific and **was not**. Diagnosed the same
day: the per-combat step budget was only ever enforced as an expedition total. See "The
220-round combat" below.

## The 220-round combat: a per-combat budget that was never per-combat (2026-08-15)

Reported out of the post-retrain eval as "darkness-specific", and **that attribution was
wrong** — the first 460-combat reproduction found no runaway at all below radiant (worst: 27
rounds at torch 99.4, 32 rounds at torch 37.6), and the median torch of long combats matched
the median torch of every other combat. Recorded here because the wrong theory was written
down before it was checked.

The real cause is arithmetic. `MAX_STEPS_PER_COMBAT = 40` is named per-combat and its own
comment calls it "a tighter per-combat step budget", but it was only ever enforced as

```python
self._max_steps = MAX_STEPS_PER_COMBAT * self._num_combats   # an expedition TOTAL
...
if self._step_count >= self._max_steps: truncated = True     # _step_count never resets
```

so **nothing bounded any single combat**: one fight could legally consume the whole pool. A
15-combat expedition pools 600 steps, and the runaway ran at ~2.7 hero steps per round —
600 / 2.7 ≈ 222. That is the 220.

**40 was the right number all along.** Measured over 483 real combats of the trained policy:
hero steps per combat p50 13, p90 22, p95 25, **p99 38**, max 70. A 40-step cap cuts 0.62% of
combats. What it cuts is the dither it exists for — the worst combat spent 53 of its 70 steps
on `__move__` (19), `battlefield_bandage` (14), `wyrd_reconstruction` (10) and `absolution`
(10).

Fixed with a second counter, `_combat_step_count`, reset in `reset()` and
`_start_next_combat()`. **It is read before the combat-transition block, not after** — read
after, a fight that ran over would silently start the next combat with a fresh budget and the
pathology would survive the fix while looking fixed. Tests:
`tests/env/test_training_torch_and_doom.py`.

Measured on the current checkpoint, which the fix does not invalidate (it touches neither the
observation nor the action space), `--episodes 50 --comps 12 --comp-seed 1`:

| | SUCCESS | wipe | stall |
|---|---|---|---|
| before | 61.9% | 22.9% | 0.8% |
| after | **61.7%** | **19.7%** | **6.7%** |

SUCCESS is unchanged (0.2pp, far inside noise) because a runaway already ended the episode in
truncation — the outcome was always a failure. What moved is the *composition*: 3.2pp of wipes
became stalls, i.e. those overlong fights were ones the party was slowly losing, and they are
now cut instead of ground out. The stall arithmetic is a second confirmation of the diagnosis:
0.62% of combats exceed 40 steps, so an ~8-combat episode contains one with probability
1 − 0.9938⁸ ≈ 4.9%, against the 5.9pp actually observed.

So the value is not in this checkpoint's score — it is that hundreds of training steps stop
being spent inside one dead-end fight, which lands on the next retrain.

**Also surfaced, not fixed:** `absolution` (Abomination) is `.target` empty, so
`get_valid_actions` appends it through the `if not sd.target:` branch, which **never consults
`_is_wasted_heal`** — and its heal arrives via named effects rather than `.heal`, which that
guard cannot see either (it returns early on `sd.heal_min is None`). Both holes are real. But
absolution also heals stress, so spamming it is *not* strictly dominated whenever the
performer has any stress at all, and stress is almost always non-zero. Narrowing the legal set
here would therefore not stop the loop; it is a reward question, which is where PLAYBOOK 3.2
already sits.

## The embark advisor against a real campaign (2026-08-15)

First run of `bridge/embark.py` against the live save — 34 heroes, 32 free, 747 802 gold, five
offered quests. It ranks every eligible comp × quest by `Rollout.evaluate`, and it is the only
place in the project where the policy is measured against a **real roster** (real quirks,
diseases, weapon/armour ranks) rather than the twelve scraped comps.

The finding is about the party that was actually selected to embark (`roster.status == 1`),
which the tool prints as `YOUR CURRENT PARTY`:

| quest | best option | current party |
|---|---|---|
| cove cleanse (difficulty 3) | Sacred Lunge **70.0%** | **20.0%** — last of six |
| weald explore (difficulty 3) | Holy Hymn: Virtue **60.0%** | **25.0%** |

±10pp standard error over 20 rollouts, so a 50pp and a 35pp gap are both far outside the
noise. The expected-deaths column says the same thing more directly: 2.65 and 2.35 deaths for
the selected party against 0.60 and 1.30 for the best alternative.

`P(success)` remains a **lower bound and a ranking, not odds** — it is the probability *the
policy* finishes intact, and the policy plays worse than the human. The gaps are what carry
information, not the levels.

## Marking which button to press, and a pass that looked like a move (2026-08-15)

**The overlay now squares the action-bar slot** as well as ringing the target, so the whole
recommendation is on screen. The bar, left to right, is the hero's four equipped skills,
then MOVE, then the narrow X — which is **PASS**, not retreat.

The slot number is the **game's**, not the action space's. `Discrete(37)` indexes the
hero's full 7-skill movepool precisely so slot *k* names the same skill for a class whatever
a comp equips; the screen shows only the four brought, in save order. So the button is
`LiveHero.selected_skills.index(skill_id) + 1`. Going through the action index would point
at the wrong icon for any hero whose loadout is not the first four of their movepool, and a
class-keyed lookup would collapse a **two-Highwayman party** — which the live roster has
(guids 245 and 2; the "phantom hero guid 2" seen earlier in the captures was simply a real
hero with a small guid). A skill not in `selected_skills` — the Abomination is never
skill-restricted — marks nothing rather than guessing. Across the whole capture replay all
74 battle advisories resolve to a real button, none to "none".

Only slots 1 and 5 are calibrated and 2-4 interpolated; the X is clicked separately because
it is **not** on that spacing (about two thirds of a step past slot 5) and is roughly a
third as wide. `bridge/overlay_slots.py` holds the two constants so `run.py` never imports
tkinter — `--replay` and the whole terminal path must run headless.

**And a marking rule.** Reported: advised `pass`, the player passed, marked DIFFER because
a rank change showed up. A pass leaves no trace at all, so a bare rank change is exactly as
consistent with "passed, then got shoved" as with "chose to move" — 46 of 246 monsters can
reposition and the diff cannot tell a pull from a step. That pair now resolves to `?`,
which is the bucket for what the instrument cannot tell; claiming a disagreement there
manufactures one.

A tighter-looking guard was tried and reverted: counting every *vanished* enemy as having
acted. It fails on the common case, because the overwhelmingly usual reason an enemy
vanishes is that the hero just killed it — it annotated every killing blow with a phantom
enemy turn. Its own test caught it; `tests/bridge/test_run.py` now pins the revert.

## Acceptance run 6: the bridge went silent mid-fight, and the magic byte was innocent (2026-08-15)

**It was one run, not two.** The log carries two tallies — 26 and 25 resolved
recommendations, 73%/19%/8% and 72%/20%/8% — and every advisory block in it
appears exactly twice, at the same second, with identical text. `Session.emit`
writes once, so that is two *processes*: `run.py --log run1.log` and
`overlay.py --log run1.log`, run together because this file listed them as a
pair. `overlay.py` builds its own `Session` and its own `_watch` thread — it
**contains** the loop rather than attaching to one. So the two tallies are the
same 26 turns counted twice, differing by one because one process crashed and
lost a turn. n is 26, not 51.

That is also the whole explanation for the crash, and the first diagnosis
written here (racing the game's own write) was wrong:

`_stage` staged into a **fixed** path, `%TEMP%/dd_live_state`. Two watchers
therefore `copy2` into the same destination *while the other reads it*. Both
wake on the same mtime change within milliseconds of each other, so this is not
a race that might happen — it is the normal case, and no game write need be
involved at all. Racing the game is possible in principle; this was the cause.
The directory is now per-PID. Every bridge tool uses `_stage`, so the same
collision was available to `town_state.py` in one terminal and `run.py` in
another.

The `?` rate is the one number here that is an assertion rather than an
observation — `tests/bridge/test_run.py` pins 5.4% over the 92 replayed pairs, and
2-of-26 is 7.7%, inside binomial noise at n=26 (95% CI roughly 1%-25%).

What the crash looked like:

```
Exception in thread Thread-1 (_watch):
  ...
  File "tools\dson.py", line 148, in parse_bytes
    raise ValueError("not a DSON buffer (bad magic)")
```

**The magic was fine.** It is the first four bytes the game writes, so it is
present throughout a torn write; `is_dson` checks only those four bytes and said
yes to a save the game was halfway through replacing, and `parse_bytes` then
indexed past the end and blamed the magic. The error message named the one part
of the header that was correct, which is why this looked like a decoder bug and
was not one.

Nor was `_stage` protecting against it, despite existing for exactly this
purpose. It retried on `OSError` — Windows *refusing* the read — and never on
the case that actually occurs: Windows granting it and returning half a file.

Three fixes, the third being what actually cost the run:

- **The staging directory is per-process** (`dd_live_state_<pid>`), which removes
  the collision rather than tolerating it.
- **`tools/dson.is_complete`** checks the header's three declared extents
  (`meta1_off + 16n`, `meta2_off + 12n`, `data_off + data_len`) against the bytes
  in hand. Cheap on purpose — no parse — so `_stage` can afford it on every poll,
  and it now retries until the copy is whole (`_STAGE_ATTEMPTS = 6`, 0.15 s
  apart) rather than until the copy call returns.
- **The watch thread no longer dies on a bad poll.** It is a daemon, and the
  overlay's only source of `Update`s, so an exception took the whole bridge
  silent *while the human kept playing and kept believing it was watching*. That
  is strictly worse than any single dropped poll, and it silently truncated the
  second run's tally. The per-tick body is wrapped, the failure is printed, and
  `last_sig` is cleared so the next tick retries **that** save instead of waiting
  for the game to write again.

`tests/tools/test_dson.py` pins the premise directly — a half-file passes `is_dson` and
fails `is_complete` — and `tests/bridge/test_live_bridge.py` drives `_stage` against a
copy that is short for its first two attempts and whole on the third, plus one
that is never whole, so the retry neither gives up early nor spins forever.

## The marker measured AoEs and corpses wrong, and both inflated DIFFER (2026-08-15)

The player's annotation of run 6 settled the five DIFFERs, and **all five were
the instrument, not the play**. He had followed every recommendation, including
the ones he thought were bad — which is the annotation that makes the rest of
this readable, because it turns each DIFFER from a disagreement into a claim
about the marker that can be checked.

**Three were AoE.** `advised_target` returned one guid and `observe` kept one
member of the healed set (`healed[0]`, dictionary order), so Divine Comfort
(`.target @~1234`, heals the whole party plus the caster) was judged by comparing
an arbitrary beneficiary against a nominal rank. The same play read MATCH or
DIFFER depending on iteration order. Both sides are now sets: the footprint comes
from the engine's own `_resolve_action_targets` — the authority, and re-resolved
against the live board, so a second implementation here would drift — and
`Observed.guids` carries everyone the diff saw change. Verified on a real seeded
board: `gods_comfort` resolves to all four party guids from any nominal rank,
`divine_grace` to exactly one.

The permissiveness this buys is real: a single-target heal aimed inside a
party-wide footprint now reads MATCH. It is **printed rather than hidden** —
`(1 of 4 in the advised footprint)` — because being stricter would need to know
who *could* have been healed, i.e. who was damaged, and `Board` carries current
HP and not max. A shortfall is visible on the line instead of vanishing into the
tally, which is the rule `?` already follows.

**Two were corpses, and that is the worse bug.** `Board.of` dropped corpses on
purpose, with a comment: a killed enemy leaves the live list and *that* is the
kill signal, so folding corpses back in would turn every kill into "an enemy
whose HP merely changed". Correct, and it silently stopped being sufficient on
2026-08-15, when corpses became legal targets. Breaking one was invisible, so a
followed recommendation to attack a corpse was marked by whatever leftover signal
remained — reported both times as "advised attack, played support" against a
player who had done exactly what he was told. `advised_target` had the matching
hole from the other side: it filtered on `not e.is_dead`, so advice aimed at a
corpse resolved to an empty set, which `judge` read as "no target claim to
check" and passed.

Corpses now live in their own `corpse_hp`/`corpse_names` dicts, which keeps both
halves. A corpse whose HP dropped is an attack on it; a corpse that *vanished*
counts only when the round did not advance, since rot resolves at end of round
and across that boundary destruction and decay are indistinguishable — the
instrument refuses to guess rather than picking the flattering reading.

The regression this exposed is worth its own note. `MOVE_PAIR` — a capture pinned
since 2026-08-11 as the anchor for "a real turn attributed as a move" — is a
Highwayman advancing a rank **and taking 6 hp off the corpse at slot 2**. It is
Duelist's Advance, and the test was pinning the blind spot as if it were the
answer. There is no move-only turn anywhere in `raidsnaps2`'s 97 in-battle
snapshots; the two that exist are in `raidsnaps4`, so the anchor moved there and
the old pair is now `CORPSE_PAIR`, the only corpse attack in 189 captured pairs.

## Retreat becomes an action, and two suspected reward bugs turn out not to be (2026-08-15)

Both open PLAYBOOK defects were **measured before anything was decided**, because "is this
the reward or the policy" has different fixes and the answer was not guessable. 6M final
