save, 36 episodes / 12 comps / `--comp-seed 1`, in `archive/logs/playbook_sharpened_final6M.log`.

**3.2, over-healing.** 413 unjustified heals. Target HP fraction **median 92%**, 41.9%
above 95%, **49%** of the skill's output wasted on a full bar, a **median of 2 HP** actually
restored. That is the live report ("Divine Comfort with three at full and one hurt") with
numbers on it. But the realized step reward is **-0.061 for an unjustified heal against
-0.023 for a justified one** — and the comparison that counts is heal-vs-HEAL, since an
attack turn (+0.36) scores higher for reasons that have nothing to do with 3.2. The reward
already prices these as a loss. It is not buying them, so a reward term is the wrong tool.

A legality narrowing is not available either, and checking that is what killed the plan I
had written down. `_is_wasted_heal` blocks a heal that cannot restore **a single point** —
a fact, not a threshold — and it is already applied on both the AoE and the single-target
branch. A hero at 92% can receive 2 HP, so these heals are legal under a rule that is
working exactly as designed. Going further needs an invented HP threshold, which is a
convention masquerading as game data. **Left open.**

**Corpses over living targets.** The sharpened CRP question, added after two live boards:
of 99 corpse attacks, **38.4%** had a living enemy in the same skill's reach that the same
hit would have killed outright (conservative — `dmg_min` before modifiers, so those kills
were never in doubt). Real, and the same diagnosis: `W_BLOCKING_CORPSE` is 0.10/4 against
an entire enemy leaving Φ on a kill, so the reward prefers the kill by a wide margin
already. Optimization slack, not a mis-specified reward.

So the retrain carries **retreat and nothing else** — which is the point of measuring first,
because both of these were tempting to "fix".

### Retreat

Every eval this project has run was taken with the party unable to leave, and a party that
cannot walk out of a losing fight overstates how dangerous the dungeon is. All three numbers
are `shared/rules.json`: 0.70 base, +0.05 per failed attempt this combat, 20 stress per
living hero on success. New Game Plus ships the same odds and **25** stress, which is what
settles the third as the price of leaving rather than an unrelated nearby constant.

- `ACTION_RETREAT` is **appended** at 37, so 0..36 still mean what they meant. `Discrete(38)`,
  observation 336 -> 337, the new global being the **live** chance (a constant would carry no
  information — the mistake torchlight made before it was sampled). **No older checkpoint
  loads**, which blocks `bridge/` until a new one exists.
- `winner` gains `"retreat"`. Not folded into `"enemies"`: nobody died, and both
  `ExpeditionSequenceEnv` and `eval_expedition` have to price the difference.
- **The price is emergent, not added.** `REWARD_RETREAT` is `0.0`; a retreat forfeits
  `_reward_win` (+8) and pays 20 stress through the existing dense `STRESS_TICK_PENALTY`.
  Against `REWARD_HERO_DEATH` (-20) that makes fleeing correct at roughly half an expected
  death. No new non-potential term, so the invariance argument is untouched.
- **A retreated combat is not a completion**: `eval_expedition` requires `retreats == 0`,
  or the option would inflate SUCCESS by definition instead of by playing better.
- **An afflicted hero never flees.** The game ships `retreat_from_combat` at weight 0 in
  every trait, so `_act_out_random_command` excludes `__retreat__` rather than reaching it
  through the back door.
- `min_hero_count_for_escape` (3) is loaded and **not** implemented: no shipped file says
  what it gates and all three modes ship the same value, so there is no contrast to read it
  from.

## The retreat retrain: what it bought, and the fidelity gap it exposed (2026-08-16)

6M steps, `--full-expedition`, from scratch (a widened action space cannot resume).
`archive/logs/train_retreat_6M.log`, evals at `eval_retreat6M_{final,best}.log`, both with
the historical baseline flags `--episodes 50 --comps 12 --comp-seed 1`.

**Read the control column, not SUCCESS.** SUCCESS now requires `retreats == 0`, so it fell
36.0% against the 66.7% of the previous bundle — a moved bar, not worse play. The column
that is measured the way archived evals measured it is `old-succ` (end reached alive, fights
skipped or not):

| | old-succ (control) | wipe | fled >=1 | fights fled/exped. | deaths/exped. | SUCCESS (strict) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| bundle 6M (no retreat) | 66.7% | 21.5% | — | — | — | 66.7% |
| **retreat 6M, final** | **67.1%** | **8.0%** | 63.0% | 1.93 | 0.69 | 36.0% |
| retreat 6M, best_model | 65.4% | 8.4% | 66.9% | 2.38 | 0.70 | 30.6% |

Parity on the control, and **wipes cut from 21.5% to 8.0%** — the party survives far more
often for the same rate of walking out alive. That is what retreat was added to buy.

The final save wins every column, which is the **third** run where the eval disagreed with
what SB3 called "best". Measure both, always.

**It flees on the right signal.** Over 24 episodes, 59 retreats against 2,387 other turns:

| | party HP | party stress | heroes alive | enemies up |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| retreated | 0.62 | 64.5 | 3.41 | 3.32 |
| did not | 0.79 | 29.0 | 3.92 | 2.23 |

Battered, stressed, a hero down, facing a fuller board. All four point the same way, so this
is not a cheap way to skip content picked at random.

**But 45 of 59 retreats are on round 0**, and 11 more on round 1 — it decides before anyone
swings, off the carried-over party state. That is legitimate play ("do not fight this one"),
and it is also where the honest limitation is: **the simulator under-prices a retreat,
because it models neither loot nor a quest goal.** A fled room in the real game costs its
treasure and can fail a `clear all` quest; here it costs the forfeited `_reward_win` and 20
stress a head, and nothing else. So **1.93 fled fights per expedition is an upper bound on
what a real player should do**, and the live advisor must keep pricing retreat through
`bridge/retreat.py` (which does read town gold) rather than deferring to the policy's action.

**The two measured defects, re-measured on the new model:**

* **Corpses over living targets: 38.4% -> 19.6%.** Halved by training alone, which is what
  "optimization slack, not a mis-specified reward" predicted. The call not to touch
  `W_BLOCKING_CORPSE` holds.
* **3.2 over-healing: 54.1% -> 55.1%, unmoved.** Total healing did fall (16.5% -> 10.3% of
  turns, n 413 -> 200) but the *proportion* that is unjustified did not, and the reward gap
  widened sharply (unjustified -0.171 against justified +0.413, delta -0.58, against -0.038
  before). The reward now discriminates clearly and the policy still does it, which settles
  it as slack that more training does not close. The honest options remain a convention or
  nothing; `_is_wasted_heal` cannot be extended without inventing an HP threshold.

## Acceptance run 7: guard redirects the hit, and the marker did not know (2026-08-15)

One process this time, so one tally over 32 recommendations: **84% / 9% / 6%**
(MATCH / DIFFER / `?`), against 73% / 19% / 8% over the 26 of run 6. The AoE and
corpse fixes account for the move — those were 5 of run 6's DIFFERs — and the `?`
rate sits on the 5.4% `tests/bridge/test_run.py` pins.

All **three** remaining DIFFERs were one situation, three turns running:

```
octotank_A guards fishman_harpoon_A | 2 enemies left
-> PICK TO THE FACE on rank 2 (Pelagic Grouper, 14 hp)   100%
   DIFFER played attack Pelagic Guardian (slot 1) - same kind, advice pointed at Pelagic Grouper (slot 2)
```

A Pelagic Guardian was guarding the Pelagic Grouper, so every hit aimed at rank 2
landed on the Guardian instead — 25 -> 16 -> 10 hp across the three turns. The
click was right, the advice was right, and the save records the wrong combatant
losing HP. Nothing about the play was a disagreement.

What makes this one embarrassing rather than merely missing: **the bridge already
printed the relation on the same screen.** `advisor.py` emits
`octotank_A guards fishman_harpoon_A` in the battle block; the marker just never
judged with it. `Board` now carries `guarded_by`, read off `stat_type` 27 — which
sits on the GUARDED combatant and whose `guarded_guid` names the guardian, the
same reading `bridge/seed.py` uses — and a guardian standing in front of an
advised target counts as reaching it.

Two rules keep that from becoming "any enemy will do". The guardians widen what
counts as **reached** but never the **footprint** the count is reported against,
so a redirected single-target hit reads `1 of 1` and not `1 of 2`; and the
redirect is named in the verdict (`guarded — the hit landed on ...`) rather than
passing silently. Without a marker on the board the same play is the
disagreement it always was.

Anchored in real data as well as a stub: 17 of `raidsnaps2`'s snapshots carry a
live guard — the Man-at-Arms (279) in front of the Highwayman (245), later the
Vestal (246). The stub pins the *direction*, which is easy to reverse; the
capture test pins that the field is read at all, so a rename would fail one and
not the other.

## The dungeon block told the player the opposite of its own advice (2026-08-15)

Three complaints from live play, one of which is a bug and not a matter of taste.

**The torch.** `Layers.block` called the torch layer with `about_to_win=True`,
hard-coded. That parameter means "is the killing blow the next thing that
happens", so between rooms the layer was answering the in-battle question and
printing *"SHUT DOWN the torch before the killing blow: 4 torches buys +30% gold
and +12 treasure draws on this fight"* — in a corridor, for a fight that was not
happening. The player's own framing was the diagnosis: it makes no sense with
nothing to open. Out of combat there is no loot roll pending, so there is nothing
darkness can buy.

It is not merely noise. On the same dark board the two answers are **opposites**:

```
about_to_win=True  -> shutdown | SHUT DOWN the torch before the killing blow: 3 torches buys +30% gold...
about_to_win=False -> relight  | RELIGHT: dark outside the killing blow is a straight loss...
```

So the block was printing the reverse of its own layer's advice, in every
corridor, for the whole run. Now it passes `state.in_battle`, and out of combat
it prints nothing at all when the answer is `hold` — the default and the usual
answer, and two lines per room to say "carry on".

**The route.** "No se entiende qué pasillo te recomienda", and the answer was
genuinely in there — in the middle of a 21-area path rendered as one line of
4-character ids. The immediate next hop gets its own line, first, with what is
waiting in it: `NEXT -> rooG  (room, 1 fight, 1 curio)`. The full path stays as
context, truncated at 8 hops. No compass direction is possible: `persist.map.json`
carries `bounds` for the whole map but no per-area coordinates, so the area id is
the only handle there is.

**The volume.** The dungeon-wide curio list — eleven lines naming areas the party
had not chosen to walk to — was reprinted on every move. It is now restricted to
the planned route, capped at three, and stripped to area + decision, since three
rooms away the only question a curio answers is what to keep in the bag. The
compulsion warnings are hoisted out of the per-curio lines (`grave_robber is
curious (20%)` is a fact about the party, not about the curio, and it was most of
the width). The camp section is suppressed when there is no firewood at all,
which on a short quest is every room for the whole run. Fifteen lines became six.

One more failure mode found while rendering it: **a Windows console is cp1252**,
several layers print an em dash, and `print` raises `UnicodeEncodeError` on
those — from inside the daemon poll thread, which is the same silent death as a
torn save. `Session.emit` now degrades the screen and keeps the log (opened
utf-8) exact.

## Two rulers, opposite verdicts: `tools/agreement.py` (2026-08-17)

The standing complaint about this project is that training longer does not improve real
play. Every measurement it had was taken **inside the simulator** — the thing whose
faithfulness is the open question — and `CLAUDE.md` already concedes the eval's own noise
is wider than any fidelity fix ever measured.

`tools/agreement.py` asks the question with no simulator in the loop: **over positions that
really happened, how often does a checkpoint advise the move that was actually played?**
`bridge/run.py` already computes that (arm a recommendation, watch the next save, mark
MATCH / DIFFER / `?`); the tool replays the capture corpus per checkpoint and pairs the
results decision by decision.

`Session` gained an `on_score(layer, verdict, key, info)` hook for it. **The key names the
decision point, never the advice** — round, whose turn it was, what the human played, all
read from the save — so a change of checkpoint cannot change it. The tool asserts the two
key sequences are identical and **refuses to print a delta if they are not**, which is the
discipline whose absence let 8.4pp of harness noise read as signal for months.
`--self-check` plays one checkpoint as both arms; every delta must be exactly zero. It is.

### The finding: the simulator's pick and real play disagree

101 markable combat decisions across `raidsnaps2/3/4`:

| layer | n | best_model | final save | delta | 95% CI | b/c | p |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| combat | 101 | **47.5%** | 35.6% | **-11.9pp** | [-23.8, +1.0] | 27/15 | 0.088 |
| route | 5 | 80.0% | 80.0% | 0.0 | — | 0/0 | 1.000 |
| torch | 9 | 66.7% | 66.7% | 0.0 | — | 0/0 | 1.000 |
| curio | 14 | 85.7% | 85.7% | 0.0 | — | 0/0 | 1.000 |

The non-combat layers coming out at exactly 0/0 discordant is the sanity property: those
layers are algorithmic and do not consult the checkpoint, so a tool that moved them would
be measuring itself.

**This runs opposite to the paired simulator eval**, where the final save beat
`best_model` by +8.3pp at p=0.020 over 216 scenarios. Here `best_model` agrees with real
play 11.9pp more often, at p=0.088.

**Neither result should be over-read, and the direction of the conflict is the point.**
p=0.088 does not clear 0.05, n is 101, and the corpus is one player's three raids. More
importantly, **MATCH is agreement, not correctness**: a DIFFER can be the policy finding
something better than the human did, and the marker's own instruction is to annotate
DIFFERs by hand as A (impossible — a bridge bug), B (legal but worse) or C (better than
the advice). So this is not evidence that `best_model` plays better. It is evidence that
**the simulator eval's verdict is not corroborated by real play**, and that "prefer the
final save" rests on one ruler rather than two.

### What the disagreement actually is: aiming, not choosing

The breakdown of combat DIFFERs by (played kind → advised kind):

| played → advised | best_model | final |
|---|---|---|
| attack → attack | 36 | 48 |
| heal → attack | 6 | 6 |
| support → attack | 4 | 6 |
| attack → support | 4 | 1 |
| move → attack | 2 | 2 |
| attack → heal | 0 | 2 |
| support → support | 1 | 0 |

~70% of both checkpoints' disagreements are `attack → attack` — the same *kind* of action
against a different target or footprint. The 12-decision gap between the arms is almost
entirely in that row (36 → 48), and the "different play entirely" rows are 17 for both.
So the two checkpoints do not choose differently, **they aim differently**, and target
selection is where policy diverges from human play.

### A doc trap this cleared up

`CLAUDE.md` and `BRIDGE.md` cite **84% combat MATCH**, and the replay corpus gives ~47%.
That is not a regression. The 84% is from **live run 7, over 32 recommendations, with a
human who could see the advice on screen**; the ~47% is the offline replay figure, and
`CHANGELOG.md`'s own signal-ranking entry already recorded **"MATCH 46% / DIFFER 46% /
`?` 7%"** for `raidsnaps2` when that ranking landed. Two populations, cited side by side
with nothing saying so. 84% on n=32 carries roughly ±13pp, so the gap is a real population
difference and not sampling — whether it is the human following the advice he was shown,
or simply easier positions, is **not resolved here**. The replay number is the one worth
tracking: it is reproducible, free to re-run, and does not move when a human does.

## The full pack: a discard rule that proposed throwing away the gold (2026-08-17)

The last routine interruption in the dungeon loop was the loot modal. A fight ends,
`act.py` presses SPACE (the game's documented take-all hotkey), and if the pack is full DD
answers with *"You don't have enough room in inventory to take all."* That modal **writes
nothing to the save**, so the actuator's generic acknowledgement — "no save change means
the keystroke was dropped, retry" — pressed SPACE into a dialog already up. Seen live.

**What the game's own files settled, so none of it had to be guessed:**

- **Discarding is SHIFT-CLICK.** `localization/miscellaneous.string_table.xml` ships
  `str_discard_item_instructions` = *"[SHIFT-CLICK] on this item to discard it."* There is
  no discard hotkey, and no confirmation dialog on PC: every "Discard this item?" string
  lives in `PSN.string_table.xml`, for the controller's hold-TRIANGLE. So the click is
  immediate and irreversible.
- **The same gesture means something else on the torch meter**, which is what stopped the
  discard being treated as atomic. The same file ships `str_reduce_torch_tip` =
  *"[SHIFT+CLICK] to reduce torch"* and `str_snuff_torch_tip` =
  *"[SHIFT+CTRL+CLICK] to snuff out torch"*. Reported from play, then confirmed in the
  strings. Nothing says whether a shift-click on a pack *stack* takes one unit or the
  whole thing — see the verification section below.
- **The save's slot index IS the on-screen cell.** `party.inventory.items` children are
  named `"0"`..`"15"`, and across all 399 raid saves in `archive/traces/` they are
  **contiguous from 0 with no gaps, max index 15** — which matches
  `inventory/base.inventory.system_configs.darkest`'s `.type "raid" .max_slots 16`. The
  grid renders them 8 across, then 8 more: `end_of_battle_rewards_1_item.png`'s top row
  reads 2,2,6,12,6,6,8,3, which is the save's slots 0-7 in order.
- **Stack limits and both gold prices** come from the five `inventory/*.darkest` files.

**The geometry is measured, not calibrated.** Edge-detected off `captures/*.png` at
2560x1440 and verified by drawing the derived grid back over the capture, so it lives as
constants in `bridge/layout.py` rather than in the hand-clicked `overlay_layout.json` —
there is nothing for a human to click and a clicked value would be strictly worse. The
detection worth remembering: **a cell border is bright at every y down the cell while an
icon is not**, so a min-over-y column profile isolates the vertical frame lines and ignores
whatever items happen to be in the pack. It returned identical lines in all three captures
that show it. Grid edges 1306.0 and 2159.3 px, pitch 106.66, rows centred 1100.5 / 1306.5.

### The bug: `sell_gold_value == 0` does not mean worthless

The first discard rule selected anything the game priced at zero to buy *and* zero to sell,
on the reasoning that nothing free in both directions could matter. Run against the first
capture, it proposed:

```
discard slot 10 (portrait x1), slot 14 (gold x625)
```

Both are 0/0. **Every heirloom sells for 0** because heirlooms are the town-upgrade
currency rather than merchandise, and **gold sells for 0** because it is gold. The prices
describe what a *shop* will do, and the two things a raid exists to bring home are not
sold in shops.

The rule is now a **positive gate**: a candidate must be `type == "supply"` *and* 0/0.
Across everything the game defines that selects exactly `firewood` and `dog_treats`, and
firewood is excluded by name because it is what pays for a campfire. A type this module
has never seen defaults to "leave it alone" — which is the property a deny-list would not
have had.

### What the corpus says about how much that buys: almost nothing

Swept over all 399 raid saves, the rule proposes **zero discards**. Of **25 distinct full
packs**, none held chaff and only **6** had a free stack merge available. So the automatic
path covers the case that prompted the work (a live pack carrying two dog-treat stacks) and
nineteen-in-twenty-five of the historical ones fall through to `--full-pack`:

- `pause` (default) prints the whole pack and hands over. Cannot lose anything.
- `abandon` clicks the loot window's X and walks on. It is a legitimate play — the modal
  itself offers "choose only what you can carry" — but **the bridge cannot see what it is
  abandoning**: loot is not in the save until it is picked up, so it can neither weigh nor
  report what was left. That asymmetry is why `pause` is the default rather than a
  compromise.

**Merging split stacks is strictly better than any discard and is deliberately not done.**
`use_stack_limits true`, so three torch stacks holding 15 of a limit of 8 fit in two slots.
`DiscardPlan.notes` reports it; nothing executes it, because merging is a click-drag and
the actuator only does discrete clicks.

### A destructive input needs a stronger acknowledgement, and the first version was wrong

Everywhere else in the bridge the ACK is "the save changed". For a discard that is not
enough — the save changing does not say the *right* slot emptied.

The first `_discard_slot` tested *"is slot 0 still holding `dog_treats x2`"*. That is
wrong if a shift-click takes a **single unit**: `x2` becomes `x1`, the label differs, the
test passes, and the runner reports the slot freed and presses SPACE straight into a second
modal — the exact bug the whole change set exists to remove. And the torch tooltips above
show the game *does* use shift-click as a decrement elsewhere, so this was never a remote
possibility.

Fixed by measuring the only thing that actually decides whether the loot fits: **the number
of occupied slots**. The loop clicks, re-reads, and stops the moment the pack is one slot
shorter; if the pack merely shrank it clicks again, bounded by the stack's own `amount`
(at most 2 for the only item the rule ever proposes). It re-checks the target's item id
every iteration, because discarding may re-pack the slots and a second click at the same
coordinate would otherwise land on whatever slid into it. So the actuator does not pick a
behaviour, it observes one — the same discipline `Heading` uses for left-vs-right.

Backends grew `click(screen, mods)` and release held modifiers in a `finally`, because a
SHIFT left down after a failed discard would turn the operator's next real click into
another one.

### The torch meter: measured, and deliberately not wired

`layout.TORCH_METER` = `(1280, 151)` of 2560x1440. The bar is centre-anchored and its lit
portion shrinks *symmetrically* as the torch burns down — across four captures the ends
move (935-1624, 956-1603, 977-1582) and the midpoint is 1279.5 every time — so the centre
is the only stable target, and it sits on the brazier's base inside both sprite and bar.

Nothing sends it. `bridge/torchlight.py` produces a `"shutdown"` action for PLAYBOOK 2.3,
but `run.py` calls it with `about_to_win=state.in_battle`, so **out of combat the advisor
never returns one** and `plan_room`'s shutdown branch is unreachable by construction.
Wiring the gesture there would be actuating dead code. A real shutdown belongs in the
battle plan ("snuff, then swing"), which changes `plan_battle` and what a battle `Update`
carries; the coordinate is banked so that work does not re-measure it.

### Curio: the worry was wrong, but it is still not actionable

The concern was that a curio is a world object with no fixed screen position.
`shovel_curio.png` disproves it: the interaction panel is HUD, opens on arrival, and sits
in the same region as the loot window. But its button row's **y flows with the description
length** — the loot panel's X is at (1954, 775) in *both* loot captures, while the Rubble
curio's is at (1950, 618): same x, 157px higher. So `LOOT_CLOSE` is a constant and the
curio's is not. Acting on a curio needs the panel located on screen, which the actuator
cannot yet do.

## Leaving a room: the geometry was in the save all along (2026-08-18)

The standing blocker on an unattended dungeon run. Corridors walk on `A`/`D`; a room is only
left through the map panel, and the bridge could bring the map up and then had nothing to
click. Three separate things were wrong, and the game's own files settled all three.

**1. The click target was the wrong kind of area.** `localization/miscellaneous.string_table.xml`
carries `str_help_raid_room_0`:

> `[CLICK]` on a room in the map to begin moving to that room.

and `tutorial_popup_map_nav_description` repeats it: *"To move forth, `[CLICK]` on another
room on the map display. This will take you to the connecting hallway."* So the destination is
a **room**, and the game walks the hallway itself. `RoutePlan.next_step` from a room IS that
hallway — the bridge would have been aiming at a thing the map does not accept. Hence
`RoomAdvice.next_room`, the first `roo*` on `plan.walk[1:]`. A corridor tile is never a target
at all; the small cells are landmarks for measuring.

**2. `mappos` was believed to be absent from live saves. It is one decode further in.** A live
`persist.map.json` splits the map: `map.static_dynamic.areas` holds what changes (knowledge,
light, tile content) and `map.static_dynamic.static_save` holds the layout generated once.
`read_dungeon_map` read only the first, so every tile came back without coordinates and the
conclusion was that the geometry only existed in the hand-authored text template.
`static_save` is a **length-prefixed nested DSON buffer** — a little-endian u32 count, then
the `01 B1 00 00` magic — exactly like a hero record's `raw_data`. Decoded, it yields
`mappos` for **100% of tiles in all three captured dungeons** (48, 41 and 57 tiles).

Two encodings, distinguished by container rather than by a magnitude heuristic: DSON writes
`mappos` and `bounds` as int32s that are **float bit patterns** (`-1050673152` is `-14.0`),
the text template writes real floats. `map.bounds` decodes to `(min_x, max_x, min_y, max_y)`
and equals the tile extent **exactly** in all three real dungeons — `[-14, 15, -7, 8]`,
`[-13, 15, -1, 8]`, `[-11.5, 12.5, -9.5, 12.5]`. (The tutorial template declares
`±10.5` against a real extent of `-10.5..-3.5`; that one is stale hand-authoring, so do not
use the template's `bounds` for anything.)

**3. The map-unit-to-pixel scale is not a constant, and a stored one would have been wrong.**
Three real captures measured **48.0, 15.3 and 10.7 px per unit**. Not calibration drift — the
game ships `[MOUSEWHEEL]` zoom, `[RIGHT-CLICK]`+`[DRAG]` pan, a "Center map on party" button
and an `Auto-Center Dungeon Map` option, so the transform is user state. `bridge/mappanel.py`
recovers it per frame instead.

Details worth keeping, because each is a wrong turn already taken:

* **The cell detector needs two passes.** Column runs, row runs within each, then column runs
  again within *those*. One pass gives a vertical corridor the full width of whatever wide
  room shares its column — moving its centre tens of pixels sideways, and the centre IS the
  click.
* **A median gap is not the scale.** Rooms swallow the door tile either side of them, so gaps
  of 2 and 3 units are common. The tutorial's six cells give 96, 48, 48, 48, 95.5, 95.5 —
  median 71.75, which matches nothing. Every candidate scale is scored by a translation vote
  instead.
* **Two constraints keep the vote honest, and neither is a tuned threshold.** No two cells can
  sit closer than one map unit, so any candidate scale above the closest observed pair is
  geometrically impossible — that is what rejects the spurious large-scale fits (a wrong
  43.5 px/unit fit scoring 25/46 on a map whose real pitch was 10.7). And a cell cannot be
  much smaller than the scale, so the size filter is applied **per candidate**, which resolves
  the circularity in "too small for what?" rather than ducking it.
* **The last check is that the destination is actually drawn.** An unscouted room is simply
  not on the panel; the fit can be perfect and the transform will still happily predict a
  point for it. Clicking there lands on the panel's empty ground — or on the world behind it,
  which moves the party or pokes a curio. So the predicted point must fall inside a cell that
  was detected, and only among the cells the fit itself accepted, so a 3-pixel glyph cannot
  stand in for a room.

**Verified against ground truth.** `captures/02_starting_room_After_map_click.png` is the Old
Road's first room with the panel open and `profile_1` is the live save it came from: **48.0
px/unit, 6/6 cells explained, worst residual 12.3 px** — a quarter of a unit, from an
unvisited room's asymmetric box, against a full unit to the nearest wrong tile. It predicts
every tile centre to within a pixel (`1597, 1645, 1693, 1741, 1789, 1837` for the corridor,
`1549` for `rooA`, `1885` for `rooB`) without ever being told which cell was which. `rooB`
resolves to fraction `(0.7361, 0.8597)`, inside its own box.

**Confirmed live on the first run, and it exposed two more bugs immediately** (2026-08-18).
The party left `rooA` through the map — the route block on the next poll reads `NEXT -> coAB
(corridor, already walked...)`, and a room has no other exit — so the click lands. Then:

* **`W` is contextual, and the bridge pressed it standing in the wrong place.**
  `tutorial_popup_using_doors_description` is explicit: "To enter a door, `[CLICK]` or press
  `[W]` **when standing in front of it**", and `str_help_raid_hallway_4` says the same for
  objects. `_interact_curio` fired `W` on arrival in `coAB` to get the tutorial tent — but a
  party entering a corridor is standing at the door it just came through, so the one thing in
  front of it is the room it just left. It walked straight back into `rooA`. Reported as "it
  exited properly but immediately used W instead of D". The fix is one held `A`/`D` before the
  `W`, which is enough because the game stops the party at the curio by itself.

  It deliberately does **not** loop on "the save still shows the curio". That reads a success
  as a failure: most curios are LOOT curios and DD writes nothing until the loot is taken —
  the same trap that made the generic three-attempt `_send_and_confirm` press `W` twice more
  into an open loot window on `coBC`'s sack. `_curio_active` exists, but only to report.

* **The bridge was corrupting its own evidence.** After the successful map click the cursor
  stayed where SendInput left it, on top of `rooB`. DD drew a hover border round that room and
  a **"Move to this room"** tooltip under it, and the next frame the bridge grabbed had
  `rooB`'s cell merged with its neighbour (111 px tall instead of 40) plus thirteen
  letter-shaped boxes in a row: **19 cells instead of 6, and no fit**. Found by reading
  `captures/_map_panel.png`, which is exactly what the banking was added for. `_park_cursor`
  now slides the pointer into `MENU_DEAD_ZONE` — moving, never clicking — before every map
  read. Best-effort: a backend that cannot move the pointer loses the defence and nothing
  else. (The tooltip also independently confirms the click semantics.)

**Not yet validated on a full-size dungeon, and the threshold reflects that.** The unpaired
real captures fit `raidsnaps2` at 15.5 px/unit — matching the 15.3 pitch measured
independently off the same image, so probably a true fit — but explain only 15 of 23 cells,
under `MIN_EXPLAINED`, and nothing proves the capture and the save are the same raid. Setting
the threshold to admit that would be tuning to unverified data, so real dungeons refuse for
now. `act.py` banks the frame to `captures/_map_panel.png` on every refusal *and* on a click
the save does not confirm, which is how the paired capture gets produced without anyone
alt-tabbing at the right moment.

## The save carried a within-area position all along (2026-08-18)

Four refusals, three "impossible" limitations, and most of `Heading`'s existence rested on
one sentence written into `bridge/act.py` and repeated in `BRIDGE.md`:

> the save is area-granular and carries no within-area position

`raid.in_area` is area-granular, and that much is true. But the **map** save is not.
`persist.map.json`'s per-tile `knowledge` rises to `KNOWLEDGE_VISITED` behind the party as it
walks, so the visited tiles of a corridor are a run growing from the end it was entered by.
Measured over the whole capture corpus: **2517 corridor observations, zero discontiguous
runs** (`tests/bridge/test_dungeon_map.py::test_the_visited_run_is_contiguous_in_every_captured_corridor`,
which asserts `broken == 0` over `seen > 2000`). Per-tile `light` falls to 0 over the same
tiles one behind, independently pinning the frontier tile.

`dungeon_map.Walk` reads it: `passed` / `total` / `from_front`, with `index` (the save's own
tile number — what a curio or a battle tile is keyed by), `remaining`, and `heading`.
`Area.walked` returns it or `None`, and the `None`s are honest: a corridor not yet entered
(`not any`), one fully walked (`all` — which genuinely no longer records which end it was
entered by), and a **room**, which is a single tile and so has no run to measure. That last
case is why `plan_room`'s room-exit refusal survives this finding intact; its wording was
corrected to say *a room* is one tile rather than *the save* has no position.

### What it fixed

**A curio was walked up to instead of pressed at.** The `W`-is-contextual fix had added one
held `A`/`D` before the press, and one press is not a corridor: *"It did walk a little but
not even 1 square of the 4, on the tutorial the battle is at the second box and the curio is
after that... it didnt walk enough distance but got away from the door"*. `_walk_up_to` is a
loop now, walking until `index` reaches the curio's tile and stopping early the moment
progress stalls. The first attempt at a loop had no position to read and gated on
`_curio_active` instead, which pressed `W` twelve times — worse than the bug, because a loot
curio writes nothing to the save until the loot is taken, so a success reads as a failure.
`_curio_active` reports and never gates.

**Left from right stopped costing a traversal.** `Heading` learned direction by walking the
wrong way and flipping — visible in a live log as `entered rooA (WRONG WAY - flipped to A)`
— and could not learn at all when the wrong way was a dead end. `Walk.heading` is `D` when
the party entered at tile 0 and `A` when it entered at the far end. `_forward_key` prefers
the measurement and updates `Heading` from it; `_walk_toward` reads it once before the loop.
When the direction was **measured**, an exhausted budget no longer triggers the walk-back:
that manoeuvre exists to tell one end of a corridor from the other by trying both, and
reversing on a known-good direction walks the party away from the exit it was heading for.

### Two traps, both caught as bugs before they shipped

- **`index` counts DOWN for a party that entered at the far end**, because it is the tile
  order, not the party's progress. A bare `index >= target` reported arrival on the first
  read and walked nowhere. The distance is measured along the direction of travel instead:
  `ahead = target - index if from_front else index - target`, reached at `ahead <= 0` (which
  also covers an overshoot), stalled at `ahead >= last`.
- **"not read" and "read, and unreadable" are different answers.** `_forward_key(area, walk)`
  takes the caller's copy, because the loop was decoding `persist.map.json` twice per step
  for a value that cannot change; but `None` is a real reading, so the not-passed case needs
  the `_UNREAD` sentinel. Defaulting to `None` would have turned every internal call back
  into a guess, silently.

**`_walk_toward` deliberately does not use the per-step signal**, though it now could. That
loop's goal is to *leave* the corridor, and the step that leaves it is exactly the step that
makes `walked` go `None` — stopping on that would stop one step short of the boundary it is
walking toward. The area change remains its acknowledgement.

## A move is a SWAP, and the rule was exactly inverted (2026-08-19)

Found in live play, in the tutorial, with a two-hero party. The bridge advised the
Highwayman at rank 2 to MOVE to **rank 4**, pressed `5`, clicked where rank 4 would be,
and the game ignored it three times over. Fran: *"it tried to move to an empty position,
as there is no way to move to a slot that exceeds your party size (2 this time)"*.

`CombatEngine.get_valid_actions` offered a move to any rank in reach that was **not**
occupied by a living ally:

```python
hi = min(3, hero.position + hd.move_skill.backward)
for p in range(lo, hi + 1):
    if p == hero.position or p in occupied:      # exactly backwards
        continue
```

An occupied rank is the **only** legal destination. A move swaps the two heroes — the
game says so in its own words (`menu_options_element_tooltip_hold_required_in_dungeon`
is about *"swap hero positions"*), and it follows from the formation rule this engine
already enforces everywhere else: the party is contiguous from rank 1, so there is no
empty rank to move into and a move that left one would contradict
`_collapse_hero_position_gap`. **The engine already knew this.** `_act_out_change_pos`
— the affliction shuffle, in the same file — picks an occupied rank and swaps both
heroes. The voluntary Move branch was the only reposition in the codebase that did not.

### The inversion broke the action in both directions at once

| party | before | after |
|---|---|---|
| Crusader r1, Highwayman r2 | HW offered **r3, r4** (neither exists) | each offered the other's rank |
| four heroes, r1-r4 | **nobody can move at all** | r1→2, r2→1/3/4, r3→2/4, r4→3 |

The second row is the bigger finding and it never produced a bug report, because it is
silent: with all four ranks occupied every candidate was skipped, so **Move has been
dead in every full-strength combat this project ever simulated**. Action indices
`[28..31]` existed and were never once legal until a hero died. It only ever fired for
an under-strength party, and then only into the ranks its dead used to hold — which is
precisely the live failure, since those ranks are not on the board.

`_execute_action` matched: it wrote `performer.position = target` and nothing else,
leaving the origin empty and the destination doubly occupied. It swaps now, and
re-looks-up the partner rather than trusting the `Action`, because a DoT or a riposte
can kill the ally between legalisation and execution.

The party-size bound is **not** a second clamp. It falls out of requiring an occupant,
which is why the old `min(3, ...)` on `hi` is gone with it.

### This supersedes part of an earlier Move finding

ASSUMPTIONS.md records a 2026-08-08 fix to the same branch — the forward/backward
directions were inverted — and credits it with 45% → 55.4% success. That fix was real
and its numbers stand, but its framing does not: **Move was still not working after it.**
Every "move-fix" checkpoint was trained on an engine where a four-hero party could not
reposition, so what that sweep actually measured was repositioning *after a death*
becoming correct. The remaining plateau was read against an engine still missing the
mechanic.

### What it costs the current checkpoints, measured

`tools/agreement.py` on `logs/full_expedition/best_model.zip`, the ruler with no
simulator in it — 101 real captured combat decisions, before and after:

| | MATCH | new `-> move` advisories |
|---|---|---|
| before | 48 (47.5%) | 0 |
| after | **45 (44.6%)** | 8 (`attack -> move` 6, `support -> move` 2) |

**−2.9pp, and every point of it is the newly-legal action.** That is the expected sign,
not a refutation: the policy has never seen a legal Move in a full party, so being
offered one is out-of-distribution. It is also the falsifiable prediction a retrain
would test — that a policy trained *with* Move legal uses it better than never having
it, and that full-expedition SUCCESS does not regress. **No retrain has been run**, so
the current checkpoints play this action untrained; that is a known cost of shipping the
fidelity fix first.

### An adjacent gap this exposed, NOT fixed here

Push and pull write an absolute position and displace nobody:

```python
new_pos = eff_target.position - eff.push_distance
eff_target.position = max(0, min(new_pos, rear))
```

Reproduced: pushing the rank-3 hero of a full party two ranks forward gives hero
positions `[0, 0, 1, 3]` — two heroes on rank 1 and a hole at rank 3. The real game
shifts the intervening line instead. This is a separate mechanic with its own evidence
requirements and is left alone deliberately; note only that it means the hero formation
is **not** guaranteed contiguous mid-fight today, so a legal-move list can be shorter
than the real game's. The swap fix degrades safely into that state — fewer legal moves,
never a click at an empty rank.

## Putting the party back in order, and a stall that fired on the first press (2026-08-19)

Three findings from one tutorial run, in the order they matter.

### The party never went back in its marching order

Combat reorders the party and nothing put it back. The Move action swaps two heroes and
where they end up is where the **next** fight starts — a Highwayman parked at rank 1
opens it with `pistol_shot` (`.launch 432`) and `grape_shot_blast` (`.launch 23`) both
greyed out, half his movepool gone. Reported as *"Didn't use the set order after the
combat ... went stuck there as it tried to use a skill that cant be used in pos 1"*, with
the fix named: *"The default party order button is the one below the center party with
the circular arrows"*.

The game agrees in its own files: `str_party_default_order` = **"Default Party order"**,
and the binary names its icon `panels/party_order_button.png` in the same string block.
That asset is what located the button — 38x46 RGBA, alpha-matched against a live frame
over a scale sweep:

| | score | scale | box |
|---|---|---|---|
| best | **0.9767** | 1.35 | (2184,1080)-(2235,1142) |
| best anywhere else | 0.4778 | 0.60 | — |

Same box in a room, in a corridor after a map click, in combat, and with the pack open,
so it is HUD furniture rather than part of a panel that flows. `layout.PARTY_ORDER`
carries the centre as a client fraction, `(2209/2560, 1111/1440)`.

`_reset_formation` clicks it once after every fight, right after the loot. Two rules:

- **Not gated on detecting disorder.** The save records the *current* formation and
  nothing records the *intended* one, so "is it already right?" cannot be asked. The
  button is idempotent, so asking it and getting it wrong costs more than not asking.
- **Reported, never confirmed.** A party already in order produces no save change, so
  demanding one reads the common success as a failure — the same shape as the
  `_curio_active` trap. It prints the order before and after and moves on.

### The curio walk called a stall after ONE press

`Area.walked` is granular to one **tile** and `WALK_HOLD` (0.9 s) covers well under one,
so a press that leaves the party on the same tile is the normal mid-tile case. The stall
rule declared "something is in the way" on the first such read, which skipped the
tutorial's tent from two tiles out after exactly one press — *"kept walking the hallway,
skipped the curio"*. `WALK_STALL_LIMIT = 3` consecutive non-advancing reads now, reset by
any progress. It is a lower bound on "that press achieved nothing", not a measurement.

### `advised_button` was suspected and is correct — the save order IS the screen order

Worth recording because the suspicion was reasonable and wrong. The Highwayman's save
order is `opened_vein, pistol_shot, grape_shot_blast, take_aim`; his canonical order in
`highwayman.info.darkest` is `wicked_slice, pistol_shot, point_blank_shot,
grape_shot_blast, take_aim, duelist_advance, opened_vein`. If DD's action bar sorted
canonically, `opened_vein` would be slot 4 and every combat click would be off by three
for this hero. The Crusader cannot discriminate — his equipped four *are* the canonical
first four — so no earlier live run could have caught it.

A live frame settles it: Dismas at rank 1 shows `opened_vein` lit at slot 1,
`pistol_shot` **greyed** at 2, `grape_shot_blast` **greyed** at 3, `take_aim` lit at 4.
That is the save order exactly, and the greying matches the launch data for rank 1
(`432` and `23` exclude it; `321` and `4321` do not). The 2026-08-15 entry's claim of
"in save order" was an assumption when written; it is measured now.

Which also means the advice that looked stuck was sound: **"Tracking Shot" is
`take_aim`'s display name**, `.launch 4321`, legal from every rank.

### The Move misplay is the cost already recorded, arriving

The policy moved the Highwayman to rank 1, where two of his four skills are dead. That is
not a new bug — it is the out-of-distribution Move action from the swap fix, being played
by a checkpoint that never saw Move legal in training. The formation reset limits the
damage to the fight it happens in rather than carrying it into every later one.

## The position signal was the wrong field, and the right one was one line away (2026-08-19)

`raid.areatile` — the game's own count of how many tiles into the current area the party
has walked. It sits three fields below `in_area`, which `bridge/route.py` has read since
the map decoded, and nothing had ever looked at it.

Yesterday's finding stands but was second best. Per-tile `knowledge` does rise behind the
party, and it *was* the first within-area position this project had — but knowledge marks
tiles as **revealed**, not as walked. Graded against `areatile` over every paired capture
in the corpus:

| signal | agrees | **disagrees** | cannot say |
|---|---|---|---|
| knowledge run (`Area.walked`) | 172 | **51** | 45 |
| dark run + `areatile` (`Area.standing_on`) | **223** | **0** | 45 |

The failure is not noise, it is structural: `coCD` reads `..#####` at areatile 3 **and**
at areatile 4. A walk steered by it looks stalled while the party is moving, which is
exactly what stopped the tutorial curio walk two tiles short of the tent — twice, the
second time after `WALK_STALL_LIMIT` had already been raised to 3, because the signal was
not slow, it was wrong. Verified from the save afterwards: the tent still read
`content 7, active True`, so the `W` fired at nothing.

### The two signals, and why both

`areatile` is exact and monotone but carries **no direction** — it counts from whichever
end the party entered by. Turning it into a `Tile.index` (what a curio or a battle tile is
keyed by) needs that end.

**The dark run supplies it.** Per-tile `lit` falls to 0 behind the party, one per tile, so
the dark run's leading edge is where they stand and the end it grows from is the end they
came in by. `Area.standing_on` returns `(absolute tile, entered at tile 0)`.

`_walk_progress` takes both and **refuses when they disagree**: two exact signals that
differ is not a position to steer by, and refusing costs one walk where guessing walks
somewhere wrong.

Two things that do NOT work and were tried:

- **Deriving the entry end from the corridor's name.** `Area.rooms` parses `coBC` to
  `(rooB, rooC)`, but a corridor's tiles do not reliably run X→Y: `coBC` entered from
  `rooB` puts the party at tile 4 of 5, not tile 0. 92 of 268 observations wrong.
- **`Area.reversed_`.** Not set on any of the failing areas; it does not mean this.

`standing_on` is silent on 45 observations, all of them corridors already walked dark end
to end. `areatile` still counts there — it just has no direction — and those are re-walks,
where there is no unvisited curio to walk up to.

## The tutorial has no default party order (2026-08-19)

Yesterday's `PARTY_ORDER` button works, and on the tutorial it does nothing. It restores
the order the **campaign** recorded at embark, and the tutorial never embarked — its save
ships mid-raid, straight into the Old Road. So the click is inert and the bridge cheerfully
reported `party order highwayman>crusader (already the marching order)` about a party that
was backwards. Reported with the fix named: *"tutorial has no 'default' party order, so
specifically during the tutorial it should move to restart the order, to do this we can use
the move action outside of combat for example"*.

**The intended order has to be watched, because nothing records it.** The save carries the
current formation and no field for the one the human arranged. `ActRunner.marching_order`
is captured from the first room block with no fight behind it, which is the party as it was
set up. `_sort_into_marching_order` then does a selection sort with the MOVE action —
select the hero, `5`, click the rank — because out of combat that is the same control, the
dungeon HUD showing the same action bar with the same circular arrows at slot 5.

Three refusals, all deliberate: an order this runner never saw un-fought is never imposed;
a party whose membership changed (a death) is left alone rather than sorted toward an order
naming someone who is gone; and each swap is verified by re-reading the formation before
the next, not by the save's mtime.

## In front of the curio, not on it — and three acknowledgements that were too impatient (2026-08-19)

One tutorial run, and the position work from earlier the same day held: the party swapped
into its marching order, walked four tiles to the tent, and entered `rooB` correctly.
Everything below is what was still wrong once those stopped hiding it.

### `W` was landing one tile past the curio

The walk ran until the save said the party stood **on** the curio's tile, and `W` acts on
what they are standing **in front of**. Live twice: `standing on tile 4, where
travellers_tent_tutorial is`, then `W`, and the tent still read `content 7, active True`
afterwards. That is conclusive rather than suggestive — `travellers_tent_tutorial` is a
**loot** curio (`curio_type_library.csv`: Loot, weight 1, 100%, 2 draws of *"Someone left
valuables inside the tent"*), so a press that worked would have opened a loot window and
the `SPACE` that follows would have changed the save. It did not.

The game's two help strings differ in exactly the way that matters, and the difference had
been read backwards:

| | text |
|---|---|
| `tutorial_popup_using_doors_description` | "[CLICK] or press [W] **when standing in front of it**" |
| `tutorial_popup_curio_description` | "[CLICK] or press [W] to investigate them" |

The door string was taken as the strict case and the curio as the loose one. It is the
other way round for *approach*: the game stops the party at a curio by itself, so the
place to press is one tile short. `ahead <= 1` now, and `_walk_up_to` says which of "on"
or "in front of" it settled for.

### A curio that does not respond now says so

`_curio_active` was consulted after every `W` and only spoke when the answer was `False`.
So two whole sessions of the tent silently reporting "still active" printed nothing at
all. It prints both answers now. "Still standing" is **not** proof of failure — a loot
curio writes nothing until the loot is taken — but a run where every curio reports it is
a run where `W` is hitting nothing, and that is the sentence that was missing.

### The modal click was being sent after every fight, into a live hallway

`_make_room` dismissed the "no room" modal **unconditionally**, on the reasoning that OK
lands on the middle of the dungeon backdrop when no modal is up. The game's own curio text
says otherwise: *"[CLICK] or press [W] to investigate them"* — a click in the middle of a
hallway is one of the two documented ways to work a curio. And `SPACE` with no loot window
is inert and writes nothing, so `_make_room` ran after every fight and every curio.

The pack is read **first** now. It lags by a pickup, not by half a pack, so a pack under
half full cannot have raised a "did not fit" modal. That keeps the case which disproved
the original slot check — one free slot, four items, save reading 15/16 — because 15 of 16
is not under half. An *unreadable* pack still dismisses: that is the case with no
information, and a dialog nobody dismissed strands the run.

### Two acknowledgements that read a success as a failure

Both the same shape: a fixed sleep shorter than the animation the save is written at the
end of.

- **A formation swap.** One 0.6 s look reported `cannot place highwayman - giving up`
  about a swap that had landed — the save read `crusader>highwayman` when checked
  afterwards, and the next combat block confirmed it with `Highwayman (rank 2)`.
  `_swap_ranks` waits for the order to actually change now, up to `SWAP_WAITS`.
- **A door.** Fixed in the same session: `still in coAB after 12 steps` about a party that
  finished in `rooB`.

## The curio press: positioning was the wrong theory, twice (2026-08-19)

Three live runs, three failures to work `travellers_tent_tutorial`, and two of them were
fixes aimed at the wrong thing.

| run | where the party pressed `W` | result |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | tile 2 (walk stalled early, position read from the knowledge run) | nothing |
| 2 | tile 4, the curio's own tile | nothing |
| 3 | tile 3, one tile short — the "in front of it" fix | nothing |

Meanwhile **the same `W` opened the door at the end of the same corridor every time.** So
the variable is not the tile. What differs is that at a door the party has **auto-stopped**
— the corridor ended — and mid-hallway it is still gliding from a 0.9 s held `A`/`D`. `W`
acts on what is in front of them *now*, and 0.35 s after the key came up that is nothing.

### What replaced the theory

Positioning is not abandoned, it is demoted. The walk still gets the party to the curio,
but the press is now **attempted and verified** rather than aimed:

1. `SETTLE` (1.2 s) before the press, so the party is at rest and not sliding.
2. Press `W`.
3. Ask `_curio_landed` whether anything happened.
4. If not, step one press further and try again, up to `CURIO_TRIES`.

### Why retrying is safe now, when it was not before

The no-retry rule came from a real bug on `coBC`'s sack: most curios are LOOT curios, DD
writes nothing until the loot is taken, so "the curio still reads active" is not evidence
the press failed — and the generic three-attempt retry, which believed it was, pressed `W`
into an open loot window twice more.

`_curio_landed` closes that in two ways at once:

- **SPACE goes out before the decision, not after.** With no loot window it is inert, so
  any window the press *did* open is already cleared before a retry can be considered, and
  `W` can never land in one.
- **A grown pack is a positive success signal**, which is exactly the acknowledgement a
  loot curio gives and the curio flag cannot. `travellers_tent_tutorial` is one
  (`curio_type_library.csv`: Loot, 100%, 2 draws of *"Someone left valuables inside the
  tent"*), so a press that worked ends with the pack one item bigger.

So the invariant is unchanged in substance — **a press that landed is never repeated** —
and only the test for "landed" got better. `tests/bridge/test_act.py` pins both halves.

## Every dropped turn says so now (2026-08-19)

A battle update that reaches the body of `ActRunner._handle` always prints something, a
plan or a refusal. So a log that shows an advisory block and then nothing means the update
was dropped *above* that point — and for three live runs there was no way to tell which of
the two silent returns did it, because both returned without a word:

- `self.paused and self._still_paused()`
- `key in self.acted`

Both speak now, through `_say_once`, which reports a state the first time and then keeps
quiet. That matters because both fire repeatedly by design — the poll loop republishes the
same turn until it is played — so an unconditional message would print several times a
second. The `paused` tag is cleared when the pause lifts, so a second pause is reported
again rather than swallowed.

This is instrumentation, not a fix: the run that prompted it ended with an advisory block
and no act line, and the cause is still unidentified.

## Every fight after the first opened with a dropped turn (2026-08-19)

Four live runs ended the same way: an advisory block for the second fight, and then the
bridge saying nothing at all while the game waited. It was reported four times as "stuck",
diagnosed three times as something else, and named by the instrumentation the first time
that instrumentation existed:

```
[act] already played this turn (1, 2, 0, 0) - waiting for the next one
```

**`turn_key` is unique within one combat, not within a raid.** It is `(battle_round, hero
guid, turns_taken, enemies_first)`, and every one of those four resets when a new fight
starts — so the opening turn of *every* combat is `(1, guid, 0, 0)`. `ActRunner.acted`
never cleared at a fight boundary, so the bridge played `(1, 2, 0, 0)` in `coAB` and then
deduped the identical key in `rooB`. Always the second fight. Always the opening turn.
Always silent, because the dedupe was one of the two paths in `_handle` that returned
without a word.

Cleared now when a room block follows a fight, which is the boundary the runner already
tracks for the loot window. `tests/bridge/test_act.py` pins both halves — the second fight's
opening turn must be played, **and** a republish of the same turn inside one fight must
still be a no-op, because a double press there plays a second move.

**The lesson is about the silence, not the key.** The state was fine — the exact live
`rooB` position replays through the whole stack to an actionable plan, one key and one
click — and three earlier sessions were spent looking for a cause downstream of a drop that
happened upstream. `_say_once` cost four lines and closed it on the next run.

## The curio press is now a search with a camera on it (2026-08-19)

`W` on `travellers_tent_tutorial` has now failed from five positions across four runs, with
a 1.2 s settle, three attempts a run, stepping one press between them — while the same `W`
opens the door at the end of the same corridor every time. Every theory so far has been
about *where* the party stands, and every one has been falsified against the save.

So the next thing is the pixels. `_bank_curio` writes `captures/_curio_miss.png` at the
moment of the final failed attempt, and each attempt now logs the position it pressed from.
This is the same move that settled the map panel: the save cannot show what the party is
standing in front of, and asking a human to alt-tab and reproduce the moment is a worse way
to get the picture than taking it automatically.

## The loop is avoided, not solved: GA comps are for playing (2026-08-22)

The comps have two customers and only one of them creates Fran's loop.

**Training wants class coverage.** That is why this line of work started:
Duelist had 2 curated comps and Runaway 1 against 9+ for every other class, so
both were nearly absent from the training distribution. But with
`--kit-random-p 0.5` and `--loadout-random-p 0.5` on, the kit and the skills
are randomised anyway — what survives as training signal is the **class
multiset and the formation**. Training does not need an *optimised* Duelist
comp. It needs plausible ones.

**Playing wants quality.** That is what the GA is for, and its customer is
Fran.

So: six training-coverage comps composed from **hero blocks a human already
authored**, recombined into new class multisets, with no fitness involved
anywhere.

**They live in their own folder, `metadata/COMPS/composed_comps/`, and that is
Fran's call rather than tidiness.** "Curated" in this project means *he played
the comp and vouches for it*; a machine recombination nobody has played is a
different claim, and filing it next to the real ones would erode the word to
nothing within a few months. `CompLoadoutLoader._json_paths` reads both folders
— both are legitimate training material — and a test fails if a composed comp
turns up in `curated_comps/` or vice versa, so provenance is enforced by
location rather than inferred from a file's contents. Duelist and Runaway now have 5 curated comps each
and **no class is under 5**. GA output stays in `GA_comps/`, read by nothing,
as a recommendation to play.

The loop then cannot close, and the cost of not closing it is near zero: 12 GA
comps against 141 curated is 8% of the pool, and half their optimised kits get
randomised away in training regardless. That is a structural circularity bought
for a marginal gain, and the trade is declined.

### Recombination breaks two things, and both had to be repaired

Neither was theoretical — the first draft shipped both.

- **The one-off budget.** Three comps each legally holding one Ancestral
  trinket compose into a party holding three, which nobody can field and which
  would train the policy against a party stronger than the game allows. The
  same rule `CompSpace.enforce_trinket_budget` exists for. Repaired rather than
  rejected, because **almost every curated slot carries a one-off** — the
  excess is swapped for an unlimited trinket another human gave that same
  class. The starved class picks first and keeps the comp's single one-off,
  since the comp exists for that hero.
- **Slots that equip nothing.** Some curated slots carry a display name that
  resolves to no id — the bug that once left 63 slots empty — and composing
  from one yields a hero wearing one trinket or none. Three did before the
  selector was made to require a slot whose two names both resolve.

Duelist and Runaway have no human-authored unlimited trinket to borrow at all
(3 and 1 curated slots between them, every one carrying a one-off), so the
shortage is recursive; those fall back to a class-eligible unlimited trinket
picked deterministically from the game's own pool, rotated so four Runaway
comps do not all hold the same stone. Recorded per comp in `provenance.repairs`.

**`--comp-seed 1` selects the identical twelve comps before and after**, because
no CSV row was added — the Fire's Edge rule doing exactly what it exists for.
Pool 136 -> 142.

## A comp scored by one policy is a comp that one policy likes (2026-08-22)

`tools/comp_ga.py --model-b CKPT`. Fran named the problem: the GA's comps are
validated by how our model performs with them, and our model is the thing the
comps are meant to improve. The loop is not closed today -- `GA_comps/` is read
by nothing, so no GA output has ever reached training -- but the fitness is
still a **biased estimator**: `success` is `P(this policy completes the run)`,
so the search selects for policy-compatibility, not comp quality. A comp whose
strength needs a play the policy never learned reads as a bad comp.

This project has already been bitten by exactly that and only caught it by
hand: the Vestal holding Illumination was the fitness faithfully reporting the
policy's habits, and the cause was an engine gap (DEBUFF RES never rolled).
Nothing in the search could have found it.

The cheap structural check is a second opinion. The comps that get written are
re-scored with an independently trained checkpoint and the result lands in the
comp file as `crossCheck`. A comp both policies play well is much harder to
explain as an artifact of either; a comp whose score collapses under the second
one is a finding the search cannot otherwise surface.

Three properties that must not be regressed:

- **Same held-out seed, same scenario.** Anything else puts the difference
  between two dungeon lists into the column alongside the difference between
  two policies -- the failure `Rollout.evaluate`'s party-free scenario key
  exists to prevent. Pinned by a test that asserts the exact `(scenario, seed)`
  the second evaluator is asked for.
- **Reported, never selected on.** Emission order stays the headline model's.
  Ranking on both would make the second model a second fitness and move the
  circularity up a layer instead of measuring it.
- **Additive.** Omitting the flag leaves `crossCheck: null` and every other
  field byte-identical; a test pins that too.

Validation-only, so it is cheap: 6 comps at `--validate-episodes`, not 48
candidates x 20 generations. Searching against an ensemble would double an
already-4x run and is not what this buys.

**What it does NOT fix.** There is still no exogenous ruler for comp *quality*.
`tools/agreement.py` is the only measurement here with no simulator in it and
it grades a policy, not a comp. Two policies agreeing is a weaker claim than an
outside opinion, and the honest ceiling on this tool is that it detects
single-policy artifacts -- it cannot confirm a comp is good.

## The seed-1 replicate: what survived, and what was noise (2026-08-22)

Same configuration as the entry below, `--seed 1`, 41 minutes. The point was
to measure training variance directly, because every delta in that entry sits
on top of it unmeasured. Saves in `archive/models/varied_3M_seed1/`.

### Retracted: the ab_compare deltas were inside seed noise

Paired seed 1 against seed 0 over the same 216 scenarios:

| metric | seed 1 | seed 0 | seed-to-seed spread |
|---|---|---|---|
| success | 75.5% | 76.9% | **1.4pp** (CI reaches 6.5pp) |
| zero_death | 76.4% | 80.1% | **3.7pp** |
| retreats/exp | 0.27 | 0.46 | 0.19 |

The entry below reported +2.8pp success and +6.0pp zero_death (p=0.041)
against the 08-21 baseline. **Both are the same order as the spread between
two runs that differ only in their seed.** Do not cite either as evidence the
varied configuration wins in the simulator; on `ab_compare` it is
indistinguishable from the baseline. The p=0.041 was one starred cell out of
eleven, which is what chance produces, and the replicate now says so
independently.

This also confirms the ~4pp seed-variance figure the tool prints in its own
caveat. It was an estimate; it is now measured for this configuration.

### Survived, and strengthened: the agreement gain

| comparison | A | B | delta | b/c | p |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| seed 1 vs seed 0 | 49.5% | 47.5% | +2.0pp | 20/18 | 0.871 |
| seed 1 vs 08-21 baseline | 49.5% | 37.6% | **+11.9pp** | 26/14 | 0.081 |
| seed 0 vs 08-21 baseline | 47.5% | 37.6% | **+9.9pp** | 28/18 | 0.184 |

Two independently seeded runs land 2.0pp apart against a baseline 10-12pp
below both. **The replicate spread is a fifth of the effect**, which is what
makes this a property of the configuration rather than of a lucky seed -- and
it is the structure, not either p-value, that carries the claim. The two
baseline comparisons share an arm and are correlated, so they cannot be pooled.

`eval_tutorial` agrees from the other side: seed 1 **100.0%** (stress 9.5),
seed 0 96.0%, baseline 98.0%. All three at the rig's ceiling, 4pp being two
episodes in fifty -- so seed 0 reading 2pp under the baseline was also seed
noise.

### The shape of the result

The varied configuration does not win more in the simulator. It plays more
like a human on real captured positions, reproducibly, and it does not lose
anything on the one exactly-reproducible rig. That is consistent with what the
knobs were turned on FOR -- a policy that judges a comp less by whether it
trained on that comp -- and it is not a performance claim.

**Ship seed 1**, on the two rulers with no circularity in them; it is what
`models/` already holds. Seed 0's 1.4pp on `ab_compare` success is inside the
noise this entry just measured.

## The varied-distribution retrain (2026-08-22)

3M steps, 12 workers, seed 0, and the first run with any of the five training-
distribution knobs on: `--kit-random-p 0.5 --loadout-random-p 0.5
--resolve-sampling --cross-region-p 0.4`. 39 minutes. The previous saves are in
`archive/models/pre_20260822_retrain/` -- `EvalCallback` overwrites
`logs/<mode>/best_model.zip` no matter what `--out` says, and `models/` is
gitignored, so backing both up is not optional.

**The purpose was the instrument, not the score.** The GA scores a comp by
P(this policy completes the run), which makes the fitness a biased estimator:
it rewards comps that are easy for THIS policy, and a comp needing a play the
policy never learned reads as a bad comp. A policy trained against random
trinket pairs and random legal 4-of-7 loadouts judges a comp much less by "did
I train on this exact configuration". Retraining also settled the debt from the
one-roll/subtractive effect shape landed earlier the same day.

Four rulers, new (A) against the archived 08-21 final save (B):

| ruler | A | B | read |
|---|---|---|---|
| `eval_tutorial.py` (exactly reproducible) | 96.0% | 98.0% | one episode in 50; the rig is at its ceiling |
| `agreement.py` (no simulator in it) | **47.5%** | 37.6% | +9.9pp, 28/18 discordant, p=0.184 |
| `ab_compare.py` success (216 paired) | 76.9% | 74.1% | +2.8pp, CI spans zero, p=0.39 |
| `ab_compare.py` zero_death | **80.1%** | 74.1% | +6.0pp, p=0.041 |

**Nothing here is significant, and the one starred cell should not be read as
if it were**: eleven metrics were reported and one at p=0.041 is about what
chance produces. `ab_compare`'s own caveat applies too -- it removes scenario
variance, not training variance, and two runs at different `--seed` differ by
~4pp on their own, which is larger than the success delta.

**The result that is worth something is the sign on `agreement`.** The
2026-08-21 retrain cost **-12.9pp** on the only ruler with no simulator in it,
which was the strongest reason to distrust that checkpoint. This one gains
9.9pp instead, and the DIFFER breakdown says where: positions where a human
healed and the old policy wanted to attack went from 6 to 0.

New `best_model.zip` vs the new final save on `agreement`: 42.6% vs **47.5%**,
p=0.442. The final save is live and stays live. Third of four times the final
save has won that call.

**The behaviour prediction could not be tested, and that is a self-inflicted
wound.** The stated falsifier was that usage would shift toward high-rank
status skills now that rank buys landing rate. Five things changed at once, and
the distribution knobs move the training data far more than the effect shape
does, so nothing in the diff is attributable. Recorded for what moved, not as
evidence: Antiquarian `festering_vapours` 18% -> 48%, Arbalest picked up `bola`
at 20%, Occultist heal share 62.3% -> 40.0%, stress-heal uses 6 -> 0,
`holy_water` 222 -> 52, and attacks reached further back (rank 1 41% -> 32%,
rank 3 22% -> 27%). To test the effect-shape hypothesis, one knob at a time.

**Every GA number predating this is on a different ruler.** Rounds 4 and 5 were
scored with the 08-21 checkpoint.

## Round 5: the synergy prior works, and it is the PRIOR that works (2026-08-22)

Same scenario and same budget as round 4 (48 x 20 generations x 16 episodes x
4 zones, seed 0), the one difference being `SYNERGY_TRINKET_BIAS` active. It is
now `--synergy-bias`, defaulting to the constant, so `--synergy-bias 0`
reproduces round 4 -- a prior with no off switch would have made the control
arm unrepeatable.

### The census moved, decisively

Both rounds regraded with one script, counting chance modifiers on **equipped**
trinkets against the payloads the hero's **equipped** kit delivers:

| | round 4 (no prior) | round 5 (prior on) |
|---|---|---|
| positive modifier, matches the kit | 2 | **19** |
| positive modifier, does not | 4 | 7 |
| negative modifier on a payload the kit uses (an accepted penalty) | 7 | 5 |
| negative modifier, irrelevant | 4 | 6 |

Round 4's cells were published here as 2/4 and 6/3 against a stated total of
17, which does not add up; those came from an earlier version of the script.
The table above regrades both arms with the same one.

### And the control says selection did not add to it

The obvious misreading is "the search learned synergy". It did not. Draw 400
random genomes **under the same prior** and 85.3% of their positive chance
modifiers match their hero's kit. The emitted comps read **75.0%** -- below the
prior's own draw rate, not above it.

So the entire census gain is the prior offering the comparison, exactly as
designed; selection then eroded it by ~10pp. Two explanations this measurement
cannot separate: **crossover drift** (a trinket drawn as a match for hero A is
inherited by hero B, where it matches nothing -- only `top_up` refills go back
through `draw_trinket`), and **polish reverting picks that do not pay**, which
is consistent with the round-4 finding that one synergy stat is worth ~1 sigma.
Do not quote the 2 -> 19 as evidence the fitness sees synergy. It sees kits.

### The comps did get better, and that is NOT attributable

| | round 4 best | round 5 best |
|---|---|---|
| duelist arm | 79.4% (warrens 60) | **80.8%** (warrens 83) |
| runaway arm | 73.8% (warrens 70) | **82.5%** (warrens 87) |
| distinct classes over 6 emitted comps | 7 | **8** |

Warrens -- round 4's hard zone everywhere -- is up 23 and 17 points on the best
comp of each arm, and diversity recovered the point it lost in round 4. But the
two rounds are separate searches with different random trajectories and are not
paired, so none of this can be attributed to the prior. It is what happened,
not what the prior did.

## Round 4: four zones, and what the trinket layer did NOT do (2026-08-22)

First run under the four-zone scenario (all four at Champion, resolve 6, gear
rank 5) and under the corrected effect-resist shape. 48 candidates x 20
generations x 16 episodes x 4 zones = 61,440 expeditions per class, ~38 minutes
each on 12 workers.

### The bias the single-zone scenario could not see

Round 3's winner read **90% in the Ruins**. Re-measured across four zones it is
**51.9% — crypts 78 / warrens 22 / weald 62 / cove 45.** It was a Ruins comp
and nothing in the old scenario could say so.

Round 4's comps are visibly flatter, which is the scenario doing its job:

| comp | overall | crypts | warrens | weald | cove |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| duelist / bounty_hunter / occultist / vestal | **79.4%** | 90 | 60 | 80 | 88 |
| bounty_hunter / vestal / duelist / flagellant | 78.1% | 88 | 65 | 88 | 72 |
| flagellant / runaway / houndmaster / vestal | **73.8%** | 82 | 70 | 82 | 60 |

**Warrens is the hard zone** — every comp's worst or second-worst, 40-72%
against 72-98% in the crypts. Worth knowing before reading any single-zone
number again.

### Class diversity went DOWN, and that is the trade

7 distinct classes across 6 comps in each run, against round 3's 9. Nothing
regressed: a four-zone fitness is harder and more discriminating, so the search
converges harder on what survives all four (bounty_hunter, vestal, flagellant,
crusader, occultist). Diversity pressure and a discriminating fitness pull in
opposite directions, and `--max-class-share` is the dial between them.

### The trinkets did not converge on synergy, and here is the measurement

Fran's expectation after the effect-resist fix: *"if you want to stun more,
+stun chance trinkets will help; if the model wants Illumination, with +debuff
chance trinkets maybe it's not that worth"*. Over the 12 emitted comps, 17
equipped trinket slots carry a chance modifier at all, and by sign:

| | matches the hero's kit | does not |
|---|---|---|
| positive modifier | **2** | 4 |
| negative modifier | 6 | 3 |

Two synergies. Six accepted penalties. That is not convergence, and the reason
is measurable rather than mysterious: swapping the Vestal's `haste_chalice`
(-0.25 stun_chance, on a Vestal who stuns) for `rampart_shield` (+0.30 stun,
+0.40 move) is worth **+3.8pp at 160 episodes against a 3.2pp sigma** — real,
and just over one sigma, which is under what a single generation can resolve.
`demons_cauldron` (+0.30 stun) read +0.0pp. A whole kit is worth 24pp and the
fitness sees it; ONE synergy stat is at the noise floor.

So the search is not ignoring synergy — it cannot see it one trinket at a time.
`SYNERGY_TRINKET_BIAS` is the answer to that, and it is a prior rather than a
fitness change: a trinket whose chance modifier matches a payload the hero's
**equipped** kit delivers is drawn 25% of the time, so the comparison gets made
often enough for selection to act on it. Only positive modifiers, and only
against the four skills actually equipped -- a Vestal holding Dazzling Light
has three stun trinkets promoted; the same Vestal holding Illumination and Hand
of Light has none, which is Fran's example in both directions. `haste_chalice`
stays in the general pool and stays reachable, because taking a stun penalty
for +SPD may well be right.

## One roll, and the resist comes off it — the effect-application shape (2026-08-22)

The Illumination thread below ended with DEBUFF RES measured at 20 points and
deliberately **not** fixed, because nobody could say which effects it gates.
Looking for that answer in the effect data found a bigger and better-evidenced
bug underneath it, and fixed both.

### 572 effects carried a number that could not matter

`_apply_effect_to_target` rolled **twice**: once against `eff.chance`, then a
second time against the payload's resist inside each of the bleed / blight /
stun / move blocks. Under that shape any `.chance` at or above 1.0 is exactly
1.0 — `roll` is drawn from [0, 1).

The shipped data says the shape is wrong. Counted over the 1973 effects the
install ships:

| `.chance` | effects |
|---|---|
| ≤ 1.0 | 1401 |
| > 1.0 | **572** |

and the values above 1.0 are not noise — they are a **ladder that tracks skill
rank**: 1.0 / 1.2 / 1.4 / 1.6 for rank 1 / 3 / 5 / 6 of the same effect, on 156
effects at 1.2 and 154 at 1.4, reaching 2.0, 4.0 and **5.0** (Guard Break, which
the wiki glosses as "applied at 500% chance"). Headroom over 100% is only worth
writing down if something is taken off it. Under the two-roll reading, **29% of
the game's effects carried dead numbers and a rank-5 skill applied its status
exactly as often as a rank-1 one.**

`rules.json` agrees independently: the crit bonus ships as
`effect_crit_roll_mod: -0.2`, a modifier on the **roll**, which is the shape of
one comparison. So does `disease_hero_disease_resist_weight: 0.33` — a *weight*
on a resist only means anything inside a formula that subtracts it.

### What replaced it

One draw, two comparisons:

* `roll < chance` gates what cannot be resisted — stress, heals, guard,
  shuffle, summons, and **Mark**, which the wiki states plainly cannot be
  resisted by anyone.
* `roll < chance - resist` gates the resistable payload.

So Occultist's Vulnerability Curse still marks when its stat debuff is resisted
— one effect, two payloads, and the shipped data has 13 more of that shape.
`CombatEngine._effect_resist` is the single place the payload → resist mapping
lives, and it is a priority list because **exactly one shipped effect in the
whole game carries two resist types** ("Baron Blight": blight + a stat debuff,
Crimson Court, outside anything this simulator fields). A test pins that count
as the tripwire.

The attacker-side `bleed_chance` / `poison_chance` / `stun_chance` /
`move_chance` bonuses keep their meaning — they lower the target's effective
resist — and `debuff_chance` (0.65 per expedition, the last inert one on the
audit list) joins them for the first time.

### DEBUFF RES, and why the predicate stopped being a guess

The blocker recorded in `ASSUMPTIONS.md` was that no shipped key says which
effects DEBUFF RES gates, and that the obvious predicate — a stat modifier
crossing to the other side — is wrong because Mark is resisted in the real
game. **That last clause was the thing that was wrong.** `WIKI_MECHANICS.md`
