has said since it was written that Mark *cannot be resisted by anyone*, and the
effect data agrees: Mark carries `.tag` and no stat field at all. With Mark out
of it the predicate is exactly the complement of the other resist types:

> a buff applied to a combatant on the **other side** is a debuff.

Nothing in an effect record distinguishes Bolster from a Weakening Curse — both
are `combat_stat_buff` — so the side test **is** the predicate, and it is what
makes a self-inflicted debuff (Leper's Vulnerability, Hellion's Exhaust)
unresistable, which is also what the game does.

### What it cost, measured

`GA_duelist_01`, 40 episodes, crypts, seed 9999, the same cell every number in
the Illumination entry below was measured in:

| | success | Illumination's share of Vestal turns | −Illumination +Mace Bash |
|---|---|---|---|
| two rolls (as shipped until today) | 85.0% | **9.7%** | −10.0pp |
| one subtractive roll (now) | **77.5%** | **1.9%** | **+2.5pp** |
| the discarded experiment (resist gates everything, no subtraction) | 65.0% | — | −2.5pp |

**Fran's judgement and the simulator now agree**: Illumination went from the
Vestal's most-played skill to her third of four, and swapping it out stopped
costing. The −20 points the experiment predicted was itself an artifact of the
two-roll shape — gate a debuff on `1 - resist` and a Champion monster resists
80% of the time; subtract it from a `chance 1.4` and it resists 40%, which is
what the game does.

### What this invalidates

Nothing loads differently — the observation is unchanged and every checkpoint
still runs. But the training distribution moved: status effects land at
different rates in both directions (a rank-5 bleed at `chance 1.4` against 30%
resist now **always** lands where it used to land 70% of the time; a debuff on a
Champion monster now lands 60% where it used to land 100%). Treat comp scores
measured before today as a different ruler, and expect a retrain to be owed
before the next checkpoint comparison.

Two consequences worth stating separately because they are easy to re-break:

* **The draw count changed** — one advance per effect application instead of
  two. Any future comparison against a captured RNG stream has to be made
  against the new shape.
* **`resisted` is computed once, above all the payload blocks.** Re-adding a
  per-payload `self.rng.random()` re-introduces both bugs at once.

## "Why did it pick Illumination?" — DEBUFF RES, measured for the first time (2026-08-22)

Fran read the emitted comps and asked why the Vestal was holding Illumination,
*"probably the worst skill in the game"*. Chasing that one question produced the
first measured cost for the largest known gap in the engine, and killed a
plausible-looking hypothesis on the way.

`tools/comp_ga.py --explain <comp.json>` was built to answer it and is the
reusable part: it reports what the policy actually PLAYED, then scores every
legal skill swap and a sample of trinket pairs, all paired on one dungeon list.

### It is not a dead slot — the policy's favourite Vestal skill is Illumination

The first guess was that a dud skill is invisible to the fitness: the policy
never plays it, so the search has no gradient to remove it. Wrong, and the
counting hook says so plainly. Over 30 expeditions the Vestal's four skills came
out at **Illumination 9.7%** of all hero turns, Divine Comfort 6.7%, Dazzling
Light 1.3%, Divine Grace 0.8%. It is her most-played skill by a factor of one
and a half.

And the fitness agrees. Every legal swap out of it **costs**:

| swap | delta |
|---|---|
| −Illumination +Judgement | −12.5pp |
| −Illumination +Mace Bash | −10.0pp |
| −Illumination +Hand of Light | −12.5pp |

So the search is not being careless. In this simulator, Illumination is good.

### The obvious culprit was innocent, and the measurement is worth keeping

`gods_illumination` carries `Dodge Curse 5`: `defense_rating_add -0.30`, applied
through `c.dodge += amt` with **no floor**, while `compute_hit_chance` returns a
bare `ACC - DODGE`, also unclamped. Champion monsters average 0.263 dodge and
apprentice ones 0.052, so the debuff routinely takes a target's dodge NEGATIVE —
measured at −0.637 at worst, on 7.4% of all attacks — and a −0.3 debuff on a
0.05-dodge monster looks like it should pay six times what it removes.

**It pays nothing.** Re-scored with the defender's dodge floored at zero — the
floor firing 470 times in a 40-episode batch — every number is byte-identical,
including the swap deltas. The reason is `cutoffAlwaysHit = 0.95`: **all 205
attacks against a negative-dodge target already had a hit chance of at least
1.04**, mean 1.33. The target was going to be hit whatever the sign of its
dodge, so the overshoot changes no outcome.

Recorded because it looks like a bug and is not, and the next person to notice
`c.dodge += amt` should not have to re-run this. It would start mattering the
moment something clamps the chance, or a low-accuracy attacker meets a
high-dodge target — so it is a latent hazard, not a live one.

### DEBUFF RES is the answer, and it is worth 20 points

`ASSUMPTIONS.md` has recorded since 2026-08-21 that `debuff_resist` is parsed on
every combatant and **never rolled**, so every stat debuff lands
unconditionally. Dodge Curse ships at `chance 1.4`; champion monsters average
**0.802 debuff resist** (min 0.4), so the real game should land it about 60% of
the time and this simulator lands it 100%.

Gating stat debuffs on the target's DEBUFF RES — 55% of them then resisted,
6115 of 11059 in a 40-episode batch — changes both things at once:

| | as shipped | DEBUFF RES gating stat debuffs |
|---|---|---|
| the comp as emitted | 85.0% | **65.0%** |
| −Illumination +Judgement | −12.5pp | **+2.5pp** |
| −Illumination +Mace Bash | −10.0pp | **−2.5pp** |
| −Illumination +Hand of Light | −12.5pp | **+0.0pp** |

**Illumination's entire advantage disappears into the noise, and the party
loses 20 points of success.** Fran's judgement of the skill and the simulator's
disagree because of exactly one unimplemented roll.

### What is NOT being shipped, and why

The gate above is an experiment, not a patch. The blocker recorded in
`ASSUMPTIONS.md` still stands: **no shipped key says which effects DEBUFF RES
applies to.** `is_stat_buff` is a guess — a reasonable one, since it is the
class of effect the wiki calls a debuff, but Mark is resisted in the real game
and carries only `.tag 1`, so the true predicate is wider than that flag.
Shipping a guess that moves every comp's score by 20 points is exactly the kind
of convention-masquerading-as-data this file exists to prevent.

What changed is the priority. Until now DEBUFF RES was "the largest known gap"
with no number attached. It now has three:

- **20 points** of simulated expedition success, on one comp at Champion;
- **55%** of stat debuffs currently landing that should not;
- and one **wrong skill preference that a human spotted by eye** — which is the
  first time a documented engine gap has been caught by looking at output
  rather than at data.

That makes N2's question 1 the highest-value item in the queue, and it is
answerable with the determinism flags in one capture session: `-alwayshit`
removes the to-hit roll, the tutorial's own monsters debuff the party at
`.chance 100%`, and whether the debuff lands is then a DEBUFF RES roll and
nothing else.

## The comp search, rebuilt on Fran's review of its first output (2026-08-22)

He reviewed the twelve comps and the verdict was that the *search space* was
wrong, not the search. Six changes, five of them from things he said outright
and one from what the output showed.

### A hero wears two trinkets. Always.

*"I don't understand why many comps keep 1 trinket only; in the game 2 trinkets
is always the go to, and if the game would allow 3 you would go with 3."*

The Duelist comps were emitting **seven trinkets across four heroes**. The cause
was `min(2, pool)` in the draw plus a `repair` that could drop one and never put
it back. `TRINKETS_PER_HERO` is now enforced by `CompSpace.top_up`, which every
path that can leave a hero short runs through: a curated comp that only listed
one, `repair` dropping a gene the class cannot hold, and the new budget pass
confiscating a trinket. The same function refills camping skills, for the same
reason — a hero short a slot looks bad to the fitness for a reason that has
nothing to do with the comp, which is the one thing a ranking must not do.

### One one-off trinket per comp, and `limit` is how the game says which

*"Trophies, Crimson Court, Crystalline and Ancestor trinkets are 1-offs, which
means that on the same comp there can only be 1 of those in all 8 trinkets."*

The emitted comps were wearing **four to six** of them each.

The rule did not need a hand-written list of families: **`limit` is the game's
own marker** for a trinket you can only ever own one of, and it lands exactly on
the four Fran named — Ancestral (9), Shambler ancestral (5), Crimson Court (38),
Color of Madness comets (28) — plus boss trophies (12), the Collector's, the
Madman's, Mildred's, the Crow's, and **eleven `very_rare` ones his list would
have missed**. 56 of a class's 154 wearable trinkets carry it.

`UNIQUE_TRINKETS_PER_COMP = 1` is stricter than the game's own rule, which is
per id: you own one Ancestor's Candle, but nothing stops another hero wearing
Ancestor's Map. Fran's cap is the useful reading — it forces the search to earn
its score with gear a roster actually has spare — and it is marked as his choice
rather than as data.

Enforcement is comp-level, not hero-level, because a hero-at-a-time draw cannot
know what its team-mates already took. `grant_one_off` places the single
allowance on a random hero at construction; `enforce_trinket_budget` runs after
**every** operator, because crossover is exactly the move that breaks it — two
parents each legally holding one produce a child holding two.

### The search was not exploiting trinkets, and could not have been

*"I feel the GA model is not evaluating trinkets properly; there are many
powerful trinkets and really useful trinket+loadout combinations and the GA is
not exploiting those."*

Correct, and the arithmetic says why. Trinkets are the widest axis in the genome
by an order of magnitude — ~98 unlimited choices per hero against 35 skill
loadouts and 4 ranks — and the first version explored them with **one swap at a
time, uniformly, over ten generations**. Three changes:

- **A class-locked bias** (`CLASS_TRINKET_BIAS = 0.5`). A class has ~6 unlimited
  class-locked trinkets out of ~98, so a uniform draw finds a *designed* synergy
  6% of the time. These are the ones built around the class's kit, which is what
  a comp is for. It is a prior on where to look, not a restriction — every
  generic trinket stays reachable and selection drops the ones that do not earn
  their slot.
- **A `trinket_pair` operator.** A combination is two trinkets that are good
  *together*, and single swaps reach those only by passing through the state
  where one of them is wrong. Trinkets now carry 0.40 of the mutation weight
  across three operators, up from 0.22 on one.
- **A polish phase.** After the GA, each comp to be emitted gets `--polish`
  rounds of trinket-only hill-climbing with its pillars, ranks and skills held
  fixed. Sound only because of the pairing — every candidate in a round is
  scored on the same dungeon list at the same seed as the incumbent.

**Polish needed a third seed, and this is the trap it walked into first.**
Hill-climbing *selects* on its seed, so a comp polished and then reported on the
same dungeon list carries a number it was fitted to — the exact mistake the
held-out validation exists to prevent, reintroduced one step later. It now tunes
on `validate_seed + 1` and re-measures on the untouched `validate_seed`, and
**keeps the polish only if the held-out seed agrees**. On a smoke run that
refused one of three polishes outright (−16.7pp held out) and accepted another
at +41.7pp.

What none of this fixes, and Fran named it himself: a trinket whose real value
is outside combat — Ancestor's Map, with no map modelled — is scored at whatever
its stats are worth. That belongs to the missing dungeon layer. `inert_trinkets`
does remove the four whose buffs move no field this engine has, probed against
the real `_apply_buff_to_stats` rather than listed, so the dispatch table is not
duplicated here.

### The same four classes is the same comp

*"Comps 2 and 4 have the same classes; this should not be a thing. You can see
the classes as the pillars of the comp, and having the same 4 pillars is
practically making the same comp — same with the Runaway comps, it's the same
comp re-ordered."*

`archetype(genome)` is the class multiset, order-insensitive, and it now
constrains three places:

- the **hall of fame keeps the best of each archetype**, not the best overall,
  so one strong pillar set cannot crowd out every rival;
- the **population caps** individuals per archetype (`--max-per-archetype`,
  default 2), which is what stops a strong class appearing in most candidates
  and collapsing the population within a few generations;
- the **emitter** writes one comp per archetype, best first.

Note what the archetype deliberately does *not* distinguish: two orderings of
the same four classes. That is the point — the search stays free to explore
ranks (a Duelist plays differently from rank 1 than from rank 3, which is the
other thing Fran asked for) while the emitter only ever keeps the best ordering
it found, so a reordered clone cannot reach a file.

### The sample comps are a starting point, not the search space

*"I gathered a sample comp for the model to learn to use the class simply, not
to copy that comp. I prefer 5 different funky comps — they will be more helpful
to fix and learn things from the engine — than 5 pseudo-clones."*

Curated seeding drops from **half the population to a quarter**
(`--curated-fraction`). The archetype cap applies to the seeded half too.

### Skills and camp skills come out in the game's order

From `metadata/HEROES/heroes.js`, which lists both per class in character-sheet
order. Presentation only — the engine resolves skills by id and a loadout is a
set — but a comp written in alphabetical-by-internal-id order is harder to read
against the screen than it needs to be.

**Ordered on IDS, before display names are looked up.** `heroes.js` says "First
Aid" in the Duelist and Runaway blocks and "Wound Care" in the nineteen vanilla
ones, and both mean `first_aid`; name-matching silently failed to place it and
dumped it at the end of the list, which is the one thing the ordering exists to
prevent. The file doubles as a spelling source for camping skills, whose
localization entries are ALL CAPS — `.title()` produces "Lash'S Anger".

### Round 2: twelve distinct comps, and the Duelist finally moves off rank 1

32 candidates x 12 generations x 16 episodes, crypts, tier 5, medium (8 combats),
resolve 6; the best of each archetype re-scored at 40 episodes on held-out seed
9999; then the top six polished on tuning seed 10000 and re-measured on 9999.
~9 minutes per class on 12 workers.

The population stayed at **23-27 distinct archetypes out of 32** the whole way
down, against a round-1 run that collapsed onto Duelist-plus-Crusaders by
generation 3.

| Duelist comps | P(success) | | Runaway comps | P(success) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| duelist / vestal / bounty_hunter / occultist | **85.0%** +-5.6 | | crusader / runaway / shieldbreaker / flagellant | **95.0%** +-3.4 |
| bounty_hunter / duelist / bounty_hunter / vestal | 82.5% +-6.0 | | flagellant / runaway / occultist / runaway | 80.0% +-6.3 |
| duelist / occultist / houndmaster / vestal | 82.5% +-6.0 | | flagellant / occultist / grave_robber / runaway | 77.5% +-6.6 |
| crusader / duelist / bounty_hunter / occultist | 80.0% +-6.3 | | flagellant / runaway / shieldbreaker / occultist | 75.0% +-6.8 |
| bounty_hunter / duelist / houndmaster / vestal | 77.5% +-6.6 | | runaway / flagellant / shieldbreaker / flagellant | 70.0% +-7.2 |
| duelist / occultist / crusader / vestal | 77.5% +-6.6 | | runaway / grave_robber / shieldbreaker / flagellant | 67.5% +-7.4 |

Twelve distinct class multisets, no Crusader monoculture, and three
duplicate-class comps arrived on their own (two Bounty Hunters, two Runaways,
two Flagellants) — which the curated corpus says is normal, 37 of 136 comps
have one.

**Against the curated comps, on the same held-out seed:**

| comp | as Fran plays it | under the search's rules |
|---|---|---|
| Ascetic Movement | 57.5% | 45.0% |
| Dancing Lunge V3.0 (Duelist) | 40.0% | 15.0% |
| Stun Control: Burn (Runaway) | 40.0% | 10.0% |
| Riposte Style (Duel) | 32.5% | 25.0% |
| African Vapors | 22.5% | 10.0% |
| Antiques Roadshow | 0.0% | 0.0% |

**"Under the search's rules" is the interesting column, and it is not a
handicap the GA also carries — it is the handicap the GA carries and the
curated comps do not.** Those comps field four to six one-off trinkets each;
capped at one, they lose 7 to 30 points. So the +45pp the best Duelist comp
holds over the best curated Duelist comp is measured against a curated comp
wearing *more* one-offs than the search was allowed to propose.

That also retires the "groomed" control from round 1. It was a quirk-only
adjustment then and it is not one now — `conform` enforces the one-off budget
too, so the column measures a different thing and is labelled for it.

**The ordering pass earns its cost, and the held-out gate earns its cost more.**
All 24 orderings of each comp are searched exhaustively on the tuning seed, and
the winner is then re-measured on the held-out one and **kept only if that
agrees**. Across the twelve: three rejected outright (-20.0, -15.0, -7.5pp held
out), three neutral, and six accepted for up to +15.0pp. A polish loop scored on
its own tuning seed would have shipped all twelve.

**The Duelist still only ever lands in rank 1 or 2** across all six comps. That
is plausible for a riposte-and-lunge melee hero and the exhaustive ordering
search did look at the alternatives, so it is a finding rather than a gap — but
it is also one region at one difficulty, and worth re-checking on a four-zone
run before reading anything into it.

### Round 3: class variance, and two measurements that change how to read the output

40 candidates x 14 generations x 16 episodes, `--max-class-share 0.30`, best of
each archetype validated at 40 episodes on held-out seed 9999, top six polished
(all 24 orderings, then trinkets) on tuning seed 10000 and re-measured on 9999.

| Duelist comps | P(succ) | | Runaway comps | P(succ) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| duelist / duelist / grave_robber / vestal | **90.0%** | | flagellant / runaway / bounty_hunter / occultist | **87.5%** |
| duelist / bounty_hunter / bounty_hunter / vestal | 85.0% | | flagellant / abomination / runaway / arbalest | 80.0% |
| duelist / abomination / crusader / vestal | 82.5% | | flagellant / crusader / arbalest / runaway | 80.0% |
| abomination / runaway / duelist / vestal | 80.0% | | flagellant / runaway / bounty_hunter / man_at_arms | 77.5% |
| duelist / bounty_hunter / musketeer / vestal | 77.5% | | bounty_hunter / crusader / occultist / runaway | 72.5% |
| duelist / highwayman / highwayman / vestal | 70.0% | | abomination / flagellant / highwayman / runaway | 67.5% |

**Class coverage went 6 -> 9 per run and 10 -> 13 across all twelve**, with the
population holding 34-46 live archetypes against round 2's 23-27. Double-hero
comps arrived on their own again (two Duelists, two Bounty Hunters, two
Highwaymen).

**It is still not enough, and the residue points at the same bug.** The Vestal
is in **6 of 6** Duelist comps and the Flagellant in 5 of 6 Runaway ones, and
**4 of those 6 Vestals still hold Illumination** — which the section above
shows is a DEBUFF RES artifact worth 20 points. A class whose best skill is
over-valued by an unimplemented roll will keep winning slots no matter how much
diversity pressure the search applies. This is not a knob that can be turned
harder; it is the engine gap showing through.

### The fitness CAN see a trinket, and the 40-episode headline is optimistic

Two things had to be measured before either could be read, and the first
attempt measured neither because of a bug I had put in.

**The cache was keyed on `(genome, seed)` and ignored the scenario**, so a
40-episode request read back a 20-episode answer. That silently broke the
polish phase — it tunes on a half-episode scenario and re-measures on the full
one at the same seed, so every re-measurement was returning the tuning number
it existed to check. Fixed, with a test; the round-3 numbers above were produced
after the fix.

With that fixed: one emitted comp against nine random re-kits of *itself* —
same classes, ranks, skills and quirks, only the eight trinkets differ.

| | 40 episodes (1σ 4.7pp) | 160 episodes (1σ 3.1pp) |
|---|---|---|
| the emitted kit | 90.0%, rank 1 of 10 | **80.6%, rank 1 of 10** |
| nine random re-kits | 50.0 – 82.5% | 56.2 – 73.1% |
| spread | 40.0pp | **24.4pp** |

So trinkets are worth about **24 points** of expedition success and the search
is genuinely finding good ones — the polished kit beats the best of nine random
kits by 7.5pp, which is over 2σ. "Weird" is about which trinkets they are, not
about whether they were chosen.

The other half of that table is a caution about every number this tool prints:
**the top comp reads 90.0% at 40 episodes and 80.6% at 160.** Forty episodes is
enough to rank candidates against each other on one dungeon list; it is not
enough to quote as a rate. The `gaProvenance` block carries the standard error
for exactly this reason and the emitted comps say so in their own text.

### Fran's data fixes, same day

He corrected the two misspelled skill names ("Judgment", "Poison Dart") and the
"Stun Control: Royal" mis-scrape in the curated files, so the fuzzy fallback and
the class-requirement check now both fire on nothing. Both tests are kept as
standing checks rather than deleted: the fallback is the net for the next
hand-written comp, and a comp equipping a trinket its class cannot hold is a
scrape defect worth noticing rather than silently repairing.

## The comps get their own folder, and five data bugs fell out of moving them (2026-08-22)

Fran asked for the comp JSONs to be isolated so a genetic search could write more of
them in the same format. The move itself is bookkeeping. Preparing the search's
*legality pools* is what turned up five separate things that were quietly wrong —
every one of them found by asking "what may a comp legally contain?" of data that
had only ever been read, never enumerated.

### The layout

`metadata/COMPS/Comps/<name>/<name>.json` -> `metadata/COMPS/curated_comps/*.json`,
flat, 136 files. Nothing else in a comp directory is read by any code: the siblings
are 1242 PNGs and a Notion `.md`. The `.md` files **stayed put**, because
`CompsCsvLoader.load_modded_hero_comp_names` globs `COMPS/Comps/*.md` and is what
keeps the 11 fan-made-class comps out of the training pool.

`CompLoadoutLoader._json_paths` reads the new folder and falls back to the old
`*/*.json` layout when it is absent — the same shape of fallback as
`csv_root`, and for the same reason: `CsvLoader.find_csv` returns `None` for a
missing directory, so a wrong path here produces a silently EMPTY store rather
than an error.

`metadata/COMPS/GA_comps/` is the search's output and is **deliberately not read**
by anything. Promoting a comp is a `mv` into `curated_comps/` — and, for a comp of
ordinary classes, a CSV row as well, which shifts which comps every archived
`--comp-seed` selects and is therefore a decision rather than a side effect.

**The CSV was not touched.** `store.comps` still has its 145 rows in the same
order, so every archived eval stays comparable.

### Bug 1: four classes could not camp at all

`CampingLoader` read `raid/camping/default.camping_skills.json` and nothing else.
There are five camping files — every DLC ships one — and a DLC class is granted the
*shared* camping skills by its own DLC re-declaring them, never by the base file,
which was written before that class existed. So **Shieldbreaker, Flagellant,
Duelist and Runaway had no camping skills beyond the generic `hobby`**, and the
camp resolver simply had nothing to spend points on for them. 64 skills -> 80.

The merge rule is the data's own and is a split, not a single rule:

* `dlc/702540_shieldbreaker/raid/camping/default.camping_skills.json` is a whole
  second copy of all 64 base skills whose only difference is `shieldbreaker`
  prepended to every `hero_classes`. Base-wins on that list drops the one thing
  the file exists to say.
* `dlc/4964110_fires_edge/.../duelist.camping_skills.json` re-declares `encourage`
  with `hero_classes: ["duelist"]` alone — sixteen classes short. Last-wins on the
  list would revoke Encourage from the entire base roster, so the DLC cannot be
  *replacing* the entry either.

A **union of `hero_classes`, base-wins on everything else** is the only reading
both files are consistent with. Curated camp-skill slots that resolve went from
under three quarters to **2126 of 2168**; the 42 that remain are comps naming a
camp skill their class is not granted by any file (17 Flagellant, 11
Shieldbreaker), which is the comps being aspirational, not the loader.

### Bug 2: 63 curated trinket slots equipped nothing

Ten trinket display names are claimed by two localization ids each, and on nine of
them **one id is a real trinket and the other exists only in localization**:
"Prophet's Eye" is both `boss_prophet` (shipped in
`trinkets/base.entries.trinkets.json`) and `prophet_5` (in no shipped JSON at all).
`_build_trinket_name_map` was a dict comprehension, so it kept whichever the scan
met last — and it met the dead one.

The result was silent: `store.trinkets[tid]` misses, the trinket is dropped, the
hero wears an empty slot. **63 of the 1039 trinket slots across the 136 curated
comps**, concentrated on exactly the boss trophies a real party earns and wears
(`prophet_5` x26, `wilbur_5` x19, `flesh_5` x13, `siren_5` x4, `crew_5` x1).
The map now prefers an id the store actually has, and iterates `sorted(raw)` so
the fallback is deterministic. 63 -> 0.

Only trinkets are affected: the quirk and camping-skill name maps have zero
colliding display names.

### Bug 3: the random-kit trinket pool is the wrong pool for designing a comp

`UNIVERSAL_TRINKET_RARITIES` is what `--kit-random-p` draws from, and it is right
for that — it is what a *sampled* party could plausibly have found. **455 of the
1039 curated trinket slots (44%) sit outside it**: Shambler trinkets,
Crimson Court, Color of Madness comets, boss trophies, the Collector's, the
Madman's. Ancestor's Candle alone is worn in 28 of them.

So `tools/comp_ga.py::wearable_rarities` reads the pool off the corpus instead:
every rarity a comp Fran has actually played equips, plus the universal ones.
That is data with an automatic falsifier — a new curated comp wearing a new
rarity widens the pool by itself — and it still leaves out the 294 `kickstarter`
backer trinkets, which no comp wears.

Doing that surfaced one comp that is wrong in its own data rather than out of the
pool's reach: **"Stun Control: Royal" hands its Plague Doctor the
Abomination-locked Broken Key and Padlock of Transference**, which the real game
would not let a player equip. Pinned by a test as a known single case, so the
count growing means the scrape has a class-alignment problem rather than a rarity
one.

### Bug 4: 23 hero slots trained with three skills instead of four

The comps are hand-written and two of their skill names disagree with the
game's own string table:

| the comp writes | the game writes | hero slots lost |
|---|---|---|
| "Judgment" | "Judgement" | 15 (Vestal) |
| "Poison Dart" | "Poison Darts" | 8 (Grave Robber) |

`CompLoadoutLoader` matched exactly and dropped what did not match, so those 23
heroes were evaluated and trained with **three** of their four skills — a comp
missing a quarter of a hero's kit is not that comp.

The fix is a fallback, and it is deliberately narrow because a fuzzy match on
skill names is a good way to silently resolve the wrong skill: exact match is
always tried first, the fallback is scoped to one hero's ~7 skills, it needs a
`difflib` ratio of 0.9, and it **refuses whenever the runner-up is within 0.05**
— ambiguity is left unresolved rather than guessed. Across all 136 curated comps
it fires exactly twice, on exactly those two names, and a test pins that set so
widening it has to be deliberate. 23 -> 0.

### Bug 5: `hobby` is a template row, and treating it as a camping skill gave every class an eighth

`hobby` is the one skill of all 80 with an empty `hero_classes`. Reading that as
"every class" — the obvious reading, and the one the first version of the search
pool used — gives nineteen classes 8 camping skills and the Flagellant 5.

Strict membership gives **exactly 7 to all nineteen and exactly 4 to the
Flagellant**, which is the real game's roster on both counts (the Flagellant
really does have only his four Lash skills and cannot use Encourage). Confirmed
independently by the corpus: **no curated comp equips Hobby, in 136 files.**

Empty means no class, not every class. Only the search pool consumed it, so
nothing else changed.

### A design choice: the search does not pick its own negative quirks

`--kit-random-p` draws 1-3 quirks of *each* polarity, and that is right for what
it models — a mid-campaign roster the player did not choose. A comp is the
opposite: it is a plan, and the Sanitarium lets a player lock the positives it
wants and remove the negatives it does not.

Letting the search choose negatives optimises a variable the player does not
control, and it flatters every result — the search finds the mildest possible
downsides and reports the score as the comp's. Fully-groomed is optimistic, but
it is a state a player can actually buy, which "conveniently harmless flaws" is
not. `--negative-quirks` restores the two-sided draw.

`CompSpace.conform` is what pulls a seeded curated comp into that space: it drops
genes the space does not contain (negative quirks, camping skills no file grants
that class, trinkets outside the rarities) and **tops back up what it dropped**,
because a hero left holding two camping skills is a weaker hero for a reason that
has nothing to do with the comp. Without it, crossover could spread genes no
mutation could ever produce.

### What the comp census actually says

The question that started this — how many comps does each class have? — over the
135-comp trainable pool:

| >= 5 comps | 18 of 20 classes, from Antiquarian (9) to Houndmaster (65) |
|---|---|
| **short** | **Duelist 2, Runaway 1** |

So "at least 5 comps with each character" is a two-class problem, not a
twenty-class one, and both of those are the Fire's Edge classes that reach the
training pool from the loadout directory alone (`FIRES_EDGE_HEROES`) — no CSV row,
no disturbance to any archived `--comp-seed`.

## The comp search (`tools/comp_ga.py`, 2026-08-22)

Step 6 of the loadout plan, which that plan deferred with a warning worth
repeating: *"a GA on a noisy fitness evolves noise."* The fitness is
`bridge/rollout.py::Rollout.evaluate` — nothing new is invented — and four
things exist purely to keep the warning from coming true.

**Common random numbers.** `Rollout.evaluate`'s scenario key is
`zone|tier|combats|band|seed` and deliberately does not mention the party, so every
candidate in a generation faces the identical dungeon list. Absolute rates stay as
noisy as they ever were; the *ranking* is far less so. This is the same pairing
`tools/ab_compare.py` exists to provide, reused rather than rebuilt.

**The seed rotates per generation.** A candidate that only wins on one dungeon
list is found out next generation, and an elite carried forward is re-scored
rather than keeping a lucky number. `--fixed-seed` turns it off.

**The hall of fame is re-measured on a held-out seed** at `--validate-episodes`,
and that number — not the generation score — is what goes into a comp's
`gaProvenance`, alongside its standard error and a line telling the reader to read
the standard error.

**The shaping term cannot outrank the headline.** Fitness is
`success_rate + W * shaping` with `shaping` a weighted mean of four rates (so in
[0, 1]) and `W = min(0.04, 0.8 / episodes)` — strictly below `1 / episodes`, the
smallest gap two success rates can have. Early generations are all zeros and need
a gradient; the bound is what stops that gradient from ever overturning a real
difference. A test asserts it across five episode counts.

### Pick a scenario that discriminates, and re-check it after every retrain

A fitness everything scores 100% on ranks nothing. Measured on the 2026-08-21
retrain, 8 candidates, 6 episodes, crypts:

| scenario | best candidate | |
|---|---|---|
| tier 3 medium | 100.0% | ceiling, no gradient |
| tier 3 long | 100.0% | ceiling, no gradient |
| **tier 5 medium** | **66.7%** | the default |
| tier 5 long | 0.0% | floor; only the shaping term separates |

Hence the defaults. A stronger policy pushes the ceiling out and the useful
scenario moves with it, so this probe is part of using the tool, not a one-off.

### First two runs: the Duelist and the Runaway, the only two classes short of comps

32 candidates x 10 generations x 16 episodes, crypts, tier 5, medium (8 combats),
resolve 6, then the hall of fame re-scored at 40 episodes on held-out seed 9999.
~9 minutes each on 12 workers. Every number below is the held-out one.

**The curated comps are scored twice**, because the search grooms its heroes and
most curated heroes carry no quirks at all (24 of 542 slots have a positive, 11 a
negative). "Groomed" runs a curated comp through `CompSpace.conform` — negatives
removed, quirk sheet topped up — so the comparison is like for like rather than a
measurement of who was handed free quirks. It is worth up to +17.5pp on its own
(Ascetic Movement 57.5% -> 75.0%) and is the control that makes the rest readable.

**That control does not survive round 2 and its numbers must not be carried
forward.** `conform` gained the one-off trinket budget on 2026-08-22, so the same
column now also confiscates a comp's boss trinkets, and the sign flips — Ascetic
Movement reads 45.0%, not 75.0%. The round-2 table labels it for what it became.

| comp | P(success) | +- | source |
|---|---|---|---|
| GA runaway 01 / 02 | **90.0%** | 4.7 | search |
| GA duelist 01 / 02 / 03 | **87.5%** | 5.2 | search |
| GA runaway 03 | 85.0% | 5.6 | search |
| Ascetic Movement, groomed | 75.0% | 6.8 | curated, reference |
| Ascetic Movement, as written | 57.5% | 7.8 | curated, reference |
| Riposte Style (Duel), groomed | 45.0% | 7.9 | curated, Duelist |
| Dancing Lunge V3.0 | 40.0% | 7.7 | curated, Duelist |
| Stun Control: Burn | 40.0% | 7.7 | curated, Runaway |
| African Vapors, groomed | 30.0% | 7.2 | curated, reference |
| Antiques Roadshow | 0.0% | 0.0 | curated, reference |

**+42.5pp over the best curated Duelist comp and +50pp over the only curated
Runaway comp**, both far outside the standard errors, and both still ahead of the
best curated comp of any class measured here.

**The first run of this search was worse and it was the tool's fault, not the
search's.** Before the `hobby` and negative-quirk fixes, the Duelist run
converged on Duelist-plus-three-Crusaders in four of six emitted comps and the
Runaway run topped out at 55.0%. Correcting the space did not just raise the
Runaway score by 35pp — it broke the monoculture, and the emitted comps are now
five distinct archetypes (Flagellant/Duelist/Plague Doctor/Grave Robber,
Crusader/Duelist/Occultist/Grave Robber, Flagellant/Vestal/Duelist/Grave
Robber, Runaway/Abomination/Crusader/Crusader, Leper/Crusader/Runaway/Crusader).
A search that keeps returning one answer is worth suspecting of a broken space
before it is worth believing.

Four things keep the headline from being as strong as it reads:

* **It optimised against this policy on this scenario.** The validation *seed* is
  held out; the scenario is not. "Best comp in crypts at Champion, for the
  2026-08-21 checkpoint" is the claim, and the third ruler currently disagrees
  with the other two about that checkpoint.
* **The curated comps are not competing on their own terms.** They carry a region
  tag and most of these are not tagged Ruins, they were built for a human player,
  and several are built for objectives the simulator has no concept of.
  **"Antiques Roadshow" scoring 0.0% is the clean illustration**: it is a
  gold-farming comp, and there is no gold in here.
* **One region is one region.** Smite carries the unholy bonus and the Ruins are
  undead, so a Crusader in every emitted comp is plausible rather than suspicious
  — but `--zones crypts,warrens,weald,cove` is what would tell a Ruins answer from
  a general one, at ~4x the cost and still under an hour.
* **The Runaway is being ranked without half her design.** Six of her seven
  skills apply `.dotBurn`, burn is two days old, and `dmg_per_burn_stack` is
  still deliberately unimplemented.

### Two rules in the operators that are not arbitrary

**Crossover inherits the slot INDEX with the gene.** A hero's rank is most of what
a comp is — `CombatEngine.reset()` assigns `position = index` — so a child slot
must come from the same slot of a parent. Splicing a back-rank Arbalest into a
front rank produces a new random comp wearing a parent's name, and the search
would never accumulate anything.

**Class mutation declines to remove the last holder of a pinned class.** The
repair would only put one back in another slot, so the mutation would be a
guaranteed no-op that still costs a generation slot.

## The retrain, measured on all three rulers, and they do not agree (2026-08-21)

Step 4 and 5 of the loadout plan, finally run — 3M timesteps,
`--full-expedition --n-envs 12 --seed 0`, from scratch (no `--resume` is valid
across any of this). It carries F4's corrected accuracy, the two Fire's Edge
classes, burn, and the twelve engine fixes that landed on 2026-08-21.

**The distribution was chosen from the sensitivity table, not from the plan's
bare command**: `--boss-quest-p 0.25 --cross-region-p 0.5
--loadout-random-p 0.5 --kit-random-p 0.25 --resolve-sampling`. Of the five
knobs only `boss` had ever measured significant (-18.75pp, p=0.0117) and only
`region` measured free (+12.50pp, because 78 of 145 comps are tagged Ruins and
the Ruins are harder than average). Loadout — the knob the whole plan was named
after — measured -6.25pp with an interval spanning zero, so it is in the run
for coverage and not because it was ever proven.

### Ruler 1, `ab_compare` — paired, 216 scenarios, +10.7pp

`--episodes 6 --comps 12 --comp-seed 1`, old final vs new final, `--self-check`
passed first (all 11 metrics bit-identical across 288 scenarios).

| metric | old (2026-08-20) | new | delta | 95% CI | p |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **SUCCESS** | 64.8% | **75.5%** | **+10.7pp** | [+5.1, +16.2] | **0.0004** |
| old-succ (control) | 67.6% | 75.5% | +7.9pp | [+2.3, +13.4] | 0.0076 |
| completed | 66.7% | **83.3%** | +16.7pp | [+11.6, +22.2] | <0.0001 |
| wiped | 28.7% | **16.2%** | -12.5pp | [-17.6, -7.9] | <0.0001 |
| deaths/exp | 1.18 | 0.75 | -0.43 | [-0.62, -0.24] | — |
| **retreats/exp** | **3.58** | **0.36** | **-3.23** | [-4.78, -2.03] | — |
| final stress | 72.2 | 58.7 | -13.5 | [-21.6, -5.9] | — |
| combats reached | 7.56 | 8.88 | +1.31 | [+0.96, +1.70] | — |

32 discordant scenarios to the new checkpoint, 9 to the old, 175 tied.

**The retreat row is the story.** The old checkpoint fled 3.6 times per
expedition; the new one fled 0.36. Retreat stopped being a paid skip earlier
the same day — before that fix, fleeing advanced `_combat_idx` and fleeing the
last combat even collected `REWARD_EXPEDITION_COMPLETE`. The old policy had
learned that exploit, and the exploit is gone. Some of the SUCCESS delta is
definitional, since SUCCESS requires `retreats == 0`; but the `old-succ`
control, `completed` and `wiped` do not depend on retreat at all and all three
move the same way.

### Ruler 2, `eval_tutorial` — exactly reproducible, +20pp

The only harness here where two checkpoints face identical fights.
`--self-check` printed 0.00 on every metric.

| | old | new |
|---|---|---|
| SUCCESS | 76.0% | **96.0%** |
| completed | 82.0% | **100.0%** |
| deaths/run | 0.42 | 0.04 |
| retreats/run | 1.48 | **0.00** |
| final stress | 45.9 | **10.8** |

### Ruler 3, `agreement` — and it points the other way, significantly

The ruler with no simulator in it: how often does a checkpoint advise the move
that was really played, over the captured corpus. `--self-check` passed.

| layer | n | old | new | delta | 95% CI | McNemar |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **combat** | 101 | **50.5%** | 37.6% | **-12.9pp** | [-24.8, -1.0] | 25/12, **p=0.047** |
| route | 5 | 80.0% | 80.0% | — | | |
| torch | 9 | 66.7% | 66.7% | — | | |
| curio | 14 | 85.7% | 85.7% | — | | |

**This is the first time the third ruler has reached significance, and it is
against the checkpoint the other two prefer.** It has to be reported as a
finding rather than explained away. Three things are true at once and none of
them settles it:

- **MATCH is not correctness.** A DIFFER can be the policy outplaying the
  human. That caveat was written into the tool before this result existed.
- **The corpus was played under the old engine**, by a human, against a policy
  that has now trained on a materially harder one. A policy that no longer
  believes retreat is free, no longer receives Ancestor's Candle in the dark
  and expects to be surprised in 40% of openings *should* play differently.
- **The divergence is mostly targeting, not plan.** The DIFFER breakdown is
  attack->attack 43 (up from 35) — the same kind of action against a different
  target — with only 6 of 60 divergences changing the kind of play.

The honest summary is that two simulated rulers say the retrain is the largest
improvement this project has measured, and the unsimulated one says the policy
now agrees with Fran less often. Ship on the first two, and treat the third as
the open question it is.

### The prediction in NEXT.md, checked

`NEXT.md` said to expect SUCCESS to **fall**, because twelve fixes had made the
game harder. It rose. The prediction was not wrong so much as aimed at the
wrong comparison: it warned against reading a new absolute number next to an
**archived** one. In a *paired* comparison the harsher engine hits both arms,
and it hits the old checkpoint harder — because a third of what the old
checkpoint knew was how to exploit a retreat rule that no longer exists.
The warning stands for any absolute number quoted against an archived eval.

### Which of the two saves

New final vs new `best_model.zip`, same 216 scenarios: **+4.6pp to the final
save, CI [-0.9, +10.2], not distinguishable** (25 discordant to final, 15 to
best). The final save leads on every metric and none of it is significant —
the same shape as the historical pairs, two of three of which also went to the
final save. Both are kept; `models/dd_combat_ppo_full_expedition.zip` is the
one measured above.

The pre-retrain pair is preserved in `archive/models/pre_20260821_retrain/`,
because a real run overwrites the live checkpoint *and*
`logs/full_expedition/best_model.zip`, `models/` is gitignored, and that has
already cost this project one checkpoint.

## Every darkness trinket in the game paid out in broad daylight (2026-08-21)

`_is_conditional_rule_active` evaluated 11 of the 44 shipped `rule_type`s and
returned `True` for everything else. That fallback is documented and was a
deliberate choice — but the choice is *which way to be wrong*, and it is
never neutral. **An unevaluated rule type is not an inert buff; it is a buff
granted unconditionally.**

Two of the unread ones were live:

| rule | defs | why it was wrong |
|---|---|---|
| `lightbelow` / `lightabove` | 130 / 104 | torchlight has been modelled all along — sampled per combat from the real decile distribution and carried in the observation |
| `in_camp` | 41 | `CombatEngine` is combat; camping is `engine/camping.py` and never touches this machinery, so the answer is a constant `False` |

Measured over 300 sampled expedition combats, the way the buff sweep ranked
`stat_type` — by what a real party **equips**, not by how many definitions
exist:

| | |
|---|---|
| newly-gated buffs carried | **1.70 per combat** |
| of those, gate now shut | 0.63 per combat (37.2%) |
| combats where at least one buff is withdrawn | **28.7%** |
| withdrawn buffs that were HELPING the party | **190 of 190** |

That last row is the one to keep. Real comps equip good trinkets and do not
keep punishing quirks, so every buff this fix takes away was a buff the party
wanted: +5%/+10%/+15% dodge, +33% stress healing, +2 SPD, +15% damage, +5%
protection, +33% stun resist. **This makes the game harder, in more than a
quarter of all combats.** It belongs on the list in `NEXT.md` of why SUCCESS
should be expected to fall on the next retrain.

The biggest single carrier is **Ancestor's Candle** (`lightabove 50`: +15%/+15%
damage, +2 SPD, +5% dodge), on 28 hero slots across the comps — and the whole
block was landing at any torch level. `sun_cloak` (22), `camouflage_cloak`
(16), `crow_tailfeather` (14) and `heavens_hairpin` (10) follow.

### The strictness question answered itself out of the data

`NEXT.md` filed this as "one convention to declare, not guess": is
`lightbelow 75` a `<` or a `<=`? It turned out not to need declaring. The
thresholds in the file are 25, **26**, 50, **51**, 60, 75 — and the two that
dominate are `lightbelow 26` (105 trinkets) and `lightabove 75` (56). Since the
darkness bands exclude their lower bound, band tops are 25/50/75, so under a
strict reading `lightbelow 26` is exactly "torch <= 25" — precisely the black
band and below — and `lightabove 75` is exactly the radiant band. Read
inclusively, each spills one point into the neighbouring band and names
nothing. **The off-by-one constants are the tell**: someone wrote 26 because
the comparison is `<`.

The light quirks confirm the direction from a completely separate corner of
the data: `lygophobia` (fear of the dark) is +20% stress taken at
`lightbelow 26`; `phengophobia` (fear of daylight) is +20% at `lightabove 75`;
`nocturnal` is -2 SPD in the light and `diurnal` -2 SPD in the dark. Five
names, five gates, all pointing the same way.

Still recorded as a *declared reading* in `ASSUMPTIONS.md`, with a falsifier,
because the shipped data is not tidy: `dark_bracer` carries `lightbelow 51` and
`lightabove 51`, so torch exactly 51 fires neither. The dead point is in the
game's numbers, and the inclusive reading replaces it with a point where a buff
and a debuff both fire — worse, not better.

### Why the last sweep missed it, which is the part worth keeping

The 2026-08-21 buff audit ranked candidates by `stat_type` and found
`stun_chance` (1.85 equipped per party, the largest inert type in the game).
It never looked at `rule_type`. Same file, same `BuffDefinition`, one axis
examined and the other not — and the axis it skipped held something worse than
an inert buff. **One axis of a data file is not the file.**

`tests/engine/test_conditional_buffs.py`, 7 new tests, including the band-alignment
table and an end-to-end check that Ancestor's Candle pays nothing at torch 30.
The unknown-rule fallback is now pinned by its own test, so adding a branch
stays a deliberate act.

Two incidental fixes in the same function: the stale justification for not
implementing `afflicted`/`virtued` ("this simulator has no personality
subsystem" — it has had one since the trait work; the real reason is that
`trait_loader` attaches those buffs *because* the hero broke that way, so the
gate is satisfied by construction, and zero trinkets or quirks carry one), and
a duplicated `burning` branch in `_actor_has_status` left over from the burn
work.

## The game will tell you how its own AI weighted a decision (2026-08-21)

`tools/brain_oracle.py`, sibling of `hit_oracle.py` and found the same way —
by reading what is already in `archive/traces/*.log` instead of capturing
something new. The debug channel prints a monster's whole deliberation:

```
MonsterBrain: Select Skill:
valid skills: cultist_pull, cultist_push, cultist_incantation
Desire: specific_skill         Skill chances: 0, 1, 0
Desire: specific_skill         Skill chances: 1, 1, 0
Desire: specific_skill         Skill chances: 1, 1, 2
Desire: performing_turn_skill  Skill chances: 1, 1, 7
MonsterBrain: selected skill at index: 2
```

One block per desire, **in the brain file's own order**, each printing the
running total — so the delta between consecutive blocks is exactly what that
one desire contributed, and every desire becomes individually gradeable. That
is `cultist_witch_B`'s brain read straight back out of the running game: +1
push, +1 pull, +2 incantation, then +5 incantation because it is her first
action of the turn.

**46 of 46 gradeable desire contributions match**, over 12 identified brains.
29 skill blocks exist; 17 are trivial (one usable skill, and the game prints
no desire at all), which is why the tool reports them separately rather than
quietly dropping them — a corpus count that does not reconcile with a plain
grep is a parser bug waiting to be believed.

On the target side, 47 blocks: every state-free `random_target` desire weights
each valid target identically, as the engine does.

Two facts about the game's arithmetic came out of it, both worth having and
neither worth copying:

- **Target weights carry an epsilon of `1e-4` per desire; skill weights carry
  none.** A target desire that does not apply still nudges by 0.0001. It can
  only decide anything when every weight would otherwise be zero — where it
  makes the pick uniform, which is exactly what `SeededRNG.weighted_choice`
  already falls back to. Recorded, not implemented.
- `performing_turn_skill` fires on `action_idx + 1 == performing_turn`. The
  Witch's `+5.0` on her first action is the confirmation.

**What it cannot grade, and says so.** The trace does not record who was
marked, who was stunned, or how hurt the monsters were, so a desire carrying a
gate is reported rather than scored: a zero contribution is equally consistent
with the gate having been shut at the time. 6 of 52 skill-side contributions
and 51 of 98 target-side ones fall in that bucket. Which also means the corpus
**cannot** confirm the gate work committed earlier today — it is consistent
with it, and that is a weaker statement.

`archive/traces/brain_blocks.json` is the tracked fixture, for the same reason
`hit_pairs.json` is: the raw captures are `*.log` and gitignored.
`tests/tools/test_brain_oracle.py`, 6 tests.

## F4's one-off analysis is now an instrument — and it had a soft row (2026-08-21)

`DECOMP.md` F4 validated the to-hit rule against real `(chance, roll)` pairs
back on 2026-08-19, and the conclusion stands. What it left behind was a
paragraph in a document: nothing re-runs it, nothing fails if the rule drifts,
and the corpus has since grown from 166 pairs to **186**.

`tools/hit_oracle.py` turns that analysis into a standing instrument — the
only ruler here with the real game in it rather than the simulator or a data
file — plus a test and a tracked fixture. Re-grading also found that one of
F4's two evidence rows was softer than it read.

### Reading an outcome out of a channel that never says "hit"

The debug stream prints `Chance to hit` and `Roll to hit` and then nothing
about what happened. What it does print is the damage band — a landed attack
draws `Battle Random int range A,B: 3 , 5 Value: 5` inside the same
`bs_turn_presentation` block, and a miss draws nothing.

That marker is sound in **one direction only**, and saying so is the whole
difference between a measurement and a mistake: a stress attack, a debuff or a
mark lands and still draws no band. So a turn in which *nothing* drew damage
is unclassifiable and is not scored. 155 of 186 pairs survive that filter, and
the first pass — which scored all 186 — read as 92.6% agreement with 14
"failures" that were nothing of the kind.

### F4's second row does not say what it says

F4 reports two groups. The first — *`roll >= 0.95` and `roll > chance`, 6/6
hit* — is untouched: all six drew damage. The second reads

> `roll > chance` and `roll < 0.95` | 17 | **17/17 missed** — no damage roll,
> turn ended

and of those 18 pairs (186 now, not 166), **17 are unclassifiable and exactly
one is a real miss.** All 17 sit in turns where *no* target drew damage, which
is equally consistent with the skill having no damage at all. The row is not
wrong about what was observed; it is wrong that the observation is evidence.
F4's conclusion does not depend on it — the 6/6 row and the 1.05 chance carry
it — but the row is annotated in `DECOMP.md` rather than left to be re-quoted.

### The result

**155 of 155.** Including that one genuine miss inside an otherwise damaging
turn, so the corpus is not simply a pile of hits.

And **the corpus discriminates**, which is new. 13 pairs are decided differently
by F4 and by the rule this project used until 2026-08-19 (`P(hit) = min(0.95,
raw + 0.05)`, compared as `roll < chance`); 7 of those are classifiable, and
the game sided with F4 on all 7 — **in both directions**:

| | chance | roll | F4 | old rule | the game |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 6 pairs | 0.60-0.90 | 0.969-0.988 | hit | miss | **damage drawn** |
| 1 pair | 0.625 | 0.673 | miss | hit | **no damage** |

The six are rolls that cleared `cutoffAlwaysHit` (0.95) while *beating* the
attack's chance — the top-5%-always-lands rule, in the wild. The seventh only
lands if you add the old hidden +5% to the chance, and it did not land. The
old rule is refuted from both sides rather than merely unsupported.

F4 already had the six; the seventh is new, and it is the one that kills the
old rule from the other side. The corpus also logs chances of **1.01 and
1.05**, which is possible only if the game never clamps the chance — the other
half of F4, and the reason `compute_hit_chance` returns a bare `ACC - DODGE`.

Two things fall out for free: a multi-target attack prints one `Roll to hit`
per target and they are **identical**, while the chances differ per target
(one roll, per-target accuracy — already implemented as `_shared_hit_roll`,
now with the evidence attached to a test); and the damage draw is
`randint(0, B-A)` reported as `A + value`, which is what the engine does.

`tools/hit_oracle.py` re-grades on demand and `--emit` refreshes
`archive/traces/hit_pairs.json`. That fixture exists because the raw captures
are `*.log` and therefore gitignored — without it the guard would vanish on a
fresh clone. `tests/tools/test_hit_oracle.py`, 6 tests, and it re-derives the fixture
from the logs when they are present so it cannot drift.

## A gate is a property of the desire, not of its type (2026-08-21)

Sixth audit sweep: `raid/ai/base.monster_brains.json`, 165 brains. Two
independent holes, one on each half of the file.

### The skill half — 21.8% of the monster pool carried a gate nobody read

A `skill_selection_desires` entry can carry a gate: *only while 2-4 monsters
are up*, *only while fewer than three heroes are marked*, *only when nobody is
guarded*. The engine checked gates **inside the branch of whichever desire type
it first met them on** — `non_stunned_heroes_min` under `stun_skill`,
`monsters_size_*` / `hp_ratio_threshold` / `first_initiative_only` under
`heal_skill` — and the shipped data puts the very same keys on `specific_skill`
(20 distinct kinds, 130+ instances) and `performing_turn_skill` (44), where
nothing looked at them.

| unread gate | share of hall/room monster slots |
|---|---|
| `monsters_size_min` / `_max` on `specific_skill` | **11.4%** |
| `marked_heroes_min` / `_max` on `specific_skill` | 9.0% |
| `marked_heroes_min` / `_max` on `performing_turn_skill` | 8.6% |
| `virtued_heroes_max` | 2.8% |
| `guarded_monsters_min` / `_max` | 2.6% |
| `non_stunned_heroes_min` on `specific_skill` | 2.5% |
| `ally_dead_skill`'s `monsters_min` | 1.1% |
| `is_valid_when_corpse_enabled` | 0.7% |

**37 monsters, 21.8% of pool slots, carry at least one.** Measured end to end
over 40 random-action expeditions: **5.2% of real enemy skill selections
(114 of 2183) were drawn from a different weighting** than the corrected
engine draws.

Concretely: a **Cultist Witch re-marked an already-marked party forever**
(`marked_heroes_max: 2`), a **Skeleton Bearer used `unholy_rally`** even though
the desire is flagged for the game's no-corpse ruleset, and every summon's
"is there room on the board" gate was ignored.

The fix is one shared predicate, `_skill_desire_gates_pass`, applied to every
desire before its type-specific logic. The per-type copies are deleted, so
there is now exactly one place a gate is spelled out.

**One behaviour deliberately kept**: a `heal_skill` with no
`hp_ratio_threshold` still requires somebody to be hurt. That was the old
branch's `threshold = ... else 1.0` default, it is right, and the shared gate
only fires when the key is present — so the heal branch keeps its own default.
Pinned by a test.

**`monsters_size_*` is total rank occupancy, not a head count — a declared
reading.** The Siren's `conch_summon` carries `monsters_min`/`monsters_max`
**and** `monsters_size_min`/`monsters_size_max` on the same desire, so the two
keys cannot denote one quantity, and only one of them is named for size. The
old `heal_skill` branch read it as `len(living_enemies)`; that convention was
inherited, never argued for, and is now replaced. 38 hall/room comps in the
pool contain a size-gated brain alongside a size-2 monster, so the two readings
genuinely diverge in training. `ASSUMPTIONS.md` has the falsifier.

### The target half — a baseline weight dropped for three monster families

`is_enemy_target_desire` was not read at all; the side filter tested
`is_friendly_target_desire == is_friendly`. **The two flags are not
complements.** 25 desires set BOTH, and every one of them is its brain's
baseline flat `random_target` — the desire that says "and any hero will do".

Reading one flag dropped that baseline from *enemy* targeting for the
Swinetaur, the Octotank and the Fishman Shaman (9 monsters, 4.8% of pool
slots). What survived were only their conditional desires, so a preference
became an absolute: a Swinetaur's `pig_spear` hit a marked hero **100%** of the
time instead of the 50% its data asks for (random 1.0 + marked 2.0 against
three unmarked at 1.0 each). Measured live at **2.5% of all enemy target
selections**.

Also newly read, all of them per-desire exclusions rather than vetoes — the
brain's flat desire still keeps an excluded target reachable:

- **`can_target_stunned`** (9.9% of pool slots) — don't aim the stun at
  someone already stunned. Note the shipped data uses *two* mechanisms for
  this and they stack: the Swinetaur carries this flag AND a `stunned_target`
  desire at **-1000**, which is the data's own way of writing "never".
  `weighted_choice` skips any weight <= 0, so with both present the hero
  really is unreachable — the shipped intent, not a clamp of ours.
- **`is_exclusive_desire`** (2.5%) — when an exclusive desire applies, only
  exclusive desires count. The Fishman Shaman's heal picks the most wounded
  ally outright instead of competing with a flat random weight.
- **`is_guarded` / `is_guarding`** (1.4%) — a Skeleton Defender does not guard
  an ally who is already guarded.
- **`can_target_deaths_door`**, **`can_target_last_hero`** — read and applied,
  and `can_target_last_hero` **cannot change an outcome here**: it means the
  last *living* hero (the Hag's `hag_grab` carries it, because putting your
  last hero in the cauldron would leave nobody able to act), and when one hero
  is left there is exactly one candidate to weigh. Implemented anyway, with a
  test recording why it is inert, so nobody later "fixes" it into something
  that bites.

### Left unread, with the reason

`control_count_min`/`_max` needs the Siren's charm subsystem; `per_round_chance`
(2 carriers) has no stated semantics; `heroes_min`, `non_deaths_door_heroes_min`
and `performing_turn_min` are implemented but have **zero** carriers in the
hall/room pool — every one of them belongs to the Siren, the Ancestor, the
Shrieker or a Color of Madness monster.

`tests/engine/test_monster_brain_gates.py`, 13 tests.

## The most-equipped buff in the game did nothing (2026-08-21)

Fifth audit sweep: `shared/buffs/base.buffs.json`. Buffs are the universal
modifier here — trinkets, quirks, traits and skill effects all cash out through
`_apply_buff_to_stats` — so a `stat_type` with no branch is a **silent no-op**.
The buff applies, nothing happens, and neither a test nor an eval can see it.

Gated on **real training exposure**, not on how many buffs exist: a buff nothing
equips is not a bug worth fixing, and the previous sweep had just spent effort
proving that lesson on the monster keys. Counting only kit actually put on a
hero across 150 sampled expeditions:

| unhandled stat_type | per expedition | verdict |
|---|---|---|
| `stun_chance` | **1.85** | fixed |
| `scouting_chance` | 1.06 | out of scope — no map layer |
| `debuff_chance` | 0.65 | **blocked**, see below |
| `resolve_check_percent` | 0.47 | fixed |
| `move_chance` | 0.19 | fixed |
| everything else | < 0.07 | left |

**`stun_chance` was the single most-equipped inert stat_type in the game.** 22
shipped buffs, nearly two on the party in an average expedition, doing nothing.

### Two of them already had a shape to copy

`bleed_chance` and `poison_chance` are handled as a reduction to the TARGET's
effective resist, for a reason worth restating: the engine has one resist roll
per payload and no separate chance-to-apply roll to add a bonus to.
`stun_chance` and `move_chance` are the same shape, so they lower effective
STUN RES and MOVE RES respectively, clamped at zero.

### `resolve_check_percent` shifts the virtue side

45 buffs, the largest group in the whole sweep. The Resolve Check is a single
binary split — `affliction_critical_success_chance` (0.25) and
`affliction_base_onset_chance` (0.75) sum to exactly 1.0 — and this modifier
moves the virtue side of it. Positive is better for the hero, which is what the
carriers say rather than what the name suggests: `mercurial` is a **positive**
quirk and carries +, while `ennui` and `never_again_affliction` carry -.

Clamped to a real probability. The rules' own `affliction_min_chance` /
`affliction_max_chance` are stated for the *affliction* chance, not this one,
so they are deliberately not reused.

### `debuff_chance` is blocked on a bigger gap: DEBUFF RES is never rolled

`debuff_resist` is parsed onto **every** hero and monster, raised +10% per
resolve level, and modified by trinkets and quirks — and **nothing ever rolls
against it**. Its only readers are the monster AI's target weighting and
`_get_resistance`. So every stat debuff in the game lands unconditionally.

That is a real mechanic and a large one. It is not implemented because **the
shipped data does not say which effects DEBUFF RES resists**, and the candidate
rules disagree:

- "an effect applying a stat modifier to the opposing side" would exclude
  Mark — but Mark IS resisted by DEBUFF RES in the real game, and it carries
  `.tag 1` rather than any stat field.
- there is no per-effect marker to read instead. `.buff_source_type` turns out
  to be `bsrc_crit` on all 14 carriers, and `.debuffenemy` exists on exactly
  **one** effect in the game ("Slow").

`.dot_irresistible` existing at all is evidence the game's default is to roll
*something* per payload, which makes the gap more likely than not — but "more
likely than not" is not the bar this project uses, and getting the effect set
wrong would silently change how a large fraction of skills resolve. Recorded in
`ASSUMPTIONS.md` with its falsifier instead. `debuff_chance` stays a no-op
until it has a roll to modify, and a test pins that so it cannot be
half-wired.

`tests/engine/test_chance_buffs.py`, 10 tests.

## Flashbang did not flash: a valueless flag lost to whitespace (2026-08-21)

The fourth audit sweep, and the first one to find a bug in the **parser** rather
than in what reads it.

Sweeping `effects/base.effects.darkest` the same way as the hero and monster
files turned up `.shuffletarget` and `.shuffleparty` — 24 effects, neither
parsed. So **Bounty Hunter Flashbang and Plague Doctor Disorienting Blast, two
skills whose entire payload is the shuffle, resolved to a plain attack**, and
the monster side lost Gargoyle `claw_flurry`, Unclean Giant `confusion_spores`
(all three tiers) and the Shambler's howl — six monsters in the hall/room
training pool.

### The parser bug

Wiring the two flags produced 11 of the 24. `.shuffleparty` worked and
`.shuffletarget` mostly did not, and the difference was **whitespace**:

```
.shuffleparty  .chance 100%      ->  parses, value ''
.shuffletarget .chance 100%      ->  KEY VANISHES
.shuffletarget<TAB>.chance 100%  ->  KEY VANISHES
```

The regex was `\.([a-zA-Z_]+)\s+(value)`: `\s+` swallowed the single separator
and left the value alternatives nothing to match, so the whole key failed and
disappeared from the record. With two spaces the second one matched *as* the
value and the key survived as the empty string.

Measured over every file the loaders read: `.shuffletarget` **dropped 17 times
and survived twice, in the same file**.

The fix makes the value optional. Greedy `?`, so a key with a real value still
binds it; and a value that exists but does not fit the character class still
drops the whole key exactly as before, because the lookahead has to hold
immediately after the key.

**Verified surgical by diffing the whole parsed store before and after**: 0
heroes changed, 0 enemies changed, **13 effects changed — every one of them a
shuffle effect.** This is the fourth bug of this exact class the file has
recorded (unquoted decimals truncating, `@`/`~` prefixes missing from the
character class, the no-space-before-next-key typo), and the docstring now says
so.

### Presence, not truthiness

`.shuffleparty` has no value, so it parses to `''` and `bool('')` is False. The
loader tests membership instead — the same trap as `.effect ""` parsing to
`['']`. **In this format an empty value means the flag is set.**

### Shuffle is not resistable

None of the 24 effects carries anything to roll against: push and pull carry
the distance that the MOVE RES roll reads, and these carry no distance at all.
So the `.chance` roll is the only gate — which is why `Shuffle Party` ships at
**80-150%**, an over-100% chance being a certainty rather than something to
resist. Pinned by a test over all 24.

`_scramble_party` (built for surprise) generalises to `_scramble_side`, and
`_scramble_one` moves a single combatant via `_shift_to_position`, so a shuffle
obeys the same rotation rule as a push or a pull: the SET of occupied ranks
cannot change. Sizes are handled by rebuilding the line with a cursor — a
permutation a size-2 monster cannot fit into is abandoned rather than
half-applied.

`tests/engine/test_shuffle.py`, 12 tests.

## The Hag fight offered an invulnerable pot as its best target (2026-08-21)

`battle_modifier: .can_be_damaged_directly false`, 12 monsters. The one that
matters is `cauldron_empty`: **100 HP, size 2**, standing on ranks 1-2 of the
Hag's boss fight and not attackable at all.

Unread, so the engine saw a fat enemy in the front rank and offered it — and
because it is the first enemy on the board, it is the first thing every
front-rank melee hero is shown. It is also the **fattest** target present: 100
HP against the Hag's 66.

`cauldron_full` — the pot with a captured hero in it — does **not** carry the
flag. That is the design: you break the pot your friend is in, never the empty
one. The capture mechanic is a documented out-of-scope exclusion, so here the
pot stays empty and stays untargetable, which is also what the real game does
until she throws someone in.

### The consequence looks alarming and is correct

Attackable ranks in the Hag fight, before and after:

| hero | rank | before | after |
|---|---|---|---|
| Crusader | 1 | 1, 2 | **—** |
| Hellion | 2 | 1, 2, 3 | 3 |
| Arbalest | 3 | 1, 2, 3, 4 | 3, 4 |
| Vestal | 4 | 1, 2, 3, 4 | 3, 4 |

**The Crusader has no attack at all**, and that is the fight: the Hag sits on
ranks 3-4 behind two ranks of cauldron, so a front-loaded party cannot reach
her. Written down in the test file because it looks like a bug in a log and
someone will otherwise "fix" it. The fight stays winnable — the back three all
reach her — and the cauldron carries `disable_stall_penalty True`, so nothing
punishes the Crusader for having nothing to do.

Applied to hero targeting **and** to AoE resolution, because
`get_valid_actions` and `_resolve_action_targets` have to move together or an
AoE offered over ranks 1-2 re-resolves to zero targets and silently burns the
turn — the rule corpses already follow.

The flag says "directly" and this reads it as "cannot be chosen or struck".
Whether an indirect source would still tick is unanswerable from shipped data
and unreachable in play: nothing can land a DoT on something it cannot hit.

### What the sweep did NOT find, which is the more useful result

The monster-side audit is **exhausted for the content this simulator fields**.
Every remaining unread monster key has **zero carriers in the hall/room training
pool**:

| key | files | in hall/room | in boss/named |
|---|---|---|---|
| `can_be_random_target` | 8 | 0 | 0 |
| `is_valid_friendly_target` | 12 | 0 | 0 |
| `does_count_towards_stall_penalty` | 12 | 0 | 0 |
| `can_relieve_stress_from_killing_blow` | 12 | 0 | 0 |
| `does_count_as_monster_size_for_monster_brain` | 12 | 0 | 0 |
| `starting_cooldown` | 4 | 0 | 0 |
| `rank_damage_modifiers` | 17 | 0 | 0 |

`starting_cooldown` and `rank_damage_modifiers` were the two named as next up,
and both turn out to be unreachable: every carrier is Crimson Court or
Farmstead content (Countess, Baron, Esquire, Miller, Plowhorse) or a roaming
boss this project does not field. Implementing them would be dead code, which
is the exact failure this audit has spent all day removing. Their semantics are
recorded in `ASSUMPTIONS.md` instead — `rank_damage_modifiers -0.8 -0.65 -0.3
0.0` on an AoE is a per-target-rank damage falloff — so a future Crimson Court
integration does not have to re-derive them.

Two reachable ones are recorded rather than guessed at: the Drowned Captain's
`companion: .heal_per_turn_percent 0.025` names companion class
`drowned_anchored`, while the skill he actually summons spawns
`drowned_anchor` — two distinct monsters with identical HP, and resolving which
one the buff attaches to needs evidence this project does not have. The Siren's
`controller: .stress_per_controlled_turn` belongs to the charm mechanic, already
excluded.

## Killing the boss did not end the fight (2026-08-21)

`life_link: .base_class "X"` sits on **177 monsters across 19 parents** and
nothing read it. It means *this monster dies when a monster of class X dies* —
the Prophet's pews, the Hag's cauldron, the Crow's nest, and the half that
matters most, every add a summoning boss calls in.

**This went from theoretical to live two commits earlier.** Every summoning boss
in the game summons monsters that are life-linked back to it:

| boss | summons, linked back |
|---|---|
| Necromancer | skeletons |
| Siren | fishmen, jellyfish, octotank |
| Drowned Captain | the anchor |
| Brigand Cannon | cutthroat, fuseman, fusilier |
| Collector | collected hero forms |

`summon_count` had just started working, so the party was standing there
clearing summons that no longer had a summoner.

### The parent-present guard IS the reading

`skeleton_arbalist_A` is linked to `necromancer`, and skeletons fill ordinary
Ruins corridors with no Necromancer anywhere. The counts settle it: of the 1266
comp slots holding a linked add, **1239 have no parent present** — and all 27
that do are `boss` or `named` comps. A rule without the guard would delete half
the Ruins on the first skeleton kill.

### Matched on the directory, not on a stripped suffix

`EnemyDefinition.base_class` is the monster's directory (`necromancer_B` lives
in `monsters/necromancer/`). A regex stripping `_[A-E]` off the id would be
wrong for `brigand_barrel_D` and `com_crocodile`, and a mis-stripped base class
silently mis-links a boss to its adds.

A consequence worth stating rather than defending: since the key names a
directory and carries no variant, **a Veteran Necromancer clears an Apprentice
skeleton too**. That is faithful — and it costs nothing, checked rather than
assumed: 16 of 1105 comps mix variant tiers, all Vvulf-adjacent brigand groups,
and **not one contains a parent and a linked add together**.

A first draft of the test asserted the opposite and was wrong; the code was
right.

### Removed, not slain

No corpse (it is not a killing blow, so `_leaves_corpse` has no say), no
killing-blow stress relief, no recursion — no add is itself a parent in the
shipped data, and looping once means a chain could not run away even if a patch
added one. Ranks close afterwards, same invariant as every other formation
change. Wired at all three monster-death sites: the killing blow, the DoT tick,
and `is_kill_effect`.

**Effect, measured**: a boss fight runs 16.9 → **14.7** steps under a
first-legal-action policy on 40 boss quests. That is a floor, not the number —
a greedy policy does not aim at the boss, and the whole point of the mechanic is
that focusing the boss now ends the fight.

`tests/engine/test_life_link.py`, 11 tests.

## A third of boss fights opened with the boss flat-footed (2026-08-21)

The `.darkest` audit sweep — third of three, and the one that found the
legality keys — also found four surprise flags on **every one of the game's 383
monster files**, in the same `battle_modifier:` record whose two stall flags the
engine has read for months:

```
battle_modifier: .can_surprise .can_be_surprised .always_surprise .always_be_surprised
```

Nothing read them. So surprise, added the same day, rolled against monsters the
game says cannot be surprised and monsters it says **always** surprise you.

### The data is self-verifying

`always_surprise` is carried by **exactly the Shambler line and the Fanatic**
and by nothing else — which is the wiki's "unavoidable vs Shambler / Fanatic /
camping ambush" list, arrived at from the opposite direction. That is what makes
these flags trustworthy rather than a guess at what four booleans mean.

162 monsters ship both `can_` flags False: the Ancestor forms, the Crimson Court
bodies, the Baron. That is how the game encodes the wiki's "impossible in the
Darkest Dungeon and the Farmstead" — **per monster, not per region**.

`always_be_surprised` is False everywhere shipped, and is honoured anyway so a
DLC that uses it is not silently ignored.

### What it costs, measured both ways on the same stream

| | flags ignored | flags honoured |
|---|---|---|
| **corridor training** — party / monsters | 14.0% / 30.0% | **14.0% / 30.0%** |
| **boss fights** — party / monsters | 18.0% / 34.0% | **2.0% / 1.3%** |

Exactly zero change in ordinary training, and that is not a disappointing
result — it is the explanation. `ENEMY_CATEGORIES_FOR_TRAINING` is `hall` and
`room`, and only **2 of the 1105 real encounter comps** in those categories
contain a restricted monster. All 75 restricted monsters live in the 37 `boss`
and 57 `named` comps.

Which is exactly the content `--boss-quest-p` started fielding two days ago. Un-
flagged, **a third of boss fights opened with the boss surprised** — a free
round against the Necromancer, the Drowned Captain and the Brigand Cannon, the
same bosses whose summoning was switched on in the commit before last.

### Two rules

- **ANY, not ALL**, in both directions: one ambusher is enough to catch a party
  out, one Shambler forces it. Nearly unobservable — 10 of 1105 comps are mixed
  on either flag — but it has to be chosen, so it is written down and pinned.
- **Both draws are taken BEFORE the flags are consulted.** Which monsters turned
  up must not shift every subsequent roll of the combat, or two arms of an A/B
  comparison stop facing the same fight the moment one meets a Shambler. Pinned
  against a monster that genuinely rolled: an Ancestor form, whose answer is
  predetermined, still costs the two draws.

`tests/engine/test_surprise.py` grows to 25.

## A death cost the survivors nothing, so the death spiral could not happen (2026-08-21)

The second config-side audit: diff every scalar in `shared/rules.json` against
every attribute access **and string literal** in `data/`, `engine/`, `env/`,
`bridge/`. 83 of them are referenced nowhere. Most are correctly out of scope —
town, diseases, quirk slots, barks, New Game+, the camera's acceleration
constants — and one, `provision_hp_heal`, was already sourced correctly under a
different name (`FOOD_HEAL_FRACTION`, with the key named in its comment).

Three were real.

### 1. `death_party_stress_chance: 0.5`, `death_party_stress_damage: 12`

**A hero dying charged the rest of the party no stress at all.** Both keys are
read by the game (parse sites `0x1404ea351`, `0x1404ea3d1`) and by nothing here.

This is the largest single reason the **death spiral** — the thing that
actually ends a Darkest Dungeon run — could not happen in this simulator. 12
stress at even odds across three survivors is ~18 points of party stress per
death, landing exactly when the party can least afford it, and it is the
mechanism that turns one loss into a cascade of afflictions and then a wipe.
Without it a death was priced purely as lost output: one fewer turn per round.

Every reward number tuned against death — `REWARD_HERO_DEATH`,
`TERMINAL_STRESS_PENALTY_WEIGHT`, the retreat trade — was tuned against a death
that was cheaper than the game's.

Now `CombatEngine._on_hero_died`, called from all three places a hero can
actually die: the deathblow roll (which the DoT path also routes through), the
heart attack that bypasses that roll, and `is_kill_effect`.

### 2. `is_kill_effect` never fired death reactions

Found by routing those three sites through one helper rather than by looking
for it. The deathblow and heart-attack paths both called
`_maybe_trigger_death_reactions`; the instant-kill path did not. So a
Flagellant executed by a kill effect skipped **Last Gift** (heals the rest of
the party 10 HP) and **Last Insult** (stuns every enemy at 125%). Flagellant-
only in practice, which is why nothing noticed.

### 3. `effect_crit_roll_mod: -0.2` was a hardcoded wiki constant

`CRIT_STATUS_CHANCE_BONUS = 0.20` was sourced from the wiki's "all status
effects applied on a critical hit have a 20% higher chance of applying". The
wiki was right, and `rules.json` has been shipping **exactly that number** the
whole time — as a modifier on the **roll**, which is why it is negative.

`roll + mod < chance` and `roll < chance - mod` are the same predicate, and
unlike the accuracy cutoffs (`DECOMP.md` F4) nothing clamps either side, so the
two forms cannot diverge. No behaviour change; one fewer invented constant, and
a patch or a mod now moves it.

### One that is NOT being implemented, and why

`deaths_door_self_stress_dmg_repeat: 4.0` and
`deaths_door_companion_stress_dmg_repeat: 2.0` are parsed, read by the game,
and read by nothing here. Both are exactly **half** their non-repeat
