counterparts (8 and 4). Two readings, and they disagree about what the engine
should do:

- **A — each subsequent hit while already at Death's Door.** The `else:` branch
  of `_handle_deaths_door` currently applies *no* stress at all, so under this
  reading we undercharge on the most common case by far. The halving fits: a
  thing that happens often is made cheaper per instance.
- **B — the second time a hero enters Death's Door.** Then the current `else:`
  branch is *correct* and the first branch should use the repeat values when
  `mortality_tier != "none"` (which is set on recovery and carries across
  combats, so it is exactly the flag such a rule would want).

Implementing A when B is true is a regression, and vice versa. No shipped file
says which, the wiki's Death's Door page does not address it, and **the capture
corpus contains no Death's Door at all** — 360 decodable raid snapshots, zero
heroes at 0 HP, zero with `visited_deaths_door` set. So it stays open.

*Falsifier, and it is cheap*: `-startatbeforedeathsdoor -alwayshit` forces the
state on demand. Read the hero's stress out of `persist.raid.json` across two
consecutive hits taken at Death's Door — a rise of 4 on the second hit is A, no
rise is B.

## The party fought the opening round naked, then got healed for it (2026-08-21)

Found while wiring surprise, and wider than surprise. Both expedition envs
called `engine.reset(...)` and only then
`_apply_persistent_equipment_for_current_roster` — but `reset()` at its default
`auto_resolve_to_hero=True` has **already walked the initiative queue and let
every faster enemy act**. So the opening blows of a combat landed on heroes
with no gear dodge, no trinket PROT and the base max-HP ceiling.

**Then the second bug, which is the same two lines pointing the other way.** On
the real-party path the HP target for a fresh party is "full", expressed as
`10**6` for the max-HP clamp to cap. Writing HP *after* the opening round
therefore **healed the party back to full and erased it**. A monster that won
initiative did nothing at all.

Measured on a four-hero rank-4 party over 200 real-party resets: **2.79 HP per
combat refunded, in 34% of combats.** Over a 15-combat expedition that is
roughly one hero's health bar of free healing — and it is not spread evenly, it
accrues to exactly the parties that lose initiative, which are the ones the
number is supposed to punish.

That path is `bridge/rollout.py`, so it is also what `bridge/embark.py` valued
parties with. Every candidate looked tougher than it is, and the fastest
monsters looked the most harmless.

### The fix is a window, not a reordering

`CombatEngine.reset(on_board_ready=...)` — a callback invoked once the board is
built and before anything else happens to it: before the torch modifiers,
before the surprise roll, before initiative, before any turn. That is the only
moment where "what the hero is carrying" can be applied without either
rebuilding a state or retconning a turn.

`DarkestDungeonEnv._on_board_ready` is a documented no-op; `ExpeditionEnv`
overrides it to apply the sampled comp's trinkets and quirks, and
`ExpeditionSequenceEnv` to apply gear ranks, trinkets, quirks and the HP
ceiling. Both resolve what to apply *before* calling `reset()` and stash it
(`_pending_kit`, `_pending_hp_targets`), cleared in a `finally` so a hook can
never fire with a stale party's kit.

### Three things this switched on that were dead

- **Round-1 defence is real.** Gear dodge, trinket PROT and the raised max-HP
  ceiling are all present for the opening blows.
- **A +SPD trinket now affects the round it is bought for.** Initiative is
  computed inside `reset()`, so a speed buff applied afterwards could not
  influence the opening order.
- **`party_surprise_chance` / `monsters_surprise_chance` reach the roll.** They
  were wired the day before and documented as unreachable for exactly this
  reason. Verified: `book_of_intuition` takes the party's radiant surprise
  chance from 10% to 0%.

`tests/bridge/test_rollout.py` — the existing "opens at full" test now takes its
reading through the hook, which is where that claim is about equipment rather
than about how the opening round went, plus a new test that the opening blows
are not undone.

## A retreat was a paid skip, and fleeing the last fight paid the completion bonus (2026-08-21)

Fran, having read the surprise work: *"a retreat doesn't really mean that you
skip the combat, as the combat is still on the map"*. He listed the three real
options a fled party has — abandon the dungeon (+20 stress on top of the
retreat's own, and the quest is failed), re-route to a different room where the
region's layout allows it, or re-take the fight having paid the stress.

The simulator modelled none of them. `ExpeditionSequenceEnv` advanced
`_combat_idx` on a retreat exactly as it does on a win, so **the encounter was
consumed**: 20 stress and a forgone +8 bought the party out of the fight
permanently. The wiki's Retreating page is explicit that this is not the game —
the party is left in the hallway it entered from and must backtrack, and a
wandering boss *"will reappear if heroes re-enter the same hallway where they
retreated"*.

**And the second-order bug is worse than the first.** A retreat sets
`terminated`. With `_combat_idx` advanced past the end, retreating from the
LAST combat fell straight through to `terminated and not party_wiped` and
collected **`REWARD_EXPEDITION_COMPLETE`** — the full bonus for walking out of
the final fight. `old-succ` has always warned that "fleeing every fight would
score 100%"; this was the reward function agreeing with it.

### What is modelled

The third option: the party faces the same room again. The other two are
**dungeon-layer decisions and this env has no map** — the same reason traversal
and curios are out of scope, and `bridge/route.py` is where re-routing already
lives. Re-taking is also the one Fran singles out as the reason to flee at all,
*"specially after being surprised"*, because it buys back the scramble and the
lost opening round.

Three rules, each one a way to get it wrong:

- **The same encounter, not a fresh draw.** `_start_next_combat(entry=, torch=)`
  reuses `self._current_entry`. `_draw_encounter` consumes `_sampler_rng`, so
  simply not advancing the index would have sent the party back into the room
  it fled and found different monsters in it.
- **No food and no camp.** `_snapshot_and_advance_roster(completed=False)`. The
  meal is what a party eats having *cleared* a room, and the camp still owes
  itself to whoever finishes this fight (`_combat_idx` has not moved, so it
  fires on the eventual win). Either one would have made fleeing a way to BUY
  recovery — the exact opposite of its price.
- **A retreat can no longer terminate the expedition**, so it cannot collect
  the completion bonus.

The price of leaving is otherwise unchanged and still nothing invented:
`_reward_retreat` (0.0) in place of `self._reward_win` (+8), plus
`combat_retreat_stress` per living hero through the existing dense term. What
changed is that the fight is still there afterwards.

**Note the two stress numbers disagree and the game file wins.** `rules.json`
ships `combat_retreat_stress: 20.0`; the wiki says 25. That gap is
long-standing and already recorded — it is the same disagreement that confirmed
the value is a stress *amount* rather than a chance.

`tests/env/test_retreat_action.py`, four new tests behind one `_flee` helper that
also documents the three traps in setting a retreat up: `reset(seed=)` does not
seed `_sampler_rng`, a surprised party may not retreat at all, and the action
mask is served from a cache.

## The dark was never frightening: surprise (2026-08-21)

Surprise was unimplemented, and `ASSUMPTIONS.md` had excluded it for years as a
*"dungeon exploration mechanic with no analog in this single-encounter combat
simulator"*. That was true when it was written. It stopped being true on
2026-08-20, when `TORCH_LEVEL_DECILES` started drawing a **real torch per
combat** instead of pinning it at 100 -- because the darkness table's entire
point is that the dark surprises **you**, and surprise was the one column of it
nobody read.

So the policy has been training on a torch it can see and a consequence it
cannot. Low torch read as a mild stat swing: a bit more enemy ACC, a bit more
stress. The thing that actually kills a party in the dark -- losing the first
round with your formation shuffled -- did not exist.

**How much of the game this is.** Measured over 400 expeditions on the real
training torch distribution:

| | share of combats |
|---|---|
| party surprised | **16.8%** |
| monsters surprised | **27.8%** |
| someone surprised | **40.5%** |

Two combats in five now open differently. That is not a margin.

### The chance

`base + darkness band`, clamped to a shipped cap. Every term is data:

```
surprise_room_party_base_chance      0.1     rules.json
heroes_surprised_increase            0 .. 40 rules.json darkness.range_table
surprise_max_party_surprised_chance  0.65    rules.json
```

The band column is percentage points and the base is a fraction, so it is `/100`
like every other band column -- the unit mismatch that would otherwise read
`25.0` as 2500%. Radiant gives party 10% / monsters 35%; black as pitch gives
party 50% / monsters 10%. **The sign is the mechanic**, and it is what makes the
torch a decision rather than a stat.

Two independent draws, both always taken. Independence is the save format's own
reading -- `persist.raid.json` carries `party_surprised` and `monsters_surprised`
as two separate booleans, and rules.json gives each side its own base and its
own cap -- and taking both draws unconditionally is what keeps the RNG stream
from forking on the first outcome.

### Three effects, all from the wiki's Surprise page

- **The surprised side acts last in the opening round.** A sort term in
  `_compute_turn_order`, gated on `self.round == 0`. It sits ahead of the stun
  term only because the two cannot co-occur -- nothing is stunned before the
  first turn is taken -- which leaves the pre-surprise ordering byte-identical
  for every later round.
- **A surprised party is scrambled.** The expensive half: a Vestal shoved into
  rank 1 has no legal heal. Implemented with the same invariant a push or a pull
  keeps -- the **set** of occupied ranks cannot change, so a scramble can neither
  stack two heroes nor open a hole.
- **A surprised party cannot retreat that round.** A legality gate in
  `get_valid_actions`, not a penalty in `attempt_retreat`, because the wiki
  states it as a property of surprise rather than of the retreat roll.

### `-1.0` is a hard never, and the sign is load-bearing

A scouted room ships `surprise_known_room_party_base_chance: -1.0`, not `0.0`.
It has to be negative because the darkness table **adds** up to `+0.40` on top:
a base of zero would let the dark surprise a party in a room it had already
scouted. Scouting is not modelled here, so the `known_` bases are parsed and
unused -- but the datum is pinned by a test so the reasoning survives whoever
adds a map layer. It goes the other way for the monsters: scouting is what makes
*them* easier to catch, 0.1 -> 0.25.

Corridor versus room costs this simulator nothing: all four unscouted bases are
0.1. A test fails if a patch ever splits them.

### Two of the twelve keys are dead data

`rules.json` ships twelve surprise keys. Ten appear in `Darkest.exe` exactly
once each, at a **contiguous run of parse sites 0x80 apart in the file's own
order** (`0x1404ea8b2` .. `0x1404ead31`). `surprise_ambush_party_base_chance`
(1.0) and `surprise_ambush_monsters_base_chance` (0.0) are absent from the image
entirely -- and there is no gap in the run for them to hide in.

That clears the bar `tools/pe_anchors.py` sets for a negative result: absence
only counts for a key the shipped data actually contains, and rules.json
contains both. So an ambush does not *roll* for surprise, it **sets** it, which
is what the wiki means by a Shambler/Fanatic/camping ambush being unavoidable.
`data/rules.py` parses the ten and deliberately not the two. `DECOMP.md` F7.

### What it did to the tests, and what that exposed

Eight failures, and only two were the mechanic doing its job.

- **`bridge/seed.py` re-rolled surprise over a live board.** The engine
  scrambled the party's ranks on top of the formation being copied from a real
  save. Fixed by passing the flags the save already carries --
  `live_state.py` has parsed `party_surprised`/`monsters_surprised` since it was
  written and nothing had ever read them. Same class of rule as
  `auto_resolve_to_hero=False`, and for the same reason: the save knows, so do
  not invent.
- **`test_hero_level` and `test_fires_edge` pinned who acts first.** The hero
  level test even documents choosing a Jester *specifically* because SPD 9
  outruns any enemy -- a surprised party acts last, which hands the brawler the
  opening turn that being fast was supposed to deny it. Both now say
  `surprise=(False, False)` out loud.
- **`test_expedition_reward` was passing by luck.** It compared a reward delta
  against a stress fraction read **before** the step, and `env.step(0)` is a
  real attack whose crit relieves stress. It only ever agreed while the opening
  turn happened not to change any; reordering that turn exposed it. Now measured
  after the step, which is where the penalty is actually charged.
- **`test_retreat_action` was order-dependent and nobody knew.** `reset(seed=4)`
  seeds the combat RNG but *not* `_sampler_rng`, so which scenario the test lands
  on depends on how many other tests in the file ran first -- the trap `CLAUDE.md`
  documents, biting inside the suite. It also used a `_Rigged` stream that forces
  only the **next** `random()`, which silently stopped working the moment
  anything else on the turn drew first. Replaced with
  `retreat_attempts = 6`, which takes the chance to a capped 1.0 and needs no
  rigging at all.

And one that is worth more than the mechanic: `env.action_masks()` is served
from a cache built by `_rebuild_action_table`, so mutating engine state after
`reset()` does not change the mask. Two tests were asserting on a stale mask.

### What it does not do, stated rather than hidden

`party_surprise_chance` (9 buffs) and `monsters_surprise_chance` (19) are real
stat types with two reachable trinkets -- `book_of_intuition` (-20% party) and
`ancestors_lantern`. The branches are wired and correct, and **they never fire
in the sampled paths**, because both expedition envs call
`_apply_persistent_equipment_for_current_roster` *after* `reset()` has already
rolled.

That ordering is a **separate and wider bug**: `reset()` with the default
`auto_resolve_to_hero=True` walks the initiative queue, so the opening blows of
every combat land on heroes whose gear rank and trinkets have not been applied
yet -- no gear dodge, no trinket PROT, base max HP. Filed in `ASSUMPTIONS.md` as
its own item rather than worked around here, because the fix is an ordering
change and deserves to have its own effect measured.

**Falsifier for the mechanic itself**: dark combats should now cost more than
the stat deltas alone predict, and a party that opens surprised should retreat
later and win less. Nothing anchors this yet -- no eval predates it.
`tests/engine/test_surprise.py`, 19 tests.

## Nothing could summon, including half the bosses (2026-08-21)

Fran asked for a systematic audit rather than more spot fixes, so the three
movement bugs got turned into a search: **a field parsed from real data that
the engine never reads.** `SkillLevelData.push` had been exactly that. Diffing
every dataclass field in `data/types.py` against every attribute access in
`engine/`, `env/`, `bridge/` and `tools/` returned 56 candidates.

**Most were false positives, and the reason is worth recording.**
`INLINE_STAT_FIELD_MAP` reads `speed_add`, `defense_rating_add`,
`max_hp_percent` and others by *string name* in a loop, which an
attribute-access regex cannot see. Re-running the scan against string literals
too dropped it to 50, and filtering to fields the shipped data actually
populates left a handful. Two more dissolved on inspection:
`affliction_base_onset_chance` is unread because `stress.py` deliberately rolls
its complement, and `extra_damage_vs_*` is zero across all 1252 skill levels
because that mechanic goes through buffs.

One survived, and it is large.

### `summon_count` was parsed and never read

54 monster skills across **35 monsters** resolved to nothing. The spawner
already existed — `_spawn_reinforcements` places monsters from
`summon_monster_ids` into open ranks — it was simply unreachable from a skill.

Who that covers is the point:

| where | monsters |
|---|---|
| normal hall/room encounters | ectoplasm (all sizes and tiers — splitting is its whole mechanic), skeleton_bearer, shambler |
| **boss encounters** | **Necromancer, Drowned Captain, Brigand Cannon, Siren** (all variants), plus ancestor and shuffler |

**Four of the eight region bosses `boss_quest_p` now fields could not summon**,
and summoning is the shape of those fights rather than a flourish. This landed
days after boss quests did, which is the argument for having audited now.

### The semantics needed three effects to pin

No two of these are consistent under a simple reading:

    NecroSummon 2      count 1, three candidates, chances 1.0 3.0 1.0
    SummonTentacle A   count 2, ONE candidate
    stall_summon_A     count 2, two candidates, + .summon_erase_data_on_roll

- The chances are **weights**, not probabilities. `NecroSummon 2` asks for one
  skeleton, militia-weighted. Rolling each candidate independently spawns all
  three, because two of the weights exceed 1.0 and can never fail a
  `random() < chance` test — which is what the old reinforcement code did.
- Draws are **with replacement**: `SummonTentacle A` is count 2 over a single
  candidate, so the Shambler must be able to draw the same tentacle twice.
- Except where **`.summon_erase_data_on_roll`** says otherwise. Exactly three
  effects carry it and all three are the `stall_summon_*` waves — which is
  precisely why "one of each" was right for reinforcements and wrong as a
  general rule. Parsing that flag let both behaviours come from one code path
  and left the existing reinforcement test green, unchanged.

`.summon_limits` (how many of that monster may live at once) and
`.summon_ranks` (preferred rank, 1-indexed) are now parsed too. Board space is
the hard cap; a summon with nowhere to stand does not appear.

### One test moved, for a good reason

`test_ally_class_target_prioritizes_matching_class` unpacked
`engine.enemies` into exactly three names. The **Collector now genuinely
summons during `reset()`'s auto-resolve**, so the board holds four before the
first hero turn. Given the test is about ally-target selection, it takes
`auto_resolve_to_hero=False` rather than being taught to expect the summon.

### Still unread, and deliberately left

`can_apply_on_death` (321 effects), `is_queued` (969), `stun_ends_turn`,
`initiative_base`, and the darkness bands' `heroes_surprised_increase` /
`monsters_surprised_increase` — **surprise is not modelled at all**, which is
the largest remaining hole and is now the top entry in `ASSUMPTIONS.md`.

## A displacement is a rotation, not a teleport (2026-08-21)

The last of the three movement bugs, and the one `CLAUDE.md` had carried as a
known gap: push, pull and skill self-movement each wrote an **absolute
position** and displaced nobody. A 2-rank push on rank 3 of a full party gave
`[0, 0, 1, 3]` — two combatants stacked on one rank and a hole behind them.
Neither is a state the game can be in.

Fran's rule, and then his correction of my first attempt at it:

> *"if you pull the position 4 to position 2, former-position 2 will go into 3
> and former-position 3 will go into 4, it never leaves a gap nor has 2 enemies
> on the same position"*
>
> *"a single enemy can't be pushed as there isn't enough positions to rotate, a
> displacement always keeps the gaps"*
>
> *"there can't be a gap on rank 3, as if there's a gap, there needs to stay at
> rank 4 exclusively"*

That third correction is what made the model click. Because gaps **only ever
sit at the back**, the occupied ranks of any legal formation are contiguous
from rank 1 — so a displacement is a **permutation of the occupants over the
ranks they already hold**, and nothing else.

`CombatEngine._shift_to_position` implements exactly that, and all three
movement paths now go through it. Three consequences fall straight out, each of
which I had wrong in an intermediate version:

- **The set of occupied ranks never changes.** A shove can neither open a hole
  nor close one. My first attempt rebuilt the line contiguously from rank 1,
  which silently *closed* gaps as a side effect of every push — smuggling
  `_collapse_enemy_position_gaps`' rule into a different mechanic.
- **A lone combatant cannot be pushed, and neither can the rearmost one.**
  There is nothing to rotate with. My second attempt was an arithmetic slide,
  which let empty space absorb the movement and parked a lone monster on rank 3
  — the case that distinguishes a rotation from a slide.
- **A rotation that would hang a large monster off the back is refused.** The
  slots come from the board as it stands, so swapping a size-3 with a size-1
  behind it would put the big one where it does not fit. Nothing moving is the
  right answer; the real game has no room for that swap either.

### Tests that were asserting the old behaviour

Three, all of which pushed a **single** enemy — which cannot move now:

- `test_push_moves_the_target_toward_the_back` (written hours earlier, in the
  direction fix) now uses four enemies.
- `test_push_and_pull_roll_against_move_resist` targeted `enemies[2]`, the
  rearmost of three; it targets the front one instead.
- `test_a_large_monster_cannot_be_moved_off_the_back_of_the_board` asserted a
  specific landing rank. Rewritten to assert the **invariant** it exists to
  guarantee — never overhangs — plus a new case with a small monster behind the
  large one, where a naive swap would overhang.

`tests/engine/test_skill_movement.py` now also fuzzes it: 60 boards × 8 random shoves,
asserting no stack and no hole survives any sequence.

## Lunge did nothing, and push went the wrong way (2026-08-21)

Two independent sign errors in combat movement, found because Fran said *"lunge
from grave robber makes her go forward to the front"* and it did not.

Both matter for the same reason the encounter-category and gear fixes did: they
are what the policy learns positioning from, and both were teaching it the
opposite of the game.

### 1. Every forward self-move in the game was discarded

A combat skill's `.move <backward> <forward>` describes the **performer**, both
numbers. `hero_loader` read `parts[1]` as a push on the *target* and stored it
in `SkillLevelData.push` — a field **the engine never reads**. So the forward
half was parsed into a black hole.

Four skills settle the format between them, because they disagree in direction
and each is unambiguous in the real game:

| skill | `.move` | `.launch` | real behaviour |
|---|---|---|---|
| `shadow_fade` | `2 0` | 2,1 | Grave Robber retreats 2 back |
| `holy_lance` | `0 1` | 3,4 | Crusader charges 1 forward |
| `lunge` | `0 2` | 4,3 | Grave Robber leaps 2 forward |
| `duelist_advance` | `0 1` | 4,3,2 | Highwayman advances 1 forward |

**55 of the 90 hero combat skills carrying a `.move` are forward-moving** (40x
`0 1`, 10x `0 2`, 5x `0 3`) and not one of them moved anybody. The 35 backward
ones worked by accident, because `parts[0]` was kept and `_apply_movement`
already adds it with the right sign.

Now `move_self = backward - forward`, net, positive meaning toward the rear.
That netting is only safe because **a `combat_skill:` never carries both
numbers non-zero** — only `combat_move_skill:` does, where they are a *range*
for the Move action, and that is parsed on a different path. Verified across
every shipped hero file. Monsters never use `.move` at all.

### 2. Push and pull were inverted

`_resolve_effect` moved a pushed target toward rank 1 and a pulled target
toward rank 4. The game's data settles it without appealing to what the English
words suggest, because two skills constrain each other:

    Daemon's Pull   .target 34   applies "Pull 2A"
    Rampart         .target 12   applies "Push 1A"

A pull is only *legal* against ranks 3-4, so it must drag the target **forward**;
a push is only legal against ranks 1-2, so it must shove it **back**. With the
signs reversed, each skill is a no-op on half its legal targets — Daemon's Pull
would try to push a rank-4 enemy further back, and Rampart would try to drag a
rank-1 enemy further forward.

This ran across **46 of 246 monsters** — cultist witches and brigands among them,
so it fired constantly — plus every hero skill that shoves or drags.

`tests/engine/test_gaps.py::test_push_2_positions` encoded the bug: it put a target on
rank 4, pushed, and asserted rank 2. Rewritten as
`test_push_moves_the_target_toward_the_back`, with the rank-4 case kept as the
one that *cannot* move.

### Still open, and untouched here

Push and pull still **write an absolute position and displace nobody**, so two
combatants can share a rank and a hole can open behind them. That is the
long-standing gap `CLAUDE.md` already records; fixing the direction does not
fix it, and it is a separate change with its own evidence requirements.

## Run 2: adding kit and boss randomisation bought nothing measurable (2026-08-20)

Second 6M run, `--loadout-random-p 0.4 --cross-region-p 1.0 --resolve-sampling
--kit-random-p 0.5 --boss-quest-p 0.15`, i.e. run 1's config plus the two knobs
that landed after it launched. Peak eval 84.17 against run 1's 102.28, but on a
harder distribution so the two are not comparable.

Run 2 vs run 1, 108 paired scenarios each (`--episodes 3 --comps 12`):

| eval distribution | run 2 - run 1 (SUCCESS) | discordant | verdict |
|---|---|---|---|
| randomised, full bundle (run 2's own config) | +3.7pp [-4.6, +12.0] | 13 / 9 | not distinguishable |
| **boss quests only** (`--eval-boss-p 1.0`) | **-5.6pp** [-14.8, +3.7] | 10 / 16 | not distinguishable, sign NEGATIVE |
| `eval_tutorial` (fixed scenario) | -6.0pp | — | worse |
| `agreement` (101 real decisions) | **+7.9pp** (42.6% -> 50.5%) | 19 / 27 | p=0.302 |

### The boss result is the one that matters, and it is negative

`distribution_sensitivity.py` had measured `boss` as the only significant knob:
a policy that had never seen one lost **-18.75pp** (p=0.0117) when the last room
held a boss. That was the argument for training against it.

**Training against it did not work.** On a test bed of 100% boss quests, run 2 —
which trained with `boss_quest_p=0.15` for 6M steps — is *not* better than run 1,
which never saw a boss at all. The point estimate is negative and the discordant
pairs lean the wrong way, 10 to 16.

The likeliest reason is signal starvation: at 15% of expeditions, and only the
LAST combat of those, a boss is roughly **1.5% of all combats trained on**.
Six million steps of that is not much boss. The honest options are a far higher
rate, a dedicated boss mode of the kind `--tutorial` already is, or accepting
that bosses need per-boss handling rather than more exposure — which is what
Fran said in the first place: *"you shouldn't approach each boss equally"*.

**Do not read this as "bosses are unlearnable".** It says one specific dosage
did not work.

### Agreement is the highest this project has recorded

50.5% on the captured corpus, against 47.5% for the best archived pre-burn
checkpoint and 42.6% for run 1. Not significant (p=0.302, 101 decisions), and
agreement is not correctness — a DIFFER can be the policy outplaying the human.
But it is the ruler with no simulator in it, and it is the only one that has
moved consistently upward across today's three checkpoints (38.6% -> 42.6% ->
50.5%).

That is worth weighing against the simulated rulers, which have now disagreed
with each other three times in one day.

### Where the day leaves the checkpoint question

Nothing measured today distinguishes run 1 from run 2 on any simulated eval.
The tie-breakers point in opposite directions: `agreement` favours run 2 by
7.9pp, `eval_tutorial` favours run 1 by 6.0pp. Run 2 is the live checkpoint
because it trained on the most complete distribution, not because it was shown
to be better.

## Which knob actually matters: the boss, by a distance (2026-08-20)

`tools/distribution_sensitivity.py` run per knob against the **pre-distfix**
checkpoint — the policy that trained on none of them — 48 paired scenarios each
(`--episodes 2 --comps 8 --comp-seed 1`). Arm A is the curated control; arm B
changes exactly one thing.

| knob | SUCCESS | delta | 95% CI | McNemar p |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **boss** | 56.25 -> 37.50 | **-18.75pp** | [-31.25, -6.25] | **0.0117** |
| kit | 56.25 -> 47.92 | -8.33pp | [-20.83, +4.17] | 0.34 |
| loadout | 56.25 -> 50.00 | -6.25pp | [-18.75, +6.25] | 0.51 |
| resolve | 56.25 -> 50.00 | -6.25pp | [-16.67, +2.08] | 0.38 |
| **region** | 56.25 -> 68.75 | **+12.50pp** | [+2.08, +25.00] | 0.07 |

**Only `boss` is significant**, and it is also the largest. A policy that has
never seen a boss loses nearly 19pp when the last room holds one — which is the
argument for having built boss quests at all, and says that of the five, it is
the shift most worth training against.

**`region` is POSITIVE, and that is the surprise.** Taking a comp out of its
tagged Location makes it do *better*, not worse. The likely reason is not that
the policy enjoys being off-distribution but that **the Ruins are harder than
average and 78 of 145 comps are tagged there** — so "the comp's own region" was
a systematically harder test bed than a free draw. It is a reminder that this
knob changes the *dungeon*, so its delta mixes "off-distribution" with "this
content is easier", and the tool says so in its own output.

`boss` carries the same caveat in the other direction: some of the -18.75pp is
simply that bosses are harder than corridor monsters, not purely that the
policy is unprepared. Both readings justify training against it; neither is
cleanly separable with this design.

**Everything except `boss` is a screening result, not a ranking.** n=48 gives
intervals 20-30pp wide, so kit / loadout / resolve are indistinguishable from
each other and from zero. What the table supports is "boss is the big one" and
"region is not a cost"; it does not support ordering the other three.

Note the loadout figure (-6.25pp) is *smaller* than the -16.7pp measured
earlier the same day. Different checkpoint, different engine (the corpse, gear
and encounter-category fixes all landed in between) and a third the scenarios —
so the two are not comparable, and neither supersedes the other.

### `ab_compare.py --eval-randomised`

A gap this exposed: `play()` drives every scenario through explicit reset
options, which is what makes two checkpoints face an identical dungeon — and
also means the env's randomisation knobs never fire. So every comparison ran on
the **curated** distribution, including comparisons between two checkpoints
that had both been *trained* on the randomised one. That reads a
robustness-trained policy's cost and none of its benefit.

`--eval-randomised` generates the variation once per scenario and hands the
identical dict to both arms, so the pairing is unchanged and only the test bed
moves. `--self-check` passes on that path too.

## The distribution fixes are worth +13pp on the CURATED eval only (2026-08-20)

**Retitled and corrected below** — the headline first read "worth +13pp, and cost
20pp of specialisation", which overstated it. The gain is real on the curated
distribution and does NOT reproduce on the randomised one the model was trained
for. See the correction section.

The first retrain in this project's history that could be measured **against
its predecessor**, because the fixes were pure distribution changes and did not
widen the observation. 6,012,928 steps in 1:17:35 at `--n-envs 8 --seed 0
--loadout-random-p 0.4 --cross-region-p 1.0 --resolve-sampling`, carrying the
corrected encounter categories, gear gating and corpse duration.

### Three rulers, and this time the disagreement is predicted

| ruler | what it measures | result |
|---|---|---|
| `ab_compare.py` (216 paired sampled expeditions) | breadth | **new +13.0pp SUCCESS**, CI [+6.9, +19.0], p=0.0001 |
| `agreement.py` (101 real captured decisions) | fidelity to human play | **new +4.0pp**, CI [-7.9, +15.8], p=0.618 |
| `eval_tutorial.py` (one fixed scenario) | depth on ONE party | **new -20.0pp** (90.0% -> 70.0%) |

**+13.0pp is the largest clean improvement this project has ever recorded**, on
the headline metric, with a tight interval and 38 discordant wins against 10.
Every prior fidelity fix was smaller than the eval's own noise.

And the third ruler disagreeing is not a contradiction — it is the thing that
was bought, showing up as the thing that was paid. The Old Road is the most
*specialised* scenario in the project: one fixed 2-hero party, one fixed
loadout, two fixed fights. A policy trained at `loadout_random_p=0.4`
deliberately stops over-fitting to any particular four skills, so it should get
worse at exactly that and better across the sampled pool. It did both.

Read the two together and the shape is consistent: on sampled expeditions the
new policy **retreats less** (1.16 -> 0.89) and **completes more** (58.3% ->
72.7%), at the cost of **more deaths** (0.26 -> 0.40) and lower `zero_death`
(87.0% -> 80.6%). It fights where the old one left. SUCCESS requires completing
everything *and* zero deaths, so a +13pp gain there despite rising deaths means
the completion gain is large.

### Correction, same day: the +13pp is a CURATED-distribution result only

Measured after the entry above was written, with `ab_compare --eval-randomised`
(108 paired scenarios, `--episodes 3 --comps 12 --comp-seed 1`):

| eval distribution | new - old (SUCCESS) | verdict |
|---|---|---|
| curated (n=216) | **+13.0pp**, p=0.0001 | distinguishable |
| randomised, **run 1's own training config** (loadout+region+resolve) | +1.9pp, CI [-7.4, +12.0] | not distinguishable |
| randomised, full bundle (also kit + boss) | +0.9pp, CI [-8.3, +11.1] | not distinguishable |

**The gain does not reproduce on the distribution the model was trained for.**
That is the opposite of what the robustness argument predicts, and it is the
single most important thing on this page.

The likeliest reading is that **the +13pp is the engine fixes, not the
randomisation knobs**. The old checkpoint trained against bosses in corridors,
stall waves as encounters, and apprentices a full gear rank short; the new one
trained against none of those. Both are *scored* on the corrected engine, so a
model that trained on the corrected distribution should win there — and does.
The randomisation knobs, which are what the "robustness" story was about, show
no measurable benefit yet.

Second reading, not excluded: 108 scenarios gives a +-10pp interval, so a real
effect of a few points would be invisible. But the point estimates are +1-2pp
rather than +13pp, so this is not only a power problem.

Also worth noting: randomised distributions are much harder for **both** arms —
absolute SUCCESS falls from ~58-71% curated to ~22-46% randomised. The knobs
are not cosmetic; they just have not yet been shown to be something training
against helps with.

**Do not quote the +13pp without this qualification.** It is real and it is
significant; it is also specific to the curated test bed, and the natural
sentence "the distribution fixes made the policy 13pp better" is not what was
measured.

### What this does not establish

**The step count is confounded.** Run 1 is 6M against the baseline's 3M. This
project's own evidence argues the confound is small — the archived 6M run
peaked at 1.07M steps and ended lower — but it is not controlled, and the clean
version is a 3M arm on the same engine.

**Which of the three knobs did it** is unknown. They landed together, which is
the attribution mistake already made twice here. `tools/distribution_sensitivity.py`
exists to answer that per knob against a fixed policy, and had not been run on
the individual knobs when this was written.

### The eval curve is still noise with an envelope

Peak 102.28, final 76.12, adjacent evals swinging 7.69 -> 80.62 -> 76.12. Nothing
about a longer run changes the standing advice to pick on a paired eval rather
than on which save SB3 called best.

## The boss is the last room, or there is no boss (2026-08-20)

Removing `boss` from `ENEMY_CATEGORIES_FOR_TRAINING` earlier today fixed a
Siren turning up in a corridor at 3-6% of every draw, and created the opposite
problem: the policy then never saw a boss in its life. That matters the moment
the bridge walks into a real boss room — it would be fully off-distribution
against the one fight that ends a quest.

This is the structural version Fran asked for, and the one the earlier entry
said was the right fix rather than a filter. An expedition is **either** a boss
quest, whose FINAL combat is that region's boss, **or** it is an ordinary one.
`ExpeditionEnv(boss_quest_p=)` / `train.py --boss-quest-p`.

**Last, specifically.** A static boss spawns in the room furthest from the
entrance, so the party arrives having already spent the dungeon's attrition on
it. A boss placed anywhere else is a different fight.

### The rosters are the game's, not a curated list

Nothing here names a boss. The pool is the mash files' own `boss:` rows, and
what falls out is exactly the two static bosses per core region that the game
ships — which is a good check on the whole approach:

| region | bosses |
|---|---|
| crypts | Necromancer, Prophet (+ 3 pews) |
| cove | Siren, Drowned Captain |
| warrens | Swine Prince (+ piglet), Formless Flesh (4 parts) |
| weald | Hag (+ cauldron), Brigand Pounder (cannon crew), Nest (+ crow) |

They sit on **tiers 1/3/5** — the real Apprentice/Veteran/Champion quest
difficulties. Tiers 2 and 4 carry only a stray Necromancer row in every region,
so a boss quest snaps its tier to the bracket's canonical odd value
(`BOSS_QUEST_TIER_FOR`) rather than fielding the Necromancer half the time
regardless of where the party is.

### Which stream it comes from, and why this one is different

The four randomisation knobs (loadout, region, resolve, kit) all draw from
`_loadout_rng` so they cannot perturb the enemy sequence. **Boss quests draw
from `_sampler_rng` instead, deliberately.** Whether a quest is a boss quest,
and which boss it is, are properties of the *scenario* in exactly the way the
monsters and the torch are — not of the party. So they belong to the stream a
reseeded scenario reproduces, and `Rollout.evaluate` comparing two parties on
"the same dungeon" now means the same dungeon including its boss.

`options["boss_quest"]` overrides the roll, because a replayed capture knows
what it was and must not have it re-rolled underneath.

### Not modelled

The four **roaming** bosses — Shambler, Collector, Fanatic, Thing from the
Stars. They spawn on their own conditions mid-expedition rather than occupying
the last room, and live in the `battle`/`quest` award categories. They need a
spawn trigger, not a slot in the schedule, so they are a separate piece of work
rather than a variation on this one.

Default is 0.0, which is the state every checkpoint so far trained in.

## The other two thirds of a hero's kit (2026-08-20)

Skills were randomisable; trinkets and quirks were not. Fran's spec put all
three together — *"Random: Selected skills (for non stance heroes), Trinkets
(Between the available ones for them, as the trinkets can be class specific or
generic), Quirks"* — so `ExpeditionEnv(kit_random_p=)` / `train.py
--kit-random-p` now fields a hero with a random legal trinket pair and quirk
sheet instead of its comp's.

### The rarity filter is the load-bearing part

**294 of the 615 loaded trinkets are `kickstarter` rarity.** Unfiltered, a
"random trinket" would be close to a coin flip on an item no dungeon awards.

The game states which ones drop, and it is not a judgement call:
`trinkets/base.rarities.trinkets.json` gives every rarity an `award_category`,
and `universal` is the ordinary drop pool — `ancestral`, `very_rare`, `rare`,
`uncommon`, `common`, `very_common`. The others are `kickstarter` (its own
category), `trophy` (boss kills), `dd` (Darkest Dungeon runs), and
`battle`/`quest` (Shambler, Collector, Courtier, Madman, Crow).

That leaves 213, of which 114 are generic — about 120 legal choices per hero
once the class filter is applied. `tests/env/test_random_kit.py` re-reads the
rarities file, so a DLC adding a universal rarity fails loudly rather than
being silently excluded.

Three of the 213 carry **no buffs at all** (`flickering_lamplight`, whose real
effect is torchlight, plus two Runaway mod items) and are dropped too: handing
one out teaches the policy from a hero wearing a no-op. Note `limit == 0` is
**not** a validity signal — it means "no limit", and 192 of 213 have it.

### Quirks are the bigger change

The engine has modelled quirks fully for a long time. The policy has never
trained against them: **516 of 534 scraped comp heroes carry zero positive
quirks and 525 carry zero negative.** The comp JSONs simply do not specify
them, so `_resolve_trinkets_and_quirks` was handing back empty lists nearly
every time.

`RANDOM_QUIRKS_PER_POLARITY = (1, 3)` is a **convention** and marked as one —
the real game caps a hero at five of each and gives recruits one or two, so 1-3
is a mid-campaign roster. No shipped file states a distribution to copy.

**`incompatible_quirks` is honoured in both directions.** The data lists each
pair from one side only — `tough` names `fragile`, and `fragile` does not name
`tough` — so a single-direction check produces a hero who is tough *and*
fragile: two buffs on one stat pulling opposite ways, a state the game cannot
reach. Diseases are excluded; they are contracted, not part of a standing quirk
sheet.

### Same invariants as the other three knobs

Default 0.0, proven byte-identical to the comp's own kit across every comp in
the pool. Drawn from `_loadout_rng` rather than `_sampler_rng`, so the monsters
and the torch are untouched and two arms of a comparison still face the same
dungeon. A pinned scenario reproduces its kit.

## A party is four heroes, not one hero four times (2026-08-20)

`TIER_TO_HERO_LEVEL` fielded every hero at the **top** of its difficulty
bracket — four resolve-2 heroes for an Apprentice dungeon, four resolve-6 for a
Champion one. That is a best case, not a party: a real Apprentice roster is a
mix of resolve 0-2.

`ExpeditionEnv(resolve_sampling=True)` / `train.py --resolve-sampling` draws
each hero's resolve **independently** from its bracket. Fran's weights, which
deliberately lean high ("lets favour the heroes a bit here"):

| bracket | mash tiers | resolve weights |
|---|---|---|
| Apprentice | 1-2 | 0 @ 20%, 1 @ 40%, 2 @ 40% |
| Veteran | 3-4 | 3 @ 40%, 4 @ 60% |
| Champion | 5 | 5 @ 40%, 6 @ 60% |

Measured over 16000 draws: 20/40/40, 40/60, 41/59.

These are a **training-distribution choice, not a game rule** — no shipped file
states how often a roster holds a given resolve — which is why they are pinned
in a test rather than derived from data, and why the knob defaults to off.

Three things this depends on, each of which would have been a quiet bug:

- **Per hero, not per party.** Drawing once and copying it four times would
  field clones and reproduce exactly the uniformity this replaces.
- **Sampled once per expedition and carried**, in
  `ExpeditionSequenceEnv._original_hero_levels`. Resolve is a property of the
  hero; redrawing it per combat would silently re-equip the party mid-dungeon,
  because gear rank is derived from resolve.
- **Drawn from `_loadout_rng`, not `_sampler_rng`** — the third knob to need
  this and for the same reason. `_sampler_rng` also picks the monsters and the
  torch, so spending draws here would shift the enemy sequence and make two arms
  of a comparison face different dungeons.

It composes with the gear fix landed alongside it: because rank now follows
resolve from the real upgrade gating, a resolve-0 hero is automatically fielded
at rank-1 gear. Mixed resolve therefore means mixed equipment, with nothing extra
said.

**Resolve 0 is safe with respect to the underleveled-hero penalty**, and this
was checked rather than assumed: that penalty (20 stress + 25% stress taken per
level short) fires on `quest_difficulty_level`, which the envs never pass, so it
stays `None` and contributes zero shortfall. A test pins it. If a caller ever
starts passing it, revisit — a resolve-0 hero on a difficulty-1 quest would then
open with 20 stress.

Writing that test also caught a trap worth recording: the first version asserted
`stress_dmg_received_mult == 1.0` after a bare `reset()` and got 1.1. That was
not the penalty — `reset()` auto-resolves enemy turns ahead of the first hero,
and the cultist brawler had already landed `BRAWLER_STRESSDMG_D1`. It reads
exactly like the thing being ruled out. `auto_resolve_to_hero=False` is the
documented way to look at an opening board nobody has acted on.

## Apprentice parties were a full gear rank under-equipped (2026-08-20)

Fran specified what a "fair battle" is: *"Apprentice heroes will have resolve
0-2, so armor should be on the same level ... a hero resolve 2 can reach lvl 3
armor, weapon and skills, lvl 1 will have 2-2-2 ... veteran ... level 3-4 so
they will both have lvl 4-4-4 ... champion ... resolve 5-6 and their equipement
will always be lvl 5-5-5."*

That is not a preference. It is **exactly what the game's own upgrade data
says**, and the data was sitting unread the whole time.

`upgrades/heroes/<class>.upgrades.json` gives each weapon and armour tree's
`prerequisite_resolve_level`. Across all 15 vanilla classes, both trees, the
thresholds are **identical**: `(1, 2, 3, 5)`. Rank 1 is free, so:

| resolve | 0 | 1 | 2 | 3 | 4 | 5 | 6 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| max rank | 1 | 2 | 3 | 4 | 4 | 5 | 5 |

`reset()` used `tier_idx = level - 1`, whose own comment conceded it was "a
deliberate simulator convention ... not a literal game rule" and even noted that
"the real weapon-tier-unlock table only gives a non-linear *minimum* resolve
level per tier — 0/1/2/3/5". The table was one file away.

The convention was **right for veterans and champions** (resolve 4 -> rank 4,
resolve 6 -> rank 5) and **wrong by a full rank for apprentices**: resolve 2
buys rank 3 and got rank 2. Since `TIER_TO_HERO_LEVEL` fields mash tiers 1-2 at
resolve 2, *every Apprentice-bracket combat this project has ever trained or
evaluated was fought under-equipped* — lower HP, dodge, PROT, damage, and a
lower `skill_tier`, which selects the skill's own per-level damage row.

Now `GEAR_RANK_RESOLVE_THRESHOLDS` + `max_gear_rank_for_resolve`, with
`tests/engine/test_gear_ranks.py` re-reading all 30 real trees so a DLC or a patch that
ships different gating fails loudly rather than silently disagreeing with one
hardcoded table.

### The desync this nearly shipped with

`apply_gear_ranks` corrects a hero to a save's REAL ranks by applying a **delta
against the tier `reset()` used** — and it computed that baseline with its own
copy of `resolve_level - 1`. Changing `reset()` without changing it inflated
every real-gear hero by the difference: the CHANGELOG's own resolve-2 rank-2
Vestal went from 34 to **39** max HP.

Caught by `tests/bridge/test_rollout.py`, which is the point of pinning that case. The
two formulas now share one function, and the comment says why they must.

That test then failed a second time for the opposite reason, which is worth
recording: its assertion `derived < real` broke because derivation now **agrees**
with the save for that hero. Agreement is the fix working. It has been restated
against gear resolve genuinely cannot produce (rank 4 at resolve 2), which is
what actually tests "the save is the authority".

### Confirmed already correct

Two of the four things in Fran's spec needed no change, and were checked rather
than assumed:

- **The enemy pool differs per zone AND per difficulty.** Sampling by tier
  gives crypts 14 distinct monsters at tier 1, 18 at tier 3, 21 at tier 5.
- **Variants track the difficulty.** Tier 1 draws are 100% `_A`, tier 3 100%
  `_B`, tier 5 `_C` with a few `_D` — so the harder dungeon fields the harder
  statline of the same monster, which is the mechanic. Mash tiers 1-2/3-4/5 map
  to Apprentice/Veteran/Champion, and `TIER_TO_HERO_LEVEL` already pairs the
  party to the bracket.

## An eighth of every training combat could not happen (2026-08-20)

Fran suspected the training data did not match the real game and asked for the
mash files to be checked against the wiki's per-region enemy lists. Three
things came out of that, and the first is the one that matters.

### The rosters are right; the CATEGORIES were not

The zone rosters check out. Sampling 6000 encounters per zone gives cove =
fishman/jellyfish/octotank, crypts = skeletons, warrens = swine/carrion_eater,
weald = fungal/ectoplasm/rabid_dog. The cross-region brigands and cultists that
look wrong at first glance are genuinely in the game's own base mash files
(`cove.1.mash.darkest` really does carry `hall: .chance 1 .types brigand_blood_A
...`), and `_sample_enemy` already weights by `.chance`, so they come out
correctly rare. No parsing error, no zone bleed.

`ENEMY_CATEGORIES_FOR_TRAINING` was the problem. It read
`{"hall", "room", "boss", "stall"}`, with a comment saying 'named' was excluded
"to keep the distribution representative of normal random encounters" -- while
including the two categories that are least like one:

    boss:  .chance 1 .types siren_A
    boss:  .chance 1 .types pew_small_B pew_medium_B pew_large_B prophet_B
    stall: .chance 2 .types fishman_harpoon_A fishman_harpoon_A

A `boss:` row is an actual boss, which the game presents only at the end of a
boss quest and never in a corridor. A `stall:` row is the **reinforcement wave**
the engine summons against a stalling party -- a thing that happens *to* you
mid-combat, not an encounter you walk into.

Measured share of every draw before the fix:

| zone | boss | stall |
|---|---|---|
| crypts | 3.3% | 8.8% |
| cove | 3.9% | 9.5% |
| warrens | 4.0% | 9.9% |
| weald | 6.1% | 9.2% |

**Roughly an eighth of all training combats were encounters that cannot
occur.** Bosses in particular are not merely unusual — they have several times
the HP of a corridor monster and mechanics the party cannot answer (the
Prophet's pews, the Hag's cauldron), so a policy meeting one at random in a
hallway learns from a fight the real game would never hand it.

Now `{"hall", "room"}`.

**Known gap, deliberately left open:** the policy now never trains against a
boss at all. The faithful model is that *some* expeditions are boss quests whose
**last** combat is the boss, which is a structural change to expedition
generation rather than a filter on a pool. Sprinkling bosses uniformly through
every expedition — what the old set did — is not a nearer approximation of that,
it is a different wrong thing.

### A comp only ever fought in its own region

`_sample_comp` handed the comp's tagged Location to every combat of the
expedition, so a Cove comp never once met a skeleton. Worse, the tags are not
balanced: **78 of 145 comps are tagged Ruins**, so measured over 3000 samples,
55% of all expeditions were in the Ruins and the other three zones split the
rest.

`ExpeditionEnv(cross_region_p=)` draws the region independently of the tag; at
1.0 the four core zones come out even (752/757/759/732). Fran's requirement:
*"the comps should work on several regions as well, not only ... the suggested
region of the composition."*

The draw comes from `_loadout_rng`, **not** `_sampler_rng`, for exactly the
reason the loadout draw does: `_sampler_rng` also picks the monsters and the
torch, so consuming from it here would shift the enemy sequence and make two
arms of a comparison face different dungeons. `tests/env/test_training_distribution.py`
pins that both arms still draw identical enemies and torch afterwards.

Default is 0.0 and byte-identical to the old behaviour.

### Nothing was guarding any of this

The whole sampler was carefully tested — chance weighting, tier restriction,
duplicate-class loadouts, reproducibility — and not one assertion described what
the distribution was *made of*. Both errors are distribution facts rather than
crashes, so nothing but a distributional assertion could have caught them.
`tests/env/test_training_distribution.py` is that, including a counterpart guard
that each zone is still dominated by its own faction — which is what says the
narrowing removed the wrong encounters and not the zone's real content.

### What this does NOT change

The observation width. Unlike the burn/Fire's Edge work, these are pure
distribution changes, so **existing checkpoints still load** and the before/after
question is answerable with `tools/ab_compare.py` for once.

## The retrain: three rulers, and only one of them is measuring what it says (2026-08-20)

The combined retrain — F4's corrected accuracy, randomised loadouts at p=0.4,
the two Fire's Edge classes, burn, and the four-round corpse — ran 3,014,656
steps in 38 minutes at `--n-envs 8`, `--seed 0`.

**`--n-envs 12` runs out of memory on this machine.** The first attempt died in
`swap_and_flatten` trying to allocate 33 MiB for a `(12, 2048, 353)` buffer:
twelve `SubprocVecEnv` workers each hold a full `DataStore`, which came to ~17 GB
of 31.8 GB. At 8 workers it is 11.2 GB and comfortable. The crash had already
overwritten `logs/full_expedition/best_model.zip`, which is why the pre-retrain
checkpoints were copied to `archive/models/pre_burn_2026-08-20/` first — they are
345-wide and unloadable now, but that was not knowable in advance.

### The eval curve is still noise with an envelope

Peak 93.20, final 62.56, adjacent evals swinging 27.33 -> 45.71 -> 62.56. Six
"new best" events across 3M steps. Nothing here contradicts the standing advice
to pick on a paired eval rather than on which save SB3 called best.

### The three rulers disagree, and the disagreement is structural

| ruler | what it says |
|---|---|
| `eval_tutorial.py` (fixed scenario, exactly reproducible) | **final** +12.0pp SUCCESS (90.0% vs 78.0%), fewer deaths AND fewer retreats |
| `agreement.py` (real captured positions, no simulator) | **indistinguishable**: 39.6% vs 38.6%, 18/17 discordant, p=1.000 |
| `ab_compare.py` (216 paired scenarios, `--self-check` clean) | **best_model** +9.3pp SUCCESS (58.3% vs 49.1%), CI [+2.8, +15.7], p=0.0078 |

The third contradicts the other two, and the rest of its own table explains why:

| metric | best_model | final |
|---|---|---|
| SUCCESS | **58.3%** | 49.1% |
| zero_death | 72.7% | **81.5%** (p=0.0043) |
| legacy_success | 71.8% | **81.0%** |
| wiped | 6.5% | **2.3%** |
| deaths/exp | 0.55 | **0.36** |
| retreats/exp | **1.13** | 1.35 |

`best_model` is not playing better; it is **fighting on where the final save
leaves**. SUCCESS requires `retreats == 0`, so a policy that retreats more is
scored down on the headline while beating the other arm on every survival
measure. `CLAUDE.md` already warns that SUCCESS is not comparable once retreat
exists; this is that warning firing on live data rather than in the abstract.

**The recommendation is the final save** (already the live checkpoint): it wins
the deterministic ruler outright — more successes *and* fewer retreats *and*
zero deaths, with no tradeoff to read around — ties the human-play ruler, and
loses the third only on a metric that penalises exactly the caution it is
rewarded for elsewhere.

### The best-vs-final question is not just unsettled, it changes sign

The archived table (kept in `CLAUDE.md`) had the **final** save ahead by +8.3pp
on SUCCESS over 216 scenarios, p=0.020. The identical comparison on this retrain
gives **best_model** ahead by 9.3pp, p=0.0078. Same tool, same flags, same
scenario count, opposite sign, both nominally significant.

That is worth stating plainly: **"prefer the final save" was never a rule, it
was one sample.** Run the comparison per retrain; do not carry the answer
forward.

### The stall tail is gone

`check_playbook.py` on the final save, against two archived baselines:

| | archived 6M | archived bundle | this run |
|---|---|---|---|
| combats measured | 281 | 304 | 307 |
| mean rounds | 3.4 | 3.2 | **2.0** |
| max rounds | 23 | **220** | **6** |
| >8 rounds | 5.3% | 1.6% | **0.0%** |

No combat in 307 exceeded six rounds. The 220-round combat that motivated the
per-combat step budget has no analogue here. Corpse-attacking also stopped being
a dead action: 8.3% of the turns where it was legal, against the 0.0% recorded
when the action was first added and promptly learned away.

**What caused the shortening is NOT established.** F4 (more attacks land above
raw 0.90), randomised loadouts, burn, and the longer-lasting corpse all landed in
one retrain, and this measurement cannot separate them. Do not attribute it.

### The thing this retrain cannot tell us

**Whether it helped.** Every pre-retrain checkpoint is 345-wide and will not
load, so there is no before/after — only "which of the two new saves is better".
The -16.7pp loadout-sensitivity result that justified the retrain was itself
measured under the old accuracy rule and the three-round corpse, so it is not a
like-for-like baseline either. Answering "did this help" requires retraining a
control at `--loadout-random-p 0.0` on the same engine and comparing paired,
which has not been done.

## A corpse blocks for four rounds, not three (2026-08-20)

`CORPSE_ALIVE_ROUND_LIMIT = 3` came straight off the game's own corpse monster
class (`monsters/corpse/corpse_A/corpse_A.info.darkest`, `life_time:
.alive_round_limit 3`), and the engine decremented it at the end of every round
including the one the corpse appeared in. So a monster killed in round 1 left a
corpse that was gone entering round **4**.

The game's own documentation says round **5**: *"a creature killed in round 1
will have its corpse on the field until the end of round 4, when it will
disappear and leave an unoccupied slot for round 5."* Fran confirmed it from
play and asked for the fix.

**Why the data did not settle this.** The file gives the *number* and not the
*comparison*. "alive_round_limit 3" is equally consistent with "tick it down
from 3 starting now" (three round-ends) and "survive three full rounds after
the one you appeared in" (four round-ends). Both readings honour the datum; only
the worked example distinguishes them.

So the fix **excludes the spawn round** rather than changing the constant to 4.
`CORPSE_ALIVE_ROUND_LIMIT` stays at 3 because 3 is what the game file says, and
overwriting a real data value with a number derived from prose would hide where
the adjustment actually lives. `CombatantState.corpse_spawn_round` records the
round, and `_tick_corpse_durations` skips it.

**Not cosmetic.** A corpse is an occupant of the monster formation — that is what
makes it block — so an extra round of corpse is an extra round the enemy line
does not collapse forward. `CHANGELOG` already records that corpse persistence
systematically penalises melee/front-rank comps that need the line to advance
into reach, and this moves that dial in the harder direction. It is also the
quantity `W_BLOCKING_CORPSE` scores, so the reward gradient shifts with it.

### The note that would have reverted this

`WIKI_MECHANICS.md` carried a footnote reading *"Data wins — the wiki's 'end of
the fourth round' is likely counting inclusively."* That is exactly the kind of
confident guess this file exists to prevent, and it dismissed the only source
that resolved the ambiguity. Corrected in place.

The general lesson, worth stating because the precedence table invites the
mistake: **game files outrank the wiki on a NUMBER, not on the semantics of a
number they never state.** A bare constant with no comparison is not a formula.

## Burn, a dead cache digest, and the first comp nobody could train on (2026-08-20)

Three findings, and the second is the one to read if you only read one.

### Burn: the third DoT, and it copies neither of the other two

Six of the Runaway's seven skills apply `.dotBurn`, which `effect_loader.py`
did not parse — so they resolved to real effect objects that did nothing. Burn
is now implemented. Three properties make it a genuinely different mechanic
rather than bleed with a new name, and each is a thing you get wrong by
copying the bleed code:

* **A stack count that decays, not an amount with a tick budget.** There is no
  `.duration` on any shipped burn effect, because there is nothing to count
  down — the stack ticks for its own value and then halves. `CombatantState`
  carries a plain `burn_stacks: int`, not a `DotInstance` list, and a second
  application *adds* to the fire rather than queueing an independent instance
  the way a second bleed does.
* **It ticks at the END of the burning actor's turn.** Bleed and blight tick at
  the START — that ordering was itself a real bug fix, and it is tactically
  load-bearing: an enemy standing on lethal blight dies before acting, which is
  what lets a player spend the turn elsewhere. Burn is the other way round. A
  burning enemy **always** gets to act, so "it is already dead, ignore it" does
  not buy the free turn against burn that it buys against blight. Three call
  sites, because a turn ends in three places: the hero path in `step()`, the
  enemy path in `_advance_to_next_hero_turn`, and the stunned `continue` branch
  — stun prevents acting, not burning.
* **It is irresistible, and that is honoured by NOT DRAWING.** Every shipped
  burn effect sets `.dot_irresistible true`, and no `burn_resist` stat exists
  in any file to roll against anyway. Rolling against a zero would give the
  same outcome and a *different RNG stream*, which silently invalidates every
  trace comparison downstream of it.

`.cure_burn` is its own key, sitting beside `.cure_bleed`/`.cure_poison` in the
binary's string table — so a bandage and an antivenom leave burn alone, as does
the bare `.cure`, which the binary scopes to bleed+blight. A test asserts no
effect cures burn as a side effect of curing something else.

**The one invented number is the base decay rate**, 0.5. `DECOMP.md` F6 has the
full argument: Fran's play observation for the magnitude, and the game's own
Spanish tooltip (*"a la disminución de Quemadura"*) for the sign, which was the
genuinely ambiguous half — decay is the fraction **lost**, so Firefly's +0.5
collapses its 7-9 stack into one burst tick instead of extending it. It is a
declared convention with a cheap falsifier that has **not** been run: capture
one burn tick with no Firefly debuff up and read whether 7 is followed by 3.

Burn reaches the observation, and it is **appended** to both the hero and enemy
slot vectors rather than inserted beside the other two DoTs — the same
discipline the retreat action was added under, so every index below it still
means what it meant. That is why a test pinning `doomed` at enemy-slot offset 8
did not have to move. Widths: `ENEMY_SLOT_SCALARS` 15 -> 16, `HERO_SLOT_SCALARS`
21 -> 22, observation **345** (base) / **353** (expedition).

Burn also counts toward `_is_doomed`, and it cannot be `amount * ticks`: a
decaying stack deals a different amount every tick, so a stack of 7 is worth 11
damage, not 7. `burn_total_outstanding` is that sum.

Still deferred, and deferred deliberately: Controlled Burn's rank *zones*
(`controlled_burn_amount`/`_duration`, a persistent effect on a rank, which is
machinery nothing else in the engine has) and Backdraft's `.dmg_per_burn_stack`
scaling. The mod's own file says those "require engine support" and ships
Backdraft as a plain `.cure_burn` instead, so the shipped behaviour is what is
implemented.

### The store cache's CSV digest had been dead since the metadata move

**This is the serious one.** `store_cache.fingerprint` built its CSV roots as
`csv_dir / s`, not `csv_root(csv_dir) / s`. The eight metadata directories moved
under `metadata/` on 2026-08-20 and that line did not follow them, so every CSV
root resolved to a path that no longer exists — and `_iter_files` skips a
missing root **silently**, by design, because a genuinely absent DLC subtree is
not an error.

Net effect: **editing or adding any metadata file did not invalidate the store
cache.** Comps, hero CSVs, quirks, trinkets — none of it. `CLAUDE.md` states the
requirement ("the digest has to stat the real files or an edit to one would not
invalidate the cache") and the code did not meet it; the comment sitting directly
above `CSV_SUBTREES` says the same thing.

It surfaced only because Fran added a comp directory while the suite was running
and a freshly-loaded store had 134 loadouts against the cache's 133. Nothing was
testing for it, because every other digest test drives a synthetic tree where
`csv_dir` and `csv_root(csv_dir)` coincide — which is exactly how a bug hides
from a well-tested function. Both new tests were confirmed to fail with the fix
reverted.

### A comp can now field a Fire's Edge class without moving any archived eval

Fran curated `COMPS/Comps/Stun Control Burn/` — the existing Stun Control comp
with one slot swapped for a Runaway holding Firefly / Run and Hide / Hearthlight
/ Backdraft. Two things were wrong with it, neither his:

1. **The Runaway was dropped silently.** Neither `CompositionLoader.HERO_CLASS_MAP`
   nor `comp_loadout_loader.HERO_DISPLAY_NAME_TO_ID` knew the two new classes, so
   the comp resolved as a **3-hero party** — the precise failure
   `modded_hero_comp_names` exists to catch for fan-made classes, except these
   two really do load. Both maps now carry `Runaway` and `Duelist`.
2. **It could never be trained on.** `_build_hero_pool` iterates `store.comps`
   (the CSV), and a comp registered only as a loadout directory has no row there.

The fix is *not* to give it a CSV row. `pick_representative_comps` samples
`store.comps`, so one extra row changes which twelve comps every archived
`--comp-seed` selects and breaks comparability with every eval this project has
run — including the -16.7pp loadout-sensitivity measurement. Instead
`_build_hero_pool` appends loadout-only comps **that field a Fire's Edge class**.

That membership rule is a *reason*, not a hand-kept list, and the distinction
matters: a rule of "any comp with no CSV row" would have swept up **The Old
Road** (the tutorial party, which must stay pinned rather than sampled) and
**Feral Vapors** (outside the pool since long before this). It also means a comp
dropped into `COMPS/Comps/<name>/` is trained on with no code change — which is
the point, because *which* four skills a Runaway should hold is a question for a
comp search, not for a hardcoded guess. Fran declined to pick one for exactly
that reason.

One incidental find: the two files speak different dialects. A loadout JSON says
`"The Cove"`, the CSV says `"Cove"`. Nothing noticed because the CSV had been the
only way into this pool.

Training pool 132 -> 133; eval selections byte-identical, asserted per seed.

## A stance is not a special case: the Duelist and the Runaway (2026-08-20)

Two Fire's Edge classes added, 18 hero classes -> **20**. The observation widens
with them (`_hero_class_index` is `sorted(store.heroes)`), so **337 -> 345 and
every existing checkpoint stops loading.** That is deliberate and it is the
third reason to retrain that has accumulated, alongside F4's corrected to-hit
rule and randomised loadouts; doing them together costs one retrain instead of
three.

### The Abomination was never a special case

`combat_engine.py` chose a stance-change's effect list at two sites with

```python
mode_effects = skill.human_effects if performer.mode == "human" else skill.beast_effects
```

which reads as "there are two modes, human and beast". There are not. The
convention in the data is **one `.<mode>_effects` list per `.valid_modes`
entry**, and the Abomination is simply the only vanilla hero who uses it. The
Duelist uses the identical shape:

```
.valid_modes stance_defensive stance_aggressive
.stance_defensive_effects  switch_mode_stance_aggressive_self duelist_aggressive_buff1
.stance_aggressive_effects switch_mode_stance_defensive_self  duelist_defensive_buff1
```

Under the old lookup **every stance change she made fell down the `beast`
branch and found nothing** — a silent no-op, not a crash: the stance simply
never changed, so she would have been stuck in one stance for the whole raid
with four of her seven skills permanently unreachable.

`SkillLevelData.mode_effects: dict[str, list[str]]` is the general form, and
both sites are now `skill.mode_effects.get(performer.mode, [])`. It is built
gated on `.valid_modes` rather than on any key ending in `_effects`, because
the latter swallows `.self_effects` and invents a mode called `self`.
`tests/data/test_fires_edge.py::test_the_abomination_still_transforms` is the guard
that matters here — generalising a special case is only safe if the special
case still works.

### Three smaller traps, each of which would have been silent

- **`CombatantState.mode` defaults to `"human"`, and the Duelist has no `human`
  mode.** Every one of her skills is stance-gated, so a hero built without
  consulting the definition would have had **zero legal skills**. `reset()`
  already used `hd.modes[0].id`, and her raid default is the defensive stance,
  so this was correct by luck rather than by intent — it is now pinned by a test.
- **`is_raid_default` was parsed with a default of `True`.** The Abomination's
  raw data marks only `human`; the loader was reporting `beast` as a raid
  default too. Nothing reads the flag today, which is exactly why it was wrong
  — and exactly why anything that starts reading it would have been wrong too.
  Now defaults to `False`, the faithful reading.
- **The Duelist is unrestricted, like the Abomination.** All seven equipped
  always, a *mode* deciding which four are usable. `UNRESTRICTED_HEROES` in
  `env/expedition_env.py` replaces the hardcoded `== "abomination"` and is
  imported by `tools/loadout_sensitivity.py`, so the arm that tool calls
  "curated" means the same thing there as in the env. Cutting her to four would
  empty a whole stance — the stance-hero version of the comp-JSON bug that
  dropped all three of the Abomination's beast attacks.

### What is verified, and what is not

Verified in a real combat: she opens in `stance_defensive` offering exactly
Anticipation/Touche/Feint/Disengage, Anticipation flips her to
`stance_aggressive` **without consuming the turn** (`.is_continue_turn`), and
the aggressive stance then offers Coup de Grace and The Boot. Two absences
there are correct rather than missing — Anticipation is `.per_turn_limit 1` and
was just spent, and Fleche is `.launch 432` while she stands in rank 1.

**The Runaway loads and plays, but her entire kit is currently inert.** Six of
her seven skills apply burn, and `dotBurn` is parsed nowhere — `data/effect_loader.py`
reads `dotBleed` and `dotPoison` only, so `RW Burn 5` resolves to a real effect
object that does nothing. She is a strictly weak hero until burn exists. See
`DECOMP.md` F6 for what the binary says about burn, including the one number
(the base decay rate) that neither the binary strings nor any shipped file
answers.

### Neither class is in any comp, so neither is trained

`store.comp_loadouts` has 133 entries covering **18** classes; the scraped comps
predate The Fire's Edge and reference neither new hero. `_build_hero_pool`
samples from those comps, so as things stand a retrain would pay the full
337 -> 345 invalidation and **never once field a Duelist or a Runaway**.

Adding comps for them is not free either: `pick_representative_comps` samples
`store.comps`, so a new row changes which comps every archived `--comp-seed`
selects — the same trap that keeps the tutorial party out of the sampled pool.
Recorded here as an open decision rather than resolved in passing.

## The policy is half as good with an unfamiliar loadout (2026-08-20)

The loadout phase's gate, run before spending any compute on it. The hypothesis:
*training only ever shows a class the four skills its comp curated, so the same class
holding a different legal four is off-distribution.*

`tools/loadout_sensitivity.py`, one model, two loadouts, the same 72 dungeons
(`logs/full_expedition/best_model.zip`, 12 comps x 3 bands x 2 episodes):

| metric | curated | random 4-of-7 | delta | 95% CI |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **SUCCESS** | 33.33% | **16.67%** | **-16.67pp** | [-27.78, -5.56] |
| completed | 36.11% | 19.44% | -16.67pp | [-27.78, -5.56] |
| reward | 29.94 | 13.65 | -16.29 | [-28.30, -4.41] |
| wiped | 12.50% | 22.22% | +9.72pp | [-1.39, +20.83] |
| combats reached | 8.65 | 8.03 | -0.62 | [-1.43, +0.14] |

Exact McNemar on SUCCESS: **15 curated-only wins, 3 random-only, 54 tied, p = 0.0075**.
Three metrics with intervals excluding zero, and the signal lives in 18 discordant pairs
rather than in a shifted average.

**Success roughly halves.** For a project whose eval noise has swallowed every fidelity
fix it ever made, that is an unusually large and unusually clean effect -- and it is the
first direct measurement of the thing Fran described as *"several classes are optimised
for certain loadouts"*.

Worth noting what does NOT move: `zero_death` is flat at 52.78%. The party is not simply
dying more; it is *finishing* less, with more wipes and slightly fewer combats reached.
A policy handed skills it has not trained with does not lose the fight so much as fail to
close it.

### How the pairing is kept honest

The randomised loadout is drawn from a **separate RNG** (`_loadout_rng`), not the scenario
sampler. Drawing it from `_sampler_rng` would advance the generator that also picks the
monsters and the torch, so the two arms would face different dungeons and the delta would
be luck -- exactly the failure `tools/ab_compare.py` exists to prevent.
`tests/env/test_random_loadouts.py` pins that both arms still draw identical enemies and torch
after resolving loadouts.

`--self-check` plays the curated loadout as both arms and every delta must print 0.00; it
does. Two measurements in this project have returned a confident, wrong zero, so the
control runs before the result is read, not after it is doubted.

### The knob itself

`ExpeditionEnv(loadout_random_p=)` fields a hero with a random legal 4-of-7 instead of its
comp's four. **The default is 0.0 and is proven byte-identical** to the previous behaviour
across every comp in the pool -- archived checkpoints keep their training distribution
until a retrain deliberately changes it. Two rules survive the change: the Abomination is
never restricted (the game gives him all seven), and duplicate classes draw independently,
because skills resolve positionally and 37 of 133 loadouts repeat a class.

## The capture run: the telemetry pays, and the curio theory finally dies (2026-08-20)

One `--boot --act` on the Old Road with `tools/dbwin_capture.py` alongside, into
`archive/traces/tutorial_20260820.log` (2476 lines). The bridge booted a fresh campaign
into the freed `profile_0`, won both fights, took the loot, correctly **skipped** the
`rooB` trapped chest (`needs skeleton_key` -- a judgement, not a failure), and reached
`-- raid over --`.

### The scenario I built is now validated against the running game

`data/tutorial_loader.py` infers the encounters from the map's `mash_index`, and
`DECOMP.md` flagged that as its one inference. The game says:

```
FillOutBattle with mash entry index:0      -> Brigand Cutthroat
FillOutBattle with mash entry index:1      -> Brigand Bloodletter + Brigand Fusilier
```

Which is exactly `tutorial_1` then `tutorial_2`. Prediction made from data files,
confirmed by the game. That is the loop the phase was built for.

### Three things the bridge was inferring, now simply announced

| line | count | replaces |
|---|---|---|
| `Moved onto areatile 1..5` | 5 | the corridor position, derived from `lit`-runs and cross-checked against `areatile` |
| `Loot: Rolled TableEntry:C in LootTable:A` -> `ItemEntry:100 gold-` | 9 | nested loot tables resolving to concrete items -- the same machinery curio outcomes use |
| `check pCurrentBattleActor #1: <name>` | 18 | who is acting, against 24 `Chance to hit` / `Roll to hit` pairs -- the attribution the 166 archived samples lacked |

### The curio: position is eliminated, with three independent witnesses

The bridge walked to tile 4 and said so (`at tile 4, on travellers_tent_tutorial (tile
4)`). The game said `Moved onto areatile 4`. Fran watched it stand in front of the tent.
Six `W` presses over two visits, and the tent stayed `active` with the pack unchanged.

And `W` is not the wrong key. The game's own tutorial text for curios reads *"[CLICK] or
press [W] to investigate them. Take a look at this tent..."* -- naming this exact tent as
its worked example.

**So the remaining lead is the half never tried: `[CLICK]`, which the game lists first.**
Five position theories have now been falsified; a sixth is not the move.

### And the instrumentation missed its own moment

No frame was banked. The sequence reached attempt 3 of 6 and then `a fight started on the
way`, which returns through a path that does not photograph anything -- `_bank_curio`
fires only at the final attempt or on `_walked_past`. **The commonest exit was the one
exit with no camera on it.** Fixed: every giving-up path banks now.

### Confirmed from live play: the second fight is a size-2 fight

Reported by Fran watching the run, and true: `brigand_blood_A` is **size 2** with
**`corpse_large_A`**, and the capture mentions `corpse_large_` 11 times. That is why the
log advises the same Bloodletter at "rank 1" under SMITE and "rank 2" under STUNNING BLOW
-- it occupies both ranks and answers to both, then leaves a two-rank corpse.

Open, and worth a look: the bridge maps rank to pixels as four equal slots, which is not
obviously right for a sprite spanning two of them, nor after a large corpse shifts the
line. No evidence yet that a click actually landed wrong -- only that the geometry
assumption is untested in exactly the case this fight creates.

## The root, sorted (2026-08-20)

Twenty directories and twenty-four loose files, of which eleven were stray run logs that
`CLAUDE.md` had already said do not belong there. Now **twelve directories and seven
files**, and each directory answers "what does this help with" on its own name.

| moved | to | why |
|---|---|---|
| `act*.log`, `run*.log` (11) | `archive/logs/` | run artefacts, untracked; the rule already existed |
| `provisions/` (1 jpg) | `captures/` | a one-file directory of reference imagery |
| `__pycache__/` | deleted | already gitignored |
| `HEROES/ SKILLS/ ENEMIES/ TRINKETS/ QUIRKS/ CAMP_SKILLS/ COMPS/ ENEMY_COMPS/` | `metadata/` | eight of twenty directories spent on one thing |
| `CHANGELOG ASSUMPTIONS BRIDGE DECOMP PLAYBOOK WIKI_MECHANICS` | `docs/` | `README`, `CLAUDE` and `AGENTS` stay: a tool looks for each in the root |

**`CSV_DIR` did not change, and that is the point.** It is still the repo root, still
hardcoded in `env/gym_env.py`, still re-declared in ~35 test files -- none of which needed
touching, because every one passes it to a loader instead of naming a subdirectory. The
